NHH · Norwegian School of Economics · FIE402 Corporate Finance (E)

Corporate FinanceThe complete exam manual

Everything needed for the three-hour school exam, built from the ground up: capital structure, payout policy, security issuance, valuation with leverage, options and real options, mergers and acquisitions, and corporate governance. Read on its own, this document is meant to replace the textbook.

Built from the 2026 syllabus, the examination formula sheet, and every past exam paper and solution key from 2015 to autumn 2025.
Exam: 3 hours, closed book, pen and paper. Calculator and one bilingual dictionary permitted. Written and answered in English.

Part 0 — Foundations

↑ contents

0 · The course, the exam, and how to use this manual

Six exercises, 100 points, three hours, no multiple choice. That is the paper Andrey Kurbatov set in H2024 and again in H2025, and it is the paper you will sit. Two of the six are pure discussion exercises at 12 points each, so a quarter of the grade is decided by prose. The rest turns on whether you show your calculations and whether your numbers survive the examiner's checks.

0.1 What corporate finance asks

The subject has one governing question: does the way a firm is financed change what the firm is worth, and if so, through which channel?

Start from the answer that is almost right. A firm is worth the present value of the cash flows its assets produce, and debt and equity are two claims on that same stream. Cutting a fixed pie into two slices does not change the pie. With no taxes, no bankruptcy costs, no conflicts between claim holders and no private information, that is exactly true: Modigliani and Miller's Proposition I, which the whole course hangs from.

The course is then the systematic removal of the phrase "with no". Each friction you put back opens one channel through which financing reaches value:

VL = VU + PV(TS) − PV(distress costs) − PV(agency costs) + PV(agency benefits)
The organising identity of the course. VU is what the firm would be worth financed entirely by equity, which MM I says is all there is. Each later term is a correction created by one named imperfection: interest deductibility (k7), costly bankruptcy (k8), shareholder-creditor conflict (k9, k10), debt disciplining managers (k11). Not on the formula sheet.
Why can financing change value at all? Because a claim is never only a share of cash flow, it is also a bundle of rights and incentives. The tax authority treats interest differently from dividends. A creditor who can force liquidation destroys real value doing it. A shareholder in a nearly bankrupt firm holds what is effectively an option, and option holders like risk. Every result here traces back to taxes, deadweight costs or incentives. When a derivation escapes you, ask which of the three is operating.

The material falls into three blocks and the exam samples all three every year: valuation machinery (k1–k5, k17–k20), capital structure and its frictions (k6–k16), and options, transactions and governance (k21–k27).

0.2 The exam you will sit

Three hours, closed book, pen and paper. A calculator and one bilingual dictionary are permitted. The paper is written in English and answered in English. The grade A to F is set on the total, so no single exercise has to be passed. Points are printed next to every sub-question, so the paper tells you how to spend your time:

PositionH2024H2025
Exercise 1M&A motives, verbal, synergies bannedBlack-Scholes intuition, verbal, no computation
Exercise 2Corporate governance, verbalCorporate governance, verbal
Exercise 3MM without taxes, recapitalisationMM without taxes, dividends, recapitalisation
Exercise 4Risk shifting and hedgingWACC and APV, two periods, nine linked steps
Exercise 5Twin firm → rU → rE → WACC → value by WACC and APVReal options: value of information, exit, indifference point
Exercise 6Real option by risk-neutral valuationM&A: implied deal probability from market prices

Read the shape, not the topics. Positions 1 and 2 are verbal at 12 points each. Position 3 has been MM with a recapitalisation both times, at 18 points. The back half carries the heavy quantitative items, with real options a fixed 20-point fixture since 2017.

writing minutes = 1.6 × points
180 minutes for 100 points is 1.8 per point, but not all of it should be writing. Reserve 10 minutes at the start to read the paper and fix your order, and 10 at the end for the checks in 0.5. The remaining 160 give 1.6 per point: 19 minutes for a 12-point exercise, 29 for an 18-point one, 32 for a 20-point one.
The chained sub-question trap Kurbatov builds his exercises so that (b) consumes your answer to (a) and (c) consumes (b); H2025 Exercise 4 ran to nine linked steps. One slip propagates through everything after it, and this is where the largest blocks of points are lost. Carry four decimals on every rate: H2025 demanded it, and the keys penalise drift beyond about 0.001. If a sub-answer will not come, give it a symbol, state the assumption and carry it forward, because later steps are marked on method.

0.3 The formula sheet, and what you must memorise

The sheet gives you the free cash flow definition, the perpetuity and growing perpetuity, the CAPM, rE = rU + (D/E)(rU − rD), the βU weighting, pre-tax and after-tax WACC, binomial replication with Δ and the risk-neutral form with ρ, and the M&A stock-swap NPVs with the exchange-ratio bound. Now read it for what it omits. These are not on the sheet:

  • VL = VU + PV(TS), and above all which rate discounts the tax shield (k7, k18)
  • The debt policy rule Dt = d·VtL and the debt adjustment between periods (k19)
  • Put-call parity and the payoff bounds on options (k21)
  • βE = N(d1)(V/E)βU, and equity as a call on firm value (k24)
  • The Myers-Majluf setup, including the rationality check on beliefs (k12)
  • Everything about real options: waiting, the value of information, the exit option (k25)

Those six lines have been worth 20 to 40 points in recent papers. Memorisation pays here and almost nowhere else.

0.4 How to write an answer that scores

The solution keys are consistent about what earns marks. Four moves, in this order, on every quantitative sub-question.

  1. State the method first. "I value this by APV: VU at rU, then the tax shield at rU because the firm rebalances to constant D/E." One sentence, and the examiner knows the choice was deliberate.
  2. Show the arithmetic. Write the substituted expression, not only the result: 40/(0.09 − 0.02) = 571.43. The H2017 key says it flatly, no calculation, no points.
  3. Name the mechanism. Write the words "this is debt overhang", "this is asset substitution", "this is the coinsurance effect". The keys award marks for the label itself.
  4. Run the check and write one line saying it passed. See 0.5.
Why naming the mechanism earns marks on its own An examiner cannot see your reasoning, only your page. Two candidates can produce the same number, one by understanding and one by pattern matching against a lecture example. The phrase "debt overhang" is the cheapest signal that separates them, because the label commits you to a claim about who gains and who loses. That is also why the verbal exercises exist: they strip the arithmetic away so only the mechanism is left to grade.
What a 12-point verbal answer looks like These questions carry sentence budgets. H2025 Exercise 2 asked what a shareholder holding 5 to 10% can do about a shirking CEO: three actions, four points each, two to three sentences per action. Answer in that shape. One sentence naming the action, one on the mechanism that disciplines the CEO, one on its limitation. H2025 also credited creative but well-argued answers, so an unusual fourth action costs nothing.
Treating FIE402 as a course in arithmetic The expensive misreading is to grind the WACC and APV exercise until it is automatic and treat the verbal material as filler. In both Kurbatov papers 24 of the 100 points went to prose with no calculation, and much of the quantitative credit went to interpretation rather than computation: who gains, who loses, is the equilibrium rational. Arithmetic alone stalls in the middle of the paper.

0.5 The five consistency checks

The examiner runs a small, fixed set of checks. Run them first and you catch your own errors while showing control of the material. Every later chapter refers back to this list by number.

#The checkWhere it appliesWhat it catches
1Value by WACC = value by APVLevered valuation (k17–k20)Wrong tax shield rate, wrong debt level, stale weights
2[E/(E+D)]βE + [D/(E+D)]βD = βUUnlever and relever (k4)Wrong weights, or the (1−τc) version this course rejects
3Creditors' gain + shareholders' gain = the project's NPVAgency problems (k9, k10)Double counting, or a transfer mistaken for value creation
4Creditors' gain = shareholders' loss when total value is fixedRisk shifting, debt overhang (k9, k10)Believing shareholders gain from an action that only moves value
5Are investors' beliefs rational given the action taken?Asymmetric information (k12)Stopping once α has been computed
Why a check is worth the two minutes Checks 1 and 2 are redundant computations: they recompute a number you already have along a route using different inputs, so a wrong weight or rate breaks the agreement. Checks 3 and 4 are conservation laws: value cannot appear or vanish when you merely move it between claimants. Check 5 is different in kind, asking whether the situation you computed could survive once investors see what the manager did. The keys single it out as the step candidates skip.

0.6 How to use this manual

Chapters run k0 to k29 and are built to be read in order the first time: short setup, formula, a mechanism box explaining why it holds, then worked numbers. The worked examples are the point of the book. Do not read them, redo them on paper with the solution covered, because that is the only study activity that resembles the exam.

The boxes have fixed jobs. A mechanism box answers "why does this work" and is the raw material for verbal answers. An examiner box says how the topic has been tested. A warning box marks a way to lose points. A link box connects forward or backward.

If you are short of time Read k4 (the twin-firm routine, present in all eleven papers), then k17–k20 (WACC and APV on one case, usually the largest exercise), k6 (MM and recapitalisation), k25 (real options, a fixed 20 points), k27 (governance, a guaranteed 12) and k26 (M&A). Those cover the large majority of the points in both Kurbatov papers. The best insurance outside them is k12, Myers-Majluf, absent from both Kurbatov papers after five earlier appearances. Finish with k28 for answer templates, and keep k29 open while you drill.
↑ contents

1 · Time value, NPV and investment rules

Almost every number the exam asks for is the end of a discounted cash flow, and most are the end of a perpetuity: the levered value FCF/(rwacc − g) in k17, a perpetual tax shield in k7, the exit option of H2025 Exercise 5 that turns a perpetuity into a truncated annuity. The formulas are printed on the exam sheet, which is why the exam never tests whether you can write them down. It tests which date the answer sits on and which cash flow belongs in the numerator.

1.1 Discounting: moving money across dates

A discount rate is a price. It says what one unit of money at date t is worth in money at date t+1, and it is set by what investors could earn elsewhere on an equally risky claim. Everything else is bookkeeping around that price:

PV0 = Cn / (1 + r)n
NPV = CF0 + CF1/(1+r) + CF2/(1+r)2 + … + CFN/(1+r)N
The first line moves a single amount from date n back to date 0, the second adds up a stream. Two rules follow, used in every later chapter: amounts dated at the same period can be added, amounts at different periods cannot, and anything a present-value formula returns is itself dated. Most errors in this course break one of those two, not the algebra.

1.2 The perpetuity, and where the formula comes from

A perpetuity pays a constant C at date 1, date 2, date 3, and on without end:

PV0 = C / r
C is the constant cash flow, r the discount rate matching its risk. The result is the value one period before the first payment. On the exam sheet.
Why is an infinite stream worth a finite amount? Do not sum the series; use self-similarity, which is faster and is the argument the examiner wants to see. Stand at date 0 holding the perpetuity. One period later you have collected C and are looking at an identical perpetuity, worth PV0 again, because nothing about it has changed. So PV0 = (C + PV0)/(1 + r), hence PV0·r = C, hence PV0 = C/r. The sum is finite because the discount factor shrinks geometrically while the cash flow stays flat. The same argument gives VL = FCF/(rwacc − g) in k17.
Which date does C/r sit on? C/r is a date-0 value only when the first payment arrives at date 1. It is always the value one period before the first cash flow, never on the same date. If the first payment lands at date 3, C/r is a date-2 value with two more periods of discounting to go. This convention costs more points than any piece of algebra here, and it is the error the keys call out when candidates use 100 rather than 100 × 1.03 as a certainty equivalent: the cash flow came from the wrong date.

1.3 The growing perpetuity

Now let the payment grow at a constant rate g. The date-1 cash flow is C1, the date-2 cash flow C1(1+g), and so on:

PV0 = C1 / (r − g), valid only when r > g
C1 is the date-1 cash flow, already grown by one period. The date convention is unchanged: the answer sits one period before C1. On the exam sheet, and the engine behind every terminal value in k2 and VL = FCF1/(rwacc − g) in k17.
What is r − g actually measuring? Each year the cash flow is (1+g) times larger while the discount factor is 1/(1+r) times smaller, so successive payments shrink in present value at roughly the net rate r − g. That net rate, not r alone, is what makes the sum converge. Push g towards r and every payment carries the same present value, the sum diverges, and the formula returns a number that explodes or turns negative. So r > g is a diagnostic, not a technicality: if terminal growth exceeds the cost of capital, you have mis-estimated one of them, and should say so rather than report a negative firm value.
Worked example 1: a growing perpetuity, and what a delay costs

A toll road concession generates a free cash flow of 40 at date 1, growing 2% per year forever. The discount rate is 9%.

InputValue
First cash flow C1, at date 140.00
Growth rate g2%
Discount rate r9%

Step 1 — value the stream. PV0 = 40/(0.09 − 0.02) = 40/0.07 = 571.4286

Step 2 — verify the date. Stand at date 1, just after the first 40 is paid. What remains is a growing perpetuity whose next cash flow is C2 = 40 × 1.02 = 40.80, worth 40.80/0.07 = 582.85714 at date 1. Roll back together with the date-1 cash flow: (40 + 582.85714)/1.09 = 622.85714/1.09 = 571.4286 ✓

Step 3 — delay by two years. Permits push the first cash flow to date 3, size and growth unchanged. The formula is the same but its output is now dated 2: PV2 = 40/0.07 = 571.4286. With 1.092 = 1.1881,
PV0 = 571.4286/1.1881 = 480.96

Step 4 — price the delay. 571.43 − 480.96 = 90.47, or 15.83% of the undelayed value.

Check: the ratio of the two values must equal the two-period discount factor. 480.96/571.43 = 0.8417 and 1/1.1881 = 0.8417 ✓ Had you discounted three periods, the ratio would be 0.7722 and the check would catch it.

1.4 Annuities: a perpetuity minus a perpetuity

An annuity pays a constant C at dates 1 through N and then stops. A growing annuity does the same with growth g:

PV0 = (C/r) × [1 − 1/(1+r)N]
PV0 = [C1/(r − g)] × [1 − ((1+g)/(1+r))N]
The bracket is the fraction of the perpetuity's value falling inside the first N periods, and (1/r)[1 − 1/(1+r)N] is the annuity factor. Neither line is on the exam sheet, and neither needs to be.
Why the annuity formula is just two perpetuities Buy a perpetuity starting at date 1, worth C/r today. Sell an identical one starting at date N+1: at date N it is worth C/r, so today it is worth (C/r)/(1+r)N. What you keep is precisely dates 1 through N. Subtracting gives (C/r)[1 − 1/(1+r)N], the annuity formula rebuilt from the one line the sheet does give you. Learn the construction, because it is what real options questions use: abandoning a project at date N converts a perpetuity into an annuity, and the exit option is worth exactly the tail you gave up.
Worked example 2: an eight-year machine under three rules

A machine costs 130 today and produces a free cash flow of 25 at the end of each of the next eight years, then is worthless. The cost of capital is 7%.

Step 1 — annuity factor. 1.078 = 1.7182, so 1/1.078 = 0.58201 and the factor is (1 − 0.58201)/0.07 = 0.41799/0.07 = 5.9713

Step 2 — present value. 25 × 5.9713 = 149.2825

Step 3 — NPV. 149.2825 − 130 = 19.2825. Positive, so buy it.

Step 4 — check by the two-perpetuity route. A perpetuity of 25 is worth 25/0.07 = 357.1429. The tail from date 9 onward is worth 357.1429 at date 8, so 357.1429/1.078 = 207.8604 today. Subtract: 357.1429 − 207.8604 = 149.2825 ✓ Same answer from different intermediate numbers.

Step 5 — IRR. Solve 25 × (annuity factor at rate y) = 130, so the required factor is 130/25 = 5.2000, giving y = 10.7088%. Above 7%, so IRR also accepts.

Step 6 — payback. Cumulative cash flow reaches 130 after 130/25 = 5.2 years.

Check: all three rules accept, and they agree because the project is conventional (one outflow, then inflows) and stands alone. Section 1.6 removes the second condition and the agreement collapses.

1.5 The NPV rule

Compute the present value of every cash flow the decision causes, at the rate matching their risk, subtract what you pay, and take the project if the result is positive:

NPV = PV(all cash flows the decision causes) − investment today
Accept if NPV > 0. Between mutually exclusive projects, take the highest NPV.
NPV is money at date 0: the amount by which the decision raises the value of the claims on the firm today. It is also additive, which is why firm value can be assembled from parts (VU plus PV(TS) in k18) or split between claimants (k9, k10), and why all five consistency checks in k0 are possible. Returns are not additive, so none of that can be done with percentages.
How the exam actually uses this Never as a question in its own right, because the formulas are on the sheet. They are the last line of a longer problem and the marks sit before them. In V2024 Problem 2 and 2016 MC3 the levered value is FCF/(rwacc − g), with all the work in getting rwacc. In H2025 Exercise 5 an exit option replaces a perpetuity with a truncated annuity. In 2023 Problem 3 and 2021 Problem 4 the real option is a stream starting at a later date, exactly as in Worked example 1 Step 3. Write the timeline before the formula.

1.6 Why NPV beats IRR and payback

Keep the machine from Worked example 2 and set it against a warehouse costing the same 130 today and paying a single 275 at date 8. Both are conventional, both acceptable alone, and you can build only one. The warehouse is worth 275/1.078 = 160.0525 today, so its NPV is 30.05 against the machine's 19.28:

Decision ruleMachine MWarehouse WIt picks
NPV at r = 7%19.2830.05W
IRR10.7088%9.8180%M
Payback, 6-year cutoff5.2 years8.0 yearsM
Why IRR reverses the ranking The IRR is the rate at which a project's own cash flows break even, so it describes the shape of the project, not the market it lives in. Early cash flows are barely discounted at any rate, giving a high break-even rate but little gain when capital is cheap. Late cash flows are crushed at high rates and rewarded enormously at low ones, giving a low break-even rate and a steep profile. The profiles therefore cross, here at 8.93%, and ranking by IRR is ranking as though the discount rate were always the crossover rate. The actual rate is 7%, below the crossing, so IRR gets it backwards. Payback fails more bluntly: it discards every cash flow after the cutoff, so a project whose value arrives late can never pass.
"The IRR is the return I earn on the project" It is not, except for one payment out followed by one payment in. It is an internal break-even discount rate, and reading it as a return produces three failures. It is blind to scale, preferring a tiny project at 40% to a large one at 15%. It reverses on a project that takes money in first and pays out later, where a high IRR is bad news. And it need not exist uniquely: two sign changes give two IRRs and no rule at all. Use IRR to describe a project, never to choose between projects.
Where this reappears The perpetuity-minus-perpetuity construction is the mechanical core of the exit and abandonment options in k25, a 20-point item in every Kurbatov paper. The delayed perpetuity of Worked example 1 is the option to wait: a project you may start in two years is a stream beginning at date 3. Whenever you value a stream, value it a second way. That route takes under a minute and stands between one misplaced date and a whole exercise of cascaded errors.
↑ contents

2 · Free cash flow

Every valuation on this exam begins with a number you have to build yourself. The examiner hands you a projection table (sales, costs, depreciation, investment) and expects free cash flow back, correctly assembled, before anything else can happen. That is 2021 Problem 3, 2022 Problem 3 and 2017V Problem 4 almost verbatim, and it is the first step of the nine-step valuation in H2025 Exercise 4. The formula itself sits on the exam formula sheet, so the points are not in remembering it. They are in the three places people reliably go wrong: taxing the wrong profit line, getting the sign on working capital backwards, and capitalising a terminal year that contains a one-off item.

2.1 What free cash flow is, and whose money it is

Free cash flow is the cash that the firm's operations generate in a period, after paying for everything those operations need in order to keep running, and before any of it is handed to the people who financed the firm. "Free" means free to be distributed: to lenders as interest and principal, and to shareholders as dividends or repurchases. It is one pot, and the whole point is that we measure the pot before we decide how to split it.

Because we measure it before the split, free cash flow does not depend on how the firm is financed. The same asset, run the same way, produces the same FCF whether it is funded entirely by equity or is loaded with debt. This is why FCF is often called unlevered free cash flow, and it is the property that makes the rest of the course work.

2.2 The formula, line by line

This is the version printed on your formula sheet, and the only one you should ever write down:

FCFt = EBITt(1 − τc) + Depreciationt − CapExt − ΔNWCt + Othert
EBIT earnings before interest and taxes  ·  τc corporate tax rate  ·  CapEx capital expenditure, the full cash cost in the year it is paid  ·  ΔNWC the change in net working capital from last period to this one  ·  Other one-off after-tax items the problem gives you, such as the proceeds from selling a machine.

Read it as four moves. First, take operating profit and tax it as if the firm had no debt at all: EBIT(1 − τc) is called unlevered net income. Second, add depreciation back. Third, subtract the cash the firm actually spent on long-lived assets. Fourth, subtract the cash it tied up in the balance sheet.

Why does depreciation go through the tax line and then come straight back out? Depreciation is not a payment. Nobody receives it. But the tax authorities allow it as a deduction, so it genuinely lowers the cash the firm pays in tax. The formula therefore lets it reduce EBIT (so the tax bill is right) and then adds the full amount back (so the cash flow is right). The net effect of a depreciation charge D on cash is τc·D of tax saved, and running it through both lines is exactly how you capture that. This is the same logic you will meet again as the interest tax shield in chapter 7: a deduction is worth its tax rate times its size.
Worked example 1: free cash flow straight from a projection

A firm projects the following for the next three years. The corporate tax rate is τc = 25%. The firm has 300 of debt paying 8%, so interest expense is 24 every year. All figures in NOK million.

LineYear 1Year 2Year 3
Sales400.0460.0500.0
Operating costs (excl. depreciation)−240.0−276.0−300.0
Depreciation−60.0−60.0−60.0
EBIT100.0124.0140.0
Interest expense (not used below)−24.0−24.0−24.0
EBIT(1 − τc) = unlevered net income75.093.0105.0
+ Depreciation+60.0+60.0+60.0
− CapEx−70.0−70.0−80.0
− ΔNWC−12.0−8.0−5.0
Free cash flow53.075.080.0

Step 1 — build EBIT. Year 1: 400.0 − 240.0 − 60.0 = 100.0. Year 2: 460.0 − 276.0 − 60.0 = 124.0. Year 3: 500.0 − 300.0 − 60.0 = 140.0.

Step 2 — tax EBIT, not EBT. Year 1: 100.0 × 0.75 = 75.0. Year 2: 124.0 × 0.75 = 93.0. Year 3: 140.0 × 0.75 = 105.0. The interest of 24 is deliberately ignored.

Step 3 — the cash adjustments. Year 1: 75.0 + 60.0 − 70.0 − 12.0 = 53.0. Year 2: 93.0 + 60.0 − 70.0 − 8.0 = 75.0. Year 3: 105.0 + 60.0 − 80.0 − 5.0 = 80.0.

Check: rebuild year 1 from the bottom of the income statement instead. Net income = (100.0 − 24.0) × 0.75 = 57.0, and the after-tax interest added back is 24.0 × 0.75 = 18.0, giving 57.0 + 18.0 = 75.0, exactly the unlevered net income from Step 2. Year 2: 75.0 + 18.0 = 93.0. Year 3: 87.0 + 18.0 = 105.0. Both routes give FCF of 53.0, 75.0 and 80.0. ✓

2.3 Why interest never enters free cash flow

This is the single most important idea in the chapter, and it is what makes chapters 17 and 18 possible.

Why is the cost of debt excluded from a cash flow that is meant to be complete? Because it is charged twice otherwise. The discount rate you will apply to FCF already contains the cost of borrowing: rwacc is a weighted average of rE and rD. If you also subtracted interest from the numerator, you would be paying the lenders once in the cash flow and again in the discount rate, and the value would come out far too low. The rule is simple: financing costs live in the denominator, never in the numerator.

There is a second, deeper reason. Keeping FCF financing-neutral separates the two questions the course keeps asking: what are the assets worth, and what does the financing add or subtract. One stream of FCF can then be valued in two different ways that must agree: discount it at the after-tax WACC (chapter 17), or discount it at rU and add the present value of the tax shield separately (chapter 18). If interest were baked into FCF, the two methods would be valuing different things and the consistency check the examiner runs would be meaningless.

The big one: FCF taxes EBIT, so the tax deduction on interest is deliberately left out Students see EBIT(1 − τc) and object, correctly, that the firm does not actually pay tax on EBIT. It pays tax on EBIT minus interest, so its real tax bill is lower. That objection is right, and the formula ignores it on purpose. The value of that deduction, the interest tax shield, is handed back to you later in exactly one place: either through the reduced discount rate in the after-tax WACC, or as a separate PV(TS) term in APV. If you also lower the tax in the FCF line, you count the shield twice and both methods break. Take the tax hit on full EBIT and move on.
Where this goes Chapter 17 discounts these FCFs at rwacc; chapter 18 discounts them at rU and adds PV(TS); chapter 20 runs both on the same case and shows they agree. The FCF numbers you build here are the input to all of it, unchanged.

2.4 Net working capital, and the sign that costs points

Net working capital is the cash the business has locked up in day-to-day operations:

NWC = Cash required for operations + Accounts receivable + Inventory − Accounts payable
ΔNWCt = NWCt − NWCt−1
Receivables and inventory are cash you have spent but not yet got back. Payables are the reverse: goods you hold but have not yet paid for, so suppliers are financing you and the amount is subtracted.

Exam problems rarely give you ΔNWC directly. They give a policy, usually "net working capital is w percent of sales". Then:

NWCt = w × Salest   ⟹   ΔNWCt = w × (Salest − Salest−1)
And in a steady state where sales grow at g, the requirement settles down to ΔNWC = g × NWC. A growing firm is permanently investing in working capital, which is why growth is not free.
Why does cutting inventory increase free cash flow? Inventory on the shelf is cash you already spent and have not recovered. When the firm sells down its stock and does not replace it, that cash comes back into the bank account. Formally, NWC falls, so ΔNWC is negative, so −ΔNWC is positive and FCF rises. Nothing has changed about profit; this is purely a balance-sheet release. The reverse holds too: a firm that lets receivables balloon can be highly profitable and still be haemorrhaging cash.
Sign discipline The formula subtracts the change, not the level. Two errors are common and both are fatal to a multi-part answer. (1) Subtracting NWCt itself instead of NWCt − NWCt−1. (2) Seeing "working capital falls by 20.8" and subtracting 20.8. It is ΔNWC = −20.8, and −(−20.8) = +20.8. Write the level in a row of its own, difference it, then feed the difference in. Never do it in your head.
Worked example 2: working capital as a percentage of sales, with an inventory release

A firm's NWC has always run at 18% of sales. Last year (year 0) sales were 500.0. Projected sales are 600.0, 660.0 and 700.0. EBIT is projected at 120.0, 132.0 and 140.0; depreciation is 40.0 and CapEx 50.0 in every year; τc = 22%. In year 3 the firm installs a new logistics system that permanently cuts inventory from 9% of sales to 5%, bringing NWC down to 14% of sales.

LineYear 0Year 1Year 2Year 3
Sales500.0600.0660.0700.0
NWC as % of sales18%18%18%14%
NWC level90.0108.0118.898.0
ΔNWC—+18.0+10.8−20.8
EBIT(1 − τc)—93.60102.96109.20
+ Depreciation—+40.0+40.0+40.0
− CapEx—−50.0−50.0−50.0
− ΔNWC—−18.0−10.8+20.8
Free cash flow—65.6082.16120.00

Step 1 — levels first. NWC0 = 0.18 × 500.0 = 90.0; NWC1 = 0.18 × 600.0 = 108.0; NWC2 = 0.18 × 660.0 = 118.8; NWC3 = 0.14 × 700.0 = 98.0.

Step 2 — check the year 3 components. Receivables 12% and payables 3% are unchanged, so the old policy was 12% + 9% − 3% = 18% and the new one is 12% + 5% − 3% = 14%. In money: receivables 84.0 + inventory 35.0 − payables 21.0 = 98.0. ✓

Step 3 — difference them. ΔNWC1 = 108.0 − 90.0 = +18.0; ΔNWC2 = 118.8 − 108.0 = +10.8; ΔNWC3 = 98.0 − 118.8 = −20.8. The third one is a cash release.

Step 4 — assemble. Year 1: 120.0 × 0.78 = 93.60, then 93.60 + 40.0 − 50.0 − 18.0 = 65.60. Year 2: 132.0 × 0.78 = 102.96, then 102.96 + 40.0 − 50.0 − 10.8 = 82.16. Year 3: 140.0 × 0.78 = 109.20, then 109.20 + 40.0 − 50.0 − (−20.8) = 120.00.

Check: the three changes must add up to the change in the level over the whole horizon. 18.0 + 10.8 − 20.8 = 8.0, and NWC3 − NWC0 = 98.0 − 90.0 = 8.0. ✓ Run this check whenever a problem changes the working capital policy mid-stream.

2.5 Terminal value with growth

No exam projects cash flows forever. You forecast explicitly for a few years and then capitalise everything after that into one number, using the growing perpetuity from chapter 1:

TVT = FCFT+1 / (r − g) = FCFT(1 + g) / (r − g)
TVT is a value at date T, not at date T+1, even though the first cash flow inside it arrives at T+1. That is simply what the growing-perpetuity formula produces. So TVT is discounted back over T periods, and it is normally added to FCFT before discounting. The rate r is rwacc in the WACC method and rU in APV, and g must be below r or the formula is nonsense.
Two terminal-value errors that cost whole sub-questions (1) Discounting TVT over T+1 periods. It already sits at date T. (2) Capitalising a year that contains a one-off item. The second is subtler and the examiner plants it deliberately. If FCFT was inflated by an asset sale or a one-time working-capital release, then FCFT(1 + g) assumes that windfall repeats every year forever. Build a normalised FCFT+1 from its components instead.
Worked example 3: terminal value on the firm from Worked example 2

After year 3 everything grows at g = 2% forever, and NWC stays at 14% of sales. The relevant discount rate is r = 9%. Value the firm at date 0.

Step 1 — normalise year 4 rather than growing FCF3. EBIT4 = 140.0 × 1.02 = 142.80, so EBIT4(1 − τc) = 142.80 × 0.78 = 111.384. Depreciation = 40.0 × 1.02 = 40.80; CapEx = 50.0 × 1.02 = 51.00. Sales4 = 700.0 × 1.02 = 714.0, so ΔNWC4 = 0.14 × (714.0 − 700.0) = 1.96.

Step 2 — FCF4. 111.384 + 40.80 − 51.00 − 1.96 = 99.224.

Step 3 — capitalise. TV3 = 99.224 / (0.09 − 0.02) = 99.224 / 0.07 = 1 417.49.

Step 4 — discount. 65.60/1.09 = 60.18; 82.16/1.09² = 69.15; (120.00 + 1 417.49)/1.09³ = 1 537.49/1.295029 = 1 187.22. Total value at date 0 = 60.183 + 69.153 + 1 187.221 = 1 316.56.

Check: TV3 × (r − g) must return the year 4 flow: 1 417.49 × 0.07 = 99.22. ✓ And note what the shortcut would have cost you: growing the reported FCF3 of 120.00 gives TV3 = 120.00 × 1.02 / 0.07 = 1 748.57, overstating the terminal value by 331.09 because it assumes the one-off inventory release of 20.8 recurs forever.

How the examiner tests this Free cash flow is never the whole exercise. It is sub-question (a) of a long chained problem, worth 4 to 6 points, and every later part depends on it, exactly as in 2021 Problem 3, 2022 Problem 3 and 2017V Problem 4. Two habits earn the points. Lay it out vertically, one labelled line per term, so that partial credit survives an arithmetic slip and the grader can follow you: H2017's key states plainly that a number with no calculation shown earns nothing. State your assumptions in one line ("NWC held at 18% of sales; year 3 policy change treated as a one-off release, so the terminal year is normalised"). The formula sheet gives you FCF and the growing perpetuity, so those you do not need to memorise; the discipline is what is being marked.
↑ contents

3 · Risk, return and the CAPM

Chapter 2 gave you the numerator of every valuation. This chapter gives you the denominator. The CAPM has appeared in all eleven exam papers from 2015 to H2025 without a single exception, and it is almost never asked as "state the CAPM". It is asked as a step inside a longer exercise, and usually backwards: a comparable firm's cost of equity is handed to you and you must recover its βE before you can unlever it. That is the opening move of H2024 Exercise 5 and it is buried inside the nine steps of H2025 Exercise 4. If you can only run the CAPM forwards, you cannot start those exercises at all.

3.1 Two kinds of risk, and what diversification removes

Split the uncertainty in any single stock's return into two pieces.

Firm-specific risk (also called idiosyncratic, unique or diversifiable risk) is everything that happens to that firm alone: a failed drug trial, a fire at the plant, a founder resigning, a lawsuit won. These events are unrelated across firms. Systematic risk (market risk, undiversifiable risk) is everything that hits the whole economy at once: a recession, an oil price collapse, a change in the general level of interest rates. Every firm is exposed to it, though not equally.

Put many stocks in a portfolio and the firm-specific pieces start cancelling. One firm's fire is offset by another firm's lucky contract. The systematic piece does not cancel, because it moves every holding in the same direction at the same time. Formally, the variance of an equally weighted portfolio of n stocks is

Var(RP) = (1/n) × (average variance) + (1 − 1/n) × (average covariance)
The first term is the firm-specific part and it dies at rate 1/n. The second term is the systematic part: it converges to the average covariance between stocks, which is a positive number, and no amount of diversification touches it. If every stock has volatility σ and every pair has the same correlation Corr, this simplifies to σP = σ√(1/n + (1 − 1/n)Corr), with a floor of σ√Corr. (Elsewhere in this manual ρ is reserved for the risk-neutral probability, so correlation is written out here.)
Worked example 1: how fast firm-specific risk disappears

Every stock has volatility σ = 40% and every pair of stocks has correlation Corr = 0.20. Build equally weighted portfolios of increasing size.

Step 1 — two stocks, the long way. Var = (0.5)²(0.40)² + (0.5)²(0.40)² + 2(0.5)(0.5)(0.20)(0.40)(0.40) = 0.0400 + 0.0400 + 0.0160 = 0.0960, so σP = √0.0960 = 30.98%. Two stocks already cut volatility by more than nine percentage points.

Step 2 — use the shortcut for larger n. σP = 0.40 × √(1/n + (1 − 1/n) × 0.20):

Stocks in portfolio, n125102050∞
Portfolio volatility σP40.00%30.98%24.00%21.17%19.60%18.59%17.89%

Step 3 — read off the split. For n = 5: 0.40 × √(0.20 + 0.80 × 0.20) = 0.40 × √0.36 = 0.40 × 0.60 = 24.00%. The floor is 0.40 × √0.20 = 17.89%.

Check: of the 40.00% volatility a single stock carries, 17.89 percentage points survive infinite diversification and the rest is removable. Twenty stocks get you to 19.60%, which is already within 1.7 points of the floor. Almost all of the benefit is captured by a couple of dozen positions, and it costs nothing to capture. ✓

3.2 Why only systematic risk is priced

Why does the market refuse to pay you for firm-specific risk? Suppose a stock offered an extra expected return purely because its own earnings are erratic. A diversified investor could buy it, hold it alongside forty other stocks, and watch that erratic component vanish from his portfolio. He would collect the extra return while bearing none of the risk it was supposed to compensate. Every diversified investor would want that trade, so they would bid the price up until the extra return disappeared. You are only compensated for risk you cannot avoid, and firm-specific risk is avoidable for free. What remains is the risk that survives in a fully diversified portfolio, and the only thing that determines how much of that a stock contributes is how it co-moves with the market.

Two consequences follow, and both are examinable in a single sentence. First, the relevant measure of a stock's risk is not its own volatility; it is its contribution to the risk of a diversified portfolio. Second, an undiversified investor who holds one stock genuinely bears more risk than a diversified one, but the market does not pay him extra for it. He is bearing a cost he chose.

"This stock is very volatile, so it must have a high required return" False, and the exam punishes it. Volatility and required return are only linked through the part of the volatility that correlates with the market. A biotech with 60% volatility whose fortunes depend on one trial result can have a lower β than a utility with 20% volatility whose earnings track the business cycle closely. In Worked example 2 below, the stock with 40% volatility ends up with a lower beta than the stock with 22% volatility. If an exam question gives you σ and asks for a cost of capital, it must also give you a correlation or a covariance, otherwise the question is not answerable. Look for it.

3.3 Beta: a covariance, not a volatility

Beta measures how much a stock amplifies or damps the market's movements:

βi = Cov(Ri, Rmkt) / Var(Rmkt) = Corr(Ri, Rmkt) × σi / σmkt
β = 1 means the stock moves one for one with the market on average. β = 1.8 means a 1% market move is associated with a 1.8% move in the stock. β = 0 means no systematic exposure at all, and such a stock earns rf however wild its own returns are. Negative betas exist and are valuable, because such assets pay off precisely when everything else does not.

The second form is the one to internalise: beta is correlation scaled by relative volatility. High volatility raises beta only to the extent that it comes with correlation.

βP = w1β1 + w2β2 + … + wnβn
Betas add up linearly, weighted by market values. Volatilities do not. This one property is what makes the whole unlever/relever machinery of chapter 4 possible: a firm is a portfolio of its debt and its equity, so its asset beta is the value-weighted average of βD and βE.
Worked example 2: two stocks, and why the wilder one is the safer one

Market volatility is σmkt = 18%. Stock Alfa has σ = 40% and correlation 0.35 with the market. Stock Bravo has σ = 22% and correlation 0.80.

Step 1 — Alfa. Cov = 0.35 × 0.40 × 0.18 = 0.0252. Var(Rmkt) = 0.18² = 0.0324. βAlfa = 0.0252 / 0.0324 = 0.7778.

Step 2 — Bravo. Cov = 0.80 × 0.22 × 0.18 = 0.0317 (0.03168 exactly). βBravo = 0.03168 / 0.0324 = 0.9778.

Check with the shortcut: β = Corr × σi/σmkt gives 0.35 × 0.40/0.18 = 0.7778 and 0.80 × 0.22/0.18 = 0.9778. ✓

Read the result: Alfa is almost twice as volatile as Bravo and yet carries the smaller beta, so it has the lower required return. Most of Alfa's 40% is noise that a diversified holder never feels.

3.4 The security market line and the CAPM

Put the two ideas together. Only β matters, and expected return must rise linearly in β because you can always manufacture any beta you like by mixing the market portfolio with risk-free borrowing or lending. The result is the CAPM, which is on your formula sheet:

ri = rf + βi × (E[Rmkt] − rf)
rf the risk-free rate, the reward for waiting  ·  (E[Rmkt] − rf) the market risk premium, the reward per unit of beta  ·  βi how many units of that risk the asset carries. Plotted against β, this is a straight line called the security market line: intercept rf, slope equal to the market risk premium, passing through the market portfolio at β = 1.
Why must the relationship be a straight line? Because you can build any beta you want out of two ingredients. Put a fraction w of your money in the market portfolio and the rest in the risk-free asset: the result has beta w and expected return rf + w(E[Rmkt] − rf), which is precisely the line. Borrow at rf and invest more than 100% in the market, and you extend the line past β = 1. So a stock offering more than the line at its beta dominates the manufactured combination, and investors holding diversified portfolios would sell the combination and buy the stock until the gap closed. A stock offering less would be sold. In equilibrium every asset sits on the line, which is why the line, not the individual stock, defines the price of risk. Note what this argument needs: it is not arbitrage, because buying the single stock instead of the market portfolio leaves you holding its idiosyncratic risk. It works because everyone is assumed to hold diversified portfolios and to care only about mean and variance, which is exactly what the CAPM assumes.
E[Rmkt] and the market risk premium are different numbers "The market risk premium is 6%" means E[Rmkt] − rf = 6%. "The expected return on the market is 6%" means E[Rmkt] = 6%, and with rf = 3% the premium is only 3%. Confusing them halves or doubles every cost of capital in the rest of the exercise, and since the exam chains sub-questions, one slip contaminates everything downstream. Underline which one you were given before you write anything.
Worked example 3: the CAPM forwards, and a portfolio

rf = 3%, E[Rmkt] = 9%, so the market risk premium is 9% − 3% = 6%. An investor holds three stocks with betas 1.20, 0.75 and 1.90 in weights 50%, 30% and 20%.

Step 1 — required return on each. r1 = 0.03 + 1.20 × 0.06 = 0.1020; r2 = 0.03 + 0.75 × 0.06 = 0.0750; r3 = 0.03 + 1.90 × 0.06 = 0.1440.

Step 2 — the portfolio's beta. βP = 0.50(1.20) + 0.30(0.75) + 0.20(1.90) = 0.600 + 0.225 + 0.380 = 1.2050.

Step 3 — the portfolio's required return. rP = 0.03 + 1.2050 × 0.06 = 0.1023, or 10.23%.

Check: average the individual required returns with the same weights: 0.50(0.1020) + 0.30(0.0750) + 0.20(0.1440) = 0.0510 + 0.0225 + 0.0288 = 0.1023. ✓ The two routes agree because the CAPM is linear in β. Remember this: weighting betas and then applying the CAPM gives the same answer as applying the CAPM and then weighting returns.

3.5 Running the CAPM backwards

The exam almost always uses the CAPM in the direction nobody practises. You are told what a firm's securities are expected to return and asked to recover the risk that justifies it. Rearranging the CAPM takes one line:

βi = (ri − rf) / (E[Rmkt] − rf)
The excess return the asset offers, divided by the excess return the market offers. This is not on your formula sheet as a separate line, but the CAPM is, and you should be able to rearrange it in your sleep. It works on equity (rE gives βE), on debt (rD gives βD) and on the unlevered firm (rU gives βU).
Why the exam needs this step at all The firm you are asked to value is typically private, newly formed, or about to change its capital structure, so it has no usable return history. What you do have is a listed comparable in the same business, with an observable rE and a known D/E. Business risk is a property of the assets, not of the financing, so you can lift it from the comparable, strip out the comparable's leverage to get βU, and then reapply your own firm's leverage. Every one of those steps runs through the CAPM, and the first one runs through it backwards.
Worked example 4: recovering the betas of a comparable firm

A listed twin firm has an equity cost of capital of rE = 13.4% and a debt cost of capital of rD = 5.6%. Its equity is 65% of firm value and its debt 35%. As before, rf = 3% and the market risk premium is 6%. Recover its betas.

InputValue
Risk-free rate rf3.00%
Market risk premium E[Rmkt] − rf6.00%
Comparable's cost of equity rE13.40%
Comparable's cost of debt rD5.60%
Equity weight E/(E+D)0.65
Debt weight D/(E+D)0.35

Step 1 — equity beta, backwards. βE = (0.134 − 0.03) / 0.06 = 0.104 / 0.06 = 1.7333.

Step 2 — debt beta, the same way. βD = (0.056 − 0.03) / 0.06 = 0.026 / 0.06 = 0.4333. The debt is not risk-free, so this beta is positive, and setting it to zero out of habit is a classic error.

Step 3 — weight them into the asset beta. This course weights βU without any tax term: βU = 0.65(1.7333) + 0.35(0.4333) = 1.12667 + 0.15167 = 1.2783.

Step 4 — turn it back into a rate, forwards this time. rU = 0.03 + 1.2783 × 0.06 = 0.03 + 0.0767 = 0.1067, that is 10.67%.

Check: rU is also the pre-tax WACC, so weight the comparable's two costs of capital directly: 0.65(0.134) + 0.35(0.056) = 0.0871 + 0.0196 = 0.1067. ✓ The two routes must agree to four decimals; if they do not, you have mixed up a weight or used the market return where the premium belonged.

This is the front half of the twin-firm routine Chapter 4 picks up exactly where Step 3 left off: take an asset beta like the 1.2783 above and relever it to your own firm's D/E to get a new βE, convert that to rE with the CAPM, and build the after-tax WACC. Chapter 6 shows why rE = rU + (D/E)(rU − rD) is the same statement as the beta weighting you just used, written in returns instead of betas.
How the examiner tests this Unlever-and-relever with the CAPM has appeared in all eleven papers from 2015 to H2025. It is never a stand-alone question worth two points; it is steps (a) and (b) of the largest exercise on the paper, such as H2024 Exercise 5 (twin firm, then rU, then rE, then WACC, then value by both WACC and APV) and H2025 Exercise 4. Three concrete habits. Write the direction you are using, "CAPM backwards to get βE", so the grader sees the method even if the arithmetic slips. Carry four decimals on all betas and rates, because H2025 asked for four decimal places and the solution keys flag rounding drift beyond about 0.001 as an error. Finish with the weighting check from Worked example 4: the value-weighted average of βE and βD must reproduce βU, and equivalently the value-weighted average of rE and rD must reproduce rU. That check is on the examiner's own list, and writing it out earns points on its own.
↑ contents

4 · Cost of capital and the unlever/relever toolkit

Of the eleven FIE402 papers mapped from 2015 to H2025, this skill appears in eleven. Not one exam has left it out. H2024 Exercise 5 hands you a comparable firm and asks you to walk all the way to a valuation by WACC and by APV; H2025 Exercise 4 does the same over two periods in nine chained steps, with 4 decimal places demanded. In both, the first three sub-questions are pure unlever/relever. Get them wrong and the entire exercise collapses, because every later step uses your rU. This chapter drills one named procedure until you can run it without thinking.

4.1 Three rates, one identity

A firm is financed by equity worth E and debt worth D, so the whole firm is worth V = E + D (market values, always, never book values). Each side of the balance sheet has its own required return. Equity holders demand rE. Debt holders expect rD. And the firm's assets, considered on their own, have a required return rU, the return investors would demand if the firm carried no debt at all.

The link between them is close to arithmetic. If you own all the equity and all the debt, you own the assets, so your return must be the value-weighted average of the two:

rU = (E/V)·rE + (D/V)·rD
rU is the unlevered cost of capital, also called the asset cost of capital or the opportunity cost of capital. In this course the right-hand side is also the definition of the pre-tax WACC, so: pre-tax WACC = rU. Write that equality on your exam paper. It is a free consistency check on every leverage question you will ever be asked.
Why is rU unaffected by leverage? Leverage does not change the cash flows the assets produce, and it does not change how risky those cash flows are. It only changes who receives them first. Slicing a cake into a safe slice and a risky slice does not change the cake. So rU is a property of the business, and rE and rD are properties of the financing. This is why the entire twin-firm method works: two firms in the same business share rU even when their debt ratios differ wildly.

4.2 Betas weight the same way, and in this course without taxes

Because expected returns and betas are linked one-to-one by the CAPM from chapter 3, the same weighting that holds for rates must hold for betas:

βU = [E/(E+D)]·βE + [D/(E+D)]·βD
rU = rf + βU·(E[Rmkt] − rf)
βU is the unlevered beta or asset beta: the systematic risk of the business itself. The second line is just the CAPM applied to the asset side.
No (1−τc) in the beta weighting Some textbooks weight debt by D(1−τc) when unlevering. FIE402 does not. This course assumes the firm rebalances to a target debt-to-value ratio, and under that assumption the tax shield carries the same risk as the assets, so it does not get its own separate treatment in the weighting. Use βU = [E/(E+D)]βE + [D/(E+D)]βD every time. If you use the (1−τc) version you will get a different βU, a different rU, a different APV, and your WACC value will no longer equal your APV value. The examiner runs that check.

4.3 The Twin-Firm Ladder

You are asked to value a project or division whose own cost of capital you cannot observe, because it is not separately traded. The exam always solves this the same way: find a listed firm in the same business, strip its financing off, then bolt your own financing on. Call it the Twin-Firm Ladder. Seven rungs, always in this order.

Written out as a checklist you can reproduce from memory in the first minute of the exercise:

RungWhat you doFormula
1Read the twin's cost of equity, or its share price datarE given
2Back out the twin's equity betaβE = (rE − rf)/(E[Rmkt] − rf)
3Get the twin's debt beta, or set it to 0 if the question says debt is risk-freeβD = (rD − rf)/(E[Rmkt] − rf)
4Unlever using the twin's weightsβU = (E/V)βE + (D/V)βD
5Turn βU into a raterU = rf + βU(E[Rmkt] − rf)
6Relever to your target D/EβE = βU + (D/E)(βU − βD), then CAPM
7Build the WACC you actually needpre-tax = rU; after-tax = (E/V)rE + (D/V)rD(1−τc)
Worked example 1: the plain ladder, risk-free debt

You are valuing a salmon-farming division. It is not listed, but Fjordline Seafoods is in exactly the same business and is listed. rf = 3.00% and the market risk premium is 5.00%. Your division will be financed at a target D/E of 0.25 and can borrow at the risk-free rate. τc = 22%.

InputValue
Fjordline equity, market value (MNOK)600
Fjordline debt, market value (MNOK)400
Fjordline cost of equity rE11.00%
Fjordline debt: risk-free, so βD0.00

Rung 1–2 — the twin's equity beta. V = 600 + 400 = 1000, so E/V = 0.6000 and D/V = 0.4000.
βE = (0.1100 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 1.60

Rung 3 — the twin's debt beta. The debt is risk-free, so βD = 0.00 and rD = rf = 3.0000%.

Rung 4 — unlever. βU = 0.6000(1.60) + 0.4000(0.00) = 0.96

Rung 5 — rU. rU = 0.0300 + 0.96(0.0500) = 0.0780 = 7.8000%
Sanity check on the twin itself: 0.6000(0.1100) + 0.4000(0.0300) = 0.0780 ✓ Same number, so rungs 2 to 5 were arithmetically sound.

Rung 6 — relever to D/E = 0.25. D/E = 0.25 means D/V = 0.2000 and E/V = 0.8000.
βE = 0.96 + 0.25(0.96 − 0.00) = 1.20
rE = 0.0300 + 1.20(0.0500) = 0.0900 = 9.0000%
Cross-check with MM Proposition II: 0.0780 + 0.25(0.0780 − 0.0300) = 0.0780 + 0.0120 = 0.0900 ✓

Rung 7 — WACC.
Pre-tax: 0.8000(0.0900) + 0.2000(0.0300) = 0.0780 = 7.8000%, equal to rU ✓
After-tax: 0.8000(0.0900) + 0.2000(0.0300)(1 − 0.22) = 0.072000 + 0.004680 = 0.076680 = 7.6680%

Check: weighted betas back to βU: 0.8000(1.20) + 0.2000(0.00) = 0.96 ✓

4.4 Relevering, and how the three rates move

Rung 6 has a rate version and a beta version, and they are the same statement. The rate version is MM Proposition II, which is on the exam formula sheet:

rE = rU + (D/E)(rU − rD)
βE = βU + (D/E)(βU − βD)
Both come from rearranging the weighted average in 4.1 and 4.2. Use whichever the question hands you inputs for. If you are given betas, use the beta line and convert at the end; if you are given rates, use the rate line. Never convert twice.
Why does leverage raise rE but leave rU alone? Debt holders get paid first, so their claim absorbs little of the swing in firm value. Whatever swing the assets produce therefore lands almost entirely on equity, and equity is now a smaller base. Same absolute risk, smaller slice, so more risk per krone of equity, so a higher required return. The rise in rE is exactly large enough that the weighted average stays at rU. That is the whole content of MM Proposition II, and it is why the exam can ask you the same question in either direction.
What the examiner is looking for here In H2024 Exercise 5 and H2025 Exercise 4 the marks are attached to the steps, not to the final number. Write each rung on its own line with the formula named ("unlevering the comparable", "MM II"), then the substituted numbers, then the result to 4 decimals. If you lose a number midway, carry rU forward as a symbol and keep going: the later sub-questions still earn their points. And always close with the identity pre-tax WACC = rU. It costs one line and it is a stated consistency check in the solution keys.

4.5 When the debt beta is not zero

Setting βD = 0 is a convenience, and it is only allowed when the question says the debt is risk-free or gives you no way to find βD. As soon as you are told the firm's debt has an expected return above rf, its beta is positive and it must appear in the weighting. A non-zero debt beta shows up in the H2025 paper's long WACC-versus-APV exercise, where finding it is the very first step.

Where does a positive βD come from? Debt is only risk-free if it is repaid in every state of the world. In bad states, and bad states for the whole economy in particular, a levered firm may not repay in full. That shortfall is correlated with the market, so it is systematic risk, and systematic risk carries a beta. A positive βD is therefore not an exotic special case: it is what happens whenever leverage is high enough for default to be a real possibility. Note what it does to the unlevering: debt now carries part of the business risk, so a given βE implies a higher βU than it would if you had wrongly assumed βD = 0.
Worked example 2: non-zero debt beta on both sides

You must value an expansion into speciality chemicals. Aurora Chemicals is the comparable. rf = 2.50%, market risk premium 6.00%, τc = 22%. Your own target is D/V = 0.30, and your bank prices your debt at βD = 0.15.

InputValue
Aurora cost of equity rE13.60%
Aurora debt-to-equity D/E0.80
Aurora expected return on debt rD4.30%

Rung 2 — Aurora's equity beta. βE = (0.1360 − 0.0250)/0.0600 = 1.85

Rung 3 — Aurora's debt beta. βD = (0.0430 − 0.0250)/0.0600 = 0.30. Not zero, so it must be carried.

Rung 4 — unlever. D/E = 0.80 gives E/V = 1/1.80 = 0.5556 and D/V = 0.80/1.80 = 0.4444.
βU = (1.00/1.80)(1.85) + (0.80/1.80)(0.30) = 1.0278 + 0.1333 = 1.1611

Rung 5 — rU. rU = 0.0250 + 1.1611(0.0600) = 0.094667 = 9.4667%
Sanity check on Aurora: (1.00/1.80)(0.1360) + (0.80/1.80)(0.0430) = 0.075556 + 0.019111 = 0.094667 ✓

Rung 6 — relever. D/V = 0.30 gives E/V = 0.70 and D/E = 0.30/0.70 = 0.4286. Your rD = 0.0250 + 0.15(0.0600) = 0.0340 = 3.4000%.
βE = 1.1611 + 0.4286(1.1611 − 0.15) = 1.1611 + 0.4333 = 1.5944
rE = 0.0250 + 1.5944(0.0600) = 0.120667 = 12.0667%
MM II cross-check: 0.094667 + 0.4286(0.094667 − 0.034000) = 0.094667 + 0.026000 = 0.120667 ✓

Rung 7 — WACC. Pre-tax: 0.70(0.120667) + 0.30(0.0340) = 0.084467 + 0.010200 = 0.094667 = 9.4667% = rU ✓
After-tax: 0.70(0.120667) + 0.30(0.0340)(0.78) = 0.084467 + 0.007956 = 0.092423 = 9.2423%

Check: 0.70(1.594444) + 0.30(0.15) = 1.116111 + 0.045000 = 1.161111 = βU ✓ Both the beta check and the rate check pass, so the two relevering routes agree.

4.6 After-tax WACC, and choosing between the two

Interest is tax-deductible, so every krone of interest paid saves the firm τc kroner in tax. The after-tax WACC prices debt at its cost net of that saving:

rwacc = (E/V)·rE + (D/V)·rD(1 − τc)
pre-tax WACC = (E/V)·rE + (D/V)·rD = rU
The two differ by exactly (D/V)·rD·τc, the per-krone value of the interest tax shield. Use rwacc to discount free cash flows in the WACC method, because FCF is computed before interest and therefore ignores the shield. Use rU to discount the unlevered firm in APV, where the shield is added separately.
Worked example 3: unlever your own firm, then relever after a recapitalisation

No twin this time. You already know your own numbers, and the board is doubling leverage. rf = 3.00%, market risk premium 5.00%, τc = 22%.

Input, before the recapitalisationValue
Equity, market value (MNOK)750
Debt, market value (MNOK)250
Cost of equity rE10.50%
Expected return on debt rD4.50%

Step 1 — betas today. E/V = 0.7500, D/V = 0.2500.
βE = (0.1050 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 1.50 and βD = (0.0450 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.30

Step 2 — unlever yourself. βU = 0.7500(1.50) + 0.2500(0.30) = 1.1250 + 0.0750 = 1.20
rU = 0.0300 + 1.20(0.0500) = 0.0900 = 9.0000%
Check: 0.7500(0.1050) + 0.2500(0.0450) = 0.078750 + 0.011250 = 0.0900 ✓
After-tax WACC today: 0.7500(0.1050) + 0.2500(0.0450)(0.78) = 0.078750 + 0.008775 = 0.087525 = 8.7525%

Step 3 — the recapitalisation. The firm issues debt and repurchases shares until D/V = 0.5000, so D/E = 1.0000. The bank reprices the debt at rD = 5.5000%, so βD = (0.0550 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.50.

Step 4 — relever. βE = 1.20 + 1.0000(1.20 − 0.50) = 1.90
rE = 0.0300 + 1.90(0.0500) = 0.1250 = 12.5000%
MM II cross-check: 0.0900 + 1.0000(0.0900 − 0.0550) = 0.1250 ✓

Step 5 — the new WACCs. Pre-tax: 0.5000(0.1250) + 0.5000(0.0550) = 0.0625 + 0.0275 = 9.0000% = rU, unchanged ✓
After-tax: 0.5000(0.1250) + 0.5000(0.0550)(0.78) = 0.062500 + 0.021450 = 0.083950 = 8.3950%

Check: 0.5000(1.90) + 0.5000(0.50) = 0.9500 + 0.2500 = 1.20 = βU ✓ And note the economics: rU did not move, rE rose from 10.5000% to 12.5000%, and the after-tax WACC fell from 8.7525% to 8.3950%. The fall is entirely the larger tax shield, not any reduction in business risk.

The consistency check verifies your algebra, not your assumptions Suppose in worked example 3 you had lazily kept rD at 4.5000% after the recapitalisation. You would get βE = 1.20 + 1.00(1.20 − 0.30) = 2.10 and rE = 13.5000%. Now run the checks: pre-tax WACC = 0.5(0.1350) + 0.5(0.0450) = 9.0000% ✓ and 0.5(2.10) + 0.5(0.30) = 1.20 ✓. Both checks pass on a wrong answer. They confirm that rE, rD and rU hang together, nothing more. If the question tells you the borrowing rate changes with leverage, you must use the new rate; the identity will not rescue you.
Book values are not market values Every weight in this chapter is a market value. If the exam gives you a share price and a share count, multiply them: that is E. Book equity from a balance sheet is the wrong number and will propagate into every subsequent step. Debt is usually close enough to book value that the exam lets you use it, but only say so explicitly if you rely on it.
Where this goes next rD in this chapter is the expected return on debt, not the promised yield printed on the bond. Chapter 5 shows how to get from one to the other and why putting the yield into the WACC overstates it. rU and rwacc are then used to value firms in chapters 17 to 20, where the whole ladder is run as the opening move of a full valuation, exactly as in H2025 Exercise 4.
↑ contents

5 · Debt, yields and credit spreads

Chapter 4 put rD into the WACC without saying where it comes from. On an exam you are almost never handed rD directly: you are handed a bond price, a promised yield, a default probability, or a credit rating. The gap between what debt promises and what it is expected to deliver has been tested on its own in 2021 MC7–9 and V2024 Problem 3, and it appears in eight of the eleven mapped papers. Kurbatov has not yet used it in H2024 or H2025, which makes it a genuine risk item rather than a certainty, but it is cheap to learn and it is the foundation of chapter 24, where debt and equity become options.

5.1 Zero-coupon bonds and the yield to maturity

A zero-coupon bond pays a single amount, its face value F, at maturity, and nothing before. Its price today is P. The yield to maturity is simply the single discount rate that makes the price and the promised payment agree:

P = F / (1 + y)n   ⟹   y = (F/P)1/n − 1
y is the promised return: the return you earn if, and only if, every promised krone arrives. n is the number of periods. For n = 1 this collapses to y = F/P − 1.

Two facts follow immediately, and both are exam-relevant. Price and yield move in opposite directions, always. And if the bond is default-free, its yield is the risk-free rate for that maturity. The risk-free rate you use in the CAPM is nothing more than the yield on a government zero-coupon bond of the right maturity.

5.2 Coupon bonds

A coupon bond pays a fixed coupon each period plus the face value at maturity. Its price is the present value of that stream, discounted at a single rate y:

P = C·[1 − (1+y)−n]/y  +  F/(1+y)n
C is the coupon per period, equal to the coupon rate times face value. The first term is the annuity from chapter 1, the second is the face value. The yield y is defined implicitly: for any realistic maturity there is no practical closed form, so you iterate.
Why the coupon rate and the yield are different animals The coupon rate is a contract term: it fixes the krone amount the borrower must pay, and it never changes. The yield is a market outcome: it is whatever discount rate reconciles today's price with those fixed payments. When the market demands more than the coupon rate, the only way for the bond to compete is to be cheap, so it trades at a discount (P below F). When the market demands less, it trades at a premium. Exactly at the coupon rate it trades at par. Reading the direction off a bond in one second is a standard multiple-choice move.
Worked example 1: pricing a coupon bond and reading its yield

Nordkraft AS has a 4-year bond outstanding: face value 1000, annual coupon 6%, so C = 60 per year. It is quoted at 94.012, meaning 94.012% of face, so P = 940.12. The 4-year government yield is 3.00%.

Step 1 — confirm the yield. Try y = 7.8000%. The discount factors are 1.0781 = 1.078000, 1.0782 = 1.162084, 1.0783 = 1.252727, 1.0784 = 1.350439.

YearCash flowDiscounted at 7.80%
16055.6586
26051.6314
36047.8955
41060784.9298
Price940.1153

The annuity form gives the same thing: 60 × [1 − 1.078−4]/0.078 = 60 × 3.326925 = 199.6155, plus 1000/1.350439 = 740.4998, total 940.1153. So y = 7.8000%.

Step 2 — if you had to search for y. Price at 7.5000% is 949.7601; price at 8.0000% is 933.7575. Linear interpolation: 0.0750 + (949.7601 − 940.1153)/(949.7601 − 933.7575) × 0.0050 = 0.0750 + 0.6027 × 0.0050 = 0.078014 = 7.8014%, within 0.0014 percentage points of the true answer. Interpolate between two brackets that are 0.5 points apart and you will be close enough for exam purposes; state that you interpolated.

Step 3 — the credit spread. 7.8000% − 3.0000% = 4.8000%, or 480 basis points.

Check: the coupon rate (6.00%) is below the yield (7.80%), so the bond must trade below par. 940.12 < 1000 ✓

5.3 Promised is not expected

The yield y assumes every krone arrives. Corporate borrowers sometimes default, and when they do, lenders recover only part of what they were promised. Let p be the probability of default over the period and let R be the recovery rate, the fraction of the promised payment creditors actually collect in default. The loss given default is then L = 1 − R. The expected payment is:

E[payment] = (1 − p)·F + p·R·F = F·[1 − p·L]
p = probability of default · R = recovery rate · L = 1 − R = expected loss rate given default. Note the shape: the promised F is scaled down by the expected fractional loss p·L.

The number the firm actually pays investors, on average, is what matters for the cost of capital. That is the expected return on debt, and the course writes it as:

rD = y − p·L
The expected return equals the promised yield minus the expected annual loss from default. This is not on the exam formula sheet. Memorise it. rD ≤ y always, with equality only if p = 0 or L = 0.
Why the WACC needs rD and not y The WACC is a weighted average of what investors expect to earn, because that is what the firm's cash flows must cover on average. The promised yield is a best case, not an average: it is the return in the states where nothing goes wrong. Using y in the WACC therefore inflates the discount rate, which undervalues the firm. The higher the credit risk, the worse the error. In a junk-bond case the gap can be several percentage points, and the examiner is watching for it.

5.4 What sits inside the credit spread

The credit spread is the whole difference between the promised yield and the risk-free rate: spread = y − rf. It contains two economically different things, and only one of them is a risk premium.

Two levels, do not mix them rf → rD is the risk premium on debt, priced by the CAPM: rD − rf = βD(E[Rmkt] − rf). rD → y is the expected loss, which is compensation for nothing: it is the padding the borrower must promise so that the average payment comes out right. Never compute βD from the yield. Compute rD first, then βD = (rD − rf)/(E[Rmkt] − rf).
Worked example 2: a one-year zero, both ways round

A one-year zero-coupon bond with face value 1000 trades at 920. The one-year risk-free rate is 3.00%. Analysts put the probability of default at 6.00% and expect creditors to recover 40% of face value if it happens.

Step 1 — promised yield. y = 1000/920 − 1 = 0.086957 = 8.6957%

Step 2 — credit spread. 8.6957% − 3.0000% = 5.6957%

Step 3 — loss given default. R = 0.40, so L = 1 − 0.40 = 0.60, and the expected loss rate is p·L = 0.06 × 0.60 = 0.0360 = 3.6000%.

Step 4 — expected return, course formula. rD = y − p·L = 0.086957 − 0.036000 = 0.050957 = 5.0957%

Step 5 — expected return, from the cash flows directly. E[payment] = 0.94(1000) + 0.06(400) = 940 + 24 = 964. Then rD = 964/920 − 1 = 0.047826 = 4.7826%.

Check: the two answers differ by 0.3130 percentage points, and that difference is exactly p·L·y = 0.06 × 0.60 × 0.086957 = 0.003130 ✓ The course formula applies the loss rate to the amount invested; the exact calculation applies it to the promised payment, which is (1 + y) times larger. Use rD = y − p·L when the question asks for it or gives you y, p and L; use the cash-flow route when you are given the payoffs. Say which you used.

5.5 From a bond price to a WACC

This is the join between chapter 4 and chapter 5, and it is the form the exam actually uses: a bond is described, and three sub-questions later you need a WACC.

Worked example 3: bond data all the way to rwacc

Back to the Nordkraft bond from worked example 1: y = 7.8000%, rf = 3.00%, market risk premium 5.00%. Default probability is 4.00% per year with a loss given default of 55%. Nordkraft's asset beta is βU = 1.10, its target capital structure is D/V = 0.35, and τc = 22%.

Step 1 — expected return on debt. rD = 0.0780 − 0.04(0.55) = 0.0780 − 0.0220 = 0.0560 = 5.6000%

Step 2 — debt beta. βD = (0.0560 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.52

Step 3 — unlevered rate. rU = 0.0300 + 1.10(0.0500) = 0.0850 = 8.5000%

Step 4 — relever. D/V = 0.35 gives E/V = 0.65 and D/E = 0.35/0.65 = 0.5385.
βE = 1.10 + 0.5385(1.10 − 0.52) = 1.10 + 0.3123 = 1.4123
rE = 0.0300 + 1.4123(0.0500) = 0.100615 = 10.0615%

Step 5 — WACC.
Pre-tax: 0.65(0.100615) + 0.35(0.0560) = 0.065400 + 0.019600 = 0.085000 = 8.5000%
After-tax: 0.65(0.100615) + 0.35(0.0560)(0.78) = 0.065400 + 0.015288 = 0.080688 = 8.0688%

Check 1: pre-tax WACC = 8.5000% = rU ✓
Check 2: 0.65(1.4123) + 0.35(0.52) = 0.9180 + 0.1820 = 1.10 = βU ✓
Check 3 — the error you avoided: using the promised yield instead of rD would give 0.065400 + 0.35(0.0780) = 0.092700 = 9.2700%, overstating the pre-tax WACC by 0.7700 percentage points. On a perpetuity that is an 8.3% valuation error: the value falls to 0.0850/0.0927 = 0.917 of the correct figure.

5.6 Backing a default probability out of a price

The exam sometimes runs the logic in reverse: the bond price is observed, and you are asked what the market must believe about default. Set the price equal to the expected payment discounted at the risk-free rate and solve for the probability:

P = F·[1 − q·L] / (1 + rf)   ⟹   q = [1 − P(1 + rf)/F] / L
q is the risk-neutral default probability, not the true one, because discounting at rf means all the risk adjustment has been pushed into q instead of into the discount rate.

Take the bond from worked example 2: q = [1 − 920(1.03)/1000]/0.60 = [1 − 0.9476]/0.60 = 0.0524/0.60 = 0.087333 = 8.7333%. Check by pricing it back: [0.912667(1000) + 0.087333(400)]/1.03 = 947.600/1.03 = 920.00 ✓ Notice that q = 8.7333% exceeds the true probability p = 6.0000%. That gap is the risk premium in disguise.

Why the risk-neutral probability is the larger one Default clusters in bad states of the world, and a krone in a bad state is worth more to investors than a krone in a good state. Rather than discount the expected payment at a rate above rf, you can equivalently overweight the bad state and discount at rf. Both routes give the same price. So q is not a forecast of anything: it is a pricing device that already contains the compensation for risk. This is exactly the trick behind binomial option pricing in chapter 22, and it is why real probabilities never appear there either.
How this has actually been examined 2021 MC7–9 and V2024 Problem 3 tested this material as short computations: given a price and a recovery assumption, find the yield, the spread, the expected return, or the implied default probability. The marks are for the distinction, so write the words "promised" and "expected" explicitly next to your two numbers. Neither H2024 nor H2025 used credit risk, so treat this chapter as insurance rather than as a guaranteed 20 points, but do not skip it: it is the entry ticket to chapter 24, which has carried up to 90 points in older papers.
Forward to chapter 24: debt and equity as options Everything here took p, L and R as given. Chapter 24 derives them instead. Equity is a call option on the firm's assets with strike equal to the face value of debt, and risky debt is risk-free debt minus a put option written on those assets. Once you see that, the yield, the credit spread and the risk-neutral default probability all fall out of an option-pricing model rather than from an analyst's assumption, credit default swaps become insurance priced off q, and βE = N(d1)(V/E)βU replaces the linear relevering formula of chapter 4. Read chapter 5 as the vocabulary lesson for that chapter.

Part I — Capital structure

↑ contents

6 · MM I and II in perfect markets

H2024 Exercise 3 and H2025 Exercise 3 are 18 points each and are this chapter alone: a perfect-market firm issues debt, then pays a dividend or buys back shares. You must give the price at announcement and afterwards, the shares repurchased, the new rE, and whether the shareholder who sold beat the one who held. All eleven mapped papers contain this material. Run it as a routine.

6.1 What "perfect capital markets" is doing for you

ConditionWhat it rules outRemoved in
No taxes, no transaction or issuance costsA wedge between what the firm pays and what investors getk7, k8
Asset cash flows independent of financingFinancing changing investmentk9–k11
Firms and investors trade the same securities at the same pricesThe firm doing what investors cannotk12, k13
Why the third condition does most of the work Borrow on the firm's terms and you can build any leverage you like in your own portfolio: buy an unlevered share, fund part with a personal loan. That is homemade leverage, and no firm can charge for a service you can perform yourself.

6.2 Proposition I, and the arbitrage that enforces it

VL = E + D = VU
MM Proposition I. All a firm's securities together are worth the cash flows its assets produce, independent of the debt-equity mix. Leverage divides the cash flow; it does not enlarge it.

It is enforced by trade, not by preferences, and worked example 1 shows exactly how.

Worked example 1: the arbitrage that kills a value gap

Two firms own identical assets paying 100 a year in perpetuity, all paid out, and rU = 10.0000%, so the assets are worth 1000. Firm U is all-equity at VU = 1000. Firm L has 400 of risk-free perpetual debt at rf = 4.0000% and equity at 700, so VL = 1100: leverage supposedly created 100.

Step 1 — what L's equity receives. Interest = 0.04 × 400 = 16.00, so equity gets 100 − 16.00 = 84.00 a year, an implied rE of 84.00/700 = 0.120000 = 12.0000%.

Step 2 — build the trade at 1% scale, and bank the cash. Short 1% of L's equity, +7.00; borrow 1% of L's debt level, 4.00, at 4.0000%, +4.00; buy 1% of U's equity, −10.00. Net today: 7.00 + 4.00 − 10.00 = +1.00.

Step 3 — cash every year after. Receive 0.01 × 100 = 1.00 from U, pay 0.04 × 4.00 = 0.16 on your loan, owe the short 0.01 × 84.00 = 0.84. Net = 1.00 − 0.16 − 0.84 = 0.00, in every state.

Check: the profit is 1% of the mispricing, 0.01(1100 − 1000) = 1.00 ✓ Not an identity: borrow the wrong amount and Step 3 stops cancelling, so that is the line to verify. Scaled up, the trade closes the gap.

6.3 Proposition II, derived

Take rU = (E/V)rE + (D/V)rD from k4 § 4.1, multiply by V and solve for rE:

(E + D)·rU = E·rE + D·rD   ⟹   rE = rU + (D/E)(rU − rD)
MM Proposition II, on the exam formula sheet. A straight line: intercept rU, slope (rU − rD), running variable D/E. The beta version in k4 § 4.4 is the same statement.
A higher rE is not bad news Every recapitalisation raises rE, and candidates routinely conclude shareholders are worse off. They are not: the rise exactly compensates for the extra risk equity bears, so the price does not move. Nor does the pre-tax WACC fall with leverage. It is flat at rU.

6.4 The recapitalisation, move by move

A leveraged recapitalisation issues debt and hands the proceeds to shareholders, as a special dividend or a repurchase. V is unchanged, so every krone of new debt Dnew comes out of equity. N is the share count, P the price.

repurchase: n = Dnew/P  ·  N1 = N0 − n  ·  P = E1/N1, unchanged
special dividend: DPS = Dnew/N0  ·  ex-dividend price = P0 − DPS
P is the price the shares are bought at, the post-announcement price. Here it equals P0, because nothing was created to announce. In k7 it does not.
Announcement and execution are different events The announcement is when information reaches the market, so it is the only moment a price can jump. Execution swaps cash for securities at prices already set. Under MM the announcement carries no news, so nothing moves at either date; execution changes the composition of your wealth, not its size.

6.5 A recapitalisation worked end to end

Worked example 2: both routes, and the three shareholders

Bjørnefjord ASA, perfect market, no taxes. 18 million shares at 50.00 and 300 of risk-free perpetual debt at rD = rf = 4.0000%, so E0 = 900 and V = 1200 (millions). Free cash flow is 96 a year in perpetuity, all paid out. The board issues 300 of new debt, taking D/V to 0.5000.

Step 1 — rU. All 96 goes to investors, so rU = 96/1200 = 0.080000 = 8.0000%.

Step 2 — rE today, MM II. D/E = 300/900 = 0.3333.
rE = 0.080000 + 0.3333(0.080000 − 0.040000) = 0.080000 + 0.013333 = 0.093333 = 9.3333%
Pre-tax WACC: 0.7500(0.093333) + 0.2500(0.040000) = 0.070000 + 0.010000 = 0.080000 ✓

Step 3 — dividend per share today. Interest = 0.04 × 300 = 12.00, so equity receives 84.00 and DPS = 84.00/18 = 4.6667. As a perpetuity: 4.6667/0.093333 = 50.00 ✓

Step 4 — announcement. No taxes, no costs, assets unchanged, so V stays 1200 and the price stays 50.00.

Step 5 — repurchase route. n = 300/50.00 = 6 million shares, so N1 = 12 million and E1 = 1200 − 600 = 600, giving 600/12 = 50.00.

Step 6 — new rate, new dividend. D/E = 600/600 = 1.0000, so rE = 0.080000 + 1.0000(0.080000 − 0.040000) = 0.120000 = 12.0000%. Interest = 24.00, equity receives 72.00, DPS = 72.00/12 = 6.00.

Step 7 — dividend route instead. A special dividend of 300/18 = 16.6667 per share leaves an ex-dividend price of 50.00 − 16.6667 = 33.3333 on the unchanged 18 million shares: 18 × 33.3333 = 600 ✓ Ongoing DPS = 72.00/18 = 4.00 and 4.00/0.120000 = 33.3333 ✓

Step 8 — three shareholders, one share each.

PositionEnds up holdingWealth
No recapitalisationshare paying 4.6667 forever at 9.3333%50.00
Sold into the buybackcash50.00
Held through itshare paying 6.00 forever at 12.0000%50.00

Check: 6.00/0.120000 = 50.00 ✓ Write it down: it can fail. P = E1/N1 cannot: n was defined as 300/P, so it re-divides what you assumed. Discounting the new dividend at the new rE uses two numbers that never entered the price. Forget to relever and you get 64.29, and the clash finds the error.

How the examiner marks this H2024 Exercise 3 runs a debt-financed dividend, then a repurchase; H2025 Exercise 3 wants dividend per share before and after, shares repurchased, the new rE, and a comparison of seller and holder. Say why the price holds ("no taxes, so this is purely financial, MM I"), not merely that it does. Then set the three shareholders side by side: all are equally well off.
Where the propositions break k7 removes the first condition by making interest deductible, and at once the announcement moves the price and the buyback executes at the new price. k9 to k11 remove the second, k12 the third; the arithmetic above survives all of it.
↑ contents

7 · Corporate taxes and the interest tax shield

The first friction the exam quantifies. 2022 Problem 1, 2023 Problem 1 and 2015 Problem 3 are built on it, and H2025 Exercise 4 makes you compute the shield year by year and discount it. One question decides more marks here than any other, which rate discounts the shield: it was multiple choice in all eight mapped papers that had multiple choice, 2021 MC3 included.

7.1 What the deduction is worth each year

Interest is deductible from taxable profit; dividends are not. A firm paying interest of rD·D therefore pays less tax than an identical all-equity firm, by the deduction times the tax rate:

TSt = τc · rD · Dt
