The interest tax shield in period t, with Dt the debt outstanding at the start of the period. A real cash flow to investors: the government's claim shrinks by exactly this much.
Why leverage creates value when the assets did not change In k6 the cash flow had two claimants. Taxes add a third, the government, whose slice depends on the financing mix. Debt shrinks that slice, and what the government does not take is left for investors. Leverage creates no cash, it redirects a payment.

7.2 Levered value, and the permanent-debt shortcut

VL = VU + PV(TS)
Not on the exam formula sheet. Memorise it. VU is the free cash flow discounted at rU. This split is the APV method of k18. Whenever you write it, say which rate you discounted the shield at.

If the debt is fixed and permanent, the shield is a level perpetuity of τc·rD·D, as safe as the interest itself, so discount at rD:

PV(TS) = τc·rD·D / rD = τc·D   ⟹   VL = VU + τc·D
rD cancels, which is why it looks so simple. Valid only for permanently fixed debt; applying τc·D to a rebalancing firm overstates its value.

7.3 Which rate discounts the shield

The shield needs a rate matching its own risk, and that risk is decided by the debt policy the question states. Three cases:

Debt policy in the questionRisk of the shieldDiscount at
Constant D/E or D/V, rebalancedFuture debt moves with firm value, so the shield carries asset riskrU
Fixed amount, permanent, never repaidAs safe as the interest paymentsrD (or rf if risk-free)
Known repayment scheduleEach year's shield is a known amountrD, period by period
Why rebalancing makes the shield risky Under a target ratio the firm borrows more when it is worth more and repays when it is worth less, so next year's debt is unknown today and moves with firm value. A cash flow that moves with the assets has their risk, hence their rate, rU. Under fixed debt D is a contract number and the interest known; only default can stop the shield, and that is what rD prices.
This is a choice of assumption, not a choice of formula Read the sentence describing the debt: "maintains a debt-to-equity ratio of 25%" is rebalancing, so rU; "200 million of perpetual debt outstanding" is fixed, so rD. If the question is ambiguous, state the policy you assume in one line, then compute. That earns marks. Applying τc·D to a target-leverage firm does not, and it breaks consistency check 1.
Worked example 1: the same debt, three policies, three answers

Fjellheim AS earns an unlevered free cash flow of 60 a year in perpetuity at rU = 10.0000%, so VU = 600. It borrows D = 200 at rD = 6.0000%, τc = 25%. Millions.

Step 1 — the annual shield, common to all three. TS = 0.25 × 0.06 × 200 = 3.00 a year.

Step 2 — policy A, fixed permanent debt. Discount at rD: PV(TS) = 3.00/0.06 = 50.00 = τc·D ✓ VL = 650.00.

Step 3 — policy B, rebalanced to a constant D/E. Discount the same 3.00 at rU: PV(TS) = 3.00/0.10 = 30.00, so VL = 630.00.

Step 4 — policy C, debt repaid after one year. One shield of 3.00 at the end of year 1, as safe as the debt:
PV(TS) = τc·rD·D/(1 + rD) = 3.00/1.06 = 2.8302, so VL = 602.8302.

Step 5 — check policy B by WACC (consistency check 1). E = 630.00 − 200 = 430.00, D/E = 0.4651.
rE = 0.100000 + 0.4651(0.100000 − 0.060000) = 0.100000 + 0.018605 = 0.118605 = 11.8605%
rwacc = (430/630)(0.118605) + (200/630)(0.060000)(0.75) = 0.080952 + 0.014286 = 0.095238 = 9.5238%
VL = 60/0.095238 = 630.00 ✓

Check: the WACC route reproduces 630.00 without using PV(TS), so it is a real second computation and it can fail. Discount at rD while still rebalancing, claim 650.00, and the same route returns rE = 11.7778%, rwacc = 9.5385%, VL = 629.03: a mismatch the examiner will spot.

7.4 Who captures the gain, and when the price moves

The price jumps the instant the market believes the plan:

ΔP = PV(TS)/N0   ·   n = Dnew/P1, where P1 = P0 + ΔP
N0 is the count before anything happens. The repurchase executes at the post-announcement price P1, not P0. That is the only difference from k6 § 6.4, and it is where the marks are.
Why the lenders capture none of it New lenders hand over 200 and receive debt worth 200: at a fair rD that is zero NPV, so they gain nothing. The shield accrues to whoever owns the firm when the plan becomes credible, the pre-announcement shareholders, seller and holder alike. Value moves when information moves.
Worked example 2: a recapitalisation that does create value

Fjellheim again, all-equity today: VU = 600 on 20 million shares, so P0 = 30.00, τc = 25%. It announces a permanent issue of 200 of risk-free debt at rD = rf = 5.0000%, proceeds used to repurchase shares.

Step 1 — value the shield. TS = 0.25 × 0.05 × 200 = 2.50 a year, permanent, so discount at rD: PV(TS) = 2.50/0.05 = 50.00 = τc·D ✓ VL = 650.00.

Step 2 — the announcement. No debt issued yet, so all 650.00 is equity on the original 20 million shares: P1 = 650.00/20 = 32.50, a jump of ΔP = 2.50 = 50.00/20 ✓

Step 3 — issue the debt. The firm holds 200 in cash plus 450 of operating value: still 650.00 of equity on 20 million shares, so nothing moves.

Step 4 — repurchase, at 32.50. n = 200/32.50 = 6.1538 million shares, leaving N1 = 13.8462 million. E1 = 650.00 − 200 = 450.00, and 450.00/13.8462 = 32.50 ✓

Step 5 — where the 50 went. Original shareholders hold 450.00 of shares plus the 200 they were paid, 650.00 against 600. Their gain is 50.00, the whole PV(TS).

Check: 450.00 + 200 = 650.00 is close to an identity and proves little alone. The check that can fail is Step 4's price: repurchase at the stale 30.00 and you buy 200/30.00 = 6.6667 million shares, leaving 13.3333 million, implying 450.00/13.3333 = 33.75. Two prices for one share after one transaction is a contradiction.

What a full answer contains 2022 Problem 1, 2023 Problem 1 and 2015 Problem 3 reduce to four lines: state the debt policy, compute TS = τc·rD·D, discount at the rate that policy implies and say why, then write VL = VU + PV(TS). H2025 Exercise 4 does it inside a two-period valuation, recomputing the shield from each year's debt and discounting at rU, because that firm holds a constant D/E.
Forward and backward Issuing debt and buying back shares is k6 § 6.4, unchanged; only the execution price moves. If the shield always adds value, why is no firm entirely debt-financed? k8 subtracts expected distress costs and nets off personal taxes on interest income. The two rules in § 7.3 return as the central decision of the APV method in k18.
↑ contents

8 · Financial distress, bankruptcy costs and the trade-off theory

Chapter 7 left leverage looking free: every krone of permanent debt adds τc to value, so the answer would be 100% debt. No firm does that. This chapter is the counterweight, the third term of the k0 identity. Distress costs reach three of the eleven mapped papers and neither Kurbatov paper alone, but k9 and k10 are built on them.

8.1 What distress actually costs

Financial distress is not economic failure: it means the firm cannot pay its debts on time. Handing productive assets to creditors changes only who owns them, which MM I says is free. What is not free is the process.

TypeWhat it isTypical estimate
DirectLawyers, advisers, court and trustee fees, management time3–4%
IndirectCustomers needing warranties leave, suppliers demand cash, staff leave, assets are fire-sold, projects shelved10–20%
Why the indirect costs are the ones that matter Direct costs are close to a fixed fee, so they bite hardest on small firms. Indirect costs are behavioural: everyone dealing with the firm changes what they do the moment default looks likely, and most arrive before any court is involved. Distress is therefore expensive for intangible-heavy firms, cheap for firms holding redeployable assets.

8.2 Who bears the cost, and why it is in the price today

Bankruptcy is paid out of the assets, so it looks like the creditors' problem. It is not:

VL = VU + PV(TS) − PV(financial distress costs)
The third term is the probability-weighted, discounted deadweight loss: only value that leaves the firm entirely, never the transfer from shareholders to creditors. It grows with leverage and asset volatility, which drive the probability of distress, and with the loss given distress. Not on the formula sheet.
Why shareholders pay for a bankruptcy they will not attend Debt is sold at a fair price. Creditors who expect to lose in some state pay less for the bond, by the present value of that loss, so the firm raises less cash and the shortfall lands on whoever sold the claim. The direction is fixed: ex post the costs fall on creditors, ex ante entirely on shareholders.
Worked example 1: who pays for the bankruptcy costs

Nordvik AS owns assets worth 260 or 104 in one year, equally likely under the risk-neutral probability ρ = 0.50. Value each claim as its expected payoff discounted at rf = 4%. No taxes; default consumes 25% of asset value. Proposed debt: K = 130, one year.

Step 1 — unlevered benchmark. VU = [0.50(260) + 0.50(104)]/1.04 = 182/1.04 = 175.00

Step 2 — sort the states. Good: 260 > 130, paid in full. Bad: 104 < 130, default, so the process burns 0.25(104) = 26.00 and creditors collect 78.00.

State (ρ)AssetsCreditorsShareholdersBurned
Good (0.50)260130.00130.000.00
Bad (0.50)10478.000.0026.00
Expected182104.0065.0013.00

Step 3 — value the claims. D = 104.00/1.04 = 100.00, E = 65.00/1.04 = 62.50, so VL = 100.00 + 62.50 = 162.50.

Step 4 — the distress cost. PV(FD) = 13.00/1.04 = 12.50, and VU − VL = 175.00 − 162.50 = 12.50. The missing value is exactly the deadweight loss.

Step 5 — who lost it. Creditors pay 100.00 for a claim worth 100.00, gain 0.00; their protection is the promised yield 130.00/100.00 − 1 = 30.0000%. The owner ends with 100.00 cash plus 62.50 of equity, that is 162.50 against 175.00 all-equity: a loss of 12.50.

Check (consistency check 3): 0.00 + (−12.50) = −12.50 = the NPV of the financing decision ✓ Check 4 would fail here, correctly: value was burned, not transferred, so total value is not fixed.

8.3 Personal taxes and the effective tax advantage τ*

The k7 shield is corporate only. Investors are taxed again on what reaches them, and interest and equity income carry different personal rates:

τ* = 1 − (1 − τc)(1 − τe) / (1 − τi)
τe = personal rate on equity income, τi = on interest income. One krone of pre-tax profit delivers (1 − τc)(1 − τe) through equity and (1 − τi) through debt; τ* is what the debt route saves. Replace τc by τ* throughout k7. Not on the formula sheet.
Why an investor's own tax bill changes a corporate decision Investors price claims on what they keep, not on what the firm pays. Interest is taxed more heavily in their hands than equity income, because capital gains are taxed only on realisation. So τi > τe in most systems, τ* < τc, and a big enough personal penalty drives the advantage to zero.

8.4 The trade-off curve

The benefit is linear, the cost convex, so the optimum is interior and the rule is marginal:

VL(D) = VU + τ*·D − PV(FD)(D)   ⟹   borrow while τ* > ΔPV(FD)/ΔD
D* is where the marginal krone of debt buys as much tax shield as it adds expected distress cost. The exam hands you the schedule as a table: compute the differences, do not eyeball the levels.
Worked example 2: τ* and the optimal debt level

Sørland ASA is worth VU = 500 unlevered, with τc = 22%, τe = 30%, τi = 35%. Debt is permanent; PV(FD) at each level is estimated below.

Step 1 — the effective tax advantage. τ* = 1 − (1 − 0.22)(1 − 0.30)/(1 − 0.35) = 1 − (0.78 × 0.70)/0.65 = 1 − 0.546/0.65 = 1 − 0.84 = 0.1600 = 16.00%, against 22.00% corporate.

Dτ*·DPV(FD)VLMarginal shieldMarginal PV(FD)
00.000.00500.00——
10016.003.50512.5016.003.50
15024.009.00515.008.005.50
20032.0018.50513.508.009.50
25040.0028.50511.508.0010.00
30048.0041.00507.008.0012.50

Step 2 — read the margin. Each extra 50 of debt buys 0.16(50) = 8.00 of shield. From 100 to 150 it costs 9.00 − 3.50 = 5.50, so take it; from 150 to 200 it costs 18.50 − 9.00 = 9.50 > 8.00, so stop. D* = 150, VL = 500.00 + 24.00 − 9.00 = 515.00.

Step 3 — ignoring personal taxes. At τc = 22% each 50 buys 11.00, and the same PV(FD) column gives 518.50, 524.00, 525.50, 526.50, 525.00: the optimum jumps to D* = 250, 100 too much debt, value overstated by 11.50.

Check (consistency check 3): the debt is fairly priced, so creditors pay 150.00 for a claim worth 150.00 and gain 0.00. Shareholders hold 150.00 cash plus equity of 515.00 − 150.00 = 365.00, that is 515.00 against 500.00, a gain of 15.00. Sum: 0.00 + 15.00 = 15.00 = 515.00 − 500.00 ✓

Two errors the schedule invites Do not subtract the transfer: in worked example 1 creditors take 78.00 rather than 130.00, but only the 26.00 nobody receives is a distress cost, not the 52.00 shortfall. And τ* replaces τc, it does not multiply it.
How this is examined Almost never a standalone computation. It arrives as the qualification a number needs: you compute a tax shield and are asked why the firm does not double its debt. Name both cost types, separate deadweight loss from transfer, say creditors price the loss so shareholders bear it ex ante, and end with the marginal condition.
The identity is not finished This still assumes shareholders and creditors want the same thing. Once distress is possible they do not: shareholders gain from bad gambles (k9) and refuse good projects (k10), and creditors price those incentives in exactly as they price bankruptcy costs here. That is the fourth term of the k0 identity; the fifth is k11.
↑ contents

9 · Agency costs of debt I: risk shifting (asset substitution)

H2024 Exercise 4 was risk shifting and hedging, 18 points, and V2024 Problem 1 was the same problem. It reaches five of eleven mapped papers, and unlike k8 it is pure computation. This chapter gives the routine, the debt level at which the incentive flips, and the agency cost the answer ends on.

9.1 Equity is a call option on firm value

Limited liability is the whole story: shareholders are paid last and never less than nothing. Against a face value K their claim on cash flow CF is:

E = E[max(CF − K, 0)] / (1 + rf)   and   D = E[min(CF, K)] / (1 + rf)
K is the face value of the debt, and also the strike of the shareholders' option, so the two words coincide here. Expectations are under the risk-neutral probability ρ. The claims sum to CF in every state: D + E = V. Not on the formula sheet.
Why convexity makes shareholders love risk A convex payoff turns a mean-preserving spread into a gain. Push the good state up and the shareholder collects all of it; push the bad state below K and he gains nothing extra, being already at zero. Downside capped, upside not. That is Jensen's inequality and the whole mechanism: name it risk shifting or asset substitution.
Forward to k24 Figure 9.1 is a call option's payoff diagram; k24 makes it formal: equity is a call on firm value struck at K, risky debt is risk-free debt minus a put on the same assets. One fact carries back: option value rises with volatility, which is why raising asset risk moves value to shareholders.

9.2 The routine, state by state

Never argue this verbally. Build the table, value both claims, compare. The number the exercise ends on is:

agency cost = V(first best) − V(chosen)
A deadweight loss, not a transfer: the value destroyed because the decision maximises equity rather than the firm. Compute both firm values first, then split each into D and E.
Worked example 1: the gamble, and what it costs

Vestbre AS runs its assets as one of two mutually exclusive projects, neither needing new investment. Payoffs arrive in one year in two equally likely states, ρ = 0.50. Set rf = 0, so value equals expected payoff; otherwise every figure is divided by (1 + rf), changing no comparison. Debt outstanding: face value K = 100.

State (ρ)CF under SDebtEquityCF under RDebtEquity
Boom (0.50)1201002017010070
Bust (0.50)100100030300
Expected110100101006535

Step 1 — the first best. VS = 0.50(120) + 0.50(100) = 110, VR = 0.50(170) + 0.50(30) = 100. An all-equity firm takes S; switching to R has NPV = 100 − 110 = −10.

Step 2 — split the payoffs. Under S the bust state still covers 100, so debt is risk-free: DS = 100, ES = 0.50(20) = 10. Under R the bust state pays creditors 30: DR = 0.50(100) + 0.50(30) = 65, ER = 0.50(70) = 35.

Step 3 — what management does. Managers act for shareholders, and 35 > 10, so they take R, which destroys 10 of firm value. This is risk shifting, or asset substitution.

Step 4 — the agency cost. First-best value minus chosen value = 110 − 100 = 10. That is the agency cost, not the 25 shareholders gained nor the 35 creditors lost.

Check (consistency check 3): creditors' gain = 65 − 100 = −35, shareholders' gain = 35 − 10 = +25. Sum: −35 + 25 = −10 = the NPV of switching to R ✓

Three ways to lose the points Compare the equity values, not the projects' NPVs: the question is what management chooses, not what is efficient. Do not report the transfer as the agency cost. And never forget the zero: equity in R's bust state is max(30 − 100, 0) = 0, not −70.

9.3 The debt level at which the incentive flips

Risk shifting needs enough debt. Write both equity values as functions of K, then set them equal:

ES(K) = ER(K)   ⟹   K*
Below K* shareholders prefer the safe project, above it they gamble. Solve inside an interval where the same states default: max(·, 0) makes both functions piecewise linear, so the algebra holds only within one piece. State the interval.
Worked example 2: the indifference face value, and a free hedge

Part A. Same firm. At which face value K is management indifferent?

Step 1 — pick the interval. For 30 < K ≤ 100, S covers its debt in both states while R defaults in the bust. Then ES(K) = 0.50(120 − K) + 0.50(100 − K) = 110 − K and ER(K) = 0.50(170 − K) + 0.50(0) = 85 − 0.5K.

Step 2 — solve. 110 − K = 85 − 0.5K ⟹ 25 = 0.5K ⟹ K* = 50, where both equal 110 − 50 = 60 and 85 − 25 = 60 ✓

Face value KESERManagement picks
0110100S
308070S
506060indifferent
605055R
1001035R

Step 3 — read it. Below 30 nothing defaults and the gap is a constant 10 favouring S. Debt turns risky at 30, but only at K* = 50 does the transfer from creditors cover the 10 the gamble destroys. Below 50, no conflict.

Part B. At K = 100, where R is chosen, a costless forward would convert R's payoff into a certain 100 in both states. Its NPV is zero: expected payoff 100 either way. Do shareholders take it?

State (ρ)CF unhedgedDebtEquityCF hedgedDebtEquity
Boom (0.50)170100701001000
Bust (0.50)303001001000
Expected10065351001000

Step 4 — no. Hedging drops equity from 35 to 0, so shareholders refuse a zero-NPV project. Removing the risk removes the option value, and the whole 35 goes to creditors.

Check (consistency check 4): total value is fixed at 100, so this is pure transfer. Creditors' gain = 100 − 65 = +35 equals shareholders' loss 35 − 0 = 35 ✓ Check 3 also holds, weakly: +35 − 35 = 0 = the hedge's NPV.

Why a zero-NPV hedge is not neutral Only with risk-free debt is a hedge neutral, because then equity is linear in CF and expectations pass straight through. Once debt is risky the claim is convex, so equity depends on the whole distribution, not its mean. Shareholders hedge willingly at low leverage, refuse in deep distress; creditors are the ones who want it.

9.4 Who really pays

The 35 transfer looks like a shareholder victory. It is not:

Why the gain vanishes once creditors see it coming If creditors know at issue that R will be chosen, they pay 65 for the bond, not 100, so the firm raises 35 less today. Shareholders then own everything: 65 raised plus 35 of equity is 100, against 110 had they been able to promise S. They bear the agency cost of 10 ex ante, exactly as they bore k8's distress costs. Hence covenants and collateral, which shareholders accept.
How H2024 Exercise 4 asked it The pattern is fixed: two projects, one levered balance sheet, then sub-questions: which project an all-equity firm takes, which the levered firm takes, the gain or loss to each claim, the indifference debt level, and the hedge. It is chained, so a slip in the first table propagates. Show the table, write "risk shifting", and close with check 3 or 4 by name.
↑ contents

10 · Agency costs of debt II: debt overhang and renegotiation

Chapter 9 had shareholders picking the wrong project. Here they pick none: a positive-NPV investment any all-equity firm would take, refused because the debt is risky. Overhang and renegotiation appear in five of the eleven mapped papers: 2017V Problem 3, 2017H Problems 1 and 4, 2021 Problem 2. The arithmetic is a state-by-state table closing with check 3.

10.1 Why shareholders refuse a good project

Everything rests on the one-period split of the firm's cash flow:

D1 = min(V1, K)   and   E1 = max(V1 − K, 0)
V1 is the cash flow at maturity, K the face value of the debt. The two add to V1 in every state, which is what makes check 3 work.

Shareholders pay for the rise in the value of their own claim, not for projects, and when debt is risky part of any new value lands on creditors who paid nothing:

Δ(shareholders' wealth) = NPV − ΔD
ΔD is the rise in the market value of the existing debt, so shareholders invest only when NPV > ΔD. Debt overhang is the case ΔD > NPV > 0: the project creates value and the owners who fund it lose. Not on the formula sheet.
Why does the creditor capture value he did not pay for? In default states creditors take the whole cash flow, so every extra krone produced there goes to them krone for krone, while shareholders, wiped out anyway, see none of it. The outlay today is paid in full by the owners. If enough of the project's cash arrives there, they are funding a transfer to the lenders.
Worked example 1: a positive-NPV project the owners reject

Fjordhus AS has one year to maturity on debt of face value K0 = 100. Next year's cash flow from assets in place is 50 in the bad state and 150 in the good, each with risk-neutral probability ρ = 0.5, and rf = 5.00%, so every claim is its expected payoff discounted at rf. A project costing I = 20 today, funded by the owners, adds a safe 30 in both states.

Step 1 — value the claims as they stand.

State (ρ = 0.5)Cash flowDebt min(CF, 100)Equity max(CF − 100, 0)
Bad50500
Good15010050
Expected1007525

D0 = 75/1.05 = 71.4286, E0 = 25/1.05 = 23.8095, V0 = 95.2381.

Step 2 — the NPV. 30/1.05 − 20 = 28.5714 − 20 = 8.5714, positive.

Step 3 — revalue with the project in place.

State (ρ = 0.5)Cash flowDebt min(CF, 100)Equity max(CF − 100, 0)
Bad80800
Good18010080
Expected1309040

D1 = 90/1.05 = 85.7143, E1 = 40/1.05 = 38.0952.

Step 4 — who gained. Creditors: 85.7143 − 71.4286 = +14.2857. Shareholders: 38.0952 − 23.8095 − 20 = −5.7143, so they reject. This is debt overhang; write the words.

Check 3 — creditors' gain + shareholders' gain = NPV: 14.2857 + (−5.7143) = 8.5714 ✓ The bad-state increment, worth 0.5(30)/1.05 = 14.2857, went wholly to the lenders while the owners paid all 20.

10.2 Where the overhang bites

The 5.7143 is not the agency cost The agency cost is the value lost because the project is skipped, so it is the NPV of 8.5714. The 5.7143 is a transfer that never happens, because the investment is never made. Measure it as first-best value minus value under the chosen action, as in chapter 9. And check 4 does not apply: total value is not fixed here.

10.3 Renegotiating the face value

The creditors would rather have the project, which lifts their claim by 14.2857 for free, but the shareholders decide. So they must pay the owners to say yes, and the instrument is the face value: cut K and the equity line above shifts left, putting more of the project's cash on equity. Two constraints bound the deal:

D(K1) ≥ D0   and   E(K1) − I ≥ E0
K1 is the renegotiated face value; K is a face value throughout this chapter, never a strike. D0 and E0 are the claim values before the deal. Any K1 meeting both is signable; where in that range it lands is bargaining power.
Why must the total gain equal the NPV exactly? Renegotiation changes only the division of the payoff, the project only how much there is to divide. Add the two gains and every division term cancels, leaving the NPV. Check 3 therefore holds at any K1 in the range; gains summing to anything else mean an error.
Worked example 2: a face value both sides accept

Same firm. The creditors offer to cut the face value to K1 if the owners inject the 20; with the project the cash flows are 80 and 180.

Step 1 — the creditors' floor. They need D(K1) ≥ 71.4286, so the expected payoff must reach 75. For K1 ≤ 80 the debt is safe and pays K1 in both states, so K1 ≥ 75.

Step 2 — the shareholders' ceiling. For K1 ≥ 80 the bad state still defaults, so equity pays 0 and 180 − K1, expected 90 − 0.5K1. They need (90 − 0.5K1)/1.05 − 20 ≥ 23.8095, so 90 − 0.5K1 ≥ 46.0000 and K1 ≤ 88.

Step 3 — the range is 75 ≤ K1 ≤ 88. Take K1 = 82.

State (ρ = 0.5)Cash flowDebt min(CF, 82)Equity max(CF − 82, 0)
Bad80800
Good1808298
Expected1308149

Step 4 — the gains. D = 81/1.05 = 77.1429, so creditors gain 77.1429 − 71.4286 = +5.7143. E = 49/1.05 = 46.6667, so shareholders gain 46.6667 − 23.8095 − 20 = +2.8571. Both positive: the project proceeds, the overhang is resolved.

Check 3: 5.7143 + 2.8571 = 8.5714 = NPV ✓ At the endpoints one side takes everything: K1 = 75 gives the owners all 8.5714, K1 = 88 the creditors.

What the check catches: forgetting to subtract I, and writing K1 rather than the cash flow in default. It cannot catch a wrong ρ or rf, which scale both claims alike.

How this is asked The listed sittings run three moves: value both claims with and without the project, show the shareholders' change is negative, solve for the face value that flips its sign. Neither Kurbatov paper used overhang, though H2024 Exercise 4 used its neighbour, risk shifting. Write "debt overhang", show both tables, close with check 3.

10.4 Contracts that pre-empt the problem

Renegotiation is costly and often impossible with dispersed bondholders, so lenders shrink the problem in advance. Covenants restrict the borrower: caps on further debt, on dividends and repurchases, on asset sales, minimum coverage ratios. They bind where the owners' incentives are worst.

Seniority attacks overhang head on. Fund the project with debt senior to the old claim, or secured on the new asset, and the new lender takes the bad-state cash flow first, so the old creditor collects no windfall and the owners invest., which is why lenders forbid it.

Why a covenant raises firm value, not just the lender's recovery Anything the borrower promises today is priced today. Commit not to shift risk, strip assets or skip good projects, and the debt is worth more at issue, so the same money is raised against a smaller promised repayment. The owners get back the deadweight loss that no longer occurs. The cost is lost flexibility: a covenant binding where the forbidden action was efficient destroys value itself.

Convertible debt lets the lender swap the bond for a fixed number of shares, blunting both agency costs at once: he shares the upside, so risk shifting no longer pays the owners, and he is readier to fund, which makes it the standard instrument for young, volatile firms.

Forward to chapter 24: pricing the convertible Here the convertible is a governance argument only. Chapter 24 makes it a valuation: equity is a call on firm value struck at K, risky debt is risk-free debt minus a put, a convertible is debt plus a call on the equity. Priced that way the kink in Figure 10.1 smooths out and overhang fades as the equity's delta rises to one.
↑ contents

11 · Agency benefits of debt, and the theories assembled

Every chapter since k6 has added one friction and one term. This one adds the last term, where debt helps, then does what the earlier chapters cannot do alone: it assembles the pieces into a framework you can run on any firm. Both Kurbatov papers put 24 of their 100 points into prose, and the interpretation parts of the quantitative exercises are graded the same way.

11.1 Free cash flow and empire building

Managers are not shareholders. A larger firm brings a larger salary, more status and more security, so a manager holding cash has a private reason to spend it even when the spending destroys value. Nothing stops him while the cash sits inside the firm: no lender to satisfy, no issue to be priced by a sceptical market. This is the agency cost of equity, and it is what the final term of the course identity offsets:

VL = VU + PV(TS) − PV(distress costs) − PV(agency costs of debt) + PV(agency benefits of debt)
The organising identity from k0, now complete. Terms 2 and 3 are the trade-off theory (k7, k8), term 4 is risk shifting and overhang (k9, k10), term 5 is this chapter. Not on the formula sheet.
Why does a debt contract commit when a promise does not? A manager can announce that surplus cash will be returned and quietly stop next year; a missed dividend costs him nothing enforceable. A missed interest payment puts the firm in default, hands control to creditors and usually ends his career. The commitment is credible precisely because the penalty is severe and outside his control, which is the same feature that made debt costly in k9 and k10. One friction, two signs.
Why the problem is worst in cash-rich, mature firms Free cash flow is what remains after every positive-NPV project is funded. A firm with many good projects has nothing left over, so discipline is not needed and flexibility is worth more. A mature firm in a shrinking industry generates cash it has nowhere good to put, and that is the cash that finances diversifying acquisitions and vanity capacity. Leveraged buyouts apply this mechanism deliberately.
Worked example 1: pricing the discipline a recapitalisation buys

Vestland Marine is all-equity, generates FCF of 100 a year in perpetuity, has rU = 10.00% and holds 300 of excess cash. The CEO intends to spend the 300 on a terminal returning 24 a year in perpetuity. τc = 22%. The alternative is to borrow 500 of permanent debt and pay out both the proceeds and the cash; at that leverage PV(distress costs) = 40. Permanent fixed debt means the tax shield is discounted at rD, so PV(TS) = τcD (k7).

Step 1 — the firm as it stands. VU = 100/0.10 = 1000, plus 300 of cash.

Step 2 — the CEO's project. PV = 24/0.10 = 240, so NPV = 240 − 300 = −60, and the market prices that in today.

Step 3 — wealth if the cash stays inside. 1000 + 240 = 1240.

Step 4 — value the recapitalisation. PV(TS) = 0.22 × 500 = 110.

ComponentValue
VU, operations1000.00
+ PV(TS)110.00
− PV(distress costs)−40.00
VL1070.00
of which debt500.00
of which equity570.00
+ paid out to shareholders800.00
Shareholder wealth1370.00

Step 5 — the gain, decomposed. 1370 − 1240 = +130, which splits into the three named terms: agency benefit +60, tax shield +110, distress −40. 60 + 110 − 40 = 130 ✓

Step 6 — the check that can actually fail. D + E = 500 + 570 = 1070 cannot fail, since E was computed as VL − D. The decomposition can: if the three terms do not rebuild the 130 you have double counted or dropped a friction. The new creditors gain nothing, paying 500 for a claim worth 500, so by the conservation logic of check 3 the shareholders take the whole 130.

Step 7 — how far it stretches. The recapitalisation stops paying once distress costs reach 60 + 110 = 170. Quoting that break-even separates an answer that understood the trade-off from one that memorised it.

11.2 The forces in one table

Each friction pushes leverage one way and predicts which firms should be levered. Learn the table by its last column, because that is the column an exam question hands you.

ForceFrictionPush on D/VPredicts high leverage for
MM I and IInoneneutralthe benchmark: leverage is irrelevant
Interest tax shieldinterest is deductibleupprofitable firms with reliable taxable income
Financial distressdeadweight bankruptcy costsdownstable cash flow, tangible redeployable assets
Agency costs of debtrisk shifting, debt overhangdownfew growth options, low asset volatility
Agency benefits of debtfree cash flow, empire buildingupmature, cash-rich firms, weak governance
Asymmetric informationadverse selection on an equity issueup, debt before equityopaque firms that must fund externally
at the optimum: marginal PV(TS) = marginal distress + marginal agency cost − marginal agency benefit
The trade-off theory in one line with the agency terms added: the last krone of debt should add as much tax shield as it costs in expected deadweight loss net of discipline. It is a target, and firms sit near it rather than on it.

11.3 What actually predicts leverage

Three regularities are worth carrying in. Leverage differs far more between industries than within them: utilities, property and shipping carry heavy debt, software and biotech almost none, which the last column above explains in one pass. Second, the most profitable firms often borrow least, which the tax story gets backwards and the pecking order explains: profits are retained, and retained earnings are the cheapest source. Third, leverage is persistent, because adjusting it is costly, so what you observe is a lagged target rather than today's optimum.

Listing the theories is not an answer The commonest way to lose points on a capital-structure discussion is a paragraph per theory, then a full stop. The examiner wants three things the list does not contain: a sign for each term given this firm's characteristics, a statement of which term dominates, and a conclusion in the direction the question asked. Name the mechanism, sign it, net it.

11.4 Writing the discussion answer

Worked example 2: a discussion answer, written out

The question, in the shape these are set: Firm A is a mature shipping company with stable cash flows, a fleet other operators would buy, no attractive expansion and a large cash balance. Firm B is a biotechnology firm with no revenue, volatile prospects and a value made almost entirely of growth options. Both are all-equity. Which should carry more debt, and why?

Move 1 — state the frame, one sentence. "Capital structure matters only through the terms of VL = VU + PV(TS) − distress − agency costs of debt + agency benefits, so the question is which terms are large for each firm."

Move 2 — sign every term for A. Tax shield large: stable taxable profits use the deduction every year. Distress small: steady cash flows, and tangible assets mean a default transfers ships rather than destroying a business. Agency cost of debt small: with no growth options there is little scope for overhang or risk shifting. Agency benefit large: idle cash and no good projects is the empire-building setup, and debt service removes the cash. Every term points one way, so A should be highly levered.

Move 3 — sign every term for B. Tax shield near zero: no taxable income, so the deduction shields nothing. Distress large: the value is intangible growth options that evaporate if the researchers leave. Agency cost of debt large: overhang would block exactly the risky positive-NPV research the firm exists to do. Agency benefit small: B has no free cash flow to waste and needs to raise money, not disburse it. Every term points the other way, so B should be all-equity.

Move 4 — close with a prediction and one caveat. "The prediction is a wide leverage gap driven by asset tangibility and growth options. The caveat is information: under the pecking order B still prefers internal funds to an equity issue, which is why such firms use staged financing from informed investors and convertible debt."

Check: four moves, about five sentences per firm, which fits the stated budgets on these questions, and every move ends in a direction rather than a description ✓

How the verbal exercises are graded The two 12-point discussion exercises come with explicit sentence budgets: H2025 asked for two to three sentences per action, and four to six sentences is stated as usually enough. Marks go to reasonable, well-explained answers, and H2025 credited creativity, so a well-argued unusual point costs nothing. Write to the budget, name the mechanism, finish with a direction.
The information term comes next, in full The last row of the table is the one treated most thinly here. k12 builds the Myers-Majluf template, which prices an equity issue under asymmetric information and then asks whether investors' beliefs are rational, consistency check 5. k13 turns it into the pecking order and market timing. Together they explain the profitability regularity above.

Part II — Information and issuance

↑ contents

12 · Asymmetric information: Myers-Majluf

Five listed sittings run this one template almost unchanged: 2016 Problem 1, 2017V Problem 1, 2020 Problem 2, 2022 Problem 2 and 2023 Problem 4. It reaches eight of the eleven mapped papers, yet neither Kurbatov paper used it, which makes it the largest single block of examinable material you have not yet seen tested in the new format. The setup is not on the formula sheet, so every line of it has to come out of your head. And it is the home of consistency check 5, the step the solution keys say candidates skip.

12.1 What the manager knows and the market does not

Everything so far has assumed that you and the market see the same firm. Drop that. The manager runs the assets every day and knows what they are worth; outsiders know only the range and the odds. Nothing else changes: markets are competitive, investors are rational, prices are fair given what investors know. That last clause is the whole chapter.

The standard exam setup has five ingredients and never more:

SymbolMeaningWho knows it
VValue of assets in place, high VH or low VLmanager only
qProbability the market attaches to the high typeeveryone
ICash the firm must raise to fund the projecteveryone
NPVNet present value of the project, over and above Ieveryone
αFraction of the post-issue firm sold to new investorsset by the market

Note which one is public. The project is transparent, the firm is not. That asymmetry is what makes the problem bite: investors cannot doubt the project, only the assets it is being bolted onto. The sequence is fixed and you should write it down before you compute anything:

  1. Nature draws the type. The manager sees it, the market does not.
  2. The manager either issues equity worth I and takes the project, or does neither.
  3. Investors observe the action, form a belief about V, and price the new shares so that they break even in expectation.
  4. The truth comes out and the payoffs are realised.

Two modelling assumptions carry the result, and an exam answer should name them. First, the manager acts for the existing shareholders, the ones who will not buy into the issue, and maximises the value of their claim rather than the value of the whole firm. Second, the project disappears if it is not funded now: there is no cash in the bank, no bank loan, no waiting until the information becomes public. Relax either one and the problem softens, which is exactly what k13 is about.

Why can the manager not simply announce that the firm is the high type? Because the announcement costs nothing to make. A low-type manager gains from being taken for a high type, so he says the same words, and investors who know this learn nothing from either. Talk moves prices only when it is expensive in a way that differs by type, which is why the credible responses in k13 are all actions with a cost: taking on debt you must service, buying back stock with real cash, holding on to a project rather than funding it. Cheap talk is not a signal. Write that sentence in a verbal answer and you have named the mechanism.

12.2 The template: pricing the issue and the issue rule

New investors are competitive, so they pay exactly what their stake is worth under their beliefs. Their stake is the fraction α of the firm as it will look after the issue, which contains three things: the assets in place as investors assess them, the cash I that has just come in, and the value the project adds beyond its cost. Break-even for them is the pricing condition:

α × (E[V | beliefs] + I + NPV) = I   ⇒   α = I / (E[V | beliefs] + I + NPV)
α is the fraction of the post-issue firm the new shareholders receive. E[V | beliefs] is what investors think the assets in place are worth given the action they have just observed, not the unconditional average. The denominator is the post-issue market value of the whole firm. Not on the formula sheet: memorise it.
The cash raised belongs in the denominator The single most common way to wreck this exercise is to write α = I/(E[V] + NPV). The firm after the issue is worth the assets in place plus the project, and the project is worth what it cost plus what it added, that is I + NPV. Leave the I out and the denominator is too small, α is too large, and every payoff after it is wrong in the same direction. Sanity check on any α you compute: a firm that raises 60 against assets of 175 and a project adding 15 has sold roughly a quarter of itself, not a third.

Now switch to the manager's side of the table. He knows V, so he knows what the firm is really worth after the issue: V + I + NPV. The old shareholders keep the complement of α:

Payoff to old shareholders = (1 − α)(V + I + NPV)
Issue and invest if (1 − α)(V + I + NPV) > V
V here is the true value known to the manager, while the α multiplying it was priced off the market's belief. The comparison is against V alone, because refusing the issue means losing the project. If the firm could fund the project from cash instead, the comparison becomes V + NPV against (1 − α)(V + I + NPV), so the underinvestment disappears: the good type never has to give anything away to invest. Note what does not disappear. An overvalued firm still gains −α(V − E[V | beliefs]) > 0 by selling shares, which is why financial slack removes the underinvestment problem without removing the incentive to time the market. That thread is picked up in k13.

Those two lines are enough to answer any sub-question that asks "will the firm issue?", but they hide the economics. Substituting the first into the second and cancelling gives the form you should actually reason with:

Payoff to old shareholders = V + NPV − α(V − E[V | beliefs])
Issue if and only if NPV > α(V − E[V | beliefs])
The second term is the dilution cost: the wealth handed to new investors because they bought a stake in a firm worth more than they believed. It is a transfer, not a cost of production. The rule says: take the project when what it adds beats what the issue gives away. If beliefs happen to be correct, V = E[V | beliefs], the dilution term is zero and the old shareholders capture the entire NPV.
Why an undervalued firm refuses free money The project has a positive NPV, the financing is fairly priced given what investors know, and still the answer can be no. The reason is that the price is fair on average and the manager is not average. Selling a quarter of a firm the market undervalues by 75 costs the existing owners about 18 in transferred wealth, and if the project only adds 15 they are better off letting a good project die than selling a good firm cheaply. Nothing is irrational here, and nobody is fooled. That is what makes adverse selection different from mispricing.
Worked example 1: the full template, and the separating outcome

Fjellstrand ASA has assets in place worth 250 if the drilling programme succeeded and 100 if it did not. Only management knows which. The market puts q = 0.50 on the good outcome. A new project requires I = 60 raised by issuing shares today, and its NPV of 15 is public information: investors can price the project, not the firm. Without the issue the project is lost. Does Fjellstrand issue?

InputValue
VH, assets in place if high250
VL, assets in place if low100
q, market's prior on the high type0.50
I, amount to raise60
NPV of the project15

Step 1 — start from the pooling belief. Always test the naive belief first: suppose investors expect both types to issue, so seeing an issue teaches them nothing and E[V | issue] equals the prior. E[V | issue] = 0.50(250) + 0.50(100) = 175.

Step 2 — price the issue. Post-issue value under that belief = 175 + 60 + 15 = 250, so α = 60/250 = 0.2400.

Step 3 — payoff to the old shareholders of each type. Use the dilution form and confirm with the direct form.

TypeDilution α(V − E[V|issue])Payoff (1−α)(V+I+NPV)Value if no issueIssue?
High, V = 2500.24 × 75 = 18.000.76 × 325 = 247.00250.00no
Low, V = 1000.24 × (−75) = −18.000.76 × 175 = 133.00100.00yes

Read the first row: 250 + 15 − 18.00 = 247.00, which is 3.00 below the 250 the old shareholders keep by walking away. The high type refuses.

Step 4 — consistency check 5: is the belief rational? No. The belief that produced α = 0.2400 was "both types issue", and we have just shown the high type will not. A belief that is contradicted by the behaviour it predicts cannot be an equilibrium, so the pooling candidate collapses. Redo it with the belief that survives: only the low type issues, so E[V | issue] = 100 and E[V | no issue] = 250.

Step 5 — reprice under the separating belief. α = 60/(100 + 60 + 15) = 60/175 = 0.3429 (12/35 exactly). The low type's old shareholders keep (1 − 12/35)(100 + 60 + 15) = (23/35)(175) = 115.00, against 100 if they do not issue, so the low type still issues ✓.

Step 6 — check the high type does not want to deviate. If the high type issued at this α it would receive (23/35)(250 + 60 + 15) = (23/35)(325) = 213.5714, far below 250. It stays out ✓. Both types are behaving as the belief assumes, so the belief is rational: this is a separating equilibrium.

Check A — correct beliefs mean no transfer. The issuing firm is correctly identified as the low type, so the dilution term is zero and the payoff must be exactly VL + NPV = 100 + 15 = 115.00. It is ✓. Whenever the issuer's type is revealed, this shortcut replaces the arithmetic.

Check B — prices must average back. Before the announcement the shares are worth 0.50(250) + 0.50(115) = 182.50. That equals the prior 175 plus the expected NPV actually captured, 0.50(15) = 7.50, since only the low type invests ✓. If your two post-announcement prices do not average to the pre-announcement price, one of them is wrong.

The answer sentence. "In the separating equilibrium only the low-value firm issues, at α = 0.3429. The high-value firm forgoes a project with NPV 15, so an expected 0.50 × 15 = 7.50 of value is destroyed by the information problem alone."

12.3 Step four: pooling, separating, and why beliefs must be checked

An equilibrium here is a pair, not a number: an action for each type and a belief for the market that is correct given those actions. Two candidates, and you test them in this order:

Pooling: both types issue  ⇒  E[V | issue] = qVH + (1 − q)VL
Separating: only the low type issues  ⇒  E[V | issue] = VL, E[V | no issue] = VH
A pooling belief is rational only if both types find it optimal to issue when priced at the pooled average. A separating belief is rational only if the low type issues at the harsher price and the high type still prefers to abstain at that same price. Check both directions, every time.

The low type never breaks a pooling equilibrium: it is always sold at or above what it is worth, so it always wants to issue. Everything therefore turns on the high type, and its condition is the compact rule from 12.2 with the pooled belief in it:

High type issues ⟺ NPV > α(VH − E[V | issue]) = I(VH − E[V]) / (E[V] + I + NPV)
Everything on the right is a dilution cost, so the whole model reduces to one comparison: project value against transferred value. It grows with the amount raised I and with the gap between what the firm is worth and what the market thinks, and it shrinks as the market's view moves closer to the truth.
Why the pooling candidate falls apart from the top down Pooling prices every issuer at the average. That is a subsidy to the worst firm in the pool and a tax on the best, so the best firm leaves first. Once it leaves, the pool that remains is worse, the price investors are willing to pay drops, and the new best firm in the pool now faces the tax the departing one just escaped. This is Akerlof's used-car market with a share issue in place of a car, and it explains why the equilibria are always of the form "the top slice abstains, the rest pool". With only two types the top slice is the high type and you are done in one round.
Worked example 2: the same firm, a more confident market

Fjellstrand again, all inputs unchanged, except that a successful test well has moved the market's prior to q = 0.80. Does the answer change, and at what prior does it change?

Step 1 — the pooled belief. E[V | issue] = 0.80(250) + 0.20(100) = 200 + 20 = 220.

Step 2 — price it. Post-issue value = 220 + 60 + 15 = 295, so α = 60/295 = 0.2034 (12/59).

Step 3 — the high type's dilution. α(VH − E[V | issue]) = 0.203390 × (250 − 220) = 0.203390 × 30 = 6.1017. That is below the NPV of 15, so the high type issues. Payoff = 250 + 15 − 6.1017 = 258.8983, against 250 by abstaining. Direct form: (1 − 12/59)(250 + 60 + 15) = (47/59)(325) = 258.8983 ✓

Step 4 — the low type. It gains 0.203390 × (220 − 100) = 24.4068, so its payoff is 100 + 15 + 24.4068 = 139.4068 against 100 ✓. Direct form: (47/59)(175) = 139.4068 ✓

Step 5 — consistency check 5. Both types issue, which is exactly what the pooling belief assumed, so E[V | issue] = 220 is confirmed by the behaviour it priced. This is a rational pooling equilibrium, and the answer is that both firms invest.

Step 6 — the prior at which it flips. Exam versions ask this as a separate sub-question. Write E[V] = 100 + 150q and set the high type's dilution equal to its NPV:

60(250 − 100 − 150q) = 15(100 + 150q + 60 + 15)  ⇒  9000 − 9000q = 2625 + 2250q  ⇒  11250q = 6375  ⇒  q* = 6375/11250 = 0.5667 (17/30).

Check: at q* the pooled belief is 100 + 150(17/30) = 185, so α = 60/(185 + 60 + 15) = 60/260 = 0.230769, and the dilution is 0.230769 × (250 − 185) = 15.0000, exactly the NPV ✓ Above q* pooling survives, below it the market is too pessimistic for the good firm to sell shares and the outcome separates. Worked example 1 sat at q = 0.50, on the separating side of 0.5667.

12.4 Rational beliefs, bad outcomes

Nobody in this model is fooled, and yet the two equilibria are not equally benign. Only one of them destroys value. Get the distinction right, because the exam asks for it in words.

Separating destroys value. The high type abandons a project with a positive NPV. That NPV is not transferred to anyone, it simply never happens. The expected loss is the probability of the good type times the NPV it walks away from, which in worked example 1 is 0.50 × 15 = 7.50. This is underinvestment through adverse selection, and it belongs in the same family as the debt overhang of k10: a positive-NPV project declined because of who captures the value, not because of what the project is worth.

Pooling only moves value. Both types invest, so nothing real is lost. What happens instead is that the good firm's old shareholders hand value to the new investors, and the bad firm's old shareholders receive value through them. In worked example 2 the good type gives up 6.1017 and the bad type gains 24.4068. Those are not equal, and they are not meant to be: what competition forces is that new investors break even on average, so q × (the good type's loss) = (1 − q) × (the bad type's gain). Here 0.80 × 6.1017 = 0.20 × 24.4068 = 4.8814 ✓ The two sides are equal in size only when q = 0.5. Total value is unchanged either way, so this is a transfer in the sense of consistency check 4, not a deadweight loss.

Worked example 3: a better project buys the good firm out of the problem

Fjellstrand once more, with q back at 0.50 and the same VH = 250, VL = 100 and I = 60. The project is now a much better one: NPV = 40. Show that the outcome pools, and find the NPV at which it flips.

Step 1 — pooled belief and price. E[V | issue] = 175 as in worked example 1, but the post-issue value is now 175 + 60 + 40 = 275, so α = 60/275 = 0.2182 (12/55). Note that the better project has lowered α: the firm sells a smaller slice for the same 60 because the firm it is selling a slice of is worth more.

Step 2 — the high type. Dilution = 0.218182 × (250 − 175) = 16.3636, comfortably under the NPV of 40. Payoff = 250 + 40 − 16.3636 = 273.6364 against 250 by abstaining, so it issues. Direct form: (43/55)(250 + 60 + 40) = (43/55)(350) = 273.6364 ✓

Step 3 — the low type. Gain = 0.218182 × (175 − 100) = 16.3636, payoff = 100 + 40 + 16.3636 = 156.3636 against 100, so it issues. Direct form: (43/55)(200) = 156.3636 ✓

Step 4 — consistency check 5. Both types issue, matching the belief that priced the shares, so E[V | issue] = 175 stands and this is a rational pooling equilibrium. Both firms invest and no NPV is lost.

Step 5 — the NPV that flips it. With q = 0.50 the high type is indifferent when NPV = n solves n(175 + 60 + n) = 60(250 − 175), that is n² + 235n − 4500 = 0. The positive root is n = (−235 + √73225)/2 = (−235 + 270.6012)/2 = 17.8006. Below that the outcome separates, above it pools, which places the NPV of 15 in worked example 1 just on the wrong side of the line.

Check — conservation. The new investors pay 60 for a stake worth 0.218182 × 350 = 76.3636 if the firm is high and 0.218182 × 200 = 43.6364 if it is low. Their expected value is 0.50(76.3636) + 0.50(43.6364) = 60.0000, exactly what they paid ✓ Competitive pricing means the two 16.3636 figures are the same krone moving between two groups of old shareholders. No value is created or destroyed here, only relabelled, which is why the pooling outcome is efficient in real terms even though the good firm's owners dislike it.

"The market is undervaluing us, so an issue is a bad idea" is only half an answer The half that is right: the good firm is diluted. The half that is missed: whether the dilution outweighs the NPV, and what happens to the price once investors work out what an issue implies. Worked examples 1 and 3 have identical asymmetric information, the same VH, VL and q, and opposite answers, because the project differs. And in worked example 3 the good firm issues while still being undervalued, so "we are undervalued" does not on its own predict anything. Compute the dilution, compare it with the NPV, then check the belief. Three numbers, in that order.

Because the loss in the separating case is real, anything that removes the asymmetry is worth money. That is the practical content of the chapter, and it is the list an exam expects when it asks what the firm should do about it:

RemedyHow it worksLimit
Financial slackCash or unused debt capacity funds the project with no issue and no dilutionIdle cash invites the empire building of k11
Issue debt insteadA fixed claim is worth nearly the same whichever type the firm is, so little is transferred (k13)Distress and agency costs of debt return (k8–k10)
Rights issueSell the new shares to the existing owners, who are then on both sides of the transfer (k14)Requires owners with cash who will take up the rights
WaitIssue after the information becomes public, for instance after a report or a completed trialOnly if the project survives the delay
CertifyUnderwriters, auditors and analysts stake reputations on the disclosure (k14)Costly, and only as credible as the certifier
Where this goes next k13 turns the second row into the pecking order: internal funds first, then debt, then equity, ranked by exactly the dilution term you have been computing. It also derives the market's reaction to an issue announcement, which is figure 12.1 read from the outside. k14 takes the third row and shows why a rights issue sidesteps the problem, and how underwriters certify. Backwards, the underinvestment in 12.4 is the same shape as debt overhang in k10, and the good firm's preference for cash is the other side of the free cash flow problem in k11.

12.5 Writing the answer

These questions are almost always in four parts, worth roughly equal points, and they build on one another, so a slip in the α propagates. Answer them in this order and label the steps.

  1. State the belief you are pricing at and why. One sentence: "Suppose investors expect both types to issue, so E[V | issue] equals the prior 175."
  2. Compute α from that belief, with the cash raised in the denominator, to four decimals.
  3. Payoff to old shareholders for each type, compared with the value of not issuing. Both types, even if the question only asks about one, because you need both for step 4.
  4. Check the belief. Name the outcome: "the high type does not issue, so the pooling belief is not rational; the equilibrium is separating with E[V | issue] = 100". Then reprice.
How this has been examined 2016 Problem 1, 2017V Problem 1, 2020 Problem 2, 2022 Problem 2 and 2023 Problem 4 are the same exercise with different numbers, which is why it is worth drilling until the four steps are automatic. Neither of Kurbatov's papers, H2024 or H2025, used it, so it is not a certainty, but eight of eleven mapped papers did and the syllabus still carries it. The solution keys single out step 4: computing α and stopping there is described as the standard incomplete answer, which is why it is consistency check 5 in the k0 list. H2025's four-decimal requirement applies here too, so write 0.3429, not "about a third", and keep the fraction 12/35 in the margin if you need to carry it forward.
Three slips that cost more than they should Comparing the issue payoff with V + NPV rather than with V: if the project is lost when the issue does not happen, the alternative is the assets alone. Applying the market's expected value where the manager's true V belongs: α is priced off beliefs, the payoff is computed off the truth, and the two are different numbers on purpose. And asserting a separating equilibrium without checking that the low type still wants to issue at the harsher price, which in worked example 1 was step 5 and is worth points on its own.
↑ contents

13 · Pecking order, signalling and market timing

k12 asked whether one firm issues one security. This chapter asks which security, and it is the part of the information block that turns into words on an exam script. 2021 Problem 1 set debt against equity under private information and is worked here in full. The rest of the material is the standard raw material for the verbal exercises: why profitable firms borrow least, why an issue announcement moves the price down, and what makes a corporate action a credible signal rather than an advertisement.

13.1 The pecking order, and where it comes from

The pecking order is a ranking of financing sources, and it falls straight out of the dilution term you computed in k12. Selling a claim to outsiders who know less than you costs the existing owners the amount by which that claim is underpriced. So rank the sources by how badly they can be underpriced:

1. Retained earnings  →  2. Debt, safest first  →  3. Hybrids, convertibles  →  4. Equity, last
The pecking order of Myers and Majluf. Not a preference over capital structures, a preference over transactions. A firm follows it every time it needs money, and its leverage is whatever falls out of a history of such choices. Not on the formula sheet.

The cost that produces the ranking has one general form. Write S(V) for the payoff of the security you are selling when the firm turns out to be worth V. Competitive investors pay its expected value under their beliefs; the existing owners give up what it is really worth:

adverse-selection cost = S(Vtrue) − E[S(V) | beliefs]
The wealth transferred out of the old shareholders' pockets when the firm is better than the market thinks. It is zero for a claim whose payoff does not depend on V at all, and largest for the claim that tracks V one for one. This is the α(V − E[V | beliefs]) of k12, written so that it also covers debt.
Why the ranking is a ranking of information sensitivity Internal cash involves no outside claim, so S is not sold at all and the cost is zero. Riskless debt promises a fixed amount that the firm pays whichever type it is, so S(VH) = S(VL) = K and the cost is zero again: the buyer's ignorance is irrelevant because the payoff does not depend on what he is ignorant about. Risky debt is fixed except in default, so its value differs across types only over the range where the firm cannot pay, which is a slice of the outcomes rather than all of them. Equity is the residual: every krone of hidden value belongs to it. The order is a ladder of exposure to the manager's private information, nothing more.

13.2 Debt or equity, when only you know what the firm is worth

The claims you are choosing between are the ones from k9 and k24. Against a face value K, and writing V1 for the firm's value when the debt matures:

Debt = min(V1, K)   and   Equity = max(V1 − K, 0)
K is the face value of the debt throughout this chapter, never a strike. The debt line is flat above K, and it is that flat stretch which makes debt insensitive to the manager's private information. Where the firm is certain to pay, the two types' debt is worth exactly the same.
Worked example 1: the same 150, raised two ways

Havbruk AS must raise I = 150 today. Investors are risk neutral and rf = 0, so every claim is worth its expected payoff, and the risk-neutral probability of each state next year is ρ = 0.50. Setting rf to zero strips out discounting so that only the information effect is left; nothing below depends on it. Management knows which type the firm is, the market puts q = 0.50 on each. Next year's firm value, with the new project already included:

TypeUp stateDown stateExpected value
High, probability 0.50600300450
Low, probability 0.5040060230
Market's pooled expectation500180340

Route A, step 1 — price an equity issue. Investors who cannot tell the types apart value the firm at 340, so selling a fraction α for 150 requires α = 150/340 = 0.4412 (15/34).

Route A, step 2 — what each type gives up. Under full information each type would sell a claim worth exactly 150 and keep the rest.

TypeOld shareholders keep (1−α)E[V]Full-information benchmark E[V] − 150Transfer
High(19/34)(450) = 251.4706300.0000−48.5294
Low(19/34)(230) = 128.529480.0000+48.5294

Cross-check with the k12 dilution formula: α(V − E[V | beliefs]) = 0.441176 × (450 − 340) = 48.5294 ✓

Route B, step 1 — find the face value that raises 150. Try a K between 60 and 300. The high type then pays K in both states, so its debt is riskless and worth K. The low type pays K in the up state and hands over 60 in the down state, so its debt is worth 0.5K + 30. Investors who cannot tell them apart pay the average:

0.50(K) + 0.50(0.5K + 30) = 0.75K + 15 = 150  ⇒  K = 180, which is indeed between 60 and 300 ✓

Route B, step 2 — what each type gives up. The high type's debt is truly worth 180 and it received 150, so it transfers 30.00. The low type's is truly worth 0.5(180) + 0.5(60) = 120 and it received 150, so it gains 30.00.

TypeTrue value of the claim soldCash receivedTransfer
High, debt180.0000150.0000−30.0000
Low, debt120.0000150.0000+30.0000
High, equity(15/34)(450) = 198.5294150.0000−48.5294
Low, equity(15/34)(230) = 101.4706150.0000+48.5294

Step 3 — the answer. The good firm loses 30.00 by issuing debt and 48.5294 by issuing equity, so it prefers debt, by 18.5294. The bad firm would rather issue equity, since it gains more from it, but it cannot say so without revealing itself, which is why in equilibrium it mimics and issues debt too.

Check A — the transfer is half the spread. With q = 0.50 the pooled price is the midpoint of the two true values, so the transfer must be half the gap between them. Debt: (180 − 120)/2 = 30.0000 ✓ Equity: (198.5294 − 101.4706)/2 = 48.5294 ✓ The gap is the information sensitivity of the security, and the whole pecking order is the observation that 60 is smaller than 97.0588.

Check B — conservation. Investors break even on average: 0.50(−30) + 0.50(+30) = 0 for debt and the same for equity. Nothing is created here, so what the good firm loses the bad firm gains, exactly as in consistency check 4.

Step 4 — how far down the ladder is free. The lowest payoff either type can produce is 60, so any face value up to 60 is repaid with certainty by both. Debt of 60 is riskless, both types' claims are worth exactly 60, and the transfer is zero. The information cost of this firm's financing is zero for the first 60 raised, 30.00 at 150, and rises from there.

What it means for investment. In k12 the good firm invests only when NPV exceeds the transfer. Here that hurdle is 30.00 with debt against 48.5294 with equity, so switching to debt does not only save the old shareholders money, it also rescues every project whose NPV lies between the two. Debt causes less underinvestment, which is the pecking order's real claim.

13.3 What the pecking order assumes, and what it does not

The assumptions are exactly k12's, carried over: managers know more than investors about the value of the assets in place; they act for the existing shareholders; they cannot credibly announce what they know; and the existing shareholders are not the buyers of the new securities. That is the entire list.

"The pecking order works because markets are inefficient" This is the standard wrong answer and it inverts the model. Every price in k12 and in worked example 1 above is fair given the information investors have. Investors are rational, they anticipate that a firm issuing equity is more likely to be the low type, and they price that in before the shares are sold. Nothing here requires prices to be wrong, only that the two sides know different things. The correct sentence for an exam script is: "the pecking order rests on asymmetric information, not on market inefficiency; prices are efficient with respect to the market's information set, and the cost arises because the manager's set is larger." Market timing, later in this chapter, is where inefficiency may or may not enter, and keeping the two apart is worth points.

Just as important is what the pecking order does not contain. There is no target leverage ratio, no optimum, and no trade-off being balanced at the margin. Observed leverage is a residue: the accumulation of past deficits funded from the top of the ladder down. The two theories therefore make different predictions from different mechanisms, and an exam answer that keeps them apart looks like this:

QuestionTrade-off theory (k7, k8, k11)Pecking order (this chapter)
Is there a target D/V?Yes, where marginal tax shield meets marginal distress and agency costNo target; leverage is a by-product of past financing needs
Who borrows most?Profitable, taxable firms with tangible assetsFirms whose investment has outrun their internal funds
Profitable firmsshould borrow more, to use the deductionborrow less, because they need less outside money
Why hold cash?It is negative debt, so it wastes tax shield and invites empire buildingFinancial slack is valuable: it funds projects without an issue
An equity issue isa routine adjustment towards the targeta last resort, and bad news when it happens
Why the profitability regularity is the pecking order's best evidence k11 flagged the awkward fact that the most profitable firms in an industry tend to carry the least debt, which is the opposite of what the tax shield alone predicts. The pecking order explains it without any optimisation: profits accumulate as retained earnings, retained earnings sit at the top of the ladder, and a firm that funds everything internally never issues debt at all. Leverage falls not because the firm wants less of it but because it never had to ask for it. That is the cleanest example in the course of a theory that predicts the sign correctly through a mechanism rather than through a target.
Do not claim the pecking order forbids equity It ranks, it does not prohibit. A firm with no cash, no debt capacity left and a large positive-NPV project issues equity and should. The prediction is about the order in which sources are exhausted and about the information released when a firm reaches the bottom rung, not about equity being unavailable. The observed fact that young growth firms issue equity constantly is a genuine limitation of the theory, best explained by their having no internal funds and no debt capacity, and by their asymmetry being reduced by venture capitalists and underwriters who are let inside (k14).

13.4 What the market does when you announce

Because an issue is a last resort, announcing one tells the market something. Two facts to carry into an exam: seasoned equity offers are met on average by a share price fall of roughly three percent on the announcement day, while straight debt issues move the price by an amount that is statistically indistinguishable from zero. The pattern is the pecking order read backwards, and it is the ladder's ranking of information sensitivity showing up in prices.

The arithmetic of the reaction is one line. After an issue priced at α = I/(E[V | issue] + I + NPV), the old shareholders' remaining fraction is worth

(1 − α)(E[V | issue] + I + NPV) = E[V | issue] + I + NPV − I = E[V | issue] + NPV
The market value of the old shares immediately after the announcement, in the market's own eyes. The cash raised cancels: investors paid full price for their slice, so all the old shareholders are left holding is the revised view of the assets plus the project's NPV. Every announcement effect in this chapter is the difference between this and the price before.
Worked example 2: decomposing an announcement drop

Nordvik ASA's assets in place are worth 900, 800 or 700, each equally likely as far as the market is concerned, and management knows which. A project needs I = 200 raised by a share issue and has a publicly known NPV of 15. Find the equilibrium, the price reaction on the announcement, and the cost to the issuing firm's owners.

Step 1 — test pooling. If all three types issue, E[V | issue] = 800 and α = 200/(800 + 200 + 15) = 200/1015 = 0.1970. The top type's dilution is 0.197044 × (900 − 800) = 19.7044, which exceeds the NPV of 15, so it will not issue. Pooling fails consistency check 5.

Step 2 — the belief that survives. Take out the top type: E[V | issue] = (800 + 700)/2 = 750, and E[V | no issue] = 900. Then α = 200/(750 + 200 + 15) = 200/965 = 0.2073.

Step 3 — check every type against that price.

TypeDilution α(V − 750)Payoff V + NPV − dilutionNo issueActs as assumed?
900+31.0881883.9119900.0000abstains ✓
800+10.3627804.6373800.0000issues ✓
700−10.3627725.3627700.0000issues ✓

All three behave as the belief assumed, so this partial pooling is an equilibrium: the best firm stays out, the other two are lumped together.

Step 4 — the prices. After an issue the market cannot tell 800 from 700 apart, so it prices both at E[V | issue] + NPV = 750 + 15 = 765.00. After no issue it prices the firm at 900.00. Before the announcement it holds neither belief and prices the average: P0 = (1/3)(900) + (2/3)(765) = 300 + 510 = 810.00.

Step 5 — the announcement return. (765 − 810)/810 = −5.5556%. Decompose it and the two forces separate: the market revises the assets in place from 800 down to 750, worth −50, and it learns the project will definitely be taken, replacing the expected NPV of (2/3)(15) = 10 with the full 15, worth +5. −50 + 5 = −45, and −45/810 = −5.5556% ✓

Step 6 — what the drop is not. The issuing firm of type 800 loses 10.3627 in dilution, which is 1.2793% of the pre-announcement price. The price fell 5.5556%. The gap is not a cost to anybody: it is the market discovering something that was already true before the announcement was made. Saying "the issue destroyed 45 of value" is wrong, and it is the standard error on this question.

Check — prices must average back to the prior. (1/3)(900) + (2/3)(765) = 810.00, which is the prior 800 plus the expected NPV actually captured, (2/3)(15) = 10 ✓ Equivalently, average the three types' true payoffs: (900 + 804.6373 + 725.3627)/3 = 2430/3 = 810.00 ✓ The market cannot expect to be surprised in one direction, so if your post-announcement prices do not weight back to the pre-announcement price, an equilibrium condition has been broken.

13.5 Signalling: making a statement expensive enough to be believed

k12 established that talk is worthless because it is free. A signal works when the same action costs the two types different amounts, so that the good type is willing to take it and the bad type is not. That single-crossing condition is the whole theory:

cost of the signal to the low type > gain from being taken for the high type > cost to the high type
If both inequalities hold, only the high type signals, the market reads it correctly, and the two types separate. If the middle term dominates both, everybody signals and it conveys nothing. Any action you propose in a verbal answer has to be tested against this line.

Four actions satisfy it, and they are the ones an exam expects you to name.

ActionWhy it is credibleTypical price reaction
Take on debtInterest must be paid in cash or control passes to creditors; a firm with weak prospects faces a higher chance of that penalty, and the manager loses his job in itpositive, or neutral for routine issues
Repurchase sharesThe firm spends real cash buying its own stock, which is expensive if the stock is dear; and management often does not tender its own holdingpositive
Raise the dividendCommitting to a payout the firm must sustain, since cutting it later is punished hard (k16)positive
Refuse to issue equityThe k12 separating outcome: walking away from a positive-NPV project is only worth doing if the firm really is undervaluedthe issuers fall, the abstainers rise
Why debt signals quality, when k8 said debt threatens the firm with distress Both are true and they are the same fact seen from two sides. The threat of distress is what makes the promise expensive, and expensive is what makes it informative. A manager who knows the cash flows are strong is buying a cheap signal, because he does not expect to default; a manager who knows they are weak is buying an expensive one. The signal is credible because the penalty in k8 is real. If bankruptcy were free, borrowing would tell the market nothing.

13.6 Market timing, spin-offs and carve-outs

Market timing is the claim that managers issue equity when they think it is dear and repurchase when they think it is cheap, and that they succeed often enough for it to shape capital structure. Firms do issue equity after price run-ups, in clusters, at market peaks, and surveys report managers naming perceived misvaluation as a first-order consideration. Under this view leverage has no target at all: it is the accumulated residue of past attempts at timing.

Two versions of market timing, and only one needs inefficient markets The information version is Myers-Majluf again: the manager knows more, the market's price is fair given its own information, and issuing when the shares are relatively dear is simply the pooling outcome of k12. No inefficiency at all. The behavioural version says the market is genuinely wrong, investors are over-optimistic, and the manager exploits it. Only that second version requires prices to be inefficient, and only that version predicts that new issues will systematically underperform afterwards, which is the evidence k14 takes up. State which version you mean and the answer is complete; blur them and it looks like the myth in 13.3.

Spin-offs, carve-outs and divestitures are the other way out of the trap. If the market cannot value your firm but can value one of its parts, sell the part. Keep the three transactions apart, because they raise different amounts of cash and send different messages. In a spin-off the shares of a subsidiary are distributed to the parent's own shareholders, who end up holding two shares where they held one, and no cash reaches the parent. In an equity carve-out a minority stake in the subsidiary is sold to the public in its own IPO, so the parent raises the value of the stake sold while keeping control. In a divestiture the unit is sold outright, usually to another company, and the parent receives the agreed price. Only the last two fund anything; the first is a pure information event, which is what makes it the interesting one.

Worked example 3: sell the part the market can price

Bergen Industrier is two businesses. Division A is a specialised technology unit whose true value management puts at 400, while the market, unable to see inside it, values it at 340. Division B is a property portfolio worth 260, and everyone agrees on that figure because comparable buildings trade every month. A project needs I = 200 and has a publicly known NPV of 50. Compare funding it with a share issue against funding it by selling division B.

Route A, step 1 — the share issue. The market values the assets in place at 340 + 260 = 600, so the post-issue firm is worth 600 + 200 + 50 = 850 in its eyes and α = 200/850 = 0.2353 (4/17).

Route A, step 2 — what the old shareholders keep. The firm is truly worth 400 + 260 + 200 + 50 = 910, so they keep (13/17)(910) = 695.8824.

Route A, step 3 — the benchmark. With no information problem they would hold 400 + 260 + 50 = 710. The issue therefore costs them 14.1176, and the dilution formula agrees: α(660 − 600) = 0.235294 × 60 = 14.1176 ✓

Route B — sell division B. It fetches its full value of 260 because nobody is uninformed about it. The old shareholders now hold division A at its true 400, the 260 of cash, of which 200 goes into the project, and the project's NPV of 50: 400 + 260 + 50 = 710.00, with 60 of cash left over. The transfer is zero.

Step 4 — the comparison. Selling the transparent asset beats issuing opaque equity by 710 − 695.8824 = 14.1176, which is precisely the transfer avoided ✓ The rule generalises: fund from the asset the market values correctly, and keep the one it cannot see.

Step 5 — the signal in the choice. Investors observing the choice learn something. A management that sells the property rather than shares is behaving like a firm whose shares are underpriced, which is why divestiture and carve-out announcements are typically met with a positive price reaction while equity issues are met with a negative one. Both reactions come out of the same model.

Why a spin-off can raise value while raising no cash at all It is pure information. One share in a conglomerate forces investors to value a bundle they cannot decompose, and the safe response is to mark the whole thing down. Split it and each part is priced against its own comparables, each gets its own analysts and its own reporting, and each management team is paid on a share price that reflects what it actually controls. The bundling discount disappears, cross-subsidy between units stops, and any future issue by either part is a less opaque transaction. Against that, the group loses the coinsurance between the units' cash flows (k24, k26) and the internal capital market that let a cash-rich unit fund a cash-poor one, so a spin-off is not automatically good, only informative.
How this material has been examined 2021 Problem 1 is the debt-versus-equity comparison under private information, worked example 1 above with different numbers. Asymmetric information as a whole reaches eight of the eleven mapped papers, though neither Kurbatov paper used it. In the new format the likeliest home for it is one of the two verbal exercises, since it is pure mechanism with almost no arithmetic. Prepare three sentences on each of: why the order is internal, debt, equity; why an issue announcement moves the price down; and what makes a signal credible.
