Write "asymmetric information", "adverse selection", "pecking order" and "signalling" as the labels, and never claim inefficiency where information is what you mean.
Where this connects Backwards: k12 supplies the α and the belief check this whole chapter runs on, k8 supplies the distress cost that makes debt a credible signal, and k11's table of forces gains its last row here. Forwards: k14 takes the equity issue itself apart, showing what the underwriter does about the asymmetry, why rights issues escape the transfer entirely, and what the post-issue long-run evidence says about the behavioural version of market timing. k16 does the same for dividends and repurchases as signals.
↑ contents

14 · Raising capital: IPO and SEO

k12 and k13 explained why selling shares is hard. This chapter is what actually happens when a firm does it anyway, and it is the cheapest chapter in the manual to convert into points: the arithmetic is addition and division, and the mechanisms have names the examiner can tick. 2016 questions 8 and 9 came from here, and in V2024 six of the ten multiple-choice questions came out of this one lecture's exercise. The format has since dropped multiple choice, so expect it as a short computation inside a larger exercise or as part of a verbal answer on financing.

14.1 Going public, and what it costs to stay there

An initial public offering converts a private company into a listed one by selling shares to the public for the first time. The shares sold come in two kinds and the distinction decides who bears the costs in 14.2. Primary shares are newly created and the cash goes to the company. Secondary shares are existing shares sold by founders or venture investors, and that cash goes to them personally. Most offerings mix the two.

Why do it, and why not:

Reasons to go publicReasons not to
Access to a deep pool of capital for growth, on repeatable termsDirect costs: spread, legal, accounting, listing, all largely fixed and therefore brutal for a small issue
Liquidity and diversification for founders and early investors, who otherwise hold everything in one firmContinuous disclosure: quarterly reporting, internal control requirements, insider rules
An observable share price, which lets you pay in stock, price acquisitions (k26) and design compensation (k27)Ownership dispersion, which weakens the monitoring a concentrated owner provides (k27)
Certification: the scrutiny of underwriters, auditors and analysts shrinks the asymmetry of k12Pressure to manage quarterly earnings rather than long-horizon value

The company hires an underwriter, usually an investment bank leading a syndicate of others. The bank does four things: it advises on structure and timing, it prepares and files the prospectus, it markets the issue on a roadshow, and it distributes the shares to investors. How it commits varies:

  • Firm commitment, the standard arrangement: the bank buys the whole issue from the company at the offer price less the spread and resells it, so the placement risk is the bank's.
  • Best efforts: the bank only promises to try, and the issue may be withdrawn if it does not sell. Used for small and risky offerings.
  • Auction: investors bid, and the price is set where the issue clears. Rare, and it has not displaced the standard method.

Pricing is done by book building. During the roadshow the bank collects non-binding indications of interest from institutions, builds a book of demand at various prices, and sets the offer price the night before trading starts, typically below what the book says the market will bear. Three further features appear in exam questions: the over-allotment option or greenshoe lets the underwriter sell about 15% more shares than planned and buy them back in the market for roughly a month afterwards, which is how the bank supports the price without risk; the lock-up bars insiders from selling for a period, commonly 180 days, so that the market is not flooded and so that insiders keep their skin in the game; and the quiet period restricts what the company may say around the offering.

Why the underwriter is worth its fee, in the language of k12 The bank is a repeat player and the issuer is not. It sells offerings every month to the same institutions, so its reputation is a hostage: certifying a bad issue costs it the next twenty deals, which is exactly the type-dependent cost that makes a signal credible under the rule in 13.5. The bank is therefore able to say something about the firm's quality that the firm cannot say about itself, which shrinks E[V | beliefs] towards the truth and shrinks the dilution term α(V − E[V | beliefs]). The fee buys certification, not just distribution.

14.2 The arithmetic: spread, net proceeds, underpricing

The bank does not invoice for its fee, it keeps a slice of the offer price. That slice is the underwriting spread or gross spread, and it clusters around 7% for mid-sized offerings in the United States, with lower rates on large deals and on seasoned offerings by companies that are already listed. Everything else is billed: legal, accounting, listing and printing costs, which are largely fixed and so hurt small issues most.

Gross proceeds = N × Poffer
Net proceeds = N × Poffer × (1 − spread) − other direct costs
N is the number of shares sold and Poffer the price paid by the investors who are allocated them. Net proceeds is the number that reaches the company's bank account and therefore the number that funds the project. Not on the formula sheet, but it is arithmetic rather than theory.

Underpricing is the jump from the offer price to the first day's closing price. It is measured as a return and it is not a cost the company is billed for, which is exactly why candidates leave it out:

Underpricing = (Pclose − Poffer) / Poffer
Money left on the table = Nprimary × (Pclose − Poffer)
First-day returns have averaged in the high teens in the United States over recent decades, with wide variation across markets and enormous variation across time: in hot markets the average has been multiples of that. The money left on the table is real: those shares could have been sold for more.
Total cost to the pre-IPO owners = Nprimary × Pclose − net proceeds
What the old owners gave away, valued at the market's own price, minus what the company received for it. It automatically equals spread + other direct costs + money left on the table. That equality is a definition, not a test, so treat it as a way to present the answer rather than as a check that could catch an error.
Worked example 1: what an IPO really cost the founders

Skagen Teknologi AS is owned by its founders and a venture fund, together holding 15,000,000 shares. It lists by selling 5,000,000 new shares at an offer price of 80 NOK. The underwriting spread is 6%, other direct costs are 12,000,000 NOK, and the shares close their first day at 96 NOK. All figures below in millions of NOK.

InputValue
Pre-IPO shares outstanding15 000 000
New shares offered, N5 000 000
Offer price80.00
Underwriting spread6%
Other direct costs12.0
First-day closing price96.00

Step 1 — gross proceeds. 5 000 000 × 80 = 400.0

Step 2 — the spread. 0.06 × 400.0 = 24.0, so the bank pays the company 376.0 for the shares.

Step 3 — net proceeds. 376.0 − 12.0 = 364.0. That, not the 400, is what funds the business.

Step 4 — underpricing. (96 − 80)/80 = 0.2000 = 20.00%. Money left on the table = 5 000 000 × 16 = 80.0.

Step 5 — the total cost, the way the examiner wants it. The founders handed over 5 000 000 shares which the market immediately valued at 96 each, that is 480.0, and the company received 364.0 for them:

ItemAmountShare of gross proceeds
Value surrendered, 5m × 96480.0
− Underwriting spread24.06.00%
− Other direct costs12.03.00%
− Money left on the table80.020.00%
= Net proceeds364.0
Total cost116.029.00%

The underpricing alone is more than three times the bank's fee, and it never appears on an invoice.

Step 6 — position afterwards. 20 000 000 shares at 96 gives a market capitalisation of 1 920.0, of which the pre-IPO owners hold 15/20 = 75%, worth 1 440.0.

Check — the same 116 from the other direction. Be clear that this one is an algebraic identity and cannot fail: it rearranges the same definition rather than recomputing anything. It is worth writing anyway, because it makes the size of the giveaway concrete in shares rather than in kroner. Had the shares been sold at the price the market turned out to be willing to pay, the company would have needed (364.0 + 12.0)/(96 × 0.94) = 376.0/90.24 = 4 166 667 shares to raise the same 364.0 net. It sold 5 000 000, so it handed over 833 333 shares more than necessary. At 96 those are worth 80.0, exactly the money left on the table ✓ Underpricing is not a payment, it is shares.

A variant to watch. If 1 000 000 of the five million had been secondary shares sold by the venture fund, the fund would have received 80 × 0.94 = 75.20 per share instead of the 96 the market paid within hours, and it, not the company, would bear that slice of the underpricing. Always read whether the shares are new or existing before you allocate the cost.

Underpricing is a cost to the owners, not to the company Two ways to get this wrong, in opposite directions. Writing "the underpricing cost the company 80" confuses the balance sheet with the shareholders: the company's accounts never see it, its cash position is 364 either way. Writing "underpricing costs nobody anything, no cash moves" is worse: the pre-IPO owners' stake was diluted by 833 333 shares more than it needed to be. State it precisely, as the examiner does: the cost of underpricing falls on the pre-issue shareholders, in shares, in proportion to how many primary shares were sold.

14.3 Why issuers put up with underpricing: the winner's curse

Why does the issuer not simply set a higher price? The dominant explanation is Rock's winner's curse, and it is an adverse-selection story in the same family as k12, with the asymmetry now among the investors rather than between the firm and the market.

Some investors are informed and know which offerings are worth more than the offer price. They bid for the good ones and stay away from the bad ones. An uninformed investor who bids for everything therefore has his order cut back precisely when the deal is good, because informed money crowds in, and filled completely when the deal is bad, because nobody else wants it. He wins what he should not want. Averaging returns across offerings makes the deal look fine; averaging across shares he actually receives does not.

E[profit to the uninformed] = Σstates Pr(state) × (allocation in that state) × (value − Poffer)
The allocation, not the order, is what multiplies the gain. Setting this expression to zero gives the offer price at which uninformed investors are just willing to participate, and that price is below the offering's expected value. The gap is equilibrium underpricing: the issuer pays it to keep uninformed money in the market at all.
Worked example 2: pricing an issue that uninformed investors will still buy

Half of all offerings turn out to be worth 120 per share once they trade, half are worth 90. Informed investors observe which before bidding; you do not. You place an order for 1 000 shares in every offering. When the offering is good, informed demand is heavy and your order is scaled back to 25%. When it is bad, the informed stay away and you are filled in full.

Step 1 — the naive benchmark. The unconditional expected value of a share is 0.50(120) + 0.50(90) = 105. Suppose the bank prices at exactly that, P = 105.

Step 2 — your expected profit at 105. Work in shares received, not shares ordered.

OfferingProbabilityShares allocatedProfit per shareExpected profit
Good, worth 1200.50250+15+1 875
Bad, worth 900.501 000−15−7 500
Total−5 625

The average first-day return across the two offerings is 0.50(14.2857%) + 0.50(−14.2857%) = 0.00%, which looks like a fair deal, and you would still lose 5 625 per round. That contrast is the winner's curse in one line: the average return across offerings is not the average return on the shares you get.

Step 3 — the price at which you would participate. Set expected profit to zero: 0.50(250)(120 − P) + 0.50(1 000)(90 − P) = 0, that is 125(120 − P) + 500(90 − P) = 0, so 15 000 + 45 000 = 625P and P = 96.00.

Step 4 — what that price implies. First-day returns become (120 − 96)/96 = 25.00% on good offerings and (90 − 96)/96 = −6.25% on bad ones. The unconditional average first-day return is 0.50(25.00%) + 0.50(−6.25%) = 9.3750%, which is the underpricing an outside observer would measure in the data.

Step 5 — the cost to the issuer. Shares worth 105 on average are sold for 96, so the issuer gives up 9.00 per share, which is 8.5714% of the value of the shares and 9.3750% of the offer price. On a five million share offering that is 45.0 million.

Check — the uninformed break even at 96. 125(120 − 96) = +3 000 and 500(90 − 96) = −3 000, summing to 0 ✓ They earn a large percentage return on the offerings they barely get into and lose a small one on the offerings they are stuffed with, and it exactly cancels. Note what this means: the measured underpricing of 9.3750% is not a profit anyone earns. It is compensation for adverse allocation.

The mechanism sentence. "Underpricing is required because uninformed investors face a winner's curse: they are rationed in offerings that are underpriced and filled in offerings that are overpriced, so the offering must be priced below its expected value for their participation constraint to hold."

Why the issuer cannot escape by excluding the informed If the bank could sell only to uninformed investors it could price at 105 and lose nothing, so the whole cost comes from the informed being in the room. But they cannot be kept out: they are the institutions whose demand builds the book in the first place, and their bids are what tells the bank what the offering is worth. Book building buys information from investors and pays for it with allocations of underpriced stock. That is a second, complementary explanation of underpricing, and it predicts what the data shows, that offerings priced above the indicated range are underpriced most.

Three further explanations are worth a sentence each, because verbal answers are graded on breadth. Litigation risk: an offering that falls below its offer price attracts lawsuits from disappointed buyers, and underpricing is cheap insurance. Cascades: early investors' willingness to buy is observed by later ones, so an offering that starts weakly can fail entirely, and a low price buys momentum. Conflicted underwriters: the bank sells to the same institutions repeatedly and an underpriced offering is an easy sale and a favour it can call in, so its incentives are not the issuer's. That last one has a governance flavour and connects to k27.

14.4 After the offering: hot markets and long-run returns

Two more regularities, both examinable in words, neither requiring a calculation.

Cycles. The number of offerings and the average underpricing move together, and both move with the market. Issuance clusters in windows in which valuations are high and investor appetite is strong, and dries up almost completely in downturns. That is what k13 called market timing seen from the aggregate: firms issue when equity is expensive.

Long-run performance. Firms that have just issued equity, whether at an IPO or in a seasoned offering, have historically underperformed comparable non-issuing firms over the following three to five years. Two readings compete and you should give both. The behavioural reading is that investors were over-optimistic at the offering and the price drifts back, so managers were timing a genuine mispricing. The rational reading is that the result is sensitive to how comparables and risk adjustments are chosen, and that issuers differ systematically from non-issuers in size, age and growth, so part of the effect is a benchmark problem rather than an anomaly.

How issuance has been examined 2016 questions 8 and 9 came from this material, and in V2024 six of the ten multiple-choice questions came from this lecture's exercise, which makes it the highest concentration of points per page of any single lecture in the mapped papers. Issuance reaches four of eleven papers overall. Since Kurbatov dropped multiple choice, the natural home is a short computation inside a financing exercise, gross proceeds through to total cost, or a verbal sub-question on why underpricing exists. Prepare the winner's curse in three sentences, prepare the split of the cost between the company and the pre-IPO owners, and be able to state the long-run evidence with the caveat attached.

14.5 Seasoned offerings and the rights issue

A seasoned equity offering is an issue by a company already listed, and the mechanics are lighter than an IPO because a market price already exists: the spread is smaller, the prospectus is shorter, and the pricing argument is about a discount to a visible price rather than about valuation from scratch. Two routes:

  • Cash offer: new shares are sold to the public at large, usually through an underwriter. Standard in the United States.
  • Rights offer: each existing shareholder receives a right to buy new shares at a set subscription price, in proportion to what he already owns. Standard in Norway and much of Europe.

The rights route matters for this course because of what it does to the problem in k12. A rights issue sells the underpriced shares to the people who already own the firm, so the wealth transfer from old shareholders to new investors has no one to flow to: the two groups are the same group. That is why a rights issue is the clean answer to "how can the firm avoid the adverse-selection cost of an equity issue?"

TERP = (Nold × Pcum + Nnew × Psub) / (Nold + Nnew)
Value of one right = Pcum − TERP
TERP is the theoretical ex-rights price: the market value of the firm plus the cash raised, divided by all the shares that then exist. Pcum is the price with the right still attached, Psub the subscription price. One right attaches to each existing share, and it is worth the drop from Pcum to TERP because that drop is what the right compensates for. Not on the formula sheet.
Why the price falls on the ex-rights date without anyone losing money Because the count of shares rises faster than the value inside the firm. Adding 400 of cash to a firm worth 2 000 raises value by 20%, while issuing shares at a discount raises the share count by 25%, so value per share must fall. That is arithmetic, not a transfer, and it is the same arithmetic as the ex-dividend drop in k15 running in the opposite direction. Nobody is worse off because the fall is exactly offset by the right, which every existing shareholder was handed for free and can either use or sell. Value only leaves a shareholder who throws the right away.
Worked example 3: a rights issue takes nothing from anyone who acts

Fjord Energi ASA has 40 000 000 shares trading at 50 NOK, a market capitalisation of 2 000 million. It raises 400 million through a 1-for-4 rights issue: every four shares held carry the right to subscribe for one new share at 40 NOK. Nils holds 4 000 shares.

Step 1 — check the issue raises what it should. New shares = 40 000 000/4 = 10 000 000, at 40 each, which is 400.0 million ✓

Step 2 — the theoretical ex-rights price. TERP = (2 000 + 400)/(40 + 10) million shares = 2 400/50 = 48.0000. The share price falls from 50 to 48 on the ex-rights date, and none of that fall is a loss.

Step 3 — the value of a right. Four rights plus 40 NOK buy a share worth 48, a gain of 8 per new share, so each of the four rights is worth 8/4 = 2.0000. Cross-check: Pcum − TERP = 50 − 48 = 2.0000 ✓

Step 4 — Nils's three options. He starts with 4 000 × 50 = 200 000.

What Nils doesShares held afterCash paid or receivedWealth
Subscribes for all 1 000 new shares5 000 × 48 = 240 000−40 000200 000
Sells all 4 000 rights at 2.004 000 × 48 = 192 000+8 000200 000
Does nothing and lets them lapse4 000 × 48 = 192 0000192 000

Step 5 — read the table. Subscribing and selling the rights leave him exactly where he started, at 200 000. Doing nothing costs him 8 000, which is the value of the rights he threw away. A rights issue does not dilute anyone who acts, and it does dilute anyone who does not.

Step 6 — the discount is cosmetic. Suppose the same 400 million were raised at a subscription price of 25, which needs 16 000 000 new shares, a 1-for-2.5 issue. Then TERP = 2 400/56 = 42.8571 and each right is worth 50 − 42.8571 = 7.1429. Nils subscribes for 1 600 shares at 25 for 40 000 and holds 5 600 × 42.8571 = 240 000, so his wealth is 240 000 − 40 000 = 200 000, exactly as before ✓ The discount looks dramatic, 41.6667% below TERP instead of 16.6667%, and changes nothing. What it does change is the penalty for inaction: letting the rights lapse now costs 28 571.43 instead of 8 000.

Check — total value is conserved. Market capitalisation after the issue is 2 000 + 400 = 2 400 million on either structure, and the share count times the price agrees: 50 million × 48.0000 = 2 400 million, and 56 million × 42.8571 = 2 400 million ✓ The number of slices changed, the cake did not, which is the k6 argument applied to an issue.

"The rights issue was priced at a 20% discount, so existing shareholders lost 20%" This is the most common misreading of a Norwegian rights issue and it is wrong twice. The subscription discount is a choice about how many new shares to create, not about how much value to give away, and worked example 3 shows two very different discounts producing identical wealth. And the ex-rights price drop is not a loss either: it is the arithmetic of adding cheap shares to the count, and the right in your hand is worth exactly the drop. The only real loss in a rights issue is the one suffered by a shareholder who neither subscribes nor sells, which is why deeply discounted issues are announced loudly.
Do not price the rights issue off the cum-rights price Every valuation after the ex-rights date uses TERP, not Pcum. If a sub-question asks for the number of new shares, the wealth of a shareholder, or a per-share figure after the issue, and you keep using 50 where 48 belongs, every later answer inherits the error. Compute TERP first, write it down, and use it from there. The same discipline applies to the ex-dividend price in k15.
What this closes and what it opens It closes Part II. k12 gave the dilution cost of selling shares to people who know less than you, k13 ranked securities by how badly they suffer from it, and k14 has now priced the transaction itself and shown the two institutional answers to the problem: an underwriter who certifies, and a rights issue that sells to the people already inside. Part III turns the flow around and asks how a firm should hand cash back, where the ex-rights arithmetic of worked example 3 reappears as the ex-dividend price in k15, and where signalling returns as the explanation for why dividends are so reluctantly cut in k16.

Part III — Payout policy

↑ contents

15 · Dividends versus repurchases in a perfect market

H2025 Exercise 3 is titled MM without taxes, dividends and recapitalisation, and H2024 Exercise 3 is the same exercise without the dividend leg: 18 points each. The paper hands you a firm, makes it issue debt, and then asks for the dividend per share, the ex-dividend price, the number of shares repurchased, the new rE, and whether the shareholder who sold did better than the one who held. Payout appears in seven of the eleven mapped papers, and it almost never appears alone: it is bolted onto the recapitalisation of k6. This chapter gives you the payout half of that machine, and it is arithmetic you can make automatic.

15.1 The payout decision, and the assumption that makes it a decision

A firm with cash in the bank has four things it can do with it: invest it in a project, hold it, pay it to shareholders as a dividend, or use it to buy back its own shares. It can also borrow in order to do any of those. Payout policy is the choice between the last three, holding the first one fixed.

That last clause is the whole subject. If paying a dividend forces the firm to skip a positive-NPV project, value changes, but it changes because investment changed, not because of the payout. So the question this chapter answers is narrow and precise: with investment policy fixed and markets perfect, does it matter whether the cash leaves as a dividend, as a repurchase, or later rather than now?

E = PV(future free cash flow) + cash − D
The market value of equity, written so the cash is visible. A payout reduces both sides: the cash leaves, and the claim on it leaves with it. Nothing in the first term moves, because investment policy is held fixed.

The ways of paying out have names, and the exam uses them, so learn the vocabulary before the arithmetic:

MethodWhat it isTypical use
Regular dividendA per-share cash payment repeated each quarter or yearThe visible, sticky part of payout. Firms cut it only under pressure
Special dividendA one-off, much larger per-share paymentDisgorging a windfall or the proceeds of a recapitalisation
Liquidating dividendPayment of the proceeds of selling the business itselfWind-ups and divestitures
Open-market repurchaseThe firm buys its own shares on the exchange at the market priceThe great majority of repurchases, and the case in this chapter
Fixed-price tender offerAn offer to all holders at a stated price, usually above market, for a stated quantityLarge one-off buybacks. The arithmetic is in k16
Targeted repurchaseBuying a specific holder's block by private negotiationRemoving a hostile blockholder, sometimes at a premium
Why a repurchase is not a payment to the shareholders who stay A dividend pays every share. A repurchase pays only the shares tendered, and the shareholders who stay receive nothing at all. What they get instead is a larger fraction of a smaller firm: the cash is gone, but so are the shares that would have shared it. Every result in this chapter is that trade, priced.

15.2 The ex-dividend price drop

A dividend passes through four dates, and only one of them is a price event.

DateWhat happensPrice effect
Declaration dateThe board announces the dividend and it becomes a liabilityOnly the news content, which under MM is nothing
Ex-dividend dateBuy from here on and you do not receive this dividendThe price falls by the dividend. This is the event
Record dateThe register is read to decide who is paidNone
Payable dateThe cash actually reaches the shareholdersNone. The claim was priced out at the ex-date
Pex = Pcum − Div
The cum-dividend price is the price of a share that still carries the right to the dividend; the ex-dividend price is the price without it. In a perfect market the drop equals the dividend exactly. Not on the formula sheet, and nothing about it is hard: a share loses the claim it just paid out.
Why the drop is exactly the dividend, and not a matter of taste Suppose a share trades at 30.00 cum-dividend with a 5.00 dividend, and you expect it to open at 26.00 ex. Buy it cum for 30.00, collect the 5.00, sell it ex for 26.00: you paid 30.00 and received 31.00, with no risk and no capital tied up beyond a day. Everyone does it, the cum price is bid up or the ex price driven down, and the gap closes at exactly 5.00. The drop is enforced by trade, the same way MM Proposition I was in k6 § 6.2. In the real market taxes and trading costs blunt this, which is k16.

15.3 The arithmetic of an open-market repurchase

The firm spends an amount R of cash buying its own shares at the market price P. Three numbers follow, and the exam asks for all three:

n = R/P  ·  N1 = N0 − n  ·  P1 = E1/N1 = (E0 − R)/(N0 − R/P) = P
n is the number of shares bought, N the share count, E the market value of equity. The last equality is the result: with P0 = E0/N0, substituting gives P1 = P0. The price does not move, because the firm handed over cash worth exactly what it bought.
Why a repurchase leaves the price where it was Buying an asset at its fair value is a zero-NPV transaction, and a firm buying its own shares is no exception. Equity falls by R and the share count falls by R/P, so the ratio is untouched. A repurchase changes the denominator, and a dividend changes the numerator; per share, the two arrive at the same place. If you ever find the price moving in a perfect-market repurchase, you have divided by the wrong share count.
Worked example 1: one firm, two payout routes, three shareholders

Nordfjord Kraft ASA is all-equity in a perfect market with no taxes. It has 20 million shares. Its operations produce a free cash flow of 40 a year in perpetuity, all paid out, and rU = 8.0000%. It also holds 100 of excess cash, which the board has decided to pay out now. Amounts in millions of kroner.

InputValue
Shares outstanding N0 (millions)20.00
Free cash flow, perpetual40.00
rU, all-equity firm8.0000%
Excess cash to be paid out100.00

Step 1 — the price before anything happens. The operations are worth 40.00/0.080000 = 500.00, and the cash is worth its face value, so E0 = 500.00 + 100.00 = 600.00 and P0 = 600.00/20.00 = 30.00.

Step 2 — route A, a special dividend. DPS = 100.00/20.00 = 5.00, so the ex-dividend price is 30.00 − 5.00 = 25.00. The share count is unchanged at 20.00 million and the market capitalisation is 20.00 × 25.00 = 500.00, which is the operations and nothing else ✓

Step 3 — route B, an open-market repurchase. n = 100.00/30.00 = 3.3333 million shares, leaving N1 = 20.00 − 3.3333 = 16.6667 million. Equity is E1 = 600.00 − 100.00 = 500.00, so the price is 500.00/16.6667 = 30.00, unchanged.

Step 4 — the ongoing dividend per share, which is what actually differs. The operations still pay 40.00 a year to whoever holds the shares.
Route A: 40.00/20.0000 = 2.00 per share, and 2.00/0.080000 = 25.00 ✓
Route B: 40.00/16.6667 = 2.40 per share, and 2.40/0.080000 = 30.00 ✓
Fewer shares means a bigger slice each, and the price picks that up exactly.

Step 5 — three shareholders, each holding 100 shares before the payout.

ShareholderShares afterShare valueCashTotal wealth
Route A, takes the dividend100.00002500.00500.003000.00
Route B, tenders 16.6667 shares83.33332500.00500.003000.00
Route B, tenders nothing100.00003000.000.003000.00

The second row is a homemade dividend: wanting 500.00 of cash under route B, he sells 500.00/30.00 = 16.6667 shares and ends exactly where route A put him. It works in reverse too. A shareholder in route A who did not want the cash reinvests his 500.00 at the ex-dividend price of 25.00, buying 500.00/25.00 = 20 shares for a total of 120, worth 120 × 25.00 = 3000.00, which is the third row.

Check: compare ownership fractions, not share counts, because the two routes leave different numbers of shares outstanding. Route A reinvested: 120/20 000 000 = 0.0000060. Route B held: 100/16 666 667 = 0.0000060 ✓ Identical claims on identical assets. This check can fail: reinvest at the stale cum-dividend price of 30.00 and you buy only 16.6667 shares, the fractions no longer agree, and the error is the one the next warning box names.

Two divisions that go wrong, every year Compute the shares repurchased at the price the firm actually pays, n = R/P1, where P1 is the price after any announcement effect. In this chapter there is no announcement effect, so P1 = P0 and it makes no difference. In k7 § 7.4, where the announcement creates value, using the stale price is the standard way to lose the sub-question. The second slip is the mirror image: a dividend is reinvested at the ex-dividend price, because that is the price at which the shares now trade.
"The share price fell on the ex-date, so the dividend destroyed value" Every large dividend is followed by a fall in the quoted price, and it never means what it appears to mean. The holder was handed the difference in cash the same day; his wealth did not move by a krone. The mirror error is to call a repurchase good news because the price stays up and earnings per share rise: the shares that would have shared those earnings were bought and cancelled, and the holder paid for the privilege out of the firm's cash. In a perfect market neither route creates or destroys anything, which is precisely why any real price reaction has to be explained by a friction. Those explanations are k16.

15.4 Paying now or paying later

Irrelevance is stronger than the two routes above. Even the timing of the payout does not matter, as long as the cash the firm holds in the meantime earns the market rate on its risk, and investment policy is unchanged:

PV(pay C×(1 + rf) in one year) = C×(1 + rf)/(1 + rf) = C
Cash held in the firm is risk-free, so it is discounted at rf, and the growth and the discounting cancel exactly. Retaining cash is negative debt: it does not create value, and neither does releasing it.
Worked example 2: homemade timing, by borrowing instead of selling

Nordfjord Kraft again, with the same 100.00 of cash and the same 20 million shares, but the board now considers holding the cash for one year in a bank account at rf = 4.0000% and paying it out then. Does a shareholder who wants his money today care?

Step 1 — value the deferred payout. The account pays 100.00 × 1.040000 = 104.00 in one year, risk-free, so its value today is 104.00/1.040000 = 100.00. Total equity is 500.00 + 100.00 = 600.00 and the price is still 30.00. Deferring changed nothing about today's price.

Step 2 — what the deferral pays per share. In one year the firm pays 104.00/20.00 = 5.20 per share.

Step 3 — the shareholder builds his own immediate dividend. Holding 100 shares and wanting 500.00 today, he borrows 500.00 at rf = 4.0000% against them. He now has 500.00 in cash and 100 shares worth 3000.00, against a debt of 500.00: net wealth 3000.00.

Step 4 — one year later, the loan clears itself. He receives 100 × 5.20 = 520.00 and repays 500.00 × 1.040000 = 520.00. The two cancel to the krone, and he is left holding 100 shares of the operating business, which is exactly the position of a shareholder who was paid 500.00 immediately and spent it.

Step 5 — the reverse direction. A shareholder who is paid the 500.00 today but wanted it next year lends it at 4.0000% and has 500.00 × 1.040000 = 520.00 in a year, again identical.

Check: Step 4 is a real check, not an identity. It works only because he borrowed exactly the present value of what he was going to receive. Borrow 520.00 instead of 500.00 and the year-end receipt of 520.00 falls short of the 540.80 repayment, so the positions differ. The same discipline made the arbitrage in k6 worked example 1 close.

Why homemade anything defeats every payout argument in a perfect market The firm has exactly one financial capability its shareholders lack: none. Whatever pattern of cash the payout policy delivers, a shareholder can undo it by selling a slice of his holding, buying more shares, borrowing, or lending, all at the same prices the firm faces. A service you can perform for yourself at no cost has a market value of zero, so no payout policy can be worth paying for. This is the same argument that killed homemade leverage in k6 § 6.1, applied to the timing and form of cash rather than to the debt ratio.

15.5 The debt-financed payout, which is what the exam actually sets

Kurbatov does not ask about payout on its own. He asks about a firm that issues debt and hands the proceeds to shareholders, and then makes you do it once as a dividend and once as a repurchase. That is k6 § 6.4 with the payout arithmetic of this chapter attached. The routine is fixed:

special dividend: DPS = Dnew/N0  ·  Pex = P0 − DPS  ·  N unchanged
repurchase: n = Dnew/P0  ·  N1 = N0 − n  ·  P unchanged
both: E1 = V − Dnew  ·  rE = rU + (Dnew/E1)(rU − rD)
In a perfect market V does not change, so equity absorbs the whole of the new debt. The two routes split differently between price and share count, and identically in every other respect. rE is the same on both routes, because leverage D/E is the same on both.
Worked example 3: the H2024 and H2025 exercise, both routes, end to end

Haugesund Marine ASA is all-equity in a perfect market with no taxes. Free cash flow is 90.00 a year in perpetuity, paid out in full, and rU = 9.0000%. There are 40 million shares. The board announces an issue of 400.00 of permanent risk-free debt at rD = rf = 4.0000%, with the proceeds paid to shareholders. Millions of kroner.

Step 1 — the starting point. V = 90.00/0.090000 = 1000.00, all equity, so P0 = 1000.00/40.00 = 25.00. The current dividend per share is 90.00/40.00 = 2.25, and 2.25/0.090000 = 25.00 ✓ The firm is unlevered, so rE = rU = 9.0000% today.

Step 2 — the announcement. No taxes, no issuance costs, no change to the assets, so there is no news to price: V stays 1000.00 and the share price stays 25.00. Say this in one sentence on the exam and name MM Proposition I, because it is the sub-question's whole content.

Step 3 — the capital structure afterwards, common to both routes. D = 400.00, so E1 = 1000.00 − 400.00 = 600.00 and D/E = 400.00/600.00 = 0.6667.
rE = 0.090000 + 0.666667(0.090000 − 0.040000) = 0.090000 + 0.033333 = 0.123333 = 12.3333%
Pre-tax WACC: (600/1000)(0.123333) + (400/1000)(0.040000) = 0.074000 + 0.016000 = 0.090000 = rU ✓ (an identity, since rE came from MM II a line earlier — it confirms the arithmetic, nothing more)
Interest is 0.040000 × 400.00 = 16.00 a year, so equity now receives 90.00 − 16.00 = 74.00 a year.

Step 4 — route A, a special dividend. DPSspecial = 400.00/40.00 = 10.00, so the ex-dividend price is 25.00 − 10.00 = 15.00 on an unchanged 40 million shares: 40.00 × 15.00 = 600.00 = E1 ✓
The ongoing dividend is 74.00/40.00 = 1.85 per share, and 1.85/0.123333 = 15.00 ✓

Step 5 — route B, a repurchase. n = 400.00/25.00 = 16 million shares, leaving N1 = 24 million, and 600.00/24.00 = 25.00, unchanged ✓
The ongoing dividend is 74.00/24.00 = 3.0833 per share, and 3.0833/0.123333 = 25.00 ✓

Step 6 — the comparison the question ends on. Take a shareholder who owned one share at 25.00 before the announcement.

What he doesHoldsCashWealth
No recapitalisationshare paying 2.25 forever at 9.0000%0.0025.00
Route A, special dividendshare paying 1.85 forever at 12.3333%10.0025.00
Route B, tenders his sharenothing25.0025.00
Route B, holds his shareshare paying 3.0833 forever at 12.3333%0.0025.00

All four are worth 25.00. The seller did not beat the holder, the dividend did not beat the repurchase, and the recapitalisation did not beat doing nothing. Write that sentence out: it is the answer, and the numbers above are the evidence for it.

Check: the price identity P = E1/N1 cannot fail, since n was defined as 400.00/P. The checks that can fail are the two in Steps 4 and 5, where the new dividend per share is discounted at the new rE: both inputs are quantities that never entered the price. Skip the relevering and discount 3.0833 at the old 9.0000% and you get 34.26 instead of 25.00, and the clash tells you which step was wrong.

How this is marked H2025 Exercise 3 runs MM without taxes together with dividends and a recapitalisation, and H2024 Exercise 3 is the same exercise one leg shorter, at 18 points apiece. The sub-questions are the six steps above, chained, so the examiner is reading for a routine rather than for insight. Three habits collect the marks that candidates leave behind: state that the price is unchanged and why ("no taxes and no costs, so the payout is purely financial, MM I"); give the dividend per share both before and after, since the question asks for a comparison and half the answers give only one; and close by setting the seller against the holder in one table. If the exercise later switches taxes on, k7 § 7.4 is the only thing that changes, and it changes exactly one number, the price at which the repurchase executes.

15.6 What irrelevance does and does not claim

The result is narrow, and stating its boundaries is what separates a full answer from a recited one. Payout policy is irrelevant only given the following, and each item is a friction that a later part of the course restores:

AssumptionWhat it rules outWhere it is removed
No taxes on dividends or capital gainsInvestors preferring the form of income that is taxed lessk16, the dividend puzzle and clienteles
No transaction or issuance costsHomemade dividends being expensive to buildk16
Symmetric informationThe payout telling the market something it did not knowk16, signalling; k13 more generally
Investment policy fixed and value-maximisingCash inside the firm being spent badlyk11 § 11.1, and k16 as an argument for paying out
The cash earns a fair return while retainedValue leaking away while the firm sits on the moneyk16
Why the irrelevance result is useful even though its assumptions are false It is a benchmark, not a description. Because the perfect-market answer is exactly zero, any observed price reaction to a payout announcement must be attributable to one of the five lines above, and your job in an exam answer is to say which one. That is why the verbal payout question is answerable at all: without the benchmark, "the price rose after the buyback" has a hundred explanations, and with it, only a short list.
Where this sits Backwards: k6 § 6.4 supplies the recapitalisation half of worked example 3, and k7 § 7.4 shows what changes when a tax shield makes the announcement real news. Forwards: k16 removes the assumptions one at a time, and it is where the tender offer, the tax disadvantage of dividends, the clientele effect and the signalling content of a dividend cut are priced. The chapters are examined together, so learn them together.
↑ contents

16 · Payout with frictions

k15 proved that payout policy cannot matter. Firms nevertheless pay out enormous sums, argue about the form, and see their share prices move on the announcement, so something in the perfect-market list must be false. This chapter puts the frictions back one at a time and prices each of them. Payout appears in seven of the eleven mapped papers, and H2025 Exercise 3 puts it in Exercise 3 in its perfect-market form, alongside the recapitalisation, which means the friction material reaches you two other ways: as the interpretation sub-question that closes a quantitative exercise, and inside the verbal exercises, where "force the firm to pay out its cash" is one of the standard answers to H2025 Exercise 2 on what a blockholder can do about a weak CEO.

16.1 The two personal tax rates, and what they do to the ex-dividend drop

k8 § 8.3 taxed all equity income at a single rate τe. Payout policy is exactly the question that rate hides, so split it: τd is the investor's tax rate on dividends, τg his rate on realised capital gains. In most tax systems τd is at least as high as τg, and gains carry the extra advantage that the investor chooses when to realise them.

Now redo the ex-dividend arithmetic of k15 § 15.2 for an investor who pays these taxes. He can sell the day before the dividend, or hold through it and sell the day after. Setting the two after-tax proceeds equal gives the drop the market must produce:

Pcum − Pex = Div × (1 − τd)/(1 − τg) = Div × (1 − τ*d)
τ*d = (τd − τg)/(1 − τg)
τ*d is the effective dividend tax rate: the penalty for receiving a krone as a dividend rather than as a capital gain. Not on the formula sheet. When τd = τg it is zero and k15's full drop returns; when τd > τg the price falls by less than the dividend.
Why the price falls by less than the dividend when dividends are taxed harder The seller and the holder are not comparing gross amounts, they are comparing what they keep. A krone of dividend is worth (1 − τd) in the hand; a krone of share price given up saves (1 − τg) of taxable gain. If dividends are taxed more heavily, each krone of dividend is worth less than a krone of price, so the price cannot fall by the full amount or nobody would hold through the date. The drop measures the dividend at its after-tax exchange rate against a capital gain, and that ratio is the entire content of τ*d.
Worked example 1: the ex-dividend drop when dividends are taxed more heavily

A share trades at 60.00 the day before it goes ex-dividend on a dividend of 4.00. The investor who is deciding whether to sell now or after the dividend pays τd = 30% on dividends and τg = 20% on realised gains, and bought the share at 40.00, so that is his cost basis.

InputValue
Cum-dividend price Pcum60.00
Dividend per share4.00
Dividend tax rate τd30%
Capital gains tax rate τg20%
Cost basis per share40.00

Step 1 — the effective dividend tax rate. τ*d = (0.3000 − 0.2000)/(1 − 0.2000) = 0.1000/0.8000 = 0.1250 = 12.50%. Dividends are taxed 10 percentage points harder than gains, but the penalty relative to a capital gain is 12.50%, because the gain itself is taxed.

Step 2 — the drop. Pcum − Pex = 4.00 × (1 − 0.3000)/(1 − 0.2000) = 4.00 × 0.8750 = 3.50, so Pex = 60.00 − 3.50 = 56.50. Under k15's perfect market it would have been 56.00.

Step 3 — verify by pricing both strategies after tax. Sell cum-dividend. Proceeds 60.00, taxable gain 60.00 − 40.00 = 20.00, tax 0.2000 × 20.00 = 4.00.
After tax: 60.00 − 4.00 = 56.00.

Step 4 — hold through the dividend, then sell. Dividend after tax = 4.00 × (1 − 0.3000) = 2.80. Sale at 56.50 with a gain of 56.50 − 40.00 = 16.50 and tax 0.2000 × 16.50 = 3.30, leaving 56.50 − 3.30 = 53.20.
Total: 2.80 + 53.20 = 56.00.

Check: the two strategies pay 56.00 and 56.00, so the investor is indifferent and 56.50 is the equilibrium ex-price ✓ This check can fail, and it is worth knowing how: assume the naive drop of the full 4.00 and the ex-price becomes 56.00, at which holding through pays 2.80 + 56.00 − 0.2000(16.00) = 55.60 against 56.00 for selling first. Everyone would sell cum-dividend, which is not an equilibrium.

Step 5 — read off the cost of the dividend form. The dividend transferred 4.00 of value but only 3.50 of price, so 4.00 × 0.1250 = 0.50 per share leaked to the tax authority purely because the payment was labelled a dividend. On 20 million shares that is 10.00 million, and it is the reason the next section exists.

The cost basis is not the same as the purchase price of the position you are asked about Capital gains tax is charged on price minus basis, and the basis is whatever the question says the investor paid, not the current price and not the ex-dividend price. If a question gives you no basis, say that you assume the shares were bought at the current price so the gain is zero, and compute on that. Stating the assumption earns the mark; silently inventing a number does not.

16.2 The tax bill under a dividend and under a repurchase

The same distribution can be routed two ways, and the two routes hand the tax authority very different amounts. A dividend is taxed in full, in the hands of every shareholder, immediately. A repurchase is taxed only on the gain, only for the shareholders who tender, and the shareholders who do not tender pay nothing until they eventually sell.

dividend: tax = τd × (amount distributed)
repurchase: tax = τg × (P − basis) × n, with n = R/P
Three separate advantages to the repurchase, and an exam answer should name all three: a lower rate (τg instead of τd), a smaller base (only the gain, not the whole payment), and deferral for everyone who holds. The last one is not a saving, only a delay, so value it as a present value rather than assuming it away.
Worked example 2: what the two routes cost in tax

A firm with 20 million shares trading at 30.00 distributes 100.00 million of cash. Its shareholders bought at 20.00, so the basis is 20.00 per share, and they face τd = 30% and τg = 20%. Whatever shares they still hold, they expect to sell in 10 years, and the discount rate for the deferred tax liability is 6.0000%. Assume the price falls by the full dividend, which is the right assumption when the marginal investor setting the price is tax-exempt, as pension funds largely are. Millions of kroner.

Step 1 — route A, a dividend. DPS = 100.00/20.00 = 5.00, the ex-price is 30.00 − 5.00 = 25.00, and the tax paid at once is 0.3000 × 100.00 = 30.00.

Step 2 — route A, what is left embedded in the shares. All 20 million shares are still held, now at 25.00 against a basis of 20.00, so the unrealised gain is 5.00 × 20.00 = 100.00 and the tax that will be due on it is 0.2000 × 100.00 = 20.00.
Discounted 10 years at 6.0000%: 1.06000010 = 1.7908, so PV = 20.00/1.7908 = 11.17.

Step 3 — route B, a repurchase. n = 100.00/30.00 = 3.3333 million shares are bought at 30.00 from holders with a basis of 20.00, so the realised gain is 3.3333 × 10.00 = 33.33 and the tax is 0.2000 × 33.33 = 6.67.

Step 4 — route B, what is left embedded. The remaining 16.6667 million shares still trade at 30.00 against a basis of 20.00, an unrealised gain of 10.00 × 16.6667 = 166.67 carrying a future tax of 0.2000 × 166.67 = 33.33, with a present value of 33.33/1.7908 = 18.61.

Step 5 — put the two columns side by side.

Present value of taxRoute A, dividendRoute B, repurchase
Paid now30.006.67
Deferred, discounted11.1718.61
Total41.1725.28

Step 6 — the answer. The repurchase saves 41.17 − 25.28 = 15.89 on a distribution of 100.00, so roughly 16% of the amount distributed, and it does so without changing the firm's assets, its investment policy or the pre-tax wealth of anybody.

Check: pre-tax wealth must be identical on the two routes, or the tax comparison is measuring something else. Route A: 20.0000 million shares at 25.00 = 500.00 plus 100.00 of cash = 600.00. Route B: 16.6667 million shares at 30.00 = 500.00 plus 100.00 of cash = 600.00 ✓ Only the tax column differs, which is the point.

The same reasoning decides whether the firm should hold cash at all rather than pay it out and let shareholders invest it themselves. Compare one krone retained for a year against one krone paid out now:

retain: 1 + r(1 − τc)  ·  pay out and invest privately: 1 + r(1 − τi)
τi is the investor's tax rate on interest income, from k8 § 8.3. The dividend tax cancels, because the same krone is eventually taxed as a dividend on both routes. Retaining cash therefore carries a tax disadvantage when τc > τi and a tax advantage when τc < τi. State which inequality the question's rates satisfy rather than reciting a conclusion.

With k8's rates, τc = 22% and τi = 35%, the firm keeps 78% of the interest while the investor would keep only 65% of it, so 100 of cash held one year at r = 4.0000% is worth 100 × 0.040000 × (0.7800 − 0.6500) = 0.52 more inside the firm. Reverse the inequality, as a system with a high corporate rate and light taxation of savings does, and the same arithmetic makes hoarding cash a slow leak.

16.3 Clienteles, and who actually sets the price

Investors do not share a tax rate. A Norwegian pension fund pays nothing on either dividends or gains; a taxable individual pays both; a corporate holder is taxed differently again; an investor holding inside a tax-sheltered account pays on withdrawal only. Each of them has a different τ*d, and therefore a different opinion about the payout policy above:

Investor typeEffective view of τ*dPrefers
Tax-exempt: pension funds, endowments, sheltered accountsZeroIndifferent, so happy to hold high-dividend shares nobody else wants
Taxable individual, τd above τgPositiveRepurchases and retained gains
Long-horizon investor who rarely sellsPositive and larger, since deferral is worth more to himRepurchases
Investor needing regular income with costly tradingPositive but outweighed by transaction costsDividends, as a free homemade-dividend service
Why clienteles can make the puzzle disappear at the margin without solving it Investors sort themselves: those who dislike dividends buy the firms that do not pay them, and those who do not care buy the firms that do. Once the sorting is complete, the investor who is indifferent at the current price, the marginal investor, is by construction one who does not mind the policy, and the price shows no penalty. That is the clientele argument, and it explains why the ex-dividend drop in real data sits closer to the full dividend than a top personal tax rate would predict: the marginal holder over the ex-date is often a tax-exempt institution or a trader with a low effective rate. What it does not explain is why the firm chose the policy in the first place, since supplying a clientele that is already well served creates nothing. The puzzle survives at the aggregate level.
Do not answer "repurchases are always better" The tax argument has a sign only relative to a specified pair of rates and a specified investor. It is silent for a tax-exempt holder, and it reverses in any system that taxes gains at least as heavily as dividends, which several do through imputation or shareholder credits. Write the condition, then the conclusion: "if τd exceeds τg, as here, the repurchase is the cheaper route by 15.89". A conditional answer with a stated condition scores; an unconditional one invites the counter-example.

16.4 Signalling: what a payout tells the market

Managers know more about the firm's prospects than investors do, which is the k12 and k13 setting applied to payout. Two regularities follow, and both are examinable as prose.

First, dividends are smoothed. Firms set the regular dividend at a level they believe they can sustain, raise it slowly and partially towards long-run earnings, and cut it only under real pressure. Dividends are therefore far less volatile than the earnings they are paid from.

Second, changes carry information. On average an initiation or an increase is received as good news and a cut as very bad news, and the reaction to a cut is much larger than the reaction to an increase of comparable size.

Why an announcement is credible when talk is not A signal is only informative if imitating it is expensive for a firm that does not have the cash flow to back it. Raising the regular dividend commits the firm to a stream of payments the market will punish it for cutting, so a manager without the earnings to sustain it faces a bigger loss later than the price gain today. That asymmetry is what makes the raise informative. The same logic explains the size of the reaction to a cut: because managers avoid cutting until they have to, a cut reveals that they have run out of alternatives. This is the information content of dividends, and it is a signalling story, not a claim that dividends create value.

A repurchase carries a different signal. Buying shares at the market price is profitable for the remaining shareholders precisely when the shares are cheap, so an announcement is read as management saying the shares are undervalued. The signal is weaker than a dividend increase, because an open-market programme is an announcement of intent rather than a commitment: the firm can complete a fraction of it and no contract is broken. It is stronger when management's own holdings are large and are not tendered.

Confusing the signal with the thing it signals The most expensive sentence in a payout answer is "the repurchase raised the share price, so the repurchase created value". It did not. The shares were already worth more than the market thought, and the announcement moved the price towards the value that was there all along. Everything the repurchase itself does is the k15 arithmetic, which is neutral. Keep the two claims apart in your writing: this is a signal about existing value is worth marks, and this creates value is the error the keys look for. The same distinction is consistency check 5 in a different costume: ask whether investors' inference is rational given what management knows.

16.5 The agency argument for paying out free cash flow

k11 § 11.1 established that cash sitting inside a firm with no good projects tends to be spent badly, on empire building, diversifying acquisitions and vanity capacity. Payout is the direct remedy: the cash that has left cannot be wasted. This is the strongest positive argument for paying dividends, and it works even against a tax cost, because a tax rate of 30% on a distribution is cheap compared with a project that destroys the whole of it.

Why a dividend disciplines more than a repurchase, and why firms still prefer the repurchase The two instruments differ in one respect that matters: commitment. A regular dividend is sticky, so raising it hands the market a stick to beat management with next year, and it forces the firm back to the capital market for funding, where every project is priced by outsiders. An open-market repurchase programme commits to nothing and can be quietly abandoned. That is precisely why management likes the repurchase and why the discipline argument favours the dividend. The same feature explains the pattern in the data: repurchases have grown to be a large share of total payout, and they absorb the transitory part of earnings, while the regular dividend absorbs the part management believes is permanent.
Where this is worth points on a verbal exercise H2025 Exercise 2 asked what a shareholder holding 5 to 10% can do about a CEO who is not working in shareholders' interest, three actions at four points each, two to three sentences per action. "Push for a payout, a leveraged recapitalisation, or a special dividend" is one of the strongest available actions, and it is worth its four points only if you write the mechanism: the cash leaves, the free cash flow that funded the empire building is gone, debt service becomes a hard constraint, and the firm has to return to outside investors to fund anything new. Close with the limitation, that a payout also removes the firm's flexibility and can force it to skip genuinely good projects later.

16.6 How repurchases are actually done

k15 assumed the firm buys shares at the market price. In practice there are four methods, and the exam vocabulary distinguishes them:

MethodMechanismPrice paidNote
Open-market repurchaseThe firm buys on the exchange over months, usually under an announced programmeThe market priceThe great majority of repurchases. No commitment: programmes are often only partly completed
Fixed-price tender offerAn offer to all shareholders to buy a stated number of shares at a stated price, open for a few weeksA stated premium above marketOversubscription is handled pro rata. Large and fast
Dutch auction tender offerShareholders state the lowest price at which they will sell; the firm finds the price that fills the quantityOne clearing price, paid to everyone who tendered at or below itUsually a smaller premium than a fixed-price offer, because the market reveals the supply curve
Targeted repurchaseA privately negotiated purchase of one large holder's blockNegotiated, sometimes far above marketUsed to remove a hostile blockholder. Paying a premium to do so is greenmail, and it transfers value from the remaining shareholders

A fixed-price tender offer is the one with arithmetic of its own. The firm offers to buy Q shares at a price PT above the market price P0:

P1 = (E0 − Q·PT)/(N0 − Q)
wealth per original share, all tendering = (Q/N0)PT + (1 − Q/N0)P1 = P0
P1 is the price after the offer closes, and it is below P0 whenever PT is above it. The second line is an algebraic identity, so it can never fail and proves nothing about your arithmetic: substitute the first line and both terms collapse to E0/N0. Its value is as a statement, not as a check. When everyone participates pro rata, no premium however large can make the shareholders as a body better off, because they are paying it to themselves out of their own firm's cash.
Worked example 3: a fixed-price tender offer, and the premium that is not a gift

Part A. Fjellstrand ASA has 20 million shares trading at 30.00, so E = 600.00 million, and it holds enough cash to fund a buyback. It offers to buy 4 million shares at 33.00, a premium of (33.00 − 30.00)/30.00 = 10%. Assume the offer is fully subscribed and every shareholder tenders, so the firm buys pro rata.

Step 1 — the outlay and the firm afterwards. Cash paid out = 4.00 × 33.00 = 132.00, so E1 = 600.00 − 132.00 = 468.00 on N1 = 20.00 − 4.00 = 16.00 million shares.

Step 2 — the post-offer price. P1 = 468.00/16.00 = 29.25, which is below the 30.00 it started at. The premium was paid out of the shares that remain.

Step 3 — what a shareholder actually gets. With everyone tendering, each holder has 4.00/20.00 = 20% of his shares taken at 33.00 and keeps 80%.
Wealth per original share = 0.2000 × 33.00 + 0.8000 × 29.25 = 6.60 + 23.40 = 30.00 ✓ Exactly where he started, premium and all.

Step 4 — the shareholder who does not tender. He keeps a share worth 29.25 and receives nothing, so he loses 0.75 per share. A shareholder who could tender everything would receive 33.00 and gain 3.00. The premium is therefore not value created, it is a transfer from those who stay to those who sell, and that is why fixed-price offers are almost always oversubscribed and almost always pro-rated.

Check: total wealth must be conserved, since nothing about the assets changed. Tendered value 132.00 plus remaining equity 468.00 = 600.00 = the equity before the offer ✓ The step that can fail is Step 2: price the remaining shares at the tender price of 33.00 and you get 16.00 × 33.00 = 528.00, so 132.00 + 528.00 = 660.00, a firm that gained 60.00 by handing out its own cash. That contradiction is how you catch the error.

Part B — the same 4 million shares by Dutch auction. Shareholders submit the lowest price each will accept, and the firm reads off the supply schedule:

PriceShares offered at this price (m)Cumulative (m)
30.501.001.00
31.001.502.50
31.502.004.50

Step 5 — clear the auction. The lowest price at which cumulative supply reaches 4.00 million is 31.50. Everyone who tendered at or below 31.50 is paid 31.50, and the 31.50 bids are pro-rated: 4.00 − 2.50 = 1.50 million of the 2.00 million offered there, or 75% of each.

Step 6 — the firm afterwards. Outlay = 4.00 × 31.50 = 126.00, so E1 = 600.00 − 126.00 = 474.00 on 16.00 million shares, giving P1 = 29.625.

Check: the auction bought the same 4 million shares for 126.00 rather than 132.00, and the remaining price is 29.625 rather than 29.25, higher by exactly 6.00/16.00 = 0.375 ✓ The saving to the shareholders who stay is the whole of the difference, which is the reason a firm that does not need speed prefers the auction.

Two payout events look like this material and are not: a stock dividend pays shareholders in new shares, and a stock split divides each share into several. No cash leaves and no claim changes hands, so in a perfect market both are pure relabelling: twice as many shares at half the price. The reasons given for them are cosmetic or informational, such as keeping the share price inside a customary trading range or signalling confidence that the price will stay there.

16.7 The payout decision assembled

Learn this table by its last column, because that is the column an exam question hands you.

ForcePushes towardsStrongest for
MM irrelevance, k15Neither. The benchmark against which everything else is measuredEvery answer's first sentence
τd above τgRepurchases, and retention over payoutTaxable individual investors with long horizons
ClientelesWhatever the firm's own holders already wantFirms with a stable, identifiable shareholder base
SignallingA smoothed, sustainable regular dividendFirms whose earnings the market cannot observe well
Agency, free cash flowCommitted payout, so dividends over repurchasesMature, cash-rich firms with weak governance and few projects
Transaction and issuance costsRetention, to avoid paying out today and issuing tomorrowFirms with lumpy investment needs or costly access to capital
FlexibilityRepurchases, which commit to nothingFirms with volatile earnings, and managers who prefer discretion
Writing the payout discussion answer The shape that scores is the one k11 § 11.4 used for capital structure, and it transfers unchanged. Open with the benchmark in one sentence: in a perfect market the form of payout is irrelevant, so the answer must come from a named friction. Then sign two or three frictions for this firm, not in general: this firm's holders are taxable and long-horizon, so τ*d bites; this firm has free cash flow and no projects, so the commitment value of a dividend is large. Then say which force dominates and conclude in the direction the question asked. Four to six sentences is the stated budget on these questions, and a well-argued unusual point is credited rather than penalised.
Where this sits Backwards: k15 is the benchmark this chapter departs from, k8 § 8.3 supplies τe and τi and the personal-tax machinery, and k11 § 11.1 supplies the free cash flow problem that § 16.5 answers. Sideways: the signalling argument here is the payout case of k13, and the same rationality question that consistency check 5 asks of an equity issue applies to a dividend increase. Forwards: k27 puts payout in the governance toolkit, which is where the verbal exercise will most likely reach it.

Part IV — Valuation with leverage

↑ contents

17 · The WACC method

Valuing one case by the WACC method and again by the APV method appears in all eleven mapped papers, and it is normally the largest single exercise on the paper. H2024 Exercise 5 walks from a comparable firm to a value by both routes; H2025 Exercise 4 does it over two periods in nine chained sub-questions and demands four decimal places. The method itself is two lines long. What the exam actually tests is whether you know which assumption you are standing on, whether you can run the calculation backwards to solve for a growth rate or a break-even price (2016 MC3, V2024 Problem 2), and whether your answer survives consistency check 1 against the APV value in k18.

17.1 One stream, one rate

You have free cash flow from k2 and an after-tax WACC from k4. The WACC method puts them together and stops:

VL0 = FCF1/(1 + rwacc) + FCF2/(1 + rwacc)2 + FCF3/(1 + rwacc)3 + …
rwacc = (E/V)·rE + (D/V)·rD(1 − τc)
VL0 is the value of the whole levered firm, debt plus equity, at date 0. It is an enterprise value: operating assets only, no excess cash, no debt netted off. FCF is unlevered free cash flow, built before interest, exactly as in k2. The WACC formula is on the exam formula sheet; the interpretation is not.

Notice what is missing. The interest tax shield never appears as a cash flow anywhere in that expression. It has been folded into the discount rate, through the single term (D/V)·rD·τc by which the after-tax WACC sits below the pre-tax WACC:

rU − rwacc = (D/V)·rD·τc
Subtract the two lines of 4.6 and everything except this term cancels. The left-hand side is what the leverage is worth per krone of firm value per year, expressed as a rate cut. Under this course's convention pre-tax WACC = rU, so the same expression tells you how far below rU your discount rate should be.
Why does lowering the rate value the tax shield correctly? Every year the firm pays interest rD·D and the tax authority refunds τc of it. Divide that annual refund by firm value: τc·rD·D/V, which is exactly the wedge above. So discounting FCF at a rate reduced by the wedge is the same as adding the shield to the numerator each year, provided the wedge stays the same size every year. It stays the same size only if D/V stays the same every year. That single condition is the entire price of admission to the WACC method, and it is why the next section matters more than this one.

17.2 The constant D/V assumption, stated properly

The WACC method uses one discount rate for every future period. That is legitimate only if the firm's capital structure, measured at market value, is the same in every future period:

Dt / VLt = d, constant, for every t
Read as a policy, not as a fact about today's balance sheet. The firm is assumed to rebalance: borrow more when the business becomes more valuable, repay when it becomes less valuable. d is the target ratio, and it is what the exam means by phrases such as "maintains a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.50" or "keeps debt at 30% of firm value".

Three consequences follow, and each is worth a mark if you state it.

Because D/V is constant……this follows
the weights E/V and D/V never changerE, and therefore rwacc, is the same in every period, so one rate discounts everything
future debt levels move with firm value, which is uncertainthe tax shield carries the risk of the assets, so in APV it is discounted at rU, not rD (k18)
the tax shield carries asset riskpre-tax WACC = rU holds exactly. This is a consequence of the policy, not an algebraic identity
"Pre-tax WACC = rU" is not free arithmetic It is easy to read the identity in 4.1 as pure algebra, since it is just a weighted average. It is not. The weights are constant only under rebalancing, and it is rebalancing that gives the tax shield the same risk as the assets and lets βU be weighted without any (1 − τc) term. Under fixed permanent debt the shield is a safe stream, the relation between rE, rD and rU changes, and a single constant WACC no longer values the firm correctly. Say "under the course's rebalancing convention" when you assert the identity, and you have shown the examiner you know why it holds.
Why is a constant ratio the natural assumption for a going concern? A firm that never rebalances drifts. Let the business do well for five years and its debt-to-value ratio collapses towards zero, so it ends up carrying almost no tax shield precisely when it can most afford the interest. Real firms do not behave that way: they issue debt as they grow and repay as they shrink, keeping leverage within a band. The rebalancing assumption is the tractable version of that behaviour, and it is the version this course examines. If the question describes a fixed loan with a repayment schedule instead, it is telling you to use APV.

17.3 The growing-perpetuity form, and the single formula the exam reuses

Most exam firms are valued as a growing perpetuity after at most a few explicit years, using the formula from k1:

VL0 = FCF1 / (rwacc − g)
FCF1 is next year's flow, not this year's. If the question gives you FCF0, write FCF1 = FCF0(1 + g) first, on its own line. g must be strictly below rwacc, and it must be the growth rate of free cash flow, not of sales or of earnings, unless the question tells you they grow together.
Worked example 1: from a comparable firm to a value, and back out through APV

Nordfjord Logistics is not listed. Its listed twin, Bergen Freight, has equity worth 600 and debt worth 400 (millions), a cost of equity of 11.0000% and an expected return on debt of 4.0000%. Nordfjord will be run at a permanent target of D/V = 0.40 and can borrow at 4.0000%. Next year's free cash flow is 50, growing at 3.0000% forever. rf = 3.0000%, market risk premium 5.0000%, τc = 25%.

InputValue
Twin equity E / debt D (MNOK)600 / 400
Twin rE / rD11.0000% / 4.0000%
Nordfjord target D/V0.40
FCF1 / g50 / 3.0000%

Step 1 — the twin's betas. βE = (0.1100 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 1.60 and βD = (0.0400 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.20. The debt beta is not zero, so it must be carried.

Step 2 — unlever. E/V = 0.6000, D/V = 0.4000.
βU = 0.6000(1.60) + 0.4000(0.20) = 0.9600 + 0.0800 = 1.04
rU = 0.0300 + 1.04(0.0500) = 0.0820 = 8.2000%
Cross-check on the twin's own rates: 0.6000(0.1100) + 0.4000(0.0400) = 0.066000 + 0.016000 = 0.082000 ✓

Step 3 — relever to Nordfjord's D/V = 0.40. D/E = 0.40/0.60 = 0.666667.
rE = 0.0820 + 0.666667(0.0820 − 0.0400) = 0.082000 + 0.028000 = 0.1100 = 11.0000%

Step 4 — the after-tax WACC.
rwacc = 0.6000(0.1100) + 0.4000(0.0400)(1 − 0.25) = 0.066000 + 0.012000 = 0.0780 = 7.8000%
Pre-tax WACC: 0.6000(0.1100) + 0.4000(0.0400) = 0.082000 = rU ✓ (again an identity, not a test) (this one cannot fail: rE was produced by MM II from the same rU a line earlier, so the weighted average must return it. It catches arithmetic, not a wrong βU or a wrong debt policy.)

Step 5 — value by WACC. VL0 = 50/(0.0780 − 0.0300) = 50/0.0480 = 1 041.6667

Step 6 — value by APV, as an independent route. VU = 50/(0.0820 − 0.0300) = 50/0.0520 = 961.5385.
Debt today is D0 = 0.40 × 1 041.6667 = 416.6667, so TS1 = 0.25 × 0.0400 × 416.6667 = 4.166667. Debt grows with value at 3.0000%, so the shield is a growing perpetuity, and under a constant target ratio it is discounted at rU:
PV(TS) = 4.166667/(0.0820 − 0.0300) = 80.1282

Check (consistency check 1): 961.5385 + 80.1282 = 1 041.6667, the WACC answer to the last øre ✓ Be honest about what this check is. It is not two independent routes: the APV route takes D0 = 0.40 × VL from the WACC answer, and with rwacc = rU − d·rD·τc the sum VU + PV(TS) collapses algebraically back to VL. It is a fixed-point re-check, and it earns its place by catching the two errors that actually happen: the wrong discount rate on the shield, and arithmetic slips. Discount that same shield at rD = 4.0000% instead and you get 4.166667/0.0100 = 416.67 and a total of 1 378.21, which no WACC could reproduce. But note what the check cannot catch: get βU wrong and the error flows into rU, into rE, into rwacc and into both routes alike, so the two still agree. Check 2 from k0 § 0.5 is the one that guards that step: 0.6000(1.60) + 0.4000(0.20) = 1.04 = βU.

17.4 Period by period, and rolling the value back

When the question gives explicit forecasts for a few years and only then a growing perpetuity, discount each year separately and capitalise the rest:

VL0 = Σt=1..T FCFt/(1 + rwacc)t + TVT/(1 + rwacc)T, with TVT = FCFT+1/(rwacc − g)
VLt−1 = (FCFt + VLt)/(1 + rwacc)
The two lines give the same VL0. The first is the way you would type it into a calculator. The second, the backward recursion, is the way you should organise a long exam answer, because it produces VLt at every date along the way, and those intermediate values are what the debt schedule in k19 and the APV shield need. Start at VLT = TVT and walk left.
Why does the recursion give you something the one-line sum does not? The one-line sum answers one question: what is the firm worth today. The recursion answers it at every date. And the moment the question mentions a target debt ratio, you need VL1, VL2 and so on, because the debt outstanding at each date is d × VLt and the tax shield in year t + 1 is τc·rD·Dt. H2025 Exercise 4 is built on exactly this: value first, then the debt schedule it implies, then the shields. Get into the habit of writing the value line for every date even when only date 0 is asked, because the next sub-question will ask for the rest.
Worked example 2: three explicit years, a terminal value, and the whole debt schedule

Sunnmøre Components has a listed twin at D/E = 0.50 with rE = 12.7000% and rD = 4.3000%. rf = 2.5000%, market risk premium 6.0000%, τc = 22%. Sunnmøre will hold D/V = 0.35 and borrows at 4.3000%. Free cash flow is forecast at 40, 50 and 56 for years 1 to 3, growing at 2.0000% thereafter. All figures in MNOK.

Step 1 — unlever the twin. βE = (0.1270 − 0.0250)/0.0600 = 1.70; βD = (0.0430 − 0.0250)/0.0600 = 0.30.
Weights: E/V = 1/1.50 = 0.666667, D/V = 0.50/1.50 = 0.333333.
βU = 0.666667(1.70) + 0.333333(0.30) = 1.133333 + 0.100000 = 1.233333
rU = 0.0250 + 1.233333(0.0600) = 0.025000 + 0.074000 = 0.0990 = 9.9000%
Cross-check: 0.666667(0.1270) + 0.333333(0.0430) = 0.084667 + 0.014333 = 0.099000 ✓

Step 2 — relever and build the WACC. D/E = 0.35/0.65 = 0.538462.
rE = 0.0990 + 0.538462(0.0990 − 0.0430) = 0.099000 + 0.030154 = 0.129154 = 12.9154%
rwacc = 0.6500(0.129154) + 0.3500(0.0430)(0.78) = 0.083950 + 0.011739 = 0.095689 = 9.5689%
Pre-tax: 0.6500(0.129154) + 0.3500(0.0430) = 0.083950 + 0.015050 = 0.099000 = rU ✓ (again an identity, not a test)

Step 3 — the terminal value. FCF4 = 56 × 1.02 = 57.12, and everything in year 3 is a normal operating year, so growing the year-3 flow is legitimate here.
TV3 = 57.12/(0.095689 − 0.020000) = 57.12/0.075689 = 754.6671

Step 4 — roll the value back one year at a time. Start with VL3 = TV3 = 754.6671.
VL2 = (56 + 754.6671)/1.095689 = 810.6671/1.095689 = 739.8697
VL1 = (50 + 739.8697)/1.095689 = 789.8697/1.095689 = 720.8886
VL0 = (40 + 720.8886)/1.095689 = 760.8886/1.095689 = 694.4385

Step 5 — verify with the one-line sum. 40/1.095689 = 36.5067; 50/1.095689² = 41.6481; 56/1.095689³ = 42.5722; 754.6671/1.095689³ = 573.7115. Total = 36.5067 + 41.6481 + 42.5722 + 573.7115 = 694.4385 ✓

Date t0123
FCFt—40.0050.0056.00
VLt694.4385720.8886739.8697754.6671
Dt = 0.35 VLt243.0535252.3110258.9544264.1335
TSt = 0.22 × 0.043 × Dt−1—2.29932.38692.4497

Step 6 — the APV route, for the check. Unlevered terminal value: TVU3 = 57.12/(0.0990 − 0.0200) = 57.12/0.0790 = 723.0380.
VU = 40/1.099 + 50/1.099² + 56/1.099³ + 723.0380/1.099³ = 36.3967 + 41.3975 + 42.1886 + 544.7134 = 664.6962
From year 4 the shield grows at 2.0000%: TS4 = 0.22 × 0.043 × 264.1335 = 2.4987, so its value at date 3 is 2.4987/(0.0990 − 0.0200) = 31.6292.
PV(TS) = 2.2993/1.099 + 2.3869/1.099² + 2.4497/1.099³ + 31.6292/1.099³ = 2.0922 + 1.9762 + 1.8455 + 23.8284 = 29.7423

Check (consistency check 1): 664.6962 + 29.7423 = 694.4385 = VL0 from Step 4 ✓ Same caveat as in worked example 1: the APV route takes the debt levels Dt = 0.35 × VLt straight from the WACC answer, so this is a fixed-point re-check rather than two independent valuations. It still earns its keep, because the shield's discount rate and the year-by-year arithmetic are genuinely free to be wrong. Notice also that PV(TS) of 29.7423 is only 4.3% of the firm's value, which is the honest scale of the tax shield at 35% leverage and a 4.3000% borrowing rate. The quick smell test: under rebalancing the shield is roughly τc·d·rD/(rU − g) of firm value, single digits at ordinary leverage. Using τc·D instead would give 53.47 here, nearly twice the right answer, and that ratio is what should make you stop.

How the marks are actually allocated In the Kurbatov format this material is a single exercise of 18 to 20 points broken into up to nine sub-questions, and H2025 Exercise 4 asked for four decimal places. The points sit on the intermediate lines: βU, rU, rE, rwacc, each VLt, each Dt, each TSt. Write them in a labelled table like the one above rather than as a running paragraph, and a later arithmetic slip costs you one line instead of the exercise. If you lose a number, carry it forward as a symbol and say so: the solution keys give credit for the correct method on a wrong input.

17.5 Running it backwards: implied growth and break-even prices

The exam rarely asks only for a value. It hands you a value, usually a price someone is asking, and asks what the market must be assuming, or what you can afford to pay. Both are the same rearrangement:

VL0 = FCF1/(rwacc − g)   ⟺   g = rwacc − FCF1/VL0
break-even purchase price P* = FCF1/(rwacc − g), the price at which NPV = 0
The first line reads the market's growth assumption out of a price: it is the growth rate at which the deal is exactly zero NPV. The second is the same equation solved for the price instead. Do this algebraically, in two lines. Guessing values of g until the price matches wastes minutes you do not have and earns no method marks.

A third variant appears when the question hands you an operating model rather than a cash flow: solve for the unit price, cost or volume that produces the free cash flow the valuation needs. The chain is the same, run in reverse: required VL → required FCF1 → required EBIT → required sales price.

Worked example 3: implied growth, break-even price, break-even unit price

Hardanger Instruments is for sale at an asking price of 600 for the whole firm. Its free cash flow next year will be 36. You would run it at D/V = 0.40, borrowing at rD = 4.5000%; βU for the business is 1.20. rf = 3.0000%, market risk premium 5.0000%, τc = 25%. Figures in MNOK.
