Step 1 — build the discount rate. rU = 0.0300 + 1.20(0.0500) = 0.0900 = 9.0000%. βD = (0.0450 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.30. D/E = 0.40/0.60 = 0.666667.
rE = 0.0900 + 0.666667(0.0900 − 0.0450) = 0.090000 + 0.030000 = 0.1200 = 12.0000%
rwacc = 0.6000(0.1200) + 0.4000(0.0450)(0.75) = 0.072000 + 0.013500 = 0.085500 = 8.5500%
Pre-tax: 0.6000(0.1200) + 0.4000(0.0450) = 0.072000 + 0.018000 = 0.090000 = rU ✓ (again an identity, not a test)

Step 2 — (a) what growth rate does the asking price imply? Set 36/(0.085500 − g) = 600 and solve:
g = 0.085500 − 36/600 = 0.085500 − 0.060000 = 0.0255 = 2.5500%
Verify forwards: 36/(0.085500 − 0.025500) = 36/0.060000 = 600.0000 ✓ So the seller is asking you to believe free cash flow grows at 2.5500% forever. State whether that is plausible; on a verbal follow-up the examiner wants that judgement, not just the number.

Step 3 — (b) what is the most you can pay if you believe g = 2.0000%?
P* = 36/(0.085500 − 0.020000) = 36/0.065500 = 549.6183
At the asking price of 600 the NPV of the acquisition is 549.6183 − 600 = −50.3817. Walk away, or negotiate to 549.62.

Step 4 — (c) what selling price per unit would justify the asking price? The business sells 2.00 million units at a price p, variable cost 40 per unit, fixed costs 12, depreciation 10, CapEx 10, no working capital movement. Work the chain backwards. To support 600 at g = 2.5500% you need FCF1 = 36. Since depreciation and CapEx cancel, FCF1 = EBIT1(1 − τc), so the required EBIT is 36/0.75 = 48.00. And EBIT = 2.00(p − 40) − 12 − 10, so:
2.00(p − 40) − 22 = 48  ⟹  2.00p − 80 = 70  ⟹  2.00p = 150  ⟹  p = 75.00
Verify forwards: EBIT = 2.00(75.00 − 40) − 22 = 70.00 − 22.00 = 48.00; FCF = 48.00(0.75) + 10 − 10 = 36.00 ✓

Check (consistency check 1) on part (a): value the same firm by APV at g = 2.5500%. VU = 36/(0.0900 − 0.0255) = 36/0.0645 = 558.1395. D0 = 0.40 × 600 = 240.00, so TS1 = 0.25 × 0.0450 × 240.00 = 2.70, growing at 2.5500% and discounted at rU because leverage is a constant ratio: PV(TS) = 2.70/(0.0900 − 0.0255) = 41.8605. Total 558.1395 + 41.8605 = 600.0000 ✓ Same on part (b): VU = 36/0.0700 = 514.2857, D0 = 0.40 × 549.6183 = 219.8473, TS1 = 2.4733, PV(TS) = 2.4733/0.0700 = 35.3326, total 549.6183 ✓

17.6 What the method will not do for you

Three limits, and the exam plays on all three.

The three ways a WACC valuation goes wrong on the paper (1) Stale weights. The E/V and D/V in the WACC are the target weights of the firm being valued, in market value. Not the twin's weights, not book values, and not the weights implied by an old balance sheet. Using the comparable's leverage to build your own WACC skips the whole relevering step. (2) A discount rate that does not match the cash flow. Discount FCF at rwacc. Discount flow to equity at rE (k19). Discount the unlevered firm at rU. Mixing them is the most expensive single error in this part of the course. (3) A changing capital structure. If the question describes a fixed loan, a repayment schedule, or leverage that is deliberately being run down, the WACC is not constant and a single-rate valuation is wrong. Switch to APV and say why in one sentence.
The tax shield is in the rate, so never also put it in the cash flow The most damaging error here is double counting. Some candidates compute FCF correctly, discount it at rwacc, and then add PV(TS) on top because they remember VL = VU + PV(TS) from k7. That adds the shield twice, once through the reduced rate and once as a separate term, and in Worked example 1 it would give 1 041.6667 + 80.1282 = 1 121.79 for a firm worth 1 041.6667. The identity VL = VU + PV(TS) is true, but the VU in it is discounted at rU, never at rwacc. Choose one method per valuation, and use the other only as the check.
Why do the two methods have to agree at all? Because they are two ways of splitting one set of cash flows, not two theories. The firm generates FCF and the government hands back τc·rD·D each year. The WACC method values the sum of those two streams in one step by shaving the government's contribution off the discount rate; APV values them one at a time and adds. Nothing has changed about the firm between the two calculations, so the totals must match whenever both methods apply — that is, whenever leverage really is rebalanced to a constant ratio. Under a fixed repayment schedule the WACC method has no constant rate to use and the agreement is not available at all; k18 works that case. When they do not, one of your inputs is inconsistent with the debt policy you assumed, and finding which one is usually a two-minute job that saves an entire sub-question.
Where this goes next k18 takes the APV route seriously and settles the question the multiple-choice papers kept asking: which rate discounts the tax shield, and why. k19 handles the circularity you may already have noticed, that Dt = d·VLt requires the value you are trying to compute, and it drills the debt adjustment between periods when VL falls. k20 runs the whole thing end to end on one case, in the nine steps of H2025 Exercise 4.
↑ contents

18 · The APV method

One question in this chapter has been asked more often than any other single question in the mapped papers: which rate discounts the interest tax shield? It appeared as multiple choice in all eight mapped papers that had multiple choice, 2021 MC3 among them. The Kurbatov format has no multiple choice, so it now arrives as a sentence buried in a long exercise, describing the firm's debt policy, and the whole valuation turns on whether you read it correctly. H2025 Exercise 4 does exactly this: it states a target leverage ratio, and the shield must therefore be discounted at rU. Get the rate wrong and consistency check 1 fails, the examiner sees it immediately, and every later sub-question inherits the error.

18.1 Two pieces, valued one at a time

Adjusted present value takes the identity you already met in k7 and treats it as a procedure:

VL = VU + PV(TS)
VU = Σt FCFt/(1 + rU)t   ·   TSt = τc·rD·Dt−1
Neither line is on the exam formula sheet. Memorise both. VU is the firm valued as if it were financed entirely by equity, so it is discounted at rU and never at rwacc. Dt−1 is the debt outstanding at the start of period t, since that is the balance the interest is charged on.

The recipe is three lines long. Value the business ignoring debt. Value the shield the debt creates. Add. Where all the difficulty sits is in the second line, and specifically in the rate you use there.

Why split the valuation at all when the WACC method does it in one step? Because the split separates the two questions the exam wants answered separately: what the operating business is worth, and what the financing adds. That has three practical consequences. It lets you value a firm whose leverage is changing, where a single WACC does not exist. It shows the size of the tax shield as a number, which is what a question like "how much of the firm's value comes from its debt policy?" is asking for. And it gives you a second, independent route to the same answer, which is consistency check 1 from k0 § 0.5.

18.2 Which rate discounts the shield: the rule, cold

The shield is a cash flow like any other, so it is discounted at a rate that matches its own risk. Its risk is decided entirely by the debt policy the question describes, because the shield in year t is τc·rD·Dt−1 and the only uncertain thing in that expression is Dt−1.

What the question says about the debtHow risky is Dt−1 seen from today?Discount the shield at
"maintains a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.50", "keeps debt at 30% of firm value", "rebalances annually to its target"Unknown. Future debt tracks firm value, so it carries the risk of the assetsrU
"has 200 million of perpetual debt outstanding", "the loan is never repaid"Known and fixed. Only default can stop the paymentsrD (rf if the debt is risk-free)
"repays 100 million a year for three years", "the loan amortises over five years"Known amount at every date, given in the questionrD, period by period
"holds debt at three times free cash flow", "debt is a fixed multiple of EBITDA"Unknown. Debt tracks a risky operating quantityrU
Why does a target ratio make a safe-looking cash flow risky? Follow one krone of shield. Under a target ratio the firm's debt next year is d × VL next year, and VL next year depends on how the business does. Good year, more debt, bigger shield. Bad year, less debt, smaller shield. So the shield moves one-for-one with the assets, which is the definition of asset risk, and asset risk is priced at rU. Under fixed debt, D is written into a loan contract and does not respond to the business at all; the shield is then as safe as the interest payment that generates it, and rD is the rate that prices exactly that risk. The rule is not a convention to memorise blindly. It follows from asking what makes next year's debt balance uncertain.
Read the debt sentence before you write anything The examiner tells you the policy in one clause and never repeats it. Underline it. Then write, as the first line of your answer, "the firm rebalances to a constant D/V, so the tax shield carries asset risk and is discounted at rU" or "the debt is fixed and permanent, so the shield is discounted at rD and PV(TS) = τc·D". That single sentence earns marks on its own in the solution keys, and it stops you from mixing the two halfway through. If the wording is genuinely ambiguous, state the assumption you are making and carry on. An answer with a stated assumption scores; an answer with no stated assumption and the wrong rate does not.

18.3 Fixed permanent debt, and the shortcut that keeps being misapplied

If the debt is a fixed amount D that is rolled over forever, the shield is a level perpetuity of τc·rD·D discounted at rD, and rD cancels:

PV(TS) = τc·rD·D / rD = τc·D   ⟹   VL = VU + τc·D
The most attractive formula in the course and the most frequently misused. It needs both conditions: the amount is fixed, and it is permanent. Drop either one and the answer is wrong. Note also that rD disappearing does not mean the discount rate was irrelevant; it means the numerator and the denominator both contain it.

Under a constant target ratio the same annual shield is discounted at rU instead, which gives the rebalanced counterpart:

PV(TS) = τc·rD·D / (rU − g)   (constant D/V, debt and shield growing at g)
Set g = 0 for a firm that is not growing. Compare the two denominators: rD under fixed debt against rU − g under rebalancing. Since rU is normally well above rD, the rebalanced shield is worth substantially less, and the ratio of the two answers is roughly (rU − g)/rD.
Worked example 1: one firm, one loan, three debt policies, three answers

Trondheim Textiles produces an unlevered free cash flow of 84 a year in perpetuity with no growth. Its unlevered cost of capital is rU = 12.0000%. It borrows 250 at rD = 6.0000%, and τc = 22%. All figures in MNOK.

Step 1 — the unlevered firm, common to all three policies. VU = 84/0.1200 = 700.0000

Step 2 — the annual shield, also common to all three. TS = 0.22 × 0.0600 × 250 = 3.30 a year. The cash flow is identical under every policy. Only its risk differs, and therefore only its discount rate differs.

Step 3 — policy A, fixed and permanent. The 250 is never repaid. The shield is a level perpetuity discounted at rD:
PV(TS) = 3.30/0.0600 = 55.0000, which is τc·D = 0.22 × 250 = 55.0000 ✓
VL = 700.0000 + 55.0000 = 755.0000

Step 4 — policy B, rebalanced to a constant D/V. The same 3.30, discounted at rU:
PV(TS) = 3.30/0.1200 = 27.5000, so VL = 700.0000 + 27.5000 = 727.5000
The implied target ratio is d = 250/727.5000 = 0.343643, so this firm is running at 34.3643% debt and holding it there.

Step 5 — policy C, the loan is repaid in full at the end of year 2. Two shields of 3.30, both known amounts, both discounted at rD:
PV(TS) = 3.30/1.06 + 3.30/1.06² = 3.1132 + 2.9370 = 6.0502, so VL = 706.0502

Step 6 — the WACC route on policy B (consistency check 1). E = 727.5000 − 250 = 477.5000, D/E = 250/477.5000 = 0.523560.
rE = 0.1200 + 0.523560(0.1200 − 0.0600) = 0.120000 + 0.031414 = 0.151414 = 15.1414%
Weights: E/V = 477.5000/727.5000 = 0.656357 and D/V = 0.343643.
rwacc = 0.656357(0.151414) + 0.343643(0.060000)(0.78) = 0.0993814 + 0.0160825 = 0.1154639 = 11.5464%
VL = 84/0.1154639 = 727.5000 ✓

Check (consistency check 1): the WACC route reproduces policy B's 727.5000 without ever mentioning PV(TS), so it is a genuine second computation and it can fail. Watch it fail on the classic error. Suppose you keep the rebalancing story but discount the shield at rD, claiming 755.0000. Then E = 505.0000, D/E = 0.495050, rE = 0.1200 + 0.495050(0.0600) = 0.149703, rwacc = 0.100132 + 0.015497 = 0.115629, and VL = 84/0.115629 = 726.46. That is 28.54 away from the 755.0000 you claimed, and the examiner needs one line to spot it. Policy A's 755.0000 is not wrong in itself; it is wrong for a firm that rebalances.

18.4 A known repayment schedule

The most common non-perpetual case on a paper is a loan with a stated amortisation profile. There is no shortcut and no formula to remember. You build a table:

PV(TS) = Σt=1..T τc·rD·Dt−1 / (1 + rD)t
Dt−1 is the balance outstanding during period t, that is the balance after the repayment at date t−1 and before the repayment at date t. Getting this off by one period is the single most common slip here, and it always overstates the shield, because it uses a balance that has not yet been reduced.
Worked example 2: an amortising loan, and a check that is not available

Voss Hydro generates 90 a year in perpetuity, rU = 12.0000%, τc = 22%. It takes a loan of 300 at rD = 5.0000%, repaid in three equal instalments of 100 at the end of years 1, 2 and 3, and stays debt-free afterwards. Interest each year is charged on the opening balance. MNOK.

Step 1 — the unlevered firm. VU = 90/0.1200 = 750.0000

Step 2 — the loan table. Write the opening balance first, then the interest, then the shield.

Year tOpening balance Dt−1Interest 5.0000%TSt = 0.22 × interestTSt/1.05t
1300.0015.003.303.142857
2200.0010.002.201.995465
3100.005.001.100.950221
4+0.000.000.000.000000

Step 3 — the shield. The amounts are contractual, so they are discounted at rD = 5.0000%:
PV(TS) = 3.142857 + 1.995465 + 0.950221 = 6.0885

Step 4 — verify along a second route. Every shield is 0.22 × 0.05 = 0.011 times the opening balance, so PV(TS) must equal 0.011 times the present value of the balance profile:
300.00/1.05 + 200.00/1.05² + 100.00/1.05³ = 285.71 + 181.41 + 86.38 = 553.50
0.011 × 553.50 = 6.0885 ✓ Same number by a route that never computed an individual shield.

Step 5 — the value. VL = 750.0000 + 6.0885 = 756.0885

Step 6 — feel the size of the difference. τc·D0 = 0.22 × 300 = 66.0000. The correct shield of 6.0885 is smaller by a factor of 10.84. Writing 66.0000 here, on the grounds that the firm has 300 of debt, would inflate firm value by almost 8%.

Check (consistency check 1) — and why it cannot be run here. Consistency check 1 asks whether the WACC method reproduces the APV value. It cannot be run in its usual form on this firm, and saying so is part of the answer. The reason is that D/V is not constant: it starts at 300.00/756.0885 = 39.6779% and falls to zero by date 3, so there is no single rwacc. To see how badly a constant WACC misfires, build one at the opening ratio using the course's rebalancing formulas: D/E = 300.0000/456.0885 = 0.657767, rE = 0.1200 + 0.657767(0.1200 − 0.0500) = 0.166044, rwacc = 0.100161 + 0.015474 = 0.115635 = 11.5635%, giving 90/0.115635 = 778.31. That overstates the firm by 22.22, and it does so because it assumes the firm keeps borrowing at 39.6779% forever when in fact it retires the loan in three years. What you can check here is Step 4's second route, and the ceiling in Step 6: PV(TS) must lie strictly between 0 and τc·D0, and 6.0885 does.

τc·D is a ceiling, not a default The formula VL = VU + τc·D is so memorable that it gets written down whenever the word "debt" appears. It is correct only for debt that is fixed in amount and never repaid, and it is the largest value the shield can take for a given D, because it assumes the shield lasts forever and is riskless enough to discount at rD. Worked example 1 puts the rebalanced answer at half of it and the two-year answer at a ninth of it. Before you write τc·D, ask two questions in order: is the amount fixed, and is it permanent? If the answer to either is no, you are building a table instead.

18.5 Debt tied to cash flow, and other policies you will meet

Between "fixed forever" and "a constant fraction of value" sit several policies the exam uses, and all of them are handled by the same question: what makes Dt−1 uncertain?

PolicyDebt scheduleShield discounted atIs a constant WACC available?
Constant D/V, rebalancedDt = d·VLtrUYes, this is the WACC method's home ground
Constant multiple of FCFDt = k·FCFt+1rUYes if FCF and value grow at the same rate, since then D/V is constant too
Fixed permanent amountDt = DrDNo, D/V drifts as value changes
Stated amortisationgiven in the questionrDNo, use APV
Debt raised for one project, repaid at the endgivenrDNo, and APV is much the fastest route
Why does debt tied to free cash flow behave like debt tied to value? Because value is the discounted stream of free cash flow. If the firm promises to hold debt at three times next year's FCF and FCF turns out 10% higher than expected, the firm borrows 10% more, and the firm is also worth about 10% more. So D/V lands in the same place either way, and the shield rides on a risky operating number in both cases. That is why the rate is rU. The one thing to check before you use a single WACC on such a firm is that FCF and value really do grow at a common rate; in a constant-growth perpetuity they do, which is the case the exam sets.
Worked example 3: debt set as a multiple of free cash flow

Kvinnherad Marine expects free cash flow of 80 next year, growing at g = 2.0000% forever. rU = 10.0000%, and it can borrow at rD = 5.0000%. τc = 25%. Its stated financial policy is to keep net debt at three times the coming year's free cash flow. MNOK.

Step 1 — the unlevered value. VU = 80/(0.1000 − 0.0200) = 80/0.0800 = 1 000.0000

Step 2 — the debt schedule the policy implies. D0 = 3 × 80 = 240.0000, and since FCF grows at 2.0000% so does the debt: D1 = 3 × 81.60 = 244.8000, and so on.

Step 3 — the shield and its rate. TS1 = 0.25 × 0.0500 × 240.0000 = 3.00, growing at 2.0000%. The debt tracks free cash flow, which is risky, so the shield carries asset risk and is discounted at rU:
PV(TS) = 3.00/(0.1000 − 0.0200) = 3.00/0.0800 = 37.5000

Step 4 — the value. VL = 1 000.0000 + 37.5000 = 1 037.5000

Step 5 — confirm that leverage really is constant. D0/VL0 = 240.0000/1 037.5000 = 0.231325. One year on, VL1 = 1 037.5000 × 1.02 = 1 058.2500 and D1 = 244.8000, so D1/VL1 = 0.231325, unchanged. The policy is a constant-D/V policy in disguise, which is why the WACC method is available and why rU was the right rate in Step 3.

Check (consistency check 1): E = 1 037.5000 − 240.0000 = 797.5000, D/E = 240.0000/797.5000 = 0.300940.
rE = 0.1000 + 0.300940(0.1000 − 0.0500) = 0.100000 + 0.015047 = 0.115047 = 11.5047%
rwacc = 0.768675(0.115047) + 0.231325(0.050000)(0.75) = 0.0884337 + 0.0086747 = 0.0971084 = 9.7108%
VL = 80/(0.0971084 − 0.0200) = 80/0.0771084 = 1 037.50, the 1 037.5000 of Step 4 ✓ The check bites: had you discounted this shield at rD you would have written 3.00/(0.0500 − 0.0200) = 100.0000 and a firm value of 1 100.0000, which no WACC built from these inputs can produce.

18.6 Choosing between APV and WACC, and what to write

Both methods answer the same question, so when both are legitimate, pick the faster one and use the other as the check. When only one is legitimate, say which and why.

SituationUseBecause
Target leverage stated, valuing a whole firm or divisionWACC, then APV as the checkOne rate does the whole job; the shield never has to be scheduled
Leverage changing, a loan being repaid, a fixed amountAPV onlyThere is no single rwacc; a constant one is simply wrong
The question asks what the debt policy is worthAPVPV(TS) is the answer, as a number, and WACC never produces it
A project financed with a specific tranche of debtAPVThe tranche's schedule is given, so the shield is a short table
Both are demanded, as in H2024 Exercise 5 and H2025 Exercise 4Both, in that orderThe exercise is testing consistency check 1 explicitly
What a full-mark APV answer looks like Four lines, in this order. One: name the debt policy in the question's own words and state the rate it implies. Two: VU, discounted at rU, with the arithmetic shown. Three: the shield, as a table if it is scheduled and as a perpetuity formula if it is not. Four: VL = VU + PV(TS), followed by the sentence "this agrees with the WACC value of …, consistency check 1" when the WACC method is available, or "the WACC method is not available here because D/V is not constant" when it is not. In H2024 Exercise 5 and H2025 Exercise 4 the APV sub-question comes after the WACC one and is worth fewer points than it looks, precisely because the examiner expects the two to agree; the marks are for the reasoning about the rate, not for redoing the discounting.
Three errors that cost the whole sub-question (1) Discounting VU at rwacc. VU is by definition the all-equity firm, so it takes rU. Using rwacc counts the shield once in VU and again in PV(TS). (2) Using the promised yield instead of rD. k5 separates the two: the expected return on debt is what enters here. On the shield's cash flow, however, the interest actually paid is the coupon the firm contracts to pay, so read the question carefully about which rate generates the deduction. (3) Using the wrong debt balance. The shield in year t comes from the balance outstanding during year t, which is Dt−1. In a rebalanced firm that means you need VLt−1 first, which is the circularity k19 resolves.
Why must APV and WACC agree when both apply? They are not two theories of value, they are two ways of grouping the same cash flows. The firm produces FCF, and the tax authority hands back τc·rD·D each year. APV values the two streams separately and adds them. The WACC method values their sum in one step, by lowering the discount rate by exactly the amount that the annual refund represents per krone of firm value. Same cash, same risk, same total. When your two answers differ, it is never because the methods disagree; it is because one of your inputs is inconsistent with the debt policy you assumed. Find that input.
Where this goes next Both k17 and this chapter have quietly assumed you already know Dt when you compute the shield, but under a target ratio Dt = d·VLt and VLt is what you are trying to find. k19 breaks that circle by working backwards from the last period, adds the third valuation method, flow to equity, and handles the debt adjustment when VL falls between two dates, which is H2025 Exercise 4(f). k20 then runs the whole machine once, end to end.
↑ contents

19 · FTE, debt policy and debt adjustments

This chapter contains the single step that separates a good script from a mediocre one. When a firm holds a target debt ratio and its levered value falls from one date to the next, the firm has to repay debt, and that repayment is a cash outflow to shareholders that must appear in the flow to equity. H2025 Exercise 4(f) asks for exactly this, and almost everybody either leaves the debt at its opening level or forgets that the change in debt is a cash flow at all. The chapter also settles the circularity you have been carrying since k17, that Dt = d·VLt needs the value you are trying to compute, and adds the third valuation method, flow to equity, so that you have all three and can say which one a question wants.

19.1 The third route: flow to equity

The WACC method and the APV method both value the whole firm and leave you to subtract debt at the end. Flow to equity goes straight for the equity, by discounting the cash that actually reaches shareholders at the rate shareholders require:

E0 = Σt FCFEt/(1 + rE)t   ·   VL0 = E0 + D0
FCFE is the free cash flow to equity, the flow to equity. Not on the formula sheet. Note the two things that make it different from the other methods: the discount rate is rE, and the result is equity value, not firm value. Forgetting to add D0 back before comparing with a WACC answer is the commonest way to fail consistency check 1 on a correct calculation.

19.2 Building FCFE, line by line

Start from free cash flow, which belongs to everybody, and strip out what the lenders take and add back what they lend:

FCFEt = FCFt − (1 − τc)·Interestt + (Dt − Dt−1)
Interestt = rD·Dt−1
Three terms, three ideas. FCFt is the whole pot. Interest is subtracted after tax, because the interest payment costs the firm rD·D but saves it τc·rD·D in tax, so the net cash leaving is (1 − τc)·rD·D. The last term is net borrowing: money the lenders hand over is cash available to shareholders, and money repaid to lenders is cash taken away from them. It is positive when debt rises and negative when debt falls.
Why does the tax shield need no separate treatment in FTE? Because it is already in the cash flow. FCF was computed by taxing full EBIT, as if there were no interest deduction. Subtracting only (1 − τc)·Interest instead of the full Interest hands the deduction straight back to shareholders, year by year, as extra cash. That is why the FTE method needs no PV(TS) term and no adjusted discount rate: the shield arrives as cash and is discounted at rE along with everything else. It also explains why FTE gives equity value directly. The shareholders are the ones who actually receive the shield.
Worked example 1: flow to equity on a firm that never changes

Ålesund Kysttransport produces 60 of free cash flow every year in perpetuity with no growth. It holds a permanent target of D/V = 0.30 and borrows at rD = 6.0000%. rU = 10.0000% and τc = 25%. MNOK.

Step 1 — the rates. D/E = 0.30/0.70 = 0.428571.
rE = 0.1000 + 0.428571(0.1000 − 0.0600) = 0.100000 + 0.017143 = 0.117143 = 11.7143%
rwacc = 0.7000(0.117143) + 0.3000(0.0600)(0.75) = 0.0820000 + 0.0135000 = 0.0955000 = 9.5500%
Pre-tax WACC: 0.7000(0.117143) + 0.3000(0.0600) = 0.082000 + 0.018000 = 0.100000 = rU ✓

Step 2 — value by WACC, so that the debt level is known. VL = 60/0.0955 = 628.2723, so D = 0.30 × 628.2723 = 188.4817 and E = 439.7906.

Step 3 — the flow to equity. Interest = 0.0600 × 188.4817 = 11.3089, after tax 0.75 × 11.3089 = 8.4817. Nothing grows, so debt never changes and net borrowing is 0.00.
FCFE = 60.0000 − 8.4817 + 0.0000 = 51.5183

Step 4 — discount at rE. It is a level perpetuity: E0 = 51.518325/0.117142857 = 439.7906, and VL = 439.7906 + 188.4817 = 628.2723 ✓

Check (consistency check 1): by APV, VU = 60/0.1000 = 600.0000; the annual shield is TS = 0.25 × 0.0600 × 188.4817 = 2.8272, and under a constant target ratio it is discounted at rU: PV(TS) = 2.8272/0.1000 = 28.2723. Total 600.0000 + 28.2723 = 628.2723 ✓ Three routes, one number. Two warnings about what this check does and does not prove. The FTE route used D = 188.4817, which came from the WACC answer, so FTE cannot catch an error in rwacc; it catches errors in the FCFE construction, which is what it is for. And the APV route is the genuinely independent one, because it never uses rE or rwacc at all.

19.3 The circularity of Dt = d·VLt, and how to break it

Worked example 1 hid a problem by being a perpetuity. In a multi-period valuation the target-ratio policy says:

Dt = d · VLt
Not on the formula sheet, and it is the source of the circularity. To know the debt at date t you need the levered value at date t. To get the levered value by APV you need the tax shields. To get the tax shields you need the debt. Round in a circle.

There are two ways out, and only one of them is fast enough for a three-hour exam.

RouteHow it worksVerdict
IterateGuess a value, derive the debt, value the shields, get a new value, repeat until it settlesCorrect but hopeless under time pressure. Never do this on the paper
Value first by WACC, then read off the debtBecause D/V is constant, rwacc is constant and known before any debt level is. Roll VLt back from the last period, then set Dt = d·VLt at every dateThe exam's intended route, and the one the nine-step structure of H2025 Exercise 4 follows
Why does the WACC method escape the circle when APV does not? Because rwacc depends on the debt ratio, not the debt level. The ratio is given to you in the question: it is d. So you can build rE and rwacc from d, rU and rD before you know a single krone of anything. APV needs the level, in kroner, of debt outstanding at every date, and that is not available until the values are. This is the practical reason the exam always asks for the WACC valuation first and the APV valuation second, and it is why you should always compute the whole path VL0, VL1, … even if only VL0 is asked for in that sub-question.
VLt−1 = (FCFt + VLt)/(1 + rwacc), starting from VLT = terminal value (or 0 if the firm ends)
The backward recursion from k17 § 17.4, now doing real work. Run it once and you have every input the debt schedule, the tax shields and the flow to equity will need.

19.4 The debt adjustment between periods

Once the value path is known, the debt path follows, and so does the cash that moves between the firm and its lenders each year:

ΔDt = Dt − Dt−1 = d·(VLt − VLt−1)
Positive means the firm borrows more, and that cash is available to shareholders. Negative means the firm repays, and shareholders fund the repayment. Not on the formula sheet. The second equality is the useful form: the debt adjustment is d times the change in levered value, so the sign of the adjustment is the sign of the change in value.
When VL falls, the firm must repay debt, and that is a cash flow This is the step the brief for H2025 Exercise 4(f) is built around, and it is missed for two different reasons. Some candidates leave debt at its opening level for the whole horizon, which quietly abandons the target ratio the question stated. Others do compute D1 correctly but never put the repayment into the flow to equity, treating the debt schedule as bookkeeping rather than as cash. Both are wrong, and both are wrong in the same direction: they overstate the cash reaching shareholders and so overstate equity value. Nearly every firm valued over a finite horizon has a falling VL in the later years, simply because there are fewer cash flows left in front of it, so this is the normal case and not an exotic one.
Worked example 2: two periods, a falling levered value, and the repayment nobody makes

Lofoten Seafood will generate free cash flow of 100 at date 1 and 120 at date 2, after which the licence expires and the firm is wound up with nothing left over. It holds a target of D/V = 0.40 throughout, borrows at rD = 5.0000%, rU = 10.0000%, τc = 25%. MNOK.

Step 1 — the rates, from the ratio alone. D/E = 0.40/0.60 = 0.666667.
rE = 0.1000 + 0.666667(0.1000 − 0.0500) = 0.100000 + 0.033333 = 0.133333 = 13.3333%
rwacc = 0.6000(0.133333) + 0.4000(0.0500)(0.75) = 0.0800000 + 0.0150000 = 0.0950000 = 9.5000%
Pre-tax: 0.6000(0.133333) + 0.4000(0.0500) = 0.080000 + 0.020000 = 0.100000 = rU ✓

Step 2 — roll the value back. The firm ends at date 2, so VL2 = 0.0000.
VL1 = 120/1.0950 = 109.5890
VL0 = (100 + 109.5890)/1.0950 = 209.5890/1.0950 = 191.4055
Verify by direct discounting: 100/1.0950 + 120/1.0950² = 91.3242 + 100.0813 = 191.4055 ✓

Step 3 — the debt schedule the policy forces. Levered value falls at every date, so debt must fall with it.

Date t012
VLt191.4055109.58900.0000
Dt = 0.40 × VLt76.562243.83560.0000
ΔDt = Dt − Dt−1—−32.7266−43.8356

Step 4 — the flow to equity. Interest is charged on the opening balance.

Year tFCFtInterest = 5.0000% × Dt−1−(1 − τc) × Interest+ ΔDtFCFEt
1100.00003.8281−2.8711−32.726664.4023
2120.00002.1918−1.6438−43.835674.5205

Row 2 deserves a note on rounding. In full precision it is 120 − 1.643836 − 43.835616 = 74.520548, so FCFE2 = 74.5205. Subtract the already-rounded 1.6438 and 43.8356 instead and you get 74.5206. Keep the digits in the calculator and round only when you write; over a nine-part exercise that discipline is the difference between agreeing with the key and drifting away from it.

Step 5 — discount at rE.
E0 = 64.4023/1.133333 + 74.5205/1.133333² = 56.8256 + 58.0177 = 114.8433
VL0 = 114.8433 + 76.5622 = 191.4055 ✓ the WACC answer from Step 2.
Direct cross-check on the equity: VL0 − D0 = 191.4055 − 76.5622 = 114.8433 ✓

Step 6 — what the missing repayment would have cost you. Suppose you had left the debt at 76.5622 through year 1 and repaid it all at date 2, which is what "the firm has 76.5622 of debt" sounds like if you stop reading at the ratio. Then FCFE1 = 100 − 2.8711 + 0 = 97.1289 and FCFE2 = 120 − 0.75(0.0500 × 76.5622) − 76.5622 = 120 − 2.8711 − 76.5622 = 40.5667. Discounting gives E0 = 117.2851, overstating equity by 2.4418, which is 2.13% of the correct equity value, and it does so on an exercise where the same debt schedule feeds three later sub-questions.

Check (consistency check 1): value the same firm by APV, which never uses rE or rwacc.
VU = 100/1.10 + 120/1.10² = 90.90909 + 99.17355 = 190.0826
TS1 = 0.25 × 0.0500 × 76.5622 = 0.957028 and TS2 = 0.25 × 0.0500 × 43.8356 = 0.547945; the ratio is a target, so both are discounted at rU:
PV(TS) = 0.957028/1.10 + 0.547945/1.10² = 0.870025 + 0.452847 = 1.3229
VL0 = 190.0826 + 1.3229 = 191.4055 ✓ All three methods land on 191.4055. Note how small the shield is here, 1.3229 on a firm worth 191.4055, because the debt is modest and lives for only two years. A candidate who wrote τc·D0 = 19.1406 would be out by a factor of 14.

19.5 Both directions in one schedule

Debt adjustments are not always repayments. When a firm's value rises from one date to the next, holding the ratio requires it to borrow more, and that extra borrowing is cash the shareholders receive. A firm with back-loaded cash flows does both, in that order.

Worked example 3: value rises, then falls, and the debt follows both ways

Same policy and rates as Worked example 2: d = 0.40, rU = 10.0000%, rD = 5.0000%, τc = 25%, so rE = 13.3333% and rwacc = 9.5000%. Vestland Offshore has a development phase followed by a single large payoff: free cash flow of 10, 40 and 160 at dates 1, 2 and 3, and then nothing. MNOK.

Step 1 — the value path. VL3 = 0.0000.
VL2 = 160/1.0950 = 146.1187
VL1 = (40 + 146.1187)/1.0950 = 186.1187/1.0950 = 169.9714
VL0 = (10 + 169.9714)/1.0950 = 179.9714/1.0950 = 164.3575
Value rises from date 0 to date 1, because the big cash flow at date 3 comes one year closer while only 10 is paid out, and then falls once the payoff is behind the firm.

Date t0123
VLt164.3575169.9714146.11870.0000
Dt = 0.40 × VLt65.743067.988658.44750.0000
ΔDt—+2.2456−9.5411−58.4475
Interest = 5.0000% × Dt−1—3.28713.39942.9224
(1 − τc) × Interest—2.46542.54962.1918
FCFt—10.000040.0000160.0000
FCFEt—9.780227.909399.3607

Step 2 — read the three FCFE rows aloud. Year 1: 10.0000 − 2.4654 + 2.2456 = 9.7802. The firm borrows an extra 2.2456 because it is worth more, and shareholders keep it. Year 2: 40.0000 − 2.5496 − 9.5411 = 27.9093. Now value has fallen, so 9.5411 of debt has to be retired. Year 3: 160.0000 − 2.1918 − 58.4475 = 99.3607. The firm winds up, so the whole remaining balance is repaid.

Step 3 — the equity value.
E0 = 9.7802/1.133333 + 27.9093/1.133333² + 99.3607/1.133333³ = 8.6296 + 21.7287 + 68.2562 = 98.6145
Cross-check: VL0 − D0 = 164.3575 − 65.7430 = 98.6145 ✓

Check (consistency check 1): by APV. VU = 10/1.10 + 40/1.10² + 160/1.10³ = 9.09091 + 33.05785 + 120.21037 = 162.35913.
The shields are TS1 = 0.25 × 0.0500 × 65.7430 = 0.821787, TS2 = 0.25 × 0.0500 × 67.9886 = 0.849857, TS3 = 0.25 × 0.0500 × 58.4475 = 0.730594, all discounted at rU:
PV(TS) = 0.747079 + 0.702361 + 0.548906 = 1.99835
VL0 = 162.35913 + 1.99835 = 164.3575 ✓ matching the WACC value from Step 1 and the FTE value plus debt, 98.6145 + 65.7430 = 164.3575 ✓ Note that TS2 is the largest of the three, because the shield in year 2 is charged on the opening balance D1 = 67.9886, the peak of the debt path. If your largest shield sits in the wrong year, you have used Dt where you needed Dt−1.

Four places the debt schedule goes wrong (1) Using Dt instead of Dt−1 for interest. Interest in year t is charged on the balance the firm carried into year t. (2) Forgetting the final repayment. If the firm ends at date T then VLT = 0, so DT = 0 and the whole balance DT−1 is repaid in the last year. That is usually the largest single number in the FCFE column. (3) Applying d to the wrong value. Dt = d·VLt, the levered value, never VU. (4) Subtracting interest before tax. Only (1 − τc) of it leaves the firm, and forgetting the (1 − τc) understates equity and quietly deletes the tax shield from the FTE method.

19.6 Choosing the method, and what to write

All three methods are correct when their assumptions hold, so the choice is about speed and about what the question is really asking.

The question…UseReason
gives a target ratio and asks for firm valueWACCOne rate, no debt schedule needed, fastest by far
gives a loan with a repayment scheduleAPVThere is no constant rwacc to use (k18 § 18.4)
asks what the debt policy is worthAPVPV(TS) is the answer as a number
asks for the cash reaching shareholders, or for equity value directlyFTEIt is the only method whose output is equity, and the only one that shows the repayments as cash
asks for a debt schedule, or names H2025-style sub-stepsWACC first, then the schedule, then APV and FTEThe value path is the input every other step needs
How to lay out a nine-part valuation so a slip costs one mark, not nine Build one table with a column per date and rows for FCFt, VLt, Dt, ΔDt, Interestt, TSt and FCFEt. Fill VL from the right, D from VL, and everything else from D. Then answer the sub-questions by pointing at cells. H2025 Exercise 4 runs nine chained sub-questions over two periods and demands four decimal places; the candidates who lose the exercise are the ones who recompute the value path from scratch inside each sub-answer and get a slightly different number each time. Write the table once. Carry full precision, round when you write, and state the debt policy in one sentence at the top so that the grader knows the rest of the page is deliberate.
Why does FTE give exactly the same answer as WACC when the two look so different? Because the two methods are the same equation rearranged. At every date the firm's value is split as VLt = Et + Dt. Discounting FCF at rwacc is a statement about the whole right-hand side; discounting FCFE at rE is the same statement about the first term only, once you have taken out what the lenders receive and put back what they lend. Nothing is added or removed anywhere, so the totals must agree. The practical value of running both is that they fail in different ways: a mistake in the debt schedule shows up loudly in FTE and can hide entirely inside a WACC calculation, since a single rwacc never asks you what the debt level actually was.
Where this goes next k20 puts the whole apparatus together on one case: free cash flow built from projections as in k2, a comparable unlevered and relevered as in k4, the WACC valuation of k17, the APV valuation of k18, the debt schedule and adjustments of this chapter, and the last step neither of them mentions, converting an enterprise value into a share price by adding excess cash and subtracting debt. It is structured as the nine steps of H2025 Exercise 4.
↑ contents

20 · A full valuation, end to end

One case, worked completely, in the shape the exam sets it. H2025 Exercise 4 runs a WACC-and-APV valuation over two periods in nine chained sub-questions and asks for four decimal places; H2024 Exercise 5 does the same in fewer steps, starting from a comparable firm. Every ingredient is already in your hands: free cash flow from k2, the twin-firm ladder from k4, the WACC method from k17, the APV method from k18, the debt schedule and the adjustments from k19. What this chapter adds is the assembly, the order, and the two ends the earlier chapters leave open, namely how the terminal value is normalised at the front and how an enterprise value becomes a price per share at the back. Do this case once with the answers covered and you will have sat H2025 Exercise 4 before you sit it.

20.1 The case, and the nine questions

Nordvik Marine AS is an unlisted supplier of vessel systems. You are valuing the whole company. All figures are in NOK million, and year 0 is the year just ended.

InputValue
Sales, year 0 (actual), then years 1, 2, 3 (forecast)700, 800, 900, 960
Operating costs excluding depreciation, as a share of sales65%
Depreciation, years 1 to 380 each year
Capital expenditure, years 1 to 390, 90, 96
Net working capital, all years12% of that year's sales
Growth of every operating item after year 32.0000% forever
Corporate tax rate τc22%
Risk-free rate rf and market risk premium3.0000% and 5.0000%
Comparable, Saltstraum ASA: equity / debt, market values900 / 600
Saltstraum rE and rD11.5000% and 4.0000%
Nordvik's target debt-to-value ratio d, maintained by rebalancing0.30
Nordvik's expected return on debt rD4.0000%
Excess cash held outside operations120
Shares outstanding (millions)30

The whole exercise is one chain, and it is worth writing the chain down before you start computing:

projections → FCFt  ·  comparable → βU → rU → rE → rwacc
FCFt and rwacc → VLt → Dt = d·VLt → TSt → VU + PV(TS) → E0 → price
Two independent inputs at the front, one number at the back, and every arrow a place an error travels. The order matters: the value path has to exist before the debt schedule can, and the debt schedule has to exist before the APV valuation can. This is why the exam asks for the WACC valuation first.

The nine sub-questions, in the order a Kurbatov paper would put them:

StepQuestionWhat it needs
(a)Compute free cash flow for years 1 to 3, and the normalised flow for year 4k2
(b)Find the unlevered beta and the unlevered cost of capital of the businessk4
(c)Find Nordvik's cost of equity at its target leveragek4
(d)Compute the pre-tax and after-tax WACCk4, k17
(e)Value the firm by the WACC method, and give VL at every datek17
(f)Give the debt outstanding at every date and the adjustment between periodsk19
(g)Value the firm by the APV methodk18
(h)Show that the two agree, and run the other checks availablek0 § 0.5
(i)Give the value of equity and the value per sharethis chapter

20.2 Steps (a) and (b): free cash flow, and the business unlevered

Worked example, part 1: build the cash flows and strip the comparable's financing off

Step (a) — free cash flow. Lay it out vertically, one labelled line per term, and put the working-capital level in a row of its own before differencing it.

LineYear 0Year 1Year 2Year 3
Sales700.00800.00900.00960.00
Operating costs (65% of sales)—−520.00−585.00−624.00
Depreciation—−80.00−80.00−80.00
EBIT—200.00235.00256.00
EBIT(1 − τc), τc = 22%—156.0000183.3000199.6800
+ Depreciation—+80.00+80.00+80.00
− CapEx—−90.00−90.00−96.00
NWC level (12% of sales)84.0096.00108.00115.20
− ΔNWC—−12.00−12.00−7.20
Free cash flow—134.0000161.3000176.4800

Year 1 in full: 200.00 × 0.78 = 156.0000, then 156.0000 + 80.00 − 90.00 − 12.00 = 134.0000. Year 2: 235.00 × 0.78 = 183.3000, then 183.3000 + 80.00 − 90.00 − 12.00 = 161.3000. Year 3: 256.00 × 0.78 = 199.6800, then 199.6800 + 80.00 − 96.00 − 7.20 = 176.4800. The working-capital check from k2: 12.00 + 12.00 + 7.20 = 31.20, and NWC3 − NWC0 = 115.20 − 84.00 = 31.20 ✓

The normalised year 4. After year 3 every operating item grows at 2.0000%, so build year 4 from its components rather than growing FCF3:
EBIT4 = 256.00 × 1.02 = 261.1200, so EBIT4(1 − τc) = 203.6736
Depreciation4 = 80.00 × 1.02 = 81.6000; CapEx4 = 96.00 × 1.02 = 97.9200
Sales4 = 960.00 × 1.02 = 979.2000, so ΔNWC4 = 0.12 × (979.2000 − 960.00) = 0.12 × 19.2000 = 2.3040
FCF4 = 203.6736 + 81.6000 − 97.9200 − 2.3040 = 185.0496

Growing FCF3 instead would have given 176.4800 × 1.02 = 180.0096, understating the perpetual flow by 5.0400. The reason is that year 3 was not a steady-state year: sales grew 6.67% that year, so it absorbed 7.20 of working capital where the steady state needs only 2.3040, and it also carried a step-up in CapEx. Normalise the terminal year, always.

Step (b) — unlever Saltstraum. Weights on market values: E/V = 900/1 500 = 0.600000, D/V = 600/1 500 = 0.400000.
βE = (0.1150 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 1.70 and βD = (0.0400 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.20
βU = 0.600000(1.70) + 0.400000(0.20) = 1.0200 + 0.0800 = 1.10
rU = 0.0300 + 1.10(0.0500) = 0.0850 = 8.5000%
Cross-check on Saltstraum's own rates: 0.600000(0.1150) + 0.400000(0.0400) = 0.069000 + 0.016000 = 0.085000 ✓ Same rU from rates as from betas.

The comparable's leverage is not your leverage Saltstraum runs at D/V = 0.400000 and Nordvik will run at 0.30. The only quantities that cross from one firm to the other are βU and rU, because they describe the business. Every candidate who computes a WACC using the comparable's weights has skipped step (c) entirely and will fail consistency check 1 in step (h). Write the two D/V ratios next to each other on your paper so you cannot confuse them.

20.3 Steps (c), (d) and (e): relever, build the rate, value the firm

Worked example, part 2: from rU to a levered value at every date

Step (c) — relever to Nordvik's target. d = 0.30 means E/V = 0.70 and D/E = 0.30/0.70 = 0.428571. Nordvik's own debt beta is βD = (0.0400 − 0.0300)/0.0500 = 0.20.
βE = 1.10 + 0.428571(1.10 − 0.20) = 1.100000 + 0.385714 = 1.485714
rE = 0.0300 + 1.485714(0.0500) = 0.030000 + 0.074286 = 0.104286 = 10.4286%
MM Proposition II as the cross-check: 0.0850 + 0.428571(0.0850 − 0.0400) = 0.085000 + 0.019286 = 0.104286 ✓

Step (d) — the two WACCs.
Pre-tax: 0.70(0.104286) + 0.30(0.0400) = 0.073000 + 0.012000 = 0.085000 = 8.5000% = rU ✓ under this course's rebalancing convention.
After-tax: rwacc = 0.70(0.104286) + 0.30(0.0400)(1 − 0.22) = 0.0730000 + 0.0093600 = 0.0823600 = 8.2360%
The gap between them is (D/V)·rD·τc = 0.30 × 0.0400 × 0.22 = 0.002640, which is the whole tax shield expressed as a rate cut of 0.2640 percentage points.

Step (e) — value by WACC. The terminal value at date 3 capitalises the normalised year-4 flow:
TV3 = 185.0496/(0.082360 − 0.020000) = 185.0496/0.062360 = 2 967.4407
Now roll back one year at a time, starting from VL3 = TV3:
VL2 = (176.4800 + 2 967.4407)/1.082360 = 3 143.9207/1.082360 = 2 904.6904
VL1 = (161.3000 + 2 904.6904)/1.082360 = 3 065.9904/1.082360 = 2 832.6900
VL0 = (134.0000 + 2 832.6900)/1.082360 = 2 966.6900/1.082360 = 2 740.9457

Verify along the one-line route. 134.0000/1.082360 = 123.80354; 161.3000/1.082360² = 137.68635; 176.4800/1.082360³ = 139.18111; 2 967.4407/1.082360³ = 2 340.27473.
Total = 123.80354 + 137.68635 + 139.18111 + 2 340.27473 = 2 740.94573 ✓ the same 2 740.9457.

Where the marks sit in steps (a) to (e) In H2024 Exercise 5 and H2025 Exercise 4 each of these is a separately numbered sub-question worth two to four points, and they are marked on the working, not on the final figure. Three habits pay. Write the formula name before the numbers ("unlevering the comparable", "MM II", "after-tax WACC"). Give rates to four decimals as percentages, because H2025 asked for four decimal places explicitly. And write the pre-tax WACC line even though nobody asked for it: it costs one line, it is a stated check in the keys, and it catches a relevering slip before that slip contaminates steps (e) to (i).

20.4 Step (f): the debt schedule and the adjustment between periods

Three lines carry the whole of step (f), and none of them is on the exam formula sheet:

Dt = d · VLt  ·  ΔDt = Dt − Dt−1 = d(VLt − VLt−1)  ·  TSt = τc·rD·Dt−1
Read them left to right. The target ratio fixes the level, differencing the level gives the cash that moves between the firm and its lenders, and the opening level generates the year's interest and therefore the year's shield. The subscript on the last term is the one candidates get wrong.
Worked example, part 3: what the target ratio forces the firm to borrow

The policy is Dt = 0.30 × VLt at every date, so the value path from step (e) hands you the debt path directly. Interest in year t is charged on the balance carried into year t, which is Dt−1.

Date t0123
VLt2 740.94572 832.69002 904.69042 967.4407
Dt = 0.30 × VLt822.2837849.8070871.4071890.2322
ΔDt = Dt − Dt−1—+27.5233+21.6001+18.8251
Interestt = 4.0000% × Dt−1—32.891333.992334.8563
(1 − τc) × Interestt—25.655326.514027.1879
TSt = 0.22 × Interestt—7.23617.47837.6684

Reading the row of adjustments. Nordvik's levered value rises every year, because the business grows, so holding leverage at 30% means borrowing more each year: 27.5233 at date 1, 21.6001 at date 2, 18.8251 at date 3. That cash is available to shareholders and enters the flow to equity with a plus sign. The shield for year 4 and beyond starts from D3 = 890.2322: TS4 = 0.22 × 0.0400 × 890.2322 = 7.83404, growing at 2.0000% forever alongside the firm.

The variation the exam actually likes. Suppose the same three years of cash flow are all there is, because a licence expires at date 3 and the terminal value is zero. Then VL3 = 0 and everything reverses:

Date t0123
VLt, no terminal value400.6710299.6703163.05110.0000
Dt = 0.30 × VLt120.201389.901148.91530.0000
ΔDt—−30.3002−40.9857−48.9153

VL2 = 176.4800/1.082360 = 163.0511, VL1 = (161.3000 + 163.0511)/1.082360 = 299.6703, VL0 = (134.0000 + 299.6703)/1.082360 = 400.6710, which is exactly the sum of the three discounted operating flows from step (e): 123.80354 + 137.68635 + 139.18111 = 400.67100 ✓ Now every adjustment is negative. The firm must repay 30.3002, then 40.9857, then the remaining 48.9153, and each repayment is cash that shareholders fund. This is H2025 Exercise 4(f), and it is where the exercise is won or lost.

Why does the sign of ΔD follow the sign of the change in value, always? Because ΔDt = d·(VLt − VLt−1) and d is positive. There is nothing more to it, and that is the point: you never have to reason about whether the firm "wants" to borrow. The target ratio removes the choice. A growing firm borrows, a shrinking firm repays, and a firm with a finite life repays everything by the end because its levered value at the terminal date is zero. When a valuation runs off a terminal value instead, the debt grows forever at g, which is exactly why the tax shield beyond the horizon is itself a growing perpetuity.
Two off-by-one errors in this table Interest is charged on Dt−1, not Dt. In the table above, year 1's interest is 4.0000% of 822.2837, the opening balance, not of 849.8070. Using the closing balance overstates every shield and every interest deduction, and the error grows with the firm. The terminal shield starts from D3. TS4 = τc·rD·D3, because D3 is the balance carried into year 4. Take it from D2 and the continuing value of the shield is understated by a full year of growth.

20.5 Steps (g) and (h): APV, and the checks the examiner runs

The APV valuation has two halves and a terminal piece in each of them:

VU = Σt=1..3 FCFt/(1 + rU)t + [FCF4/(rU − g)]/(1 + rU)3
PV(TS) = Σt=1..3 TSt/(1 + rU)t + [TS4/(rU − g)]/(1 + rU)3
Both terminal pieces sit at date 3 and are therefore discounted over three periods, not four. Both use rU in the denominator, the first because the unlevered firm always does, the second because Nordvik rebalances to a constant D/V and the shield therefore carries asset risk (k18 § 18.2). Under fixed debt the second line would use rD and the answer would be much larger.
Worked example, part 4: value the same firm the other way

Step (g), first half — the unlevered firm. Discount the same free cash flows at rU = 8.5000%, and capitalise the normalised year-4 flow at rU − g:
TVU3 = 185.0496/(0.085000 − 0.020000) = 185.0496/0.065000 = 2 846.9169
134.0000/1.085 = 123.50230; 161.3000/1.085² = 137.01714; 176.4800/1.085³ = 138.16762; 2 846.9169/1.085³ = 2 228.87432
VU = 123.50230 + 137.01714 + 138.16762 + 2 228.87432 = 2 627.56138

Step (g), second half — the tax shield. Nordvik rebalances to a constant D/V, so future debt moves with firm value and the shield carries asset risk. Discount it at rU. The three explicit shields come from the table in step (f), and the shields from year 4 onward are a growing perpetuity valued at date 3:
PV3(shields from year 4) = 7.83404/(0.085000 − 0.020000) = 7.83404/0.065000 = 120.5237
7.2361/1.085 = 6.66921; 7.4783/1.085² = 6.35248; 7.6684/1.085³ = 6.00364; 120.5237/1.085³ = 94.35902
PV(TS) = 6.66921 + 6.35248 + 6.00364 + 94.35902 = 113.38435

Step (g), the answer. VL0 = VU + PV(TS) = 2 627.56138 + 113.38435 = 2 740.94573

Step (h) — the checks, in the order worth running them.

Consistency check 1, value by WACC = value by APV. 2 740.9457 from step (e) against 2 740.9457 from step (g) ✓ This is the check that matters and it is a real one: the APV route used rU, the debt levels in kroner and the choice of discount rate for the shield, none of which the WACC route touched. Discount the shield at rD = 4.0000% instead and the continuing piece alone becomes 7.83404/(0.0400 − 0.0200) = 391.70, PV(TS) rises to 368.9090, and the APV total of 2 996.4703 misses the WACC total by 255.5246. Be clear about the one thing this check cannot catch: βU feeds rU, which feeds rE, which feeds rwacc, so a wrong βU moves both routes together and they still agree.

Consistency check 2, weighted betas return βU. 0.70(1.485714) + 0.30(0.20) = 1.040000 + 0.060000 = 1.100000 = βU ✓ Say plainly what this proves. βE was produced by relevering βU in step (c), so reversing the operation must return βU: as run here the check is an identity and cannot fail. It is still worth thirty seconds, because it does catch a slip in the weights or the use of the (1 − τc) version of the unlevering formula that this course rejects. What it cannot catch is a wrong input, exactly as k4 warns.

The check that is not on the numbered list but is worth running here. Value the equity by flow to equity and see whether it agrees with VL0 − D0. FCFEt = FCFt − (1 − τc)·Interestt + ΔDt:
FCFE1 = 134.0000 − 25.6553 + 27.5233 = 135.8680
FCFE2 = 161.3000 − 26.5140 + 21.6001 = 156.3861
FCFE3 = 176.4800 − 27.1879 + 18.8251 = 168.1172
The equity continuing at date 3 is E3 = VL3 − D3 = 2 967.4407 − 890.2322 = 2 077.2085. Discount everything at rE = 10.4286%:
135.8680/1.104286 = 123.0370; 156.3861/1.104286² = 128.2435; 168.1172/1.104286³ = 124.8440; 2 077.2085/1.104286³ = 1 542.5375
E0 = 123.0370 + 128.2435 + 124.8440 + 1 542.5375 = 1 918.6620, and VL0 − D0 = 2 740.9457 − 822.2837 = 1 918.6620 ✓

Check: three methods, one number. By WACC 2 740.9457, by APV 2 740.9457, by FTE 1 918.6620 + 822.2837 = 2 740.9457. Consistency check 1 passes, and the FTE route confirms in addition that the debt schedule in step (f) is internally consistent, which neither of the other two would have revealed on its own. Note the scale of the financing gain while you are here: PV(TS) of 113.3844 is 4.14% of firm value, which is what a 30% target ratio at a 4.0000% borrowing rate and a 22% tax rate is worth. Anything an order of magnitude larger means you have used τc·D on a rebalancing firm.

A check that passes is not a proof that the answer is right Steps (e) and (g) agree to four decimals, and that agreement is the strongest evidence available on the paper. It is still not proof. Suppose you had misread Saltstraum's equity as 600 and its debt as 900. Then βU = 0.40(1.70) + 0.60(0.20) = 0.80, rU = 7.0000%, rE would follow, rwacc would follow, both valuations would be built on the same wrong rU, and consistency check 1 would pass perfectly on a badly wrong firm value. The checks verify that your algebra hangs together. They cannot verify that you read the question correctly. Reread the inputs once before step (e), because after step (e) every number on the page depends on them.

20.6 Step (i): from enterprise value to a price per share

The valuation so far has produced an enterprise value: the value of the operating business, financed by debt and equity together. The last step converts it into what a shareholder owns:

E0 = VL0 + Excess cash − D0   ·   Price per share = E0 / N
Not on the formula sheet. Excess cash is cash the operations do not need, so it never appeared in free cash flow and never entered the discounting; it is a separate asset and is simply added at the end. Cash required for day-to-day operations is different: it is already inside net working capital and must not be added again. N is the number of shares outstanding.
Worked example, part 5: the last line of the answer

Nordvik holds 120 of excess cash and has 30 million shares outstanding.

Step (i). E0 = 2 740.9457 + 120.0000 − 822.2837 = 2 038.6620
Price per share = 2 038.6620/30 = 67.9554

Check: the flow-to-equity route in step (h) gave the equity in the operating business as 1 918.6620, and 1 918.6620 + 120.0000 = 2 038.6620 ✓ The two routes to equity agree, which is the only thing left to verify once consistency check 1 has passed. One more sanity line worth writing: D0/VL0 = 822.2837/2 740.9457 = 0.300000, the target ratio you were given ✓ That confirms that the debt level used in step (g) really is the one implied by the policy in the question, and it is the fastest way to catch a firm value that was computed at one leverage and then levered at another.

Why is excess cash added at the end instead of discounted? Because it is already worth its face value today. Discounting is how you convert a future, risky amount into a present one, and cash in the bank is neither future nor risky: 120 of cash is worth 120. It also never appeared in free cash flow, since FCF measures what the operations generate and idle cash generates nothing operational. The distinction that matters on the paper is between excess cash and operating cash. Operating cash is part of net working capital, so it has already been paid for inside the ΔNWC line and adding it again double counts. If the question says "cash required for operations", it is inside NWC; if it says "excess cash" or "marketable securities", it goes on at the end.
Three ways step (i) is lost after a perfect valuation (1) Forgetting excess cash. It is stated separately in the question precisely because it is not operating value, and the marks for it are free. (2) Subtracting the wrong debt. Under a target-ratio valuation the debt to subtract is D0 = d·VL0, the level consistent with the policy you valued on, not a book figure from a balance sheet. If the question also gives a current book debt that conflicts with the target, say which one you are using and why in one sentence. (3) Reporting an enterprise value when a share price was asked for. Read the final sub-question again before you write the last line.
The answer template, and the time budget An 18 to 20 point exercise of this shape gets roughly 30 minutes on the 1.6 minutes per point rule from k0. Spend the first three building one table with a column per date and rows for FCF, VL, D, ΔD, interest, TS and FCFE, then answer the sub-questions by pointing at cells rather than recomputing. State the debt policy in one sentence at the top. Give rates as percentages to four decimals and money to four decimals, since H2025 asked for four decimal places. Close with two lines: consistency check 1, and pre-tax WACC = rU. If a number goes missing midway, carry it forward as a symbol, say which assumption you are making, and keep going; the later sub-questions still earn their marks on a correct method.
Why is this one exercise worth more preparation than any other? Because it is the only place on the paper where six chapters are examined at once, and because the sub-questions are chained. Free cash flow feeds the valuation, the comparable feeds the discount rate, the discount rate feeds the value path, the value path feeds the debt schedule, the debt schedule feeds the shield and the flow to equity, and the last sub-question needs all of it. One arithmetic slip in step (b) travels through seven answers. That is also the opportunity: run the checks in step (h) before you write the final line and you catch your own slip while there is still time to fix it, which is worth more marks than any single formula in this part of the course.
Where to go from here k28 turns this case into a reusable answer template alongside the recapitalisation, the real option, the state-by-state agency problem and the M&A swap, and gives the time budget for the full six-exercise paper. k29 § 29.5 holds every formula used above on one page, in the order you need them. If a step above felt shaky, the source chapters are k2 for free cash flow, k4 for the unlever and relever ladder, k17 for the WACC method, k18 for the tax shield's discount rate, and k19 for the debt adjustment.

Part V — Options

↑ contents

21 · Option basics

Everything in Part V rests on this chapter. The binomial model in chapter 22, Black-Scholes in chapter 23, equity-as-a-call in chapter 24 and the real options in chapter 25 all assume you can write down an option's payoff without hesitating, and that you know put-call parity cold. Parity has been tested directly in four of the eleven mapped papers, including 2023 MC9–10 and V2024, and it is not on the exam formula sheet: you must carry C = P + S − PV(K) in your head. The bounds in 21.4 are the other cheap points here, because they let you reject an impossible price in one line and because they are the reason an American call on a non-dividend-paying share is never exercised early.

21.1 What an option is, and the four positions

An option is a contract that gives its holder a right but no obligation. A call gives the right to buy one share at a fixed price; a put gives the right to sell one share at a fixed price. The fixed price is the strike K. The date the right expires is T. The price paid for the contract today is the premium, C for a call and P for a put. The party who sells the contract is the writer, and the writer has the mirror-image obligation: if the holder exercises, the writer must deliver.

A European option can only be exercised on the expiry date. An American option can be exercised any time up to it. Unless a question says otherwise, assume European, because every formula in this part is derived for European options and parity holds only for them.

At expiry the holder exercises whenever it pays and walks away otherwise, so the payoff is a maximum:

Call payoff at T = max(ST − K, 0)
Put payoff at T = max(K − ST, 0)
ST is the share price on the expiry date. The payoff is what the contract itself delivers. The profit subtracts what you paid for it: profit on a long call = max(ST − K, 0) − C. Both diagrams appear on exams and they are not the same picture, so read the wording before you draw.

Because there are two contracts and two sides of each, there are four elementary positions. Learn the table, because almost every structure in Part V is a combination of these four plus a bond.

PositionPayoff at TMaximum gainMaximum lossYou are betting that
Long call (buy)max(ST − K, 0)UnlimitedCthe share rises a lot
Short call (write)−max(ST − K, 0)CUnlimitedthe share does not rise much
Long put (buy)max(K − ST, 0)K − PPthe share falls
Short put (write)−max(K − ST, 0)PK − Pthe share does not fall much
Why the payoff is asymmetric, and why that is the whole subject The holder chooses whether to exercise after seeing ST. That single feature truncates the left tail: the worst outcome for a long call is that the right expires worthless, and no state of the world can make it worse. The upside is not truncated. So the holder owns the good states and has sold away nothing but the premium. Every later result in Part V, including the fact that option value rises with volatility (chapter 23), is a consequence of this one-sided max(·, 0) and nothing else. When an exam asks you to explain an option result verbally, start from the truncation.
Payoff diagram or profit diagram A payoff diagram starts at zero and never goes below it for a long position. A profit diagram is the same shape shifted down by the premium, so a long call's profit line sits at −C for all low ST. Candidates lose marks by drawing the payoff and labelling it "profit", or by forgetting that the premium is sunk and subtracting it a second time from the exercise decision. The exercise decision never looks at the premium. You exercise a call whenever ST > K, even if you still end up losing money overall.

21.2 Payoff and profit diagrams

Throughout this chapter one contract is priced on Nordlys ASA. The share trades at S0 = 190.00. The one-year risk-free rate is rf = 5.00%. A one-year European call with strike K = 210 trades at C = 13.60, and a one-year European put with the same strike and maturity trades at P = 23.60. Section 21.5 shows that those two premiums are exactly consistent with each other.

Worked example 1: payoff, profit and break-even, state by state

You buy one Nordlys call (K = 210) for 13.60 and one Nordlys put (K = 210) for 23.60. Tabulate both at expiry. The premium is paid today, the payoff arrives at T, so the simple profit figures below ignore the one year of interest on the premium; step 3 corrects that.

STCall payoffCall profitPut payoffPut profitWritten call profit
140.000.00−13.6070.0046.4013.60
170.000.00−13.6040.0016.4013.60
190.000.00−13.6020.00−3.6013.60
210.000.00−13.600.00−23.6013.60
223.6013.600.000.00−23.600.00
240.0030.0016.400.00−23.60−16.40
270.0060.0046.400.00−23.60−46.40

Step 1 — the call's break-even. Profit is zero when max(ST − 210, 0) = 13.60, so ST = 210 + 13.60 = 223.60. Below 210 the call expires worthless and the loss is the full premium, 13.60.

Step 2 — the put's break-even. Profit is zero when max(210 − ST, 0) = 23.60, so ST = 210 − 23.60 = 186.40. Note that at today's price of 190.00 the put would lose money: 20.00 − 23.60 = −3.60.

Step 3 — break-even with the premium financed. If you borrowed the premium at rf = 5.00%, you owe 13.60(1.05) = 14.28 at expiry, so the call's true break-even is 210 + 14.28 = 224.28. For the put you owe 23.60(1.05) = 24.78, so its break-even is 210 − 24.78 = 185.22. Use the simple version unless the question mentions financing, and say which one you used.

Check: at every share price, long call profit + written call profit = 0. Reading down the two columns: −13.60 + 13.60 = 0, and 46.40 + (−46.40) = 0. ✓ An option is a zero-sum contract between holder and writer, which is exactly why it can be priced by arbitrage in chapter 22.

21.3 Moneyness, intrinsic value and time value

Moneyness is a one-word description of where the share sits relative to the strike right now. A call is in the money (ITM) if S > K, at the money (ATM) if S = K, and out of the money (OTM) if S < K. For a put the inequalities flip: a put is in the money when S < K. In the money means the option would pay something if it expired this instant, not that it is a good trade.

That immediate payoff has a name, and it splits every premium into two pieces:

Option premium = intrinsic value + time value
Intrinsic value of a call = max(S − K, 0)  ·  of a put = max(K − S, 0)
Intrinsic value uses today's share price S, not ST, and the undiscounted strike K. Time value is defined as the remainder, so it is never negative for an American option and it shrinks to zero at expiry. It is the market's price for the chance that the share moves in your favour before T.

For Nordlys, with S = 190.00 and K = 210:

ContractMoneynessPremiumIntrinsic valueTime value
Call, K = 210out of the money13.600.0013.60
Put, K = 210in the money23.6020.003.60
Why an out-of-the-money option is worth anything at all The Nordlys call would pay nothing if it expired today, yet it costs 13.60. All of it is time value, and time value exists for the reason given in 21.1: the option holder keeps the good states and is protected in the bad ones. Over a year the share might reach 240, in which case the call pays 30. It might fall to 140, in which case the call pays nothing, exactly as it would have if the share had merely drifted to 200. The holder is indifferent between those two disasters, so only the upside states carry weight in the price. That is why time value is largest for options near the money with a long time left, and why it collapses for deep in-the-money and deep out-of-the-money contracts, where the outcome is close to settled either way.

21.4 The bounds: what an option cannot cost

Before any model, four inequalities follow from arbitrage alone. They are worth memorising because they let you reject a quoted price in a single line, and because the exam has used them as short questions.

max(S − PV(K), 0) ≤ C ≤ S
max(PV(K) − S, 0) ≤ P ≤ PV(K)
PV(K) = K/(1 + rf)T. Both are for European options on a share paying no dividends. The call's upper bound says a call can never be worth more than the share itself. The call's lower bound is above the intrinsic value max(S − K, 0), because the strike is only paid at T and money has a time value.

Each bound is one sentence of reasoning. C ≤ S: the call gives you the share only if you also hand over K, so it cannot be worth more than the share handed over for free. C ≥ S − PV(K): buy the call and lend PV(K); at T you have max(ST − K, 0) + K = max(ST, K), which is at least ST, so this package beats simply holding the share and must cost at least as much: C + PV(K) ≥ S. P ≤ PV(K): the most a put can ever pay is K, when the share is worthless. P ≥ PV(K) − S: the same argument in reverse.

Worked example 2: a quoted price that violates the lower bound

Nordlys also has a one-year European call with strike K = 168, quoted at 27.00. The share is at 190.00 and rf = 5.00%. Is that price possible?

Step 1 — the present value of the strike. PV(K) = 168/1.05 = 160.00

Step 2 — the lower bound. max(S − PV(K), 0) = max(190.00 − 160.00, 0) = 30.00. The quote of 27.00 is 3.00 below the floor, so it is impossible: there is an arbitrage.

Step 3 — build the arbitrage. The call is cheap relative to the share, so buy the call, sell the share short, and lend the present value of the strike.

TransactionCash flow todayCash flow at T
Buy the call−27.00max(ST − 168, 0)
Short one share+190.00−ST
Lend 160.00 at 5%−160.00+168.00
Net+3.00max(168 − ST, 0) ≥ 0

Step 4 — verify the T column in states. If ST = 120: 0 − 120 + 168 = +48.00. If ST = 168: 0 − 168 + 168 = 0.00. If ST = 250: 82 − 250 + 168 = 0.00. Never negative.

Check: you take in 3.00 today and can never have to pay anything later. That is money for nothing, so no equilibrium price can be 27.00. The minimum consistent quote is 30.00, and at exactly 30.00 the strategy nets 0.00 today, which is what a bound at equality should give. ✓

Why an American call on a non-dividend share is never exercised early Exercising early gets you S − K in cash. But the lower bound says the unexercised contract is already worth at least S − PV(K), and PV(K) < K whenever rf > 0, so S − PV(K) > S − K. You would be throwing away the difference, plus all the remaining time value. It is always better to sell the option than to exercise it early. Two consequences the exam likes: an American call on a share paying no dividends has exactly the same value as the European one, so Black-Scholes prices it correctly; and an American put has no such argument, because early exercise gets you K now rather than later, which is worth more, so an American put can be worth strictly more than a European put. Chapter 22 shows that difference numerically on a two-period tree.
Do not use intrinsic value as the floor Writing "a European call must be worth at least S − K" is the most common slip in this material, and it fails exactly in the cases the exam constructs. For Nordlys with K = 168 the intrinsic floor is 190 − 168 = 22.00, which would have accepted the arbitrage-ridden quote of 27.00 as perfectly fine. The correct floor is S − PV(K) = 30.00. Discount the strike before you compare.

21.5 Put-call parity

Parity is not a model. It is an accounting identity between two portfolios that happen to have identical payoffs in every state, so they must cost the same today or there is free money. Consider these two:

  • The protective put: buy one share and one put with strike K. Cost today: S + P. Payoff at T: ST + max(K − ST, 0) = max(ST, K).
  • The fiduciary call: buy one call with strike K and lend PV(K) risk-free. Cost today: C + PV(K). Payoff at T: max(ST − K, 0) + K = max(ST, K).

Both deliver max(ST, K) whatever happens: a floor of K with all the upside above it. Equal payoffs in every state means equal prices today, and rearranging gives the identity:

C + PV(K) = P + S
C = P + S − PV(K),   where PV(K) = K/(1 + rf)T
Not on the formula sheet — memorise it. It requires: European options, the same strike K, the same expiry T, the same underlying, and no dividends before T. S is today's share price and rf the risk-free rate for horizon T. Nothing about volatility, expected returns or probabilities enters, which is why parity holds no matter what pricing model is correct.
Why parity survives when every pricing model fails Black-Scholes needs a lognormal share price, constant volatility and continuous trading. The binomial model needs exactly two future states. Parity needs none of that: it compares two bundles of cash flows that are equal state by state, so it holds under any distribution, any risk preferences and any expected return. That is what makes it the safest tool in Part V. If an exam gives you a put price and asks for a call, do not reach for a model. Use parity, and say in one line why it is exact.

Three conditions get violated in exam questions on purpose, so write down the assumption before you apply the formula. Dividends: if the share pays a dividend with present value PV(Div) before T, the identity becomes C = P + S − PV(Div) − PV(K), because the share you hold inside the protective put pays that dividend out while the call holder receives nothing. American options: parity weakens to a pair of inequalities, because either side can be exercised early. Different strikes or maturities: there is no identity at all, only bounds.

21.6 How the exam uses parity

Parity questions come in two shapes. The first hands you three of the four quantities in C + PV(K) = P + S and asks for the fourth, sometimes for the fourth being rf or S rather than an option price. The four useful rearrangements are:

C = P + S − PV(K)   ·   P = C − S + PV(K)
S = C − P + PV(K)   ·   PV(K) = S + P − C
The last one is the sneaky one. Once you have PV(K) you have the risk-free rate implied by the option market: rf = [K/PV(K)]1/T − 1. The third builds a synthetic share out of options and a bond, which is the same trick used in chapter 24 to build synthetic debt out of a firm's assets and a put.
Worked example 3: parity in all four directions

Part (a) — find the put. Nordlys: S = 190.00, K = 210, T = 1, rf = 5.00%, and the call trades at C = 13.60. Find the price of the European put with the same strike and maturity.

PV(K) = 210/1.05 = 200.00
P = C − S + PV(K) = 13.60 − 190.00 + 200.00 = 23.60

That is where the put price used all through this chapter comes from. Notice C − P = −10.00 and S − PV(K) = 190.00 − 200.00 = −10.00, which is parity in its most compact form: C − P = S − PV(K). ✓

Part (b) — find the implied risk-free rate. Suppose instead you observe all three of S = 190.00, C = 13.60 and P = 23.60, and want the one-year rate the option market is using.

PV(K) = S + P − C = 190.00 + 23.60 − 13.60 = 200.00
rf = 210/200.00 − 1 = 1.05 − 1 = 0.0500 = 5.0000%

Part (c) — find the implied share price. Given C = 13.60, P = 23.60 and PV(K) = 200.00, the share must be worth
S = C − P + PV(K) = 13.60 − 23.60 + 200.00 = 190.00 ✓ — the three parts are the same equation solved for three different unknowns.

Part (d) — a two-year contract, so the exponent matters. Fjordkraft ASA trades at S = 320.00. A two-year European put with K = 300 costs 18.40, and rf = 3.00%. Find the two-year European call with the same strike.

Step 1: (1.03)2 = 1.0609
Step 2: PV(K) = 300/1.0609 = 282.7788
Step 3: C = P + S − PV(K) = 18.40 + 320.00 − 282.7788 = 55.6212

Check: run it backwards. P = C − S + PV(K) = 55.6212 − 320.00 + 282.7788 = 18.4000 ✓ And sanity-check the bounds from 21.4: C = 55.6212 is below S = 320.00 and above max(S − PV(K), 0) = 37.2212, and P = 18.40 is below PV(K) = 282.7788 and above max(PV(K) − S, 0) = 0. ✓ Had you forgotten to square the discount factor you would have got PV(K) = 291.2621 and C = 47.1379, an error of 8.4833 that no consistency check inside the question would catch. The exponent is T, always.

The second shape gives you four prices that do not satisfy parity and asks what to do. The answer is always the same: buy the cheap portfolio, sell the expensive one, and show the payoffs cancel in every state.

Worked example 4: trading a parity violation

Nordlys again: S = 190.00, K = 210, T = 1, rf = 5.00%, C = 13.60. But the put is quoted at 23.00, not the parity value of 23.60. Show the arbitrage.

Step 1 — price both portfolios.
Fiduciary call: C + PV(K) = 13.60 + 200.00 = 213.60
Protective put: P + S = 23.00 + 190.00 = 213.00
Both pay max(ST, 210) at T, yet one costs 0.60 more. The fiduciary call is expensive; the protective put is cheap.

Step 2 — buy cheap, sell dear. Buy the share and the put. Write the call and borrow 200.00 (which is selling the fiduciary call).

TransactionTodayAt T if ST = 150At T if ST = 250
Buy the share−190.00+150.00+250.00
Buy the put−23.00+60.000.00
Write the call+13.600.00−40.00
Borrow 200.00 at 5%+200.00−210.00−210.00
Net+0.600.000.00

Step 3 — say why it works in every state, not just these two. The share plus the put always delivers max(ST, 210). The written call plus the loan always costs max(ST − 210, 0) + 210 = max(ST, 210). They cancel identically, not just at 150 and 250.

Check: the profit today, 0.60, equals the parity gap exactly: (C + PV(K)) − (P + S) = 213.60 − 213.00 = 0.60 ✓ If the check does not reproduce the gap, you have bought the expensive side. A quick test of direction: here the put was too cheap, so you must be buying the put.

How this has actually been examined Parity appears in four of the eleven mapped papers, and 2023 MC9–10 together with V2024 used it as short computations of exactly the shape of worked example 3: three prices given, the fourth demanded. The marks go to the three lines PV(K) = …, then C − P = S − PV(K), then the number. Two habits pay: state the assumptions (European, same K and T, no dividends) in half a sentence, because that is often a separate mark, and quote rf to four decimals as H2025 demanded. If a question gives you a call and asks for a put but also hands you a volatility, resist it. Parity gets you there exactly and in thirty seconds; a model gets you there approximately and in five minutes.
Believing the call must cost more than the put Options look like mirror images, so it is tempting to assume the more likely-to-pay contract is the dearer one, or that calls are systematically more expensive because share prices drift up. Both are wrong, and parity settles it in one line: C − P = S − PV(K). The sign of the difference depends only on whether today's share price is above or below the discounted strike. For Nordlys, S = 190.00 sits below PV(K) = 200.00, so the put must cost exactly 10.00 more than the call, and it does: 23.60 − 13.60 = 10.00. When S = PV(K) the two are worth exactly the same whatever the volatility, whatever the expected return on the share, and whatever anyone thinks about the market's direction. Expected returns and beliefs simply do not enter the identity. That last point is the bridge to chapter 22.
