Forward to chapters 22, 23 and 24 This chapter said what an option must be worth relative to other options. It never said what a call is worth on its own, because that needs a model of how the share moves. Chapter 22 supplies the simplest one, two states and one period, and prices the call by building a portfolio of Δ shares and a loan that copies its payoff exactly. Chapter 23 takes that to the continuous limit and gets Black-Scholes, where you will meet the same bounds again as the limits of the formula. Chapter 24 turns the whole thing around: a levered firm's equity is a call on the firm's assets with strike equal to the face value of debt, and parity rewritten as D = PV(K) − Put is exactly the statement that risky debt is a risk-free bond minus the default option. The identity you memorised here is doing real work three chapters later.
↑ contents

22 · The binomial model and risk-neutral valuation

This is the engine room of Part V. The binomial model has appeared in seven of the eleven mapped papers, most recently as 2022 Problem 4 and as H2024 Exercise 6, where a real option was valued by risk-neutral probabilities for 20 points. The formula sheet gives you Δ, B and ρ, so the arithmetic is not what separates candidates. Two things are. The first is knowing which of the two routes to take and showing that they agree. The second is being able to say, in three sentences, why the true probability of the share going up never appears in the answer, because that is a standing discussion question and because misusing real probabilities is how the largest errors in this material get made. Everything here also feeds chapter 25, where the underlying is a project rather than a share.

22.1 One period, two states

Strip the world down to the minimum that still has risk in it. Today is date 0 and the only other date is date 1. A share trades at S0. At date 1 it will be worth either Su (up) or Sd (down), and nothing else can happen. You can also borrow and lend freely at rf. A derivative on that share, a call for now, pays Cu in the up state and Cd in the down state.

Before anything else, check that the tree is not already an arbitrage:

Sd < (1 + rf)S0 < Su
The share must be able to beat the risk-free investment in one state and lose to it in the other. If (1 + rf)S0 ≤ Sd the share dominates the bond and you would borrow without limit to buy it; if (1 + rf)S0 ≥ Su the reverse. This inequality is exactly the condition that makes ρ in 22.3 lie strictly between 0 and 1.

The running example for the whole chapter: Fjellkraft ASA trades at S0 = 100.00. In one year the share will be worth either 130.00 or 80.00. The risk-free rate is rf = 4.00%. A one-year European call with strike K = 105 therefore pays Cu = max(130.00 − 105, 0) = 25.00 or Cd = max(80.00 − 105, 0) = 0.00. Check the tree first: 80.00 < 104.00 < 130.00. ✓

Why two states is enough to pin down a unique price With two possible future states and two traded instruments, the share and the risk-free bond, you can build any pattern of payoffs across those two states. Two unknowns, two equations, one solution. In the language of the subject the market is complete, and completeness is what makes the option redundant: it is a package of things that already trade. A redundant security can have only one price, the price of the package, because otherwise you buy the cheap version and sell the dear one. Nothing in that argument mentions how likely the up state is, or how investors feel about risk. Add a third state without adding a third asset and the argument collapses, which is why the model is built out of binary steps rather than realistic ones.

22.2 Replication: Δ, B, and the price that follows

Build a portfolio of Δ shares and B krone of risk-free borrowing or lending, and choose Δ and B so the portfolio pays exactly what the option pays in both states. Two equations:

Δ·Su + B(1 + rf) = Cu  and  Δ·Sd + B(1 + rf) = Cd. Subtract the second from the first, and B drops out:

Δ = (Cu − Cd)/(Su − Sd)
B = (Cd − Sd·Δ)/(1 + rf)
C = S0Δ + B
All three are on the exam formula sheet. Δ is the number of shares, and it is called the hedge ratio or the option's delta. B is a cash position: B < 0 means you borrow. The numerator and denominator of Δ must describe the same two states, in the same order. For a call Δ is between 0 and 1; for a put it is between −1 and 0, so the put is replicated by shorting shares and lending.
Worked example 1: replicate the call, then trade against a wrong price

Fjellkraft: S0 = 100.00, Su = 130.00, Sd = 80.00, rf = 4.00%, K = 105.

Step 1 — the option's payoffs. Cu = max(130.00 − 105, 0) = 25.00, Cd = max(80.00 − 105, 0) = 0.00

Step 2 — the hedge ratio. Δ = (25.00 − 0.00)/(130.00 − 80.00) = 25.00/50.00 = 0.5000. The call behaves like half a share.

Step 3 — the borrowing. B = (0.00 − 80.00 × 0.5000)/1.04 = −40.00/1.04 = −38.4615. Negative, so you borrow 38.4615 today and repay 40.00 next year.

Step 4 — the price. C = S0Δ + B = 100.00(0.5000) − 38.4615 = 50.0000 − 38.4615 = 11.5385

Step 5 — verify the portfolio state by state. This is the step that earns the marks, so write it out.

State0.5 shares are worthLoan repaymentPortfolioCall payoff
Up, Su = 130.0065.00−40.0025.0025.00
Down, Sd = 80.0040.00−40.000.000.00

Step 6 — what to do if the market quotes 13.00. The call is dear relative to its replicating cost of 11.5385, so write the call and buy the portfolio: sell the call for 13.00, buy 0.5 shares for 50.00, borrow 38.4615. Net cash today = 13.00 − 50.0000 + 38.4615 = +1.4615. At date 1 the portfolio delivers 25.00 or 0.00 and the written call costs exactly 25.00 or 0.00, so the two cancel in both states and you owe nothing.

Check: the arbitrage profit, 1.4615, equals the mispricing 13.0000 − 11.5385 = 1.4615 exactly. ✓ If your arbitrage profit does not reproduce the mispricing you have taken the wrong side. A quick test of direction: the quoted price was too high, so you must be selling the option.

Δ is a ratio of differences, not of levels Two slips recur. The first is writing Δ = Cu/Su, which gives 25.00/130.00 = 0.1923 here and is simply not the solution to the two equations. The second is losing the sign for a put, where Cu − Cd is negative and Δ must come out negative too. Also keep B and its repayment straight: B is the amount today, and it is B(1 + rf) that appears in the date-1 equations. Writing B = −40.00 instead of −38.4615 discounts nothing and overprices the call by 1.5385.

22.3 The risk-neutral probability, and why the real one never shows up

Rearranging C = S0Δ + B algebraically, with no new assumption at all, produces a formula that looks like an expectation:

ρ = [(1 + rf)S0 − Sd] / (Su − Sd)
C0 = [ρ·Cu + (1 − ρ)·Cd] / (1 + rf)
Both are on the formula sheet. ρ is the risk-neutral probability of the up state. It is not a forecast and it is not anybody's belief: it is the weight that makes the share itself earn exactly rf, and the second line then discounts at rf rather than at any risk-adjusted rate. The two lines together give the same number as replication, always, because they are the same equation rewritten.

Read ρ as the answer to a question: what probability would a risk-neutral investor have to attach to the up state for today's share price to make sense? Solve ρ·Su + (1 − ρ)·Sd = (1 + rf)S0 for ρ and you get the formula above. Once the weights price the share correctly they price everything else on the same tree correctly too, because everything else is a portfolio of the share and the bond.

Worked example 2: the same call by risk-neutral valuation, then the put, then a parity check

Same tree: S0 = 100.00, Su = 130.00, Sd = 80.00, rf = 4.00%, K = 105.

Step 1 — the risk-neutral probability.
ρ = [(1.04)(100.00) − 80.00]/(130.00 − 80.00) = (104.00 − 80.00)/50.00 = 24.00/50.00 = 0.4800
1 − ρ = 0.5200

Step 2 — check that ρ prices the share. [0.4800(130.00) + 0.5200(80.00)]/1.04 = (62.4000 + 41.6000)/1.04 = 104.0000/1.04 = 100.00 ✓ Do this check before you use ρ on anything else. It costs one line and it catches a transposed Su and Sd.

Step 3 — price the call. C0 = [0.4800(25.00) + 0.5200(0.00)]/1.04 = 12.0000/1.04 = 11.5385 — identical to worked example 1. ✓

Step 4 — price the put with the same strike, by replication. Pu = max(105 − 130.00, 0) = 0.00 and Pd = max(105 − 80.00, 0) = 25.00.
ΔP = (0.00 − 25.00)/(130.00 − 80.00) = −0.5000 (short half a share)
BP = (25.00 − 80.00 × (−0.5000))/1.04 = (25.00 + 40.00)/1.04 = 65.00/1.04 = 62.5000 (lend)
P = 100.00(−0.5000) + 62.5000 = 12.5000

Step 5 — price the put risk-neutrally. P = [0.4800(0.00) + 0.5200(25.00)]/1.04 = 13.0000/1.04 = 12.5000 ✓

Check — put-call parity from chapter 21. PV(K) = 105/1.04 = 100.9615, so P + S0 − PV(K) = 12.5000 + 100.0000 − 100.9615 = 11.5385, which is the call. ✓ This is a genuine check rather than an identity you cannot fail: the call and the put were computed from different payoff vectors and different hedge ratios, and parity was never used to produce either. It would catch a sign error in ΔP or a strike discounted at the wrong rate. Note also that ΔC − ΔP = 0.5000 − (−0.5000) = 1.0000, which is parity differentiated: a call minus a put is one share.

Why the true probabilities never appear, and what to write when you are asked The replicating portfolio matches the option's payoff in every state. Not on average, in every state. Because the match is exact state by state, it does not matter how likely the states are: two things that are equal in all circumstances have the same value under any beliefs and any risk preferences. Two investors who violently disagree about whether Fjellkraft will rise must still agree that the call is worth 11.5385, because both agree it is half a share minus a loan, and they can look up the price of half a share.

Where did the disagreement go? Into the share price itself. S0 = 100.00 already embeds the market's probabilities and its risk aversion; that information is used, but it is used through the observed price rather than through a probability you have to guess. This is why ρ deserves the name it has: it is what the probability would be if nobody demanded a risk premium, and it differs from the real probability by exactly the amount of risk adjustment the market applies. A four-sentence exam answer: the option is replicated exactly state by state · exact replication means beliefs cancel · the market's beliefs and risk aversion already sit inside S0 · therefore ρ is a pricing weight, not a forecast, and it should never be presented as one.

It is worth seeing what goes wrong if you insist on the real probabilities. Suppose the true probability of the up state is p = 0.65.

QuantityComputationValue
Expected share price at date 10.65(130.00) + 0.35(80.00)112.5000
Expected return on the share112.5000/100.00 − 10.1250 = 12.5000%
Expected call payoff0.65(25.00) + 0.35(0.00)16.2500
Expected payoff discounted at rf16.2500/1.0415.6250 — wrong
Expected payoff discounted at the share's 12.50%16.2500/1.125014.4444 — also wrong
The option's own expected return16.2500/11.5385 − 10.4083 = 40.8333%
Expected payoff discounted at 40.8333%16.2500/1.40833311.5385 ✓

Discounting the true expected payoff does work, but only at 40.8333%, and you cannot know that rate until you already know the price. That circularity is the practical reason the risk-neutral route wins. The 40.8333% is not arbitrary either: the replicating portfolio is 50.0000 of shares financed by 38.4615 of debt against 11.5385 of your own money, so it is a levered position in the share, and the levered expected return is rf + (S0Δ/C)(rS − rf) = 0.0400 + (50.0000/11.538462)(0.1250 − 0.0400) = 0.0400 + 4.333333(0.0850) = 0.0400 + 0.368333 = 0.408333. ✓ That is chapter 4's relevering formula reappearing, with the option playing the part of the equity.

How this has actually been examined 2022 Problem 4 and H2024 Exercise 6 are the two most recent instances, and H2024 wrapped it in a real option worth 20 points. The pattern of the marks is stable. You get credit for computing ρ, for stating that discounting is at rf because ρ already carries the risk adjustment, and for saying in words that the true probability is irrelevant. A cheap extra mark: run the ρ-prices-the-underlying check in one line, as in step 2 above. If the question gives you a real probability, it is often there as a distractor, or it is needed only for a separate sub-question about expected returns. Read which one is being asked before you use it.

22.4 Two periods: work backwards, and rebalance

Two periods add nothing conceptually and one thing practically. Conceptually you solve the one-period problem three times, starting from the end. Practically the hedge ratio changes as you move through the tree, so the replicating strategy must be rebalanced at date 1. That is the feature examiners ask about verbally.

Keep the same up and down factors at every node, u = 1.30 and d = 0.80, and the tree recombines: up-then-down and down-then-up both land on 100.00 × 1.30 × 0.80 = 104.00. Recombination is what stops the number of nodes exploding, and it also means ρ is the same at every node, because ρ depends only on u, d and rf.

Worked example 3: a two-period call, node by node

Same share and rate: S0 = 100.00, u = 1.30, d = 0.80, rf = 4.00% per period, two periods. This time the strike is K = 100. Value the European call.

Step 1 — build the share tree. Su = 130.00, Sd = 80.00; Suu = 169.00, Sud = 104.00, Sdd = 64.00.

Step 2 — the risk-neutral probability. It is unchanged because u, d and rf are unchanged: ρ = (104.00 − 80.00)/50.00 = 0.4800. Verify it once at the middle: from S = 130.00 the next prices are 169.00 and 104.00, so [0.4800(169.00) + 0.5200(104.00)]/1.04 = (81.1200 + 54.0800)/1.04 = 135.2000/1.04 = 130.00 ✓

Step 3 — date-2 payoffs. Cuu = 169.00 − 100 = 69.00, Cud = 104.00 − 100 = 4.00, Cdd = max(64.00 − 100, 0) = 0.00

Step 4 — roll back to date 1.
Cu = [0.4800(69.00) + 0.5200(4.00)]/1.04 = (33.1200 + 2.0800)/1.04 = 35.2000/1.04 = 33.8462
Cd = [0.4800(4.00) + 0.5200(0.00)]/1.04 = 1.9200/1.04 = 1.8462

Step 5 — roll back to today.
C0 = [0.4800(33.8462) + 0.5200(1.8462)]/1.04 = (16.2462 + 0.9600)/1.04 = 17.2062/1.04 = 16.5444

Step 6 — the hedge ratios, and what rebalancing means.
Today: Δ0 = (33.8462 − 1.8462)/(130.00 − 80.00) = 32.0000/50.00 = 0.6400
After an up move: Δu = (69.00 − 4.00)/(169.00 − 104.00) = 65.00/65.00 = 1.0000
After a down move: Δd = (4.00 − 0.00)/(104.00 − 64.00) = 4.00/40.00 = 0.1000
So you start holding 0.64 shares. If the price rises you must buy up to a full share; if it falls you must sell down to 0.10. The strategy is self-financing: the money to buy the extra shares comes from additional borrowing, and the value of the position at each node is exactly the option value at that node.

Check 1 — replication at the root. B0 = (Cd − SdΔ0)/1.04 = (1.8462 − 80.00 × 0.6400)/1.04 = (1.8462 − 51.2000)/1.04 = −49.3538/1.04 = −47.4556, and C0 = 100.00(0.6400) − 47.4556 = 64.0000 − 47.4556 = 16.5444 ✓ Same as the backward induction, as it must be.

Check 2 — the direct two-period formula. Skipping the intermediate node values, the risk-neutral weights on the three terminal nodes are ρ² = 0.2304, 2ρ(1 − ρ) = 0.4992 and (1 − ρ)² = 0.2704, which sum to 1.0000. Then
C0 = [0.2304(69.00) + 0.4992(4.00) + 0.2704(0.00)]/(1.04)² = (15.8976 + 1.9968 + 0.0000)/1.0816 = 17.8944/1.0816 = 16.5444 ✓
The factor 2 on the middle term is there because two different paths reach 104.00. Forgetting it is a standard slip.

Why the hedge ratio has to change, and what it costs you Δ measures how much the option's value moves when the share moves, and that sensitivity is not constant. Deep in the money a call moves almost one-for-one with the share, so Δ approaches 1; far out of the money it barely moves at all, so Δ approaches 0. In the two-period tree above, one up move takes the call from "might pay" to "will certainly pay something", and Δ jumps from 0.64 to 1.00. A hedger who set up 0.64 shares and then went on holiday would be under-hedged after a rise and over-hedged after a fall. This is exactly what makes real delta hedging expensive and imperfect, and it is the reason chapter 23 can talk about a trade that profits from volatility being different from what was assumed: the hedge has to be adjusted, and how often the adjustment loses money depends on how far the share actually moves.

The same tree also settles the early-exercise question left open in chapter 21. Value an American put with K = 100 on this tree. The date-2 payoffs are Puu = 0.00, Pud = max(100 − 104.00, 0) = 0.00 and Pdd = 100 − 64.00 = 36.00.

NodeValue if held (continuation)Value if exercised nowAmerican value
Up, S = 130.00[0.4800(0.00) + 0.5200(0.00)]/1.04 = 0.0000max(100 − 130.00, 0) = 0.00000.0000
Down, S = 80.00[0.4800(0.00) + 0.5200(36.00)]/1.04 = 18.0000100 − 80.00 = 20.000020.0000 — exercise
Today, S = 100.00[0.4800(0.0000) + 0.5200(20.0000)]/1.04 = 10.0000100 − 100.00 = 0.000010.0000 — hold

The European put on the same tree is worth [0.4800(0.0000) + 0.5200(18.0000)]/1.04 = 9.3600/1.04 = 9.0000, so the right to exercise early is worth 1.0000. And the European values obey parity: C0 − P0 = 16.5444 − 9.0000 = 7.5444, while S0 − PV(K) = 100.00 − 100/(1.04)² = 100.0000 − 92.4556 = 7.5444 ✓

Rolling an American option back as if it were European The expensive error on a tree is to compute the terminal payoffs, discount them all the way to the root with the risk-neutral weights, and hand that in as the American value. It gives 9.0000 above instead of 10.0000, and no consistency check inside the calculation will complain, because 9.0000 is a perfectly correct European price. The rule is that at every intermediate node you must take the maximum of the continuation value and immediate exercise, and then carry that maximum backwards. Chapter 21 already told you where this bites: never for a call on a share paying no dividends, where continuation always wins, and often for a put, where getting K early beats getting it late. Write one line at each node showing both candidates and which one you took.

22.5 Backing the risk-neutral probability out of observed prices

The exam frequently runs the machinery in reverse. You are not given a neat tree for the thing you must value. Instead you are given the price of something else that lives on the same two states, and you must extract ρ from it. Any asset works, because on a two-state tree every asset must be priced by the same weights:

ρ = [X0(1 + rf) − Xd] / (Xu − Xd)
X0 is any observed price today and Xu, Xd its two possible values one period later. Put X = S and you get the formula on the sheet. But X can equally be a traded commodity certificate, a claim to an annuity, or an option whose price you can see. If two assets on the same tree imply different ρ, one of them is mispriced and there is an arbitrage, which is a legitimate answer to "comment on these prices".

Two quick illustrations on the running tree. From the share: ρ = (104.00 − 80.00)/50.00 = 0.4800, as before. From the call price of 11.5385: ρ = [11.5385(1.04) − 0.00]/(25.00 − 0.00) = 12.0000/25.00 = 0.4800. Same weight, as it must be.

Worked example 4: a commodity price, an annuity, and a project you may walk away from

Nordmalm ASA is deciding whether to develop a copper deposit. There is no option quoted on copper for you to look at, but a traded copper certificate is: it holds copper, pays nothing along the way, and trades today at 50.00. In one year it will be worth 70.00 if copper is strong and 40.00 if it is weak. The risk-free rate is rf = 4.00%.

The mine works like this. In one year the copper price becomes known. At that point Nordmalm may build for a cost of 45.00, or walk away for nothing. If it builds, the mine produces a certain cash flow for five years, at dates 2 through 6: 14.00 per year if copper is strong, 5.00 per year if it is weak. Management believes the strong state has probability 0.70. What is the project worth today?

Step 1 — extract ρ from the certificate.
(1.04)(50.00) = 52.0000
ρ = (52.0000 − 40.00)/(70.00 − 40.00) = 12.0000/30.00 = 0.4000, so 1 − ρ = 0.6000
Check that ρ reprices the certificate: [0.4000(70.00) + 0.6000(40.00)]/1.04 = (28.0000 + 24.0000)/1.04 = 52.0000/1.04 = 50.00 ✓

Step 2 — the annuity factor. Five payments, the first one period after the valuation date, discounted at rf because the cash flows are certain once the state is known:
(1.04)5 = 1.21665290, so (1.04)−5 = 0.82192711
Annuity factor = (1 − 0.82192711)/0.04 = 0.17807289/0.04 = 4.45182225, which I carry as 4.451822. Every figure below follows from that rounded factor, so the intermediate numbers add up exactly as printed.

Step 3 — value the mine in each state, at date 1.
Strong: 14.00 × 4.451822 = 62.325508, so NPV1 = 62.325508 − 45.0000 = 17.325508
Weak: 5.00 × 4.451822 = 22.259110, so NPV1 = 22.259110 − 45.0000 = −22.740890

Step 4 — apply the option. Nordmalm builds only in the strong state, so the date-1 value is max(17.325508, 0) = 17.325508 and max(−22.740890, 0) = 0.0000.

Step 5 — discount at the risk-free rate with the risk-neutral weights.
V0 = [0.4000(17.325508) + 0.6000(0.0000)]/1.04 = 6.930203/1.04 = 6.663657, so the deposit is worth 6.6637 today

Check 1 — what the flexibility is worth. Value the same mine with a commitment to build whatever happens: [0.4000(17.325508) + 0.6000(−22.740890)]/1.04 = (6.930203 − 13.644534)/1.04 = −6.714331/1.04 = −6.456088, that is −6.4561. A pre-committed developer would reject the deposit. The right to wait one year and then decide is therefore worth 6.663657 − (−6.456088) = 13.1197, and it turns a negative-value asset into a positive one. That is chapter 25's whole subject, arrived at from here.

Check 2 — the error to avoid. Using management's real probability of 0.70 together with the risk-free rate gives 0.7000(17.325508)/1.04 = 12.127856/1.04 = 11.6614, which overstates the project by 11.6614 − 6.6637 = 4.9977. The 0.70 belongs with a risk-adjusted discount rate you have not been given, or nowhere at all. Never pair a real probability with rf. The only probability entitled to rf is ρ.

ρ is not a forecast, and it is not "the market's probability" Two sentences to keep separate. "The risk-neutral probability of the strong copper state is 0.4000" is a statement about prices. "The probability of the strong copper state is 0.4000" is a statement about the world, and it is not one you have made. For an asset whose payoff is high in good times, ρ sits below the true probability, because the market discounts good-state payoffs; for an asset that pays off in bad states, such as insurance or the default event behind a credit spread in chapter 5, ρ sits above it. Writing "the market thinks there is a 40% chance" when you mean ρ is a real answer-quality error, and the same distinction is what separates 1 − N(d2) from an actual default forecast in chapter 24.
Forward to chapters 23, 24 and 25 Chapter 23 lets the number of periods go to infinity while each step gets small, and the binomial price converges to Black-Scholes. Everything transfers: N(d1) is Δ, PV(K)·N(d2) is −B, and C = S·N(d1) − PV(K)·N(d2) is C = SΔ + B written for the continuous case. Chapter 24 replaces the share with the whole firm's assets and the strike with the face value of debt. Chapter 25 replaces the share with a project and keeps this chapter's method exactly as it stands: build the tree, extract ρ from a traded twin, take max(0, NPV) at each node, discount at rf. Worked example 4 is a chapter-25 exercise done with chapter-22 tools, which is precisely how H2024 Exercise 6 was built.
↑ contents

23 · Black-Scholes and volatility

The formula sheet stopped carrying tables of N(d) in 2021, and that changed what this chapter is for. You will almost certainly not be asked to compute a Black-Scholes price by hand. You will be asked what the formula means. H2025 opened the paper with exactly that: Exercise 1 was pure Black-Scholes intuition with no computation, asking what happens to a call when the true volatility turns out to be lower than the volatility implied by its market price, and what you should do about a mispriced option. That question is the whole of sections 23.4 and 23.5 below. The comparative statics, and above all the fact that option value rises with volatility, are the highest-value paragraphs in Part V per minute of study. The formula itself also earns its keep in chapter 24, where N(d1) turns into an equity beta and 1 − N(d2) turns into a default probability.

23.1 From the binomial tree to Black-Scholes

Nothing new happens between chapter 22 and this one. Take the binomial tree, cut the year into n periods instead of one, and let n grow. To keep the share's total volatility fixed at σ per year while the steps get shorter, set the up and down factors from σ itself: u = eσ√(T/n) and d = 1/u. As n grows, the distribution of the share price at T settles down into a lognormal one, and the tree price converges to a closed-form limit. That limit is the Black-Scholes formula.

Take the Fjellkraft call from chapter 22 and price it this way: S0 = 100.00, K = 105, rf = 4.00%, T = 1 year, and now an assumed volatility of σ = 30%.

Steps nudρCall value
11.34990.74080.491214.1636
21.23630.80890.493511.2042
41.16180.86070.495311.5402
101.09950.90950.497011.6491
501.04330.95850.498611.5603
2001.02140.97900.499311.5057
10001.00950.99060.499711.5089
Black-Scholes———11.5061

Two things to notice. The convergence is not monotone: it oscillates as the terminal nodes shuffle around the strike, which is why a coarse tree can be badly wrong in either direction. And a one-step tree is genuinely crude, 14.1636 against 11.5061, so do not present a single binomial step as an approximation to Black-Scholes. The two models agree in the limit, not at n = 1.

Why the answer is a formula and not a simulation Everything that made the tree work survives the limit. At each instant the option is still replicated by a portfolio of shares and borrowing, so it is still redundant, so its price is still fixed by no arbitrage. What the limit adds is a closed form for the two pieces of that portfolio, because a lognormal distribution has tractable partial expectations. That is the sense in which Black-Scholes is not a new theory: it is chapter 22's replication argument with the arithmetic done once and for all. It also inherits chapter 22's punchline. The share's expected return does not appear in the formula, and neither does anybody's probability of the share rising.

23.2 The formula, and what N(d1) and N(d2) mean

C = S·N(d1) − PV(K)·N(d2)
d1 = ln[S/PV(K)]/(σ√T) + (σ√T)/2   ·   d2 = d1 − σ√T
PV(K) = K/(1 + rf)T   ·   P = C − S + PV(K)  (parity)
S is today's share price, K the strike, T the time to expiry in years, σ the annualised standard deviation of the share's return, and N(·) the cumulative standard normal distribution. It prices a European call on a share paying no dividends. The share's expected return does not appear. Neither does any probability of the share rising: the only distribution in the formula is the risk-neutral one.

One note on compounding, because you will meet both versions. This manual discounts the strike the same way as every other chapter, PV(K) = K/(1 + rf)T. Textbooks that state Black-Scholes in continuous time write PV(K) = K·e−rfT instead, and then expand the same d1 as [ln(S/K) + (rf + σ²/2)T]/(σ√T). Substitute the continuous PV(K) into the expression above and you get exactly that, so it is one formula and two ways of quoting rf, not two models. Chapter 24 uses the continuous version because option-based credit models are stated that way. Whichever you use, say which rf you mean and keep it consistent inside a question.

The five inputs are S, K, T, rf and σ. Four of them you can read off a screen. Only σ has to be estimated, and that single fact is what sections 23.4 and 23.5 are about.

The two N(·) terms are not decoration. Set them against chapter 22's C = SΔ + B and they line up term for term:

C = S·N(d1) − PV(K)·N(d2)   is   C = S·Δ + B
So Δ = N(d1), the number of shares in the replicating portfolio, always between 0 and 1 for a call. And B = −PV(K)·N(d2), the borrowing that finances them. Separately, N(d2) is the risk-neutral probability that the call finishes in the money, that is the probability under ρ that ST > K. The second term is therefore "the strike, discounted, paid only in the states where you actually pay it".
Why there are two different normal probabilities, not one A call pays ST − K when it is exercised, and that is two payments, not one. You receive a share, and you hand over K. The two legs happen in the same states but they are not the same size, so they need different weights. The K leg is a fixed amount, so its value is the discounted amount times the risk-neutral probability of paying it at all: PV(K)·N(d2). The share leg is not fixed: when the call finishes in the money, the share tends to be worth more than K, and the deeper in the money the bigger the share. N(d1) is the probability-weighted value of receiving the share, not the probability of receiving it, which is why N(d1) > N(d2) always. The gap between them is exactly σ√T inside the normal, and it widens with volatility and with time.
Worked example 1: a full Black-Scholes valuation, once

Fjellkraft ASA, the same firm as chapter 22. S = 100.00, K = 105, rf = 4.00%, T = 1 year, σ = 30%. Price the European call and the European put.

Step 1 — discount the strike. PV(K) = 105/1.04 = 100.9615385

Step 2 — the log moneyness. ln[S/PV(K)] = ln(100.0000/100.9615385) = ln(0.9904762) = −0.00956945. It is negative, so the call is slightly out of the money even after discounting the strike.

Step 3 — σ√T. With T = 1, σ√T = 0.30√1 = 0.3000000.

Step 4 — d1 and d2.
d1 = −0.00956945/0.3000000 + 0.3000000/2 = −0.0318982 + 0.1500000 = 0.1181018
d2 = 0.1181018 − 0.3000000 = −0.1818982

Step 5 — the two normal probabilities. N(0.1181018) = 0.5470065 and N(−0.1818982) = 0.4278313. Note N(d1) > N(d2), as it must be, and that N(d2) is below 0.5: under the risk-neutral distribution this call is slightly more likely to expire worthless than not.

Step 6 — put the formula together.
S·N(d1) = 100.0000 × 0.5470065 = 54.7006500
PV(K)·N(d2) = 100.9615385 × 0.4278313 = 43.1945063
C = 54.7006500 − 43.1945063 = 11.5061

Step 7 — the put, by parity. P = C − S + PV(K) = 11.5061437 − 100.0000000 + 100.9615385 = 12.4677

Check 1 — read the answer as a portfolio. The call is 0.5470 shares, worth 54.7007, financed by borrowing 43.1945. Both numbers are sensible: Δ is between 0 and 1, and the borrowing is below PV(K) = 100.9615 because you only repay it in the states where you exercise. The bounds from chapter 21 also hold: 0 ≤ 11.5061 ≤ 100.00, and the lower bound max(S − PV(K), 0) = 0 is satisfied.

Check 2 — what four-decimal tables would have cost you. Read N to four decimals, as every printed table gave it, and you get 100.0000(0.5470) − 100.9615(0.4278) = 54.7000 − 43.1913 = 11.5087, which is 0.0026 away from the exact 11.5061. That is a two-øre error on a 100-krone share and it is the honest reason nobody asks for this arithmetic under exam conditions any more. If a question hands you N(d1) and N(d2) directly, do steps 6 and 7 and nothing else.

23.3 Comparative statics: the five inputs, and dividends

This is the examinable core. For each of the five inputs, and for dividends, know the sign for a call and for a put, and know the sentence that explains it.

Input risesCallPutWhy
Share price S↑↓The call buys the share, so a dearer share makes the right to buy it worth more and the right to sell it worth less
Strike K↓↑You pay K on a call and receive K on a put
Volatility σ↑↑Both rise. The downside is truncated at zero, so extra dispersion adds upside without adding downside. See 23.4
Time to expiry T↑ambiguous for a European putMore time means more dispersion and a smaller PV(K). Both help a call. For a put they pull against each other, and for an American put the right to exercise early settles it: ↑
Risk-free rate rf↑↓A higher rate shrinks PV(K). Paying the strike later is better for a call holder and worse for a put holder, who is waiting to receive it
Dividends before T↓↑The share drops on the ex-dividend date and the option holder does not receive the cash
Worked example 2: move one input at a time, and check every row against parity

Start from worked example 1: S = 100.00, K = 105, rf = 4.00%, T = 1, σ = 30%, giving C = 11.5061 and P = 12.4677. Change one input at a time.

ChangeNew CΔCNew PΔPΔC − ΔP
S: 100 → 11017.6023+6.09618.5638−3.9039+10.0000
K: 105 → 1157.9582−3.548018.5351+6.0674−9.6154
σ: 30% → 40%15.4519+3.945716.4134+3.94570.0000
T: 1 → 2 years18.0548+6.548715.1332+2.6655+3.8831
rf: 4% → 6%12.3449+0.838711.4015−1.0662+1.9049

The check that ties the whole table together. Put-call parity says C − P = S − PV(K), so the last column must equal the change in S − PV(K) and nothing else. Verify all five:

Row 1. S rose by 10.0000 and PV(K) did not move, so ΔC − ΔP must be +10.0000. ✓
Row 2. PV(K) went from 105/1.04 = 100.961538 to 115/1.04 = 110.576923, a rise of 9.615385, so ΔC − ΔP must be −9.6154. ✓
Row 3. σ appears nowhere in S − PV(K), so ΔC − ΔP must be exactly 0.0000, which is why the call and the put gained the identical 3.9457. ✓
Row 4. PV(K) went from 100.961538 to 105/(1.04)² = 105/1.0816 = 97.078402, a fall of 3.883136, so ΔC − ΔP must be +3.8831. ✓
Row 5. PV(K) went from 100.961538 to 105/1.06 = 99.056604, a fall of 1.904934, so ΔC − ΔP must be +1.9049. ✓

Why this check is worth running. It is not an identity that passes automatically: C and P were computed from the formula independently, and parity was never used to make either of them. A sign error in d2, a strike discounted for the wrong number of years, or a σ√T left out of d2 would all break a row. Row 3 is the strongest of the five, because it turns the abstract claim "volatility raises calls and puts alike" into an equality you can test to four decimals.

The European put and time to expiry "Options are worth more with more time" is true for calls, true for American options of either kind, and not always true for a European put. A European put holder is waiting to receive K, and pushing that receipt further into the future is costly. When the put is deep in the money and rates are high, that cost beats the extra dispersion. Concretely, with S = 60.00, K = 100, rf = 6.00% and σ = 20%, the European put is worth 37.1295 at T = 0.5, 34.4008 at T = 1, 29.7622 at T = 2 and 20.6590 at T = 5: it falls steadily. An American put with the same inputs cannot fall below its immediate exercise value of 100 − 60.00 = 40.00, because you can always take the money today. Say "for a call" or "for an American option" when you claim the effect of T, and you will not be caught.

23.4 Volatility, and why more of it is always better for an option holder

This is the single result most likely to be examined, and it is counter-intuitive on first meeting: everywhere else in finance, risk is a cost. Here it is a benefit, and it is a benefit to the holder of a call and to the holder of a put alike.

Why volatility raises the value of a call and a put Hold the strike fixed and spread the distribution of ST out. Two things happen at once, and only one of them reaches the option holder. The extra probability of very high prices raises the call's payoff without limit: every krone above K is a krone in your pocket. The extra probability of very low prices does not lower it at all, because max(ST − K, 0) is already zero at K and stays zero all the way down. The loss is capped at the premium; the gain is not capped. Extra dispersion is therefore all upside for the holder, and the price rises.

The same argument works upside down for a put: extra probability of very low prices raises max(K − ST, 0), and extra probability of very high prices costs nothing because the put is already worthless there. So a single increase in σ raises both contracts, which is not a contradiction: the writer of both is the one paying for it. And note what is not the reason. It is not that risk is rewarded. Under the risk-neutral distribution the share still earns only rf, spread or no spread. The gain comes entirely from the kink in the payoff, not from a risk premium.
This is also chapter 9's risk shifting, and chapter 25's real options Recognise the shape. In chapter 9 shareholders of a distressed firm preferred a riskier project even at a lower NPV, because equity is a claim with a floor at zero. That is this result: equity is a call on the firm's assets, so more volatility raises its value at the creditors' expense. Chapter 24 makes the identification exact. And in chapter 25, the value of the option to wait comes from Σpi·max(0, NPVi), where the same max(0, ·) truncates the bad states. Every "risk is good here" result in this course is the same max(0, ·) seen from a different angle. Saying so explicitly in a verbal answer is exactly the kind of mechanism-naming the solution keys reward.

23.5 Implied volatility, and what to do with a mispriced option

Four of the five inputs are observable. The fifth, σ, is not, so in practice the formula is used backwards. Take the option's market price as given, and ask what volatility the formula would need to reproduce it:

σimplied solves   Cmarket = S·N(d1(σ)) − PV(K)·N(d2(σ))
The implied volatility. There is no closed form, so it is found by trial and error, but there is exactly one answer: 23.4 showed that C is strictly increasing in σ, so the mapping from σ to price is one-to-one. Quoting an option's implied volatility is therefore just another way of quoting its price, and it is how traders actually talk, because it strips out S, K, T and rf and leaves the one number in dispute.

Implied volatility is the market's forecast of how much the share will move between now and T. Your own estimate, from historical returns or from knowing something about the firm, need not agree. When it does not, you have a view, and the H2025 question is what to do with it.

Worked example 3: true volatility below implied volatility (the H2025 Exercise 1 question)

Fjellkraft's one-year call with K = 105 trades at 15.4519 when S = 100.00 and rf = 4.00%. You have studied the share's returns and conclude that its true volatility is 25%, not what the market is using.

Step 1 — what is the market assuming? Work the formula backwards. The price 15.4519 is reproduced by σ = 40.00%. That is the implied volatility. (You would not be asked to invert it under exam conditions; a question of this type either states the implied volatility or asks only for the direction of the answer.)

Step 2 — what is the call worth at your volatility? Rerun the formula at σ = 25%:
d1 = −0.00956945/0.2500000 + 0.2500000/2 = −0.0382778 + 0.1250000 = 0.0867222
d2 = 0.0867222 − 0.2500000 = −0.1632778
N(d1) = 0.5345538, N(d2) = 0.4351499
C = 100.0000(0.5345538) − 100.9615385(0.4351499) = 53.4553800 − 43.9334034 = 9.5219766, that is 9.5220

Step 3 — answer the question that was actually asked. Because value rises with volatility and your volatility is lower, the call is worth less than its market price: 9.5220 against 15.4519. The call is overpriced by 5.9299, which is 38.38% of the quoted price. This is the whole of the H2025 answer to "what happens to the value if the true volatility is lower than the implied volatility". Say the direction, name the comparative static that produces it, and only then quantify.

Step 4 — what you should do about it. Sell the overpriced contract, and hedge away everything you do not have a view on. Concretely: write the call at 15.4519 and buy Δ = N(d1) = 0.5345538 shares at your own volatility, costing 53.4554, financed by borrowing. The share position cancels the call's exposure to S, so you are not betting on Fjellkraft's direction, only on its volatility. If you are right and the share moves as little as 25% volatility implies, the hedged position ends up cheaper to run than the 15.4519 you took in, and the difference is your profit.

Step 5 — the put version, for free. Parity says C − P = S − PV(K), and σ is nowhere in that expression, so the put must be overpriced by exactly the same amount. At σ = 40% the put is worth 16.4134; at σ = 25% it is worth 10.4835; the difference is 5.9299, identical to the call's. ✓ So "sell the call" and "sell the put" are equally valid answers, and a complete answer says so.

Check — state the risks, because the marks are there. The trade is not an arbitrage, and saying so is worth writing down. You may simply be wrong about σ; realised volatility can exceed 25% even if 25% was the right expectation. The hedge has to be rebalanced as Δ changes, which costs money and cannot be done continuously. And Black-Scholes itself may be the wrong model, in which case "implied volatility" is measuring model error rather than anyone's forecast. An unhedged written call has unlimited loss. Chapter 22 gave the reason the hedge must be adjusted: Δ is not constant.

How this has actually been examined H2025 Exercise 1 was a verbal Black-Scholes question with no computation at all, and it opened the paper. Two sub-questions of exactly the shape above: what happens to a call's value when true volatility is below implied, and what an investor should do about a mispriced option. The marks go to a structured answer, not to arithmetic. Use four moves: name the comparative static ("call value is increasing in σ"), give the direction ("so the call is worth less than the market price, that is, it is overpriced"), state the action ("write the call and delta-hedge with N(d1) shares, so the position bets on volatility and not on direction"), and name one limitation ("this is not an arbitrage: it relies on my volatility estimate being right"). H2025 also gives credit for creative but well-argued answers, so a sensible extra point costs nothing. Black-Scholes has been tested numerically in only two of the eleven mapped papers, and the N(d) tables came off the formula sheet in 2021, so do not spend your study time on the arithmetic.
Treating implied volatility as a fact about the share The expensive misreading is to say "the share's volatility is 40%" when what you observed was an option price. Implied volatility is an output of a model applied to a price, not a measurement of anything. Two consequences follow, and both have shown up as discussion questions. First, if the model is wrong, the implied number absorbs the error: the reason options on the same share with different strikes trade at different implied volatilities, the volatility smile, is not that the share has several volatilities, but that real returns have fatter tails than the lognormal distribution Black-Scholes assumes. Second, implied volatility is a risk-neutral quantity, so it contains a premium for bearing volatility risk and typically sits above the volatility that is subsequently realised. This is the same distinction as chapter 22's ρ against the true probability, and chapter 24's 1 − N(d2) against a real default forecast. The manual makes this point three times because the exam rewards it three times.

23.6 What the model assumes, and where it breaks

Being able to list the assumptions and say what each one buys is a standard verbal sub-question, and it is also what tells you when the formula is the wrong tool.

AssumptionWhat it buysWhat goes wrong without it
European exerciseOnly the terminal distribution mattersAmerican puts are worth more; price them on a tree with the max at every node (chapter 22)
No dividends before TThe share you replicate with pays nothing outCalls are worth less, puts more, and early exercise of an American call can become optimal just before an ex-dividend date
Constant, known σOne number describes the whole distributionThe volatility smile: implied volatility varies by strike and maturity, so the model is being asked to absorb the mismatch
Lognormal returnsClosed-form partial expectationsReal returns have fatter tails and jumps, so far out-of-the-money options are systematically underpriced by the formula
Continuous, costless tradingThe hedge can be rebalanced perfectlyThe replication is approximate; hedging costs are real and rise with how often you rebalance
Constant rf, no frictions, no defaultPV(K) is unambiguousMatters most for long-dated contracts and for the option-based credit models in chapter 24
Three input errors that cost more than they look σ is a volatility of returns, not of the price level. A share that moves by 30 krone on a base of 100 has σ near 30%, not 30. If a question gives you a variance, take the square root before it goes anywhere near the formula.
Everything must be in the same time unit. σ is annual, so T is in years, and rf is the annual rate. A three-month option has T = 0.25 and σ√T = σ/2, not σ. Halving √T while leaving T at 3 is a classic slip.
Discount the strike before you take the logarithm. The formula above takes ln[S/PV(K)], not ln(S/K). Writing ln(S/K) drops the interest-rate effect entirely and mis-prices the option in a direction that depends on the sign of ln(S/K), so it will not always look obviously wrong.
Forward to chapter 24: this formula applied to the firm itself Chapter 24 puts firm value V where S is and the face value of debt where K is, and everything here reappears with a new name. N(d1) becomes the elasticity that gives βE = N(d1)(V/E)βU, which explains why the equity beta of a firm near distress explodes even as the option's delta falls: V/E rises faster than N(d1) falls. N(d2) becomes the risk-neutral probability that the firm repays, so 1 − N(d2) is the risk-neutral default probability, the number a credit default swap is priced off. And 23.4's result that volatility raises a call becomes the statement that asset volatility transfers value from creditors to shareholders, which is chapter 9's risk shifting with a formula attached. Learn the meanings here and chapter 24 is mostly relabelling.
↑ contents

24 · Equity and debt as options

Two threads that have run separately since chapter 5 meet here. Chapter 5 handed you a default probability and a recovery rate and asked for a yield; chapters 9 and 10 showed you shareholders behaving like option holders without pricing the option. This chapter closes both: the whole right-hand side of the balance sheet is a pair of options on the firm's assets, so the credit spread, the default probability, the beta of equity and the price of default insurance all fall out of one model. It has been examined heavily in the older papers, 2015 Problem 4 was worth 90 points and 2016 Problem 4, 2021 MC7–9 and V2024 Problems 3 and 4 all sit here, and it reaches eight of eleven mapped papers. Neither Kurbatov paper has used it yet, so treat it as a live risk item rather than a certainty, and note that H2025 Exercise 1 tested Black-Scholes verbally, which is exactly the register this chapter is most likely to reappear in.

24.1 The balance sheet as a pair of options

Take a firm whose assets will be worth V at a single date, the date the firm's only debt matures. The debt promises a face value K. Limited liability means shareholders can walk away, so at maturity exactly two things can happen: either V ≥ K, the debt is repaid in full and shareholders keep the rest, or V < K, creditors take the whole firm and shareholders get nothing. Write the two claims:

ET = max(VT − K, 0)
DT = min(VT, K) = VT − max(VT − K, 0)
K is the face value of the debt and the strike price of the shareholders' option: in this chapter the two words name the same number. VT is the market value of the firm's assets at maturity. The two claims add to VT in every state, which is what makes the consistency checks work. Not on the formula sheet.

The first line is the payoff of a European call option on the firm's assets, struck at the face value of the debt, expiring when the debt matures. That is the central sentence of the chapter:

E = Call(V, K, T)   and   D = V − Call(V, K, T)
Equity is a call on firm value. Debt is what is left of the firm once that call has been sold away. The underlying is the firm's assets, not a share price, so V is the enterprise value and its volatility σ is asset volatility.

There is a second reading of the debt, and it is the more useful one for credit. Add and subtract the promised payment:

DT = K − max(K − VT, 0)
D = PV(K) − Put(V, K, T)
Risky debt = risk-free debt with the same face value, minus a put option on the firm's assets struck at K. Lending to a levered firm is lending safely and writing a put to the shareholders at the same time. PV(K) is K discounted at rf.
Why does the lender end up short a put? Limited liability is not a courtesy, it is an option the creditor has granted. The shareholders hold the right to hand over the assets in exchange for cancelling a debt of K, and they will use it exactly when the assets are worth less than K. That right is a put struck at K, written by the creditor, and its value is the whole difference between what the lender would have paid for a government bond and what he pays for this bond. Every credit concept in chapter 5 is that put seen from a different angle: the credit spread is its price expressed as a rate, the default probability is the risk-neutral chance it ends in the money, and a credit default swap, as section 24.4 shows, is a contract that simply buys it back.
Worked example 1: split a binomial firm, then read the credit numbers off it

Bergen Marine AS has assets worth V0 = 100 today. In one year they will be worth either 130 or 80. The firm has one zero-coupon bond outstanding with face value K = 90, maturing in one year. The risk-free rate is rf = 5.00%. Assume the market prices assets consistently with the firm's own traded value, so chapter 22's risk-neutral machinery applies directly to V.

Step 1 — the risk-neutral probability. ρ = [(1 + rf)V0 − Vd]/(Vu − Vd) = [1.05(100) − 80]/(130 − 80) = 25/50 = 0.5000

StateρVTDebt min(V, 90)Equity max(V − 90, 0)Put max(90 − V, 0)
Up0.500013090400
Down0.50008080010
Risk-neutral expectation10585205

Step 2 — value the two claims. Discount the risk-neutral expectations at rf:
E = 20/1.05 = 19.0476    D = 85/1.05 = 80.9524    Sum = 100.0000 = V0 ✓

Step 3 — the promised yield. The bond costs 80.9524 and promises 90 in one year:
y = 90/80.9524 − 1 = 0.111765 = 11.1765%

Step 4 — the credit spread. y − rf = 11.1765% − 5.0000% = 6.1765%

Step 5 — the risk-neutral default probability and the recovery rate. Default happens in the down state, so q = 1 − ρ = 0.5000. Creditors then collect 80 of a promised 90, so R = 80/90 = 0.8889 and the loss given default is L = 1 − R = 0.1111, which is exactly 1/9 and is worth carrying as a fraction. Chapter 5's reverse-engineering formula reproduces the price: D = K(1 − q·L)/(1 + rf) = 90(1 − 0.5000/9)/1.05 = 90(0.944444)/1.05 = 85.0000/1.05 = 80.9524 ✓

Step 6 — the expected return on debt. Now use a true probability. Suppose the down state has real probability 0.35. Expected payment = 0.65(90) + 0.35(80) = 58.5000 + 28.0000 = 86.5000, so
rD = 86.5000/80.9524 − 1 = 0.068529 = 6.8529%, and with a 5% market risk premium βD = (0.068529 − 0.050000)/0.050000 = 0.3706.

Check A (the claims add up): 19.0476 + 80.9524 = 100.0000 = V0 ✓ This one can fail, and it catches the commonest slip, forgetting the max(·, 0) in one state.

Check B (debt as risk-free minus a put): the put is worth 5/1.05 = 4.7619 and PV(K) = 90/1.05 = 85.7143, so PV(K) − Put = 85.7143 − 4.7619 = 80.9524 = D ✓ Equivalently put-call parity from chapter 21: C = P + V − PV(K) = 4.7619 + 100.0000 − 85.7143 = 19.0476 = E ✓

Check C (the three rates are ordered): rf = 5.0000% < rD = 6.8529% < y = 11.1765%. Chapter 5's decomposition must hold: the promised yield exceeds the expected return by the expected loss, and the expected return exceeds rf by a genuine risk premium ✓

ρ is not p, and only one of them prices anything Steps 1 to 5 used the risk-neutral probability ρ = 0.5000 and never touched a real probability, because prices are risk-neutral expectations discounted at rf. Step 6 needed a real probability because an expected return is a real-world average. Mixing them is the fastest way to lose a whole question: never discount risk-neutral expectations at rD, and never compute a promised yield from real probabilities. Chapter 5's shortcut rD = y − p·L is an approximation here, giving 0.111765 − 0.35(0.111111) = 0.111765 − 0.038889 = 7.2876% against the exact 6.8529%; the gap is p·L·y = 0.35 × 0.111111 × 0.111765 = 0.004346, exactly as chapter 5 warned. Use the cash-flow route when you have the payoffs, as you do in this chapter.

24.2 Yield, credit spread and default probability from Black-Scholes

The binomial split is exact but coarse. With a continuous distribution for V the same two claims are priced by the Black-Scholes formula of chapter 23, applied to the firm's assets rather than to a share. Because Black-Scholes is a continuous-time model, rf is continuously compounded here and PV(K) = K·e−rfT; say so in your answer.

E = V·N(d1) − K·e−rfT·N(d2)
d1 = [ln(V/K) + (rf + σ²/2)T] / (σ√T),    d2 = d1 − σ√T
D = V − E = K·e−rfT·N(d2) + V·N(−d1)
σ is the volatility of the firm's assets, not of its shares. N(d2) is the risk-neutral probability that the firm does not default, so N(−d2) = 1 − N(d2) is the risk-neutral default probability. The second form of D reads as: the promised payment received when solvent, plus the assets received when not.

Once you have D, the credit numbers are arithmetic. The yield is the rate that makes the promised K and the observed D agree, exactly as in chapter 5:

y = (1/T)·ln(K/D)   (continuously compounded)   or   y = (K/D)1/T − 1   (annual effective)
credit spread = y − rf, quoted on the same compounding basis
Compare like with like. A continuously compounded y must be set against a continuously compounded rf. State which convention you used and the examiner will follow you.
N(d2) is a probability, N(d1) is not The two look symmetric and they are not. N(d2) is the risk-neutral probability that VT ends above K, so N(−d2) really is a default probability, but a risk-neutral one: like chapter 5's q it already contains the risk premium and it overstates the true default frequency, often by a factor of two or more. N(d1) is not a probability at all. It is the option's delta, the number of units of the firm's assets a replicating portfolio holds, which is why it will reappear in section 24.3 as the elasticity that scales beta. Calling N(d1) "the probability of exercise" and N(−d2) "the chance the firm goes bankrupt" are the two standard ways to lose the interpretation marks on a verbal question of the H2025 Exercise 1 type.
Worked example 2: value equity and debt of a levered firm, then price its credit

Havstrøm ASA has assets with a market value of V = 500 million and asset volatility σ = 35% a year. It has one zero-coupon bond outstanding, face value K = 400 million, maturing in T = 4 years. The continuously compounded risk-free rate is rf = 3.00%. The firm's asset beta is βU = 0.85. (An exam will hand you the N(d) values, since the formula sheet no longer carries the tables.)

Step 1 — the two arguments. ln(V/K) = ln(1.25) = 0.223144. Numerator = 0.223144 + [0.030000 + 0.35²/2](4) = 0.223144 + [0.030000 + 0.061250](4) = 0.223144 + 0.365000 = 0.588144. Denominator = σ√T = 0.35(2) = 0.700000.
d1 = 0.588144/0.700000 = 0.8402    d2 = 0.8402 − 0.7000 = 0.1402

QuantityValueReading
N(d1)0.7996delta of equity with respect to firm value
N(d2)0.5558risk-neutral probability of no default
N(−d2)0.4442risk-neutral probability of default over 4 years
N(−d1)0.2004delta of debt with respect to firm value

Step 2 — present value of the promise. e−0.03(4) = e−0.12 = 0.886920, so PV(K) = 400(0.886920) = 354.7682

Step 3 — equity as a call. E = 500(0.7996) − 354.7682(0.5558) = 399.8000 − 197.1802 = 202.6198

Step 4 — debt as the residual. D = 500.0000 − 202.6198 = 297.3802

Step 5 — the promised yield. K/D = 400/297.3802 = 1.345079, ln(1.345079) = 0.296453, so
y = 0.296453/4 = 0.074113 = 7.4113% continuously compounded.
As an annual effective rate: y = 1.3450790.25 − 1 = 0.076929 = 7.6929%.

Step 6 — the credit spread. Continuously compounded: 7.4113% − 3.0000% = 4.4113%. On an annual effective basis the risk-free rate is e0.03 − 1 = 3.0455%, so the spread is 7.6929% − 3.0455% = 4.6474%. Same credit, two conventions; quote one and name it.

Step 7 — the default probability. N(−d2) = 0.4442, a 44.42% risk-neutral probability of default before year 4. The true probability is lower, because ρ overweights bad states.

Check A (the put route, an independent recombination): P = PV(K)·N(−d2) − V·N(−d1) = 354.7682(0.4442) − 500(0.2004) = 157.5880 − 100.2000 = 57.3880. Then D = PV(K) − P = 354.7682 − 57.3880 = 297.3802 ✓ identical to step 4. This catches arithmetic slips in steps 3 and 4; it cannot catch a wrong d1, because both routes use it.

Check B (the ordering): E < V, D < PV(K) = 354.7682 and y > rf. Risky debt must be worth less than a government bond with the same face value and maturity, and by exactly the value of the put, 354.7682 − 297.3802 = 57.3880 ✓

A note on rounding. Everything above uses N(d) rounded to four decimals, which is what an exam hands you. Carrying full precision instead gives E = 202.6389, D = 297.3611 and y = 7.4129%, so the rounding of the inputs moves the yield by 0.0016 percentage points. That is well inside tolerance, but say which precision you used and keep it fixed through the whole chain, because the drift compounds in a multi-part question.

Two comparative statics carry all the marks on a verbal question, and both are visible in the formula. The spread rises with leverage, measured by PV(K)/V, because the put moves towards the money. And the spread rises with asset volatility σ, because the put is an option. The grid below is computed from the same formula with T = 4 and rf = 3.00%, holding V at 100.

PV(K)/Vσ = 20%σ = 35%σ = 50%
0.500.2362%2.1074%5.2869%
0.701.2339%4.3000%8.1173%
0.903.1237%6.7441%10.7966%

One warning about reading those spreads. Three separate things push a spread up: a higher risk-neutral default probability, a lower recovery rate, and the risk premium hidden inside ρ, and only the first two are statements about the borrower's finances. Because default clusters in bad states of the world, N(−d2) sits well above the physical default probability, exactly the wedge chapter 5 found when an implied q of 8.7333% sat above a true p of 6.0000%. A spread read as a forecast will therefore always predict too many defaults. The model is built to price and to hedge, not to predict.

24.3 The beta of levered equity

Chapter 4 relevered beta with a straight line, βE = βU + (D/E)(βU − βD), and had to assume a value for βD. The option view produces both betas from the same N(d1) you already computed. Equity is a call, so a 1% move in the firm's assets moves the equity by its elasticity, delta times V over E, and beta is a measure of sensitivity to market moves, so it scales the same way:

βE = N(d1)·(V/E)·βU
βD = [1 − N(d1)]·(V/D)·βU
N(d1) is the delta of equity with respect to firm value, so 1 − N(d1) is the delta of the debt. βE = N(d1)(V/E)βU is not on the formula sheet: memorise it. Both betas are instantaneous, so they change as V moves.
Why an option's beta is its delta times its elasticity A call is a levered position in the underlying. Replicating it means holding N(d1) units of the firm's assets financed partly by borrowing, so the position has an embedded debt ratio. Systematic risk scales with how much of the underlying you effectively hold per krone invested, which is exactly N(d1)V/E. Two consequences the examiner wants named: βE is largest when the firm is close to default, because E is small while N(d1)V is not, and βD is strictly positive whenever there is any chance of default, since 1 − N(d1) > 0. Setting βD = 0 on risky debt understates βU, exactly the direction error chapter 4 warned about.
Worked example 3: betas, and what happens when the firm gets riskier

Continue with Havstrøm from worked example 2: V = 500, E = 202.6198, D = 297.3802, N(d1) = 0.7996, βU = 0.85.

Step 1 — equity beta. V/E = 500/202.6198 = 2.4677, so βE = 0.7996(2.4677)(0.85) = 1.9732 × 0.85 = 1.6772

Step 2 — debt beta. 1 − N(d1) = 0.2004 and V/D = 500/297.3802 = 1.6813, so βD = 0.2004(1.6813)(0.85) = 0.3369 × 0.85 = 0.2864

Step 3 — the market values as weights. E/V = 202.6198/500 = 0.4052 and D/V = 297.3802/500 = 0.5948

Consistency check 2: 0.4052(1.6772) + 0.5948(0.2864) = 0.6796 + 0.1704 = 0.8500 = βU ✓

But be honest about that check. It cannot fail. Substituting the two formulas gives N(d1)βU + [1 − N(d1)]βU = βU for any N(d1) whatsoever, so it verifies your arithmetic and nothing else. The same is true of chapter 4's linear relevering, which is this identity rearranged: βU + (D/E)(βU − βD) = 0.8500 + (1.4677)(0.8500 − 0.2864) = 0.8500 + 0.8272 = 1.6772 ✓ The two formulas are the same statement, and the option version's contribution is that it tells you βD instead of asking you to guess it.

Step 4 — now raise asset volatility to σ = 50%, holding V at 500. This is chapter 9's risk shifting done inside Black-Scholes. Recompute: numerator = 0.223144 + [0.030000 + 0.125000](4) = 0.223144 + 0.620000 = 0.843144, denominator = 0.50(2) = 1.000000, so d1 = 0.8431 and d2 = 0.8431 − 1.0000 = −0.1569. The new values are N(d1) = 0.8004 and N(d2) = 0.4377.
E = 500(0.8004) − 354.7682(0.4377) = 400.2000 − 155.2820 = 244.9180
D = 500.0000 − 244.9180 = 255.0820
y = ln(400/255.0820)/4 = 0.449879/4 = 0.112470 = 11.2470%, spread 8.2470%
βE = 0.8004(500/244.9180)(0.85) = 0.8004(2.0415)(0.85) = 1.6340 × 0.85 = 1.3889

Consistency check 4: total value is fixed at 500 by assumption, so this is a pure transfer. Shareholders gain 244.9180 − 202.6198 = +42.2982 and creditors lose 297.3802 − 255.0820 = −42.2982. Equal and opposite ✓ Volatility is not value, it is a redistribution, and it is why lenders write covenants.

Read the beta result carefully: βE fell from 1.6772 to 1.3889 even though the firm got riskier. Nothing is wrong. The equity is now a much bigger claim, 244.9180 rather than 202.6198, so the same asset risk is spread over more equity value; the leverage embedded in the option has gone down. The shareholders are better off in value terms and less levered at the same time, which is precisely why the transfer is attractive to them.

24.4 Credit default swaps

A credit default swap is insurance on a bond. The protection buyer pays a premium; if the borrower defaults, the protection seller makes up the shortfall between the face value and what the bond actually recovers. Compare that payoff with figure 24.1 and there is nothing new to price:

CDS payoff at maturity = max(K − VT, 0) = the put the lender wrote
upfront CDS price = Put(V, K, T) = PV(K) − D
A CDS buys back the put embedded in risky debt. Hence the no-arbitrage statement worth memorising: risky bond + CDS = risk-free bond. If the two sides do not match in the market, the difference is the CDS basis and it is a trade.

Market CDSs quote a running spread s on the notional rather than an upfront price. Equate the present value of the premiums to the present value of the protection:

s = q·L   over one period, where q is the risk-neutral default probability and L = 1 − R
The CDS spread is the risk-neutral expected loss rate per krone of notional. The bond credit spread is defined on the bond's discounted price instead, so the two are related but not equal: y − rf = q·L(1 + rf)/(1 − q·L) over one period. They converge as q·L becomes small.
Worked example 4: price the CDS on the Bergen Marine bond, three ways

Return to worked example 1: V0 = 100, up 130 or down 80, K = 90, rf = 5.00%, ρ = 0.5000, D = 80.9524, PV(K) = 85.7143. A bank offers a one-year CDS on the full 90 of notional.

Step 1 — the protection payoff, state by state. Up: the bond pays 90, no claim, 0. Down: the bond recovers 80, so the insurer pays 90 − 80 = 10. That is exactly max(K − VT, 0).

Step 2 — upfront price, route one, as an expectation. [0.5000(0) + 0.5000(10)]/1.05 = 5/1.05 = 4.7619

Step 3 — upfront price, route two, as the missing put. PV(K) − D = 85.7143 − 80.9524 = 4.7619 ✓ Identical, as it must be, and this is the answer to give when the exam hands you a bond price instead of a payoff tree.

Step 4 — upfront price, route three, as expected loss. q·L·K/(1 + rf) = 0.5000(0.111111)(90)/1.05 = 5.0000/1.05 = 4.7619 ✓

Step 5 — convert to a running spread. With the premium paid at the end of the year on a notional of 90: s(90)/1.05 = 4.7619, so s = 4.7619(1.05)/90 = 5.0000/90 = 0.055556 = 5.5556%, which is just q·L = 0.5000(0.111111) ✓ As an upfront percentage of notional it is 4.7619/90 = 5.2910%.

Check (the two spreads are consistent, not equal): the bond credit spread from worked example 1 was 6.1765%. The formula predicts q·L(1 + rf)/(1 − q·L) = 0.055556(1.05)/(1 − 0.055556) = 0.058333/0.944444 = 0.061765 = 6.1765% ✓ The bond spread is the larger of the two because it is charged on the discounted price 80.9524 while the CDS spread is charged on the full notional 90. Quoting the CDS spread as if it were the bond spread is a real error, not a rounding difference: here it is 62 basis points.

Check (the hedged position is risk-free): buy the bond for 80.9524 and the protection for 4.7619, total 85.7143, and receive 90 with certainty. Return = 90/85.7143 − 1 = 0.050000 = 5.0000% = rf ✓

Why a CDS can be read backwards, and usually is Everything in worked example 4 runs in either direction. Given a traded CDS spread you can invert s = q·L for the market's risk-neutral default probability, provided you assume a recovery rate, and the market convention is R = 40%. That is the standard use: CDS quotes are the cleanest observable price of credit risk, cleaner than bond spreads because they carry no funding or coupon effects. It is also the same logic as chapter 5's implied q, and the same logic H2025 Exercise 6 used on M&A, where an observed price was inverted to recover the market's implied probability that a deal would close. Whenever the exam gives you a price and asks what the market believes, the move is always: write the price as a probability-weighted payoff, then solve for the probability.

24.5 Convertible debt, and why it blunts risk shifting

A convertible bond lets the holder give up the promised K and take a fixed fraction of the firm's equity instead. Write that fraction as γ, so if the bond converts into n new shares and there are N old ones, γ = n/(N + n). The holder does whatever is better for him at maturity:

convertible payoff = max[ min(VT, K), γ·VT ]
convertible = straight bond + γ calls on the firm struck at K/γ
Conversion is worth it when γVT > K, that is when VT > K/γ. Below that the bond behaves exactly like straight debt; above it the holder rides the firm's value at rate γ. The second line is the decomposition to quote: a convertible is debt plus an equity option, which is why it is cheaper in coupon terms than straight debt.
Worked example 5: the same gamble, straight debt and then convertible

Sognefjell AS will be worth, in one year, whatever its chosen project delivers, in two equally likely states with risk-neutral probability ρ = 0.5000. Set rf = 0 so value equals the risk-neutral expected payoff; discounting scales every figure below by the same factor and changes no comparison. The projects are mutually exclusive and need no new investment.

ProjectUp (ρ = 0.5000)Down (ρ = 0.5000)Firm value V
S, the safe one140100120
R, the gamble19040115

Part A — straight debt with face value K = 60.

ProjectDebt min(V, 60)DEquity max(V − 60, 0)E
S60 / 6060.000080 / 4060.0000
R60 / 4050.0000130 / 065.0000

Step 1 — what the owners do. 65.0000 > 60.0000, so they take R, destroying 120 − 115 = 5 of firm value. Name it: risk shifting, or asset substitution, exactly as in chapter 9.

Consistency check 3: creditors' gain = 50.0000 − 60.0000 = −10.0000, shareholders' gain = 65.0000 − 60.0000 = +5.0000, and the sum −5.0000 is the NPV of switching to R ✓

Part B — replace the bond with a convertible, same face value 60, convertible into γ = 0.5000 of the firm. Conversion pays off when VT > K/γ = 60/0.5000 = 120.

Project and stateVTConvert?Convertible payoffEquity payoff
S, up140yes, 0.5(140) = 70 > 607070
S, down100no, 0.5(100) = 50 < 606040
R, up190yes, 0.5(190) = 95 > 609595
R, down40no, the firm defaults400

Step 2 — value the claims.
Under S: convertible = 0.5000(70) + 0.5000(60) = 65.0000, equity = 0.5000(70) + 0.5000(40) = 55.0000, sum 120.0000 ✓
Under R: convertible = 0.5000(95) + 0.5000(40) = 67.5000, equity = 0.5000(95) + 0.5000(0) = 47.5000, sum 115.0000 ✓

Step 3 — what the owners do now. 55.0000 > 47.5000, so they choose S. The gamble no longer pays them, the first-best value of 120 is restored and the agency cost of 5 disappears.

Step 4 — the decomposition, as a check on step 2. A convertible is a straight bond plus γ calls struck at K/γ = 120. Under S the call component pays 0.5000(140 − 120) = 10 in the up state and 0 down, worth 5.0000, so the convertible is 60.0000 + 5.0000 = 65.0000 ✓ Under R it pays 0.5000(190 − 120) = 35 up and 0 down, worth 17.5000, so 50.0000 + 17.5000 = 67.5000 ✓

Check: D + E = V in all four columns, and the convertible holder is better off than the straight lender under both projects, 65.0000 against 60.0000 and 67.5000 against 50.0000. That is the point: he pays for that upside at issue, so the firm raises the same money on a lower coupon.

Why conversion kills the incentive, in one line In every state where the bond converts, the shareholders' payoff is (1 − γ)VT, which is linear in firm value. Linear claim holders want the largest V, full stop, so they rank projects by NPV like an all-equity firm. Convexity is what made risk shifting profitable, and conversion removes convexity at the top exactly where a gamble would have paid. The same mechanism weakens chapter 10's debt overhang from the other end: the convertible holder is already partly an owner, so he captures less of a new project's cash flow as a windfall and the shareholders' hurdle NPV > ΔD is easier to clear. That is why convertibles are the standard instrument for young, volatile, heavily monitored firms, and why chapter 10 listed them among the contracts that pre-empt agency costs.

24.6 Coinsurance: what a merger does to the two claims

Merge two firms with imperfectly correlated cash flows and, even with no synergies whatsoever, the combined firm defaults less often than the two separately, because a good outcome at one firm now covers a shortfall at the other. Creditors of both firms gain. Since the total value has not changed, somebody must lose, and consistency check 4 tells you who before you compute anything.

Call(VA + VB) ≤ Call(VA) + Call(VB)   ⟹   Emerged ≤ EA + EB
An option on a portfolio is worth no more than a portfolio of options, with equality only under perfect positive correlation. Equity is a call, so pooling assets destroys equity value; debt is what is left, so it must gain the same amount. This is the coinsurance effect. Name it in an answer.
Worked example 6: a merger with no synergies, state by state

Two independent firms, A and B, are identical. Each will be worth 100 or 40 in one year, with risk-neutral probability 0.5000 each, and each has a zero-coupon bond outstanding with face value K = 60. Their cash flows are statistically independent. Set rf = 0 again, so values are risk-neutral expected payoffs. The two merge; the merged firm assumes both bonds, which rank equally, giving total face value 120. There are no synergies: the merged assets are simply the sum.

Step 1 — stand-alone claims, per firm.

StateρVDebt min(V, 60)Equity max(V − 60, 0)
Good0.50001006040
Bad0.500040400
Value70.000050.000020.0000

Two firms together: V = 140.0000, D = 100.0000, E = 40.0000. Each bond yields 60/50.0000 − 1 = 0.200000 = 20.0000%, a spread of 20.0000% over rf = 0, with a risk-neutral default probability of 0.5000.

Step 2 — the merged firm, all four states. Independence makes each combination equally likely at 0.2500.

State (A, B)ρVmergedDebt min(V, 120)Equity max(V − 120, 0)
Good, good0.250020012080
Good, bad0.250014012020
Bad, good0.250014012020
Bad, bad0.250080800
Value140.0000110.000030.0000

Step 3 — who gained and who lost. Creditors as a group: 110.0000 − 100.0000 = +10.0000. Shareholders as a group: 30.0000 − 40.0000 = −10.0000.

Consistency check 4 — creditors' gain = shareholders' loss when total value is fixed: firm value is 140.0000 before and after, since there are no synergies, so the merger cannot create or destroy value, only move it. +10.0000 against −10.0000 ✓ This is the check to quote by number in an exam answer, and it is also the fastest way to catch an arithmetic error: if your two changes do not cancel, you have mis-stated a payoff.

Step 4 — the split within each side. The two bonds rank equally, so in the bad-bad state they share the 80 pro rata, 40 each. Each bond is then worth 0.2500(60 + 60 + 60 + 40) = 0.2500(220) = 55.0000, a gain of 5.0000 per bond. By symmetry each shareholder group holds half the merged equity, 15.0000 against 20.0000 stand-alone, a loss of 5.0000 each. Creditors' gain equals shareholders' loss firm by firm as well ✓

Step 5 — the credit numbers. The merged bonds promise 120 and are worth 110.0000, so y = 120/110.0000 − 1 = 0.090909 = 9.0909%, down from 20.0000%. The risk-neutral default probability falls from 0.5000 to 0.2500, the single bad-bad state, while the recovery rate given default is unchanged at 80/120 = 40/60 = 0.6667. The entire improvement is fewer defaults, not better recoveries.

Check (the option route, and it is independent of steps 2 to 4): debt = PV(K) − put, and with rf = 0 the risk-free part is 60 per firm either way. Stand-alone, each put pays 0 or 20, worth 10.0000, so two puts are worth 20.0000. Merged, the put pays 120 − 80 = 40 only in the bad-bad state, worth 0.2500(40) = 10.0000. The puts lost 10.0000 of value, which is precisely the creditors' gain ✓ Equity the same way: two calls worth 20.0000 each sum to 40.0000, against a call on the pooled assets worth 30.0000 ✓

Why coinsurance is not a reason to merge The gain is real, and it goes to the wrong people. Shareholders decide on mergers and coinsurance takes 10 out of their pockets, so an acquirer who pays a premium on top is worse off still. Two responses are standard and both are examinable: lever up after the deal, issuing new debt and paying out the proceeds, which pushes the default risk back up and reclaims the transfer, or ring-fence the subsidiaries so the cash flows never actually pool. It is also the reason diversification is a weak merger motive, since shareholders can diversify by holding both shares themselves and pay no premium for it. What coinsurance does justify is more debt capacity, and therefore a larger tax shield, which is a genuine gain that chapter 7 already priced.
Back to chapters 9 and 10, forward to chapter 26 Three chapters now say the same thing in different vocabularies. Chapter 9's risk shifting is figure 24.2's arrow, a move up the volatility curve that transfers value to the equity call. Chapter 10's debt overhang is figure 24.1's flat stretch, where the equity delta N(d1) is near zero and a new krone of value lands on the lender. Coinsurance is the same transfer run backwards. Chapter 26 takes coinsurance into merger motives proper, and it matters there because H2024 explicitly banned "synergies" as an answer, so being able to write "the coinsurance effect makes the merged firm's debt safer, which raises debt capacity and the tax shield" is worth real points.
Three ways this goes wrong under exam pressure First, the underlying is firm value, not the share price, and σ is the volatility of assets. An exam that gives you equity volatility expects you to say the two differ, since equity is a levered claim and its volatility is N(d1)(V/E)σ. Second, K is the face value, so a coupon bond has to be reduced to a single promised payment before the model applies, and you should say you are approximating. Third, do not price the debt directly with Black-Scholes as if it were an option: it is not one, it is V minus a call or PV(K) minus a put, so compute the option first and subtract.
What a full answer looks like here The older papers built long chained exercises on this material: 2015 Problem 4 carried 90 points and 2016 Problem 4, 2021 MC7–9 and V2024 Problems 3 and 4 all draw on it. The pattern is always the same five moves, and each is worth its own marks: (1) state that equity is a call on firm value struck at the face value of the debt, (2) value it, (3) get the debt as V − E or as PV(K) − put and say which route you used, (4) convert to a yield and a credit spread, quoting the compounding convention, (5) close with a check the examiner recognises, either D + E = V or, in a transfer question, consistency check 4. On a verbal version of the H2025 Exercise 1 type, the marks are for the interpretation: what N(d2) is a probability of, why a risk-neutral probability is not a forecast, and why higher asset volatility helps shareholders and hurts lenders while leaving firm value alone.
↑ contents

25 · Real options

This is the one chapter you cannot skip. Real options have appeared in eight of the eleven mapped papers, they have been a fixed item since 2017, and both of Kurbatov's papers carry them at 20 points: H2024 Exercise 6 valued a real option with the risk-neutral method of chapter 22, and H2025 Exercise 5 asked for three things in a row, the value of information, an exit option, and an indifference point. That is a fifth of the paper from one chapter, and none of it is on the formula sheet. Everything here is a variation on a single idea: a manager is not a machine that runs the NPV rule once at date zero, he can wait, expand, shrink, abandon, restart, and each of those rights is an option whose value the plain NPV calculation misses.

25.1 Why a positive NPV can still mean wait

The NPV rule you learned in chapter 1 answers one question: is this project, taken today, worth more than it costs? It quietly assumes two things that are often false. It assumes the decision is now or never, and it assumes the decision is irreversible in one direction only, that you commit and then live with the outcome. Relax either and the rule needs a correction.

Three ingredients have to be present before an option has any value at all, and the examiner expects you to name them:

IngredientWhat it meansIf it is missing
UncertaintySomething you do not yet know will be revealedNothing to learn, so waiting only delays cash flow
Information arriving over timeWaiting actually resolves some of that uncertaintyYou wait and are none the wiser
Irreversibility, or a cost of reversingInvesting, or exiting, cannot be undone for freeCommit now and simply undo it later at no cost

The standard catalogue is short, and every exam question is one of these:

Real optionFinancial analogueHeld by
Wait, delay, defer investingAmerican call on the project, struck at the investment costWhoever controls the timing
Expand, or invest in stagesCall on a follow-on projectThe firm that took stage one
Abandon, exit, sell the assetsPut on the project, struck at the salvage valueThe operator
Shut down and restartA strip of options, one per period, with switching costsThe operator
Switch inputs or outputsExchange optionThe flexible plant
Why waiting can beat a positive NPV Investing today buys you the average outcome: the good states and the bad states, weighted by their probabilities, all of it.
