Waiting buys you the good states only, because once you know which state you are in you can decline the bad ones, and NPV cannot go below zero when you can simply not invest. That is worth something, and what it costs you is a year of cash flow plus a year of discounting. The option to wait is valuable exactly when the first effect beats the second, which happens when uncertainty is large, when the bad state is really bad, and when the project is not about to be taken by a competitor. Notice the shape: max(0, NPV) is a convex function of the underlying, so this is the same convexity that made shareholders love risk in chapters 9 and 24.
E[max(0, NPV)] is not max(0, E[NPV]) Every number in this chapter is a difference between those two expressions, so if you collapse them the chapter disappears. max(0, E[NPV]) is what you get by averaging first and deciding afterwards: one decision, taken in the dark, for all states at once. E[max(0, NPVi)] is what you get by deciding state by state after seeing which state you are in. The second is never smaller, and the gap between them is the entire value of flexibility. Write the max inside the expectation and you have already earned most of the marks; write it outside and you have computed the answer to the question the exam is not asking.

25.2 The value of the option to wait

Set the problem up as a two-date tree. Today, date 0, you can invest and get NPVnow. Or you can wait one year, learn which state i has occurred, and then invest only where it pays. The value of waiting is the discounted expectation of the state-by-state decisions, and the option is what waiting adds:

value of waiting = Σi pi·max(0, NPVi) / (1 + r)
value of the option to wait = Σi pi·max(0, NPVi) / (1 + r) − max(0, NPVnow)
pi are the real probabilities and r is the project's cost of capital. NPVi is the project's NPV measured at the date you would invest in state i, so it is discounted once more by (1 + r) to bring it to today. The outer max(0, ·) matters: if the project is not worth doing today, the alternative to waiting is doing nothing, worth zero, not a negative number. None of this is on the formula sheet.
Two discounting traps, and both are cheap to avoid First, do not discount NPVi twice. It is already a present value as of the investment date, so it needs exactly one more factor of (1 + r) to reach today. Second, state your timing assumption in one sentence. The formula above assumes the whole project shifts by a year, cost and cash flows together, so NPVi is unchanged in size and only its date moves. If instead the cash flows are tied to fixed calendar dates, waiting means losing the first year's flow permanently, and you must rebuild NPVi from the remaining stream. Examiners accept either reading; they do not accept silence.
Worked example 1: a project worth 100 today that you should not build today

Nordvind AS can build a wind farm for I = 1000, payable at once. The farm then produces a cash flow every year forever, starting one year after it is built. The power price is unknown: from next year on it is high, giving 150 a year forever, with probability 0.50, or low, giving 70 a year forever, with probability 0.50. The regime, once revealed, is permanent. The cost of capital is r = 10.00%, and the 1000 cost is the same next year.

Step 1 — the naive answer, invest now. The expected annual cash flow is 0.50(150) + 0.50(70) = 110, so
NPVnow = 110/0.10 − 1000 = 1100 − 1000 = 100. Positive, so the plain NPV rule says build.

Step 2 — the state-by-state NPVs, measured at the investment date.
High: NPVh = 150/0.10 − 1000 = 1500 − 1000 = +500
Low: NPVl = 70/0.10 − 1000 = 700 − 1000 = −300

StatepiCash flow per yearNPVi at the investment dateInvest if you know?
High0.5000150+500yes
Low0.500070−300no, take 0

Step 3 — check that step 1 is consistent with step 2. Investing today means accepting both branches: 0.50(500) + 0.50(−300) = 250 − 150 = 100 ✓ the same NPVnow. Getting these two routes to agree is worth doing, because it proves your state NPVs are right before you build the option on top of them.

Step 4 — the value of waiting. Wait a year, then invest only in the high state:
[0.50(500) + 0.50(0)]/1.10 = 250/1.10 = 227.2727

Step 5 — the option to wait. 227.2727 − max(0, 100) = 127.2727. Positive, so wait, even though today's NPV is positive. State it in words: the project is worth 227.27 held as an option and 100 exercised immediately, so exercising immediately destroys 127.27.

Check (decompose the 127.2727 and see where it comes from): waiting does two things. It avoids the low branch, worth 0.50(300) = +150.0000 in today's money, since that branch's NPV was already a date-0 figure. And it delays the good branch by a year, costing 0.50(500) − 0.50(500)/1.10 = 250.0000 − 227.2727 = −22.7273. Net: 150.0000 − 22.7273 = 127.2727 ✓ Same number by a different route, and the decomposition is itself worth marks because it names the two forces.

When the probabilities must be risk-neutral instead Worked example 1 uses real probabilities and a risk-adjusted r, which is the convention in most of the listed papers. H2024 Exercise 6 did it the other way: when the uncertainty is the price of something traded, you build the tree in the underlying, extract ρ = [(1 + rf)S0 − Sd]/(Su − Sd) exactly as in chapter 22, and discount at rf. Which method the exam wants is signalled by the data: a given probability and a cost of capital means the method here, a traded price with up and down moves and a risk-free rate means risk-neutral valuation. Never mix them, and never discount a risk-neutral expectation at anything but rf.
Why the single discount rate is an approximation, not a theorem Applying a single r to both branches is exactly that, an approximation. An option on a project is riskier than the project itself, so its true cost of capital is higher and changes as the tree moves. The course accepts the single-rate calculation because it keeps the arithmetic examinable, and the risk-neutral route is the clean fix when the data supports it. If you have half a sentence to spare, say this. It reads as understanding rather than as hedging.

25.3 The value of information

Now change the question. Instead of waiting for the world to reveal itself, you can buy the answer: a survey, a test well, a pilot plant, a market study. If it is perfect and instantaneous, its value is simply what you would have done differently:

value of perfect information = E[max(0, NPVi)] − max(0, E[NPV])
The first term is the informed strategy, deciding state by state. The second is the uninformed one, a single decision taken on the average. No discounting appears, because the information arrives now and the project is undertaken now. This is the upper bound on what any study can be worth: imperfect information is worth less, never more.

If the study takes time, everything it can teach you arrives a year late, and the whole informed strategy must be discounted back. That turns the value of information into the value of the option to wait from section 25.2:

value of information delayed by one year = E[max(0, NPVi)]/(1 + r) − max(0, E[NPV])
The two expressions differ only by the discount factor on the informed term, so the cost of the delay is E[max(0, NPVi)]·[1 − 1/(1 + r)]. If the uninformed decision is to reject, the second term is 0 and the value of information is the whole informed value.
Worked example 2: what a market study is worth, now and in a year

Fjelltind AS is considering a product launch costing I = 500 today. The present value of the cash flows depends on a demand state which the firm cannot currently observe. The cost of capital is r = 12.00%.

Demand statepiPV of cash flowsNPVi = PV − 500Informed decision
Strong0.30900+400launch
Base0.50520+20launch
Weak0.20200−300do not launch

Step 1 — the uninformed decision. E[NPV] = 0.30(400) + 0.50(20) + 0.20(−300) = 120.0000 + 10.0000 − 60.0000 = 70.0000. Positive, so with no information the firm launches and the launch is worth 70.0000. Note that max(0, E[NPV]) = 70.0000 here, because the uninformed decision happens to be "go".

Step 2 — the informed strategy. E[max(0, NPVi)] = 0.30(400) + 0.50(20) + 0.20(0) = 120.0000 + 10.0000 + 0.0000 = 130.0000

Step 3 — the value of perfect information, available today. 130.0000 − 70.0000 = 60.0000. That is the most the firm should ever pay for the study. Offer it at 65 and refuse.

Check: information changes exactly one decision, the weak state, where it stops you losing 300. Its value must therefore be pweak × 300 = 0.20(300) = 60.0000 ✓ This check is genuinely independent of steps 1 and 2 and it catches the commonest error, dropping the max(0, ·) so that the weak state contributes −300 to both terms and the value of information collapses to zero.

Step 4 — now suppose the study takes a year. The launch, and all its cash flows, shift with it. The informed strategy is worth 130.0000/1.12 = 116.0714 today, so
value of the delayed information = 116.0714 − 70.0000 = 46.0714

Step 5 — read the difference. The delay costs 60.0000 − 46.0714 = 13.9286, which is 130.0000(1 − 1/1.12) = 130.0000(0.107143) = 13.9286 ✓ a fall of 23.2143% in the value of the study. Waiting a year for the same answer is worth 46.0714, so a study that costs 50 is worth buying only if it can be done immediately.

Step 6 — the same numbers, one assumption changed. Suppose the base state's PV were 420 rather than 520, so NPVbase = −80. Then E[NPV] = 0.30(400) + 0.50(−80) + 0.20(−300) = 120.0000 − 40.0000 − 60.0000 = 20.0000, still a launch, and E[max(0, NPVi)] = 120.0000, so the study is worth 120.0000 − 20.0000 = 100.0000. More states in which the uninformed decision is wrong means more valuable information. If E[NPV] had been negative, the uninformed firm would do nothing and the study would be worth the full 120.0000.

Why information is worth nothing when it changes no decision Value comes from the action the information permits, never from the knowledge itself. If you would launch in every state, learning the state changes nothing and the study is worth zero, however precise it is. If you would launch in none, likewise. Information is valuable in proportion to how close the uninformed decision was to being wrong, which is why its value is largest for marginal projects and smallest for obvious ones. Two consequences to write down in an exam: a study can never have negative value, since you can ignore it, and its value is capped by the perfect-information figure even if the seller promises certainty.

25.4 The option to abandon or exit

The mirror image of waiting is quitting. A project already running is not a life sentence: you can usually stop it, sell the assets, sublet the premises, or hand back the licence. That right is a put option on the project, struck at whatever you can walk away with. The exam form is almost always the same, and it is the form H2025 Exercise 5 used: a perpetuity that looks bad in one branch is replaced by a truncated annuity plus a salvage value.

value at the exit date = max( PV(continuing), salvage value S )
bad branch, no exit: C/r   →   bad branch with exit at date n: C·[1 − (1 + r)−n]/r + S/(1 + r)n
C is the cash flow per period while you keep operating and S is what you get on exit. The perpetuity from chapter 1 becomes the annuity from chapter 1, truncated at the exit date, with the salvage value discounted from that date. If exit is immediate, n = 0 and the perpetuity is replaced by S alone.
Why the exit option is a put, and why that tells you when it is valuable On the exit date you hold something worth PV(continuing) and you have the right, not the duty, to exchange it for S. That is a put struck at S written on the project. Everything you know about puts from chapters 21 and 24 carries over: it is worth more when the project's value is volatile, when S is large relative to what continuing is worth, and when the exit date is far away. It also explains the standard exam sentence: an exit option raises the value of the project without changing any cash flow, because it truncates the left tail. Assets that are easy to resell, standardised ships, generic machinery, unspecialised property, are worth more than their cash flows suggest for exactly this reason.
Worked example 3: a facility you can sell, and what a lock-in costs

Havbruk AS can build a fish farm for I = 1200 today. From date 1 the annual cash flow is 200 forever if the licence regime stays favourable, probability 0.60, and 40 forever if it tightens, probability 0.40. The regime is revealed by the date-1 cash flow itself and is then permanent. The cost of capital is r = 10.00%. At any date from 1 onwards the whole facility can be sold to a competitor for S = 700.

Step 1 — value at date 1 in each branch, assuming the firm is committed to operating. At date 1 the firm has just received that year's cash flow and owns a perpetuity of the same amount starting at date 2:
Favourable: 200 + 200/0.10 = 200 + 2000 = 2200
Tight: 40 + 40/0.10 = 40 + 400 = 440

Step 2 — the committed value. [0.60(2200) + 0.40(440)]/1.10 = [1320.0000 + 176.0000]/1.10 = 1496.0000/1.10 = 1360.0000, so NPV = 1360.0000 − 1200 = 160.0000. The project is worth doing even with no flexibility.

Step 3 — now allow the exit. At date 1, compare continuing with selling:
Favourable: continuing is worth 2000 against a salvage of 700, so keep operating, branch value 2200.
Tight: continuing is worth 400 against a salvage of 700, so sell, branch value 40 + 700 = 740.

Branch at date 1piContinueSell for 700DecisionBranch value
Favourable0.60200 + 2000 = 2200200 + 700 = 900continue2200
Tight0.4040 + 400 = 44040 + 700 = 740sell740

Step 4 — value with the exit option. [0.60(2200) + 0.40(740)]/1.10 = [1320.0000 + 296.0000]/1.10 = 1616.0000/1.10 = 1469.0909, so NPV = 269.0909.

Step 5 — the option's value. 1469.0909 − 1360.0000 = 109.0909

Check: the option changes one branch only, and by the difference between salvage and continuation there: 0.40(700 − 400)/1.10 = 0.40(300)/1.10 = 120.0000/1.10 = 109.0909 ✓ Independent of step 4 and it catches a mis-stated branch value.

Step 6 — a three-year lock-in, where the truncated annuity appears. Suppose a supply contract forbids exit before date 3. In the tight branch the firm must run at 40 for dates 1, 2 and 3, then sell. The annuity factor from chapter 1 is [1 − 1.10−3]/0.10 = [1 − 0.751315]/0.10 = 2.486852, so measured at date 0:
tight branch = 40(2.486852) + 700/1.10³ = 99.4741 + 700(0.751315) = 99.4741 + 525.9204 = 625.3945
Against the same branch with immediate exit, 740/1.10 = 672.7273, and with no exit at all, 440/1.10 = 400.0000.

Step 7 — value under the lock-in. 0.60(2200)/1.10 + 0.40(625.3945) = 1200.0000 + 250.1578 = 1450.1578, NPV = 250.1578, so the option is worth 1450.1578 − 1360.0000 = 90.1578 and the three-year lock-in costs 1469.0909 − 1450.1578 = 18.9331.

Check: the three values of the tight branch must be ordered 400.0000 < 625.3945 < 672.7273, no exit worst, immediate exit best, lock-in in between ✓ If your locked-in figure falls outside that range you have mis-set the annuity or the discounting of S.

25.5 Switching costs and the band of inaction

Shutting down and reopening are rarely free. Mothballing a plant costs money, so does restarting it, and once you put a number on both the decision rule stops being "close whenever cash flow is negative". You keep running at a loss for a while, because closing and possibly reopening costs more than the losses you avoid. That stubbornness has a name, hysteresis, and it is the whole point of the switching-cost question.

close now if: PV(losses avoided) > cost of closing + PV(expected cost of reopening)
Both sides are present values as of the decision date. The reopening cost enters weighted by the probability that you will in fact want to restart, which is what makes the problem a tree rather than a comparison of two numbers.
Worked example 4: mothball the mine, or run it at a loss?

Kobbergruva AS has a concession with exactly two years left, so cash flows arrive at dates 1 and 2 and the mine is then handed back at no cost. The mine is open today. This year's price is already known to be low: operating through year 1 produces −40. Next year's price is high with probability 0.50, giving +60, or low again with probability 0.50, giving −40. Mothballing costs 20, paid when the mine is closed; reopening costs 30, paid when it is restarted; a mothballed mine produces nothing and costs nothing. The cost of capital is r = 8.00%. Decisions are made at date 0 for year 1 and at date 1 for year 2, after the year-2 price is known.

Step 1 — strategy A, keep operating through year 1, then decide. Year-1 cash flow is −40. At date 1:
High: continue and collect 60 at date 2, worth 60/1.08 = 55.5556, against closing for −20. Continue.
Low: continue and lose 40 at date 2, worth −40/1.08 = −37.0370, against closing for −20. Close.
Value at date 1 = −40 + 0.50(55.5556) + 0.50(−20) = −40.0000 + 27.7778 − 10.0000 = −22.2222
Value at date 0 = −22.2222/1.08 = −20.5761

Step 2 — strategy B, mothball now. Pay 20 at date 0, produce nothing in year 1. At date 1:
High: reopen for 30 and collect 60 at date 2: −30 + 55.5556 = +25.5556. Worth doing.
Low: stay closed: 0.
Value at date 1 = 0.50(25.5556) + 0.50(0) = 12.7778
Value at date 0 = −20.0000 + 12.7778/1.08 = −20.0000 + 11.8313 = −8.1687

StrategyYear 1Date 1, highDate 1, lowValue at date 0
A: keep the mine open−4055.5556−20.0000−20.5761
B: mothball now−20 at date 025.55560.0000−8.1687

Step 3 — the decision. −8.1687 beats −20.5761, so mothball now, and the flexibility is worth 12.4074 relative to grinding on. Both numbers are negative because the mine is in a bad patch with two years to run; the question is which loss is smaller, and answering "neither, so shut permanently" would be wrong, since B keeps the profitable high branch alive.

Step 4 — what the switching costs themselves cost. If closing and reopening were free, the firm would simply be idle in year 1 and open in year 2 only if the price were high: 0.50(55.5556)/1.08 = 27.7778/1.08 = 25.7202. The switching costs therefore destroy 25.7202 − (−8.1687) = 33.8889 of value. That is the number to quote when a question asks what flexibility is worth or what an inflexible technology costs.

Step 5 — where the band of inaction shows up. Look again at the low branch at date 1 in strategy A. Running loses 37.0370 in present value against 20.0000 to close, so you close. But suppose the low price cost only 15 a year: running would then be worth −15/1.08 = −13.8889 against −20.0000 to close, so the firm keeps operating at a loss. Between those two cases lies a band where cash flow is negative and shutting down is still wrong. Name it hysteresis, and note that it works in both directions: the same firm will not reopen the moment the price turns positive either, because reopening costs 30.

Check: every strategy must be evaluated with the same discounting convention, cash flows at the end of each year and switching costs at the moment of the decision. Recomputing A with the date-1 closing cost discounted a second time would give −40 + 27.7778 − 18.5185 = −30.7407 at date 1, a different and wrong answer. Say where each payment lands on the timeline before you start.

Why switching costs make firms look irrational, and why they are not An outside observer sees a mine losing money and staying open, or a profitable price and an idle plant, and concludes that management is asleep. It is not. Each switch consumes real resources and buys an option position that may have to be paid for again in a year, so the rule is not "operate when the margin is positive" but "switch when the gain exceeds the round trip". The wider the switching costs, the wider the band, and the longer the observed inertia. The same logic explains why firms hold on to loss-making divisions, why exit from an industry lags the price collapse that caused it, and why a plant designed to be cheaply switchable is worth more than one that is not.

25.6 Solving for the indifference point

The last standard move, and the one H2025 Exercise 5 finished on, is to run the calculation backwards. Instead of asking which strategy wins at the given numbers, the question fixes everything except one input, calls it X, and asks for the value at which you no longer care. The method is fixed:

write both strategies as functions of X  →  set them equal  →  solve  →  check the solution lies in its own branch interval
The max(0, ·) operators make each strategy piecewise linear in X, so an algebraic solution is only valid inside the interval where the same branches are taken. This is the same discipline chapter 9 used to find the face value at which risk shifting begins, and it is where the marks are lost.
Worked example 5: two indifference points in the Nordvind problem

Back to worked example 1: the wind farm gives 150 a year forever in the high state and 70 in the low state, each with probability 0.50, r = 10.00%, and at I = 1000 waiting beat investing by 127.2727.

Part A — at what investment cost I would you be indifferent?

Step 1 — write both strategies as functions of I.
Invest now: NPVnow(I) = 1100 − I
Wait: [0.50·max(0, 1500 − I) + 0.50·max(0, 700 − I)]/1.10

Step 2 — pick the interval. For 700 < I < 1500 the high state is still worth building and the low state is not, so the wait branch is 0.50(1500 − I)/1.10. Below 700 both states are profitable, above 1500 neither is. Do the algebra inside one interval only.

Step 3 — set them equal.
1100 − I = 0.50(1500 − I)/1.10
1.10(1100 − I) = 750 − 0.50I
1210 − 1.10I = 750 − 0.50I
460 = 0.60I   ⟹   I* = 766.6667

Step 4 — verify, and check the interval. 766.6667 lies in (700, 1500) ✓ At that cost, investing now gives 1100 − 766.6667 = 333.3333 and waiting gives 0.50(1500 − 766.6667)/1.10 = 366.6667/1.10 = 333.3333 ✓ equal, as required.

Investment cost IInvest nowWaitBetter
600500.0000454.5455invest now
720380.0000354.5455invest now
766.6667333.3333333.3333indifferent
800300.0000318.1818wait
1000100.0000227.2727wait

Step 5 — say which side is which. Below I* = 766.6667 build immediately, above it wait. The intuition is worth a sentence: a cheap project has little to lose in the low state, so the insurance the option provides is not worth a year of delay.

Part B — hold I at 1000 and solve for the low-state cash flow X instead.

Step 6 — the two functions. With cash flows of 150 and X,
Invest now: [0.50(150) + 0.50X]/0.10 − 1000 = 750 + 5X − 1000 = 5X − 250
Wait, for X < 100, where the low state is still not worth building: [0.50(500) + 0.50(0)]/1.10 = 227.2727, which does not depend on X at all.

Step 7 — solve. 5X − 250 = 227.2727 ⟹ 5X = 477.2727 ⟹ X* = 95.4545, and 95.4545 < 100 ✓ so the interval assumption holds.

Step 8 — check the other interval too, because it is free. For X ≥ 100 the low state is worth building, so waiting gives [0.50(500) + 0.50(10X − 1000)]/1.10 = (5X − 250)/1.10, which is exactly the invest-now value divided by 1.10. Waiting is then strictly worse whenever the project is worth doing at all, so the option to wait is worthless once every state is profitable, and there is no second crossing ✓

Step 9 — the answer in words. If the low state pays more than 95.4545 a year, build now; below that, wait. At the original 70 the firm waits, consistent with worked example 1 ✓ And at exactly 100 the option value is zero, which is the general result: the option to wait is worth nothing when there is no state in which you would decline.

How the 20 points are actually awarded The listed sittings, 2017H MC1–5, 2020 Problem 3, 2021 Problem 4, 2023 Problem 3, H2024 Exercise 6 and H2025 Exercise 5, all reward the same five habits. (1) Compute the static NPV first and say what the naive rule would do, because the exam usually asks for it as a sub-question and it anchors everything after. (2) Draw the tree, with squares for decisions and circles for chance, and put the numbers on it. (3) Take the maximum inside the tree, at each node, then discount, never the other way round. (4) Report the option's value as a difference between two strategies you have both computed, not as a single mysterious number. (5) Answer the question that was asked: if it asks whether to invest, write "wait" or "invest now" in words, and if it asks for an indifference point, state which side of it does what. On a value-of-information question, add the sentence that the answer is the most the firm should pay for the study.
Where this connects Chapter 1 gives you the perpetuity and annuity formulas that every branch value here is built from, and chapter 22 gives you the risk-neutral alternative that H2024 Exercise 6 required. Chapter 24 explains why the shapes recur: the option to wait is a call on the project struck at the investment cost, the option to abandon is a put struck at the salvage value, and the reason a levered firm's shareholders sometimes exercise these options badly is the agency conflict of chapters 9 and 10. Chapter 28 puts the whole routine into a timed answer template.
The four errors that cost the most here Averaging before taking the maximum, which erases the option. Discounting NPVi twice, or forgetting to discount it at all. Solving an indifference equation outside the interval where its branch assumptions hold, so that the answer is arithmetically right and economically impossible. And forgetting that the alternative to a bad project is zero, not a negative number, so that max(0, ·) is missing where the reference strategy is "do nothing". Each of these produces a plausible-looking number, which is exactly why the examiner can price them so precisely.

Part VI — Transactions and governance

↑ contents

26 · Mergers and acquisitions

Both of Kurbatov's papers put M&A on the front page and on the back page. H2024 opened with Exercise 1, twelve verbal points, and forbade the word "synergies" outright, so the whole answer had to come from the other motives. H2025 closed with Exercise 6, twenty points, and asked for the probability the market assigned to a deal completing, backed out of four share prices and a risk-free rate. Those two questions have almost nothing in common except the topic, which tells you what this chapter has to deliver: a repertoire of motives you can argue without ever saying "synergies", and the full price arithmetic of a stock swap, including the two questions candidates confuse most, the offered premium and the actual premium.

26.1 The transaction, and what the two share prices do

An acquirer buys a target. If the target's board agrees the deal is friendly, and the target's shareholders vote on it; if the board refuses, the acquirer can go over its head with a tender offer, an open invitation to every target shareholder to sell at a stated price, which is what makes a takeover hostile. Payment is cash, acquirer shares, or a mix. The rest of this chapter is mostly about the share-exchange case, because that is the case the formula sheet supports and the case both Kurbatov papers used.

Three empirical facts frame every exam question here, and you should be able to state them in one sentence each.

FactTypical magnitudeWhat it tells you
Target shareholders gain a lotpremium of roughly 20% to 40% over the pre-announcement priceThe target's price jumps on announcement, and jumps to near the offer, not to it
Acquirer shareholders gain about nothingannouncement return close to zero, and negative on average when the deal is paid in stockWhatever value the deal creates is largely competed away in the price
Deals cluster in wavespeaks in market booms, concentrated by industryBoth a technology or regulation shock story and an overvaluation story fit
Why does the acquirer capture so little of the gain? Because the target's shareholders can refuse. Consider a raider who can lift a widely held target from 45 to 60 per share. Each individual holder owns a negligible fraction, so his decision to tender cannot change whether the bid succeeds. If he expects it to succeed, holding out is worth 60 to him and tendering at anything below 60 is worse, so he holds out. Every holder reasons the same way, the bid fails at any price under 60, and the raider must offer essentially the whole improvement to get the shares. This is the free-rider problem in takeovers, and it explains both large premiums and near-zero acquirer returns from one mechanism. The standard escapes are to build a secret toehold before announcing, so part of the gain accrues to shares already owned, and to make the bid effectively coercive, for example by freezing out non-tendering holders afterwards.
The target's post-announcement price is not the offer price It sits below the offer, and the gap is not a rounding error. It is the market discounting the possibility that the deal never closes: a competition authority blocks it, financing collapses, either set of shareholders votes it down. Section 26.6 turns that gap into a number, and it was worth twelve points in H2025. If a question hands you a post-announcement target price and you treat it as the offer, you have thrown away the only information the question contained.

26.2 Why one firm buys another, without saying "synergies"

Synergies mean the combined firm's cash flows exceed the sum of the two stand-alone streams, through cost savings, cross-selling or scale. It is the honest first answer, and it is exactly the answer H2024 Exercise 1 refused to accept. So learn the rest. Each row below is a mechanism with a direction: who gains, who loses, and under what condition.

MotiveThe mechanismDoes it create value?The condition or the catch
UndervaluationThe acquirer believes the market price understates the target's true worth, so buying it is a positive-NPV investment with no operating change at allNot for society, but yes for the acquirer's shareholdersRequires genuinely superior information. Plausible for a rival in the same industry, implausible as a general claim, and a claim the target's board will contest
Market powerRemoving a competitor raises prices or lowers input prices, so the combined firm earns rentsYes for the shareholders, at customers' expenseThis is precisely what competition authorities exist to block, so the larger the effect the lower the probability the deal closes
TaxTwo channels: a profitable acquirer can use a target's accumulated tax losses immediately, and the combined firm's steadier cash flow supports more debt, so PV(TS) rises (k7)Yes, a genuine transfer from the tax authorityLoss carry-forwards are usually restricted precisely to stop acquisitions motivated purely by them. The debt-capacity gain requires the firm actually to lever up afterwards
Efficiency: the disciplinary takeoverThe target is run badly, the acquirer replaces its management and runs the same assets better. This is the market for corporate control (k27)Yes, real operating valueThe threat only works if takeovers are possible, which is why the defences in k27 are a governance issue and not a legal footnote
Access to capitalA financially constrained target passes up positive-NPV projects because it cannot fund them, whether from debt overhang (k10) or adverse selection on an equity issue (k12). A cash-rich acquirer's balance sheet unlocks themYes, the NPV of the projects that now get fundedYou must be able to name the constraint. "Deep pockets" alone is not a mechanism, since a good project can normally be financed by someone
Buy rather than buildAcquiring a licence, a patent portfolio, a distribution network or a trained team is cheaper and faster than building one, and avoids adding capacity to the industryYes, if the price is below the build costThe relevant comparison is the price paid including the premium against the cost of building, not against the target's stand-alone value
Diversification and coinsuranceTwo imperfectly correlated cash flow streams pooled behind one balance sheet default less often, so the debt of both firms becomes safer (k24)No. Total value is unchanged; value moves from shareholders to creditorsConsistency check 4 applies directly. Shareholders can diversify far more cheaply by holding both shares, and pay no premium for it
Agency: empire buildingA larger firm brings the CEO a larger salary, more status and more security, so a manager sitting on free cash flow acquires even at a negative NPV (k11)No. Value is destroyedThe prediction is testable: cash-rich firms, few investment opportunities, weak governance, and a negative acquirer announcement return
Hubris and the winner's curseSeveral bidders each estimate the target's value with error. The one who wins is disproportionately the one who overestimated most, so the winning bid is biased high even if every bidder is honest (k14)No. The acquirer overpaysHubris adds a manager who does not believe he can be wrong. The remedy is to shade the bid for the fact of winning, which is exactly what an overconfident CEO will not do
Why coinsurance is a transfer and not a gain, in one line of arithmetic k24 works it state by state: two independent firms, each worth 70.0000 with debt worth 50.0000 and equity worth 20.0000, merge with no synergies at all. Combined firm value is unchanged at 140.0000, but the debt is now worth 110.0000 and the equity 30.0000. Creditors gained 10.0000 and shareholders lost 10.0000. That is consistency check 4, creditors' gain equals shareholders' loss when total value is fixed, and it is the fastest way to catch an arithmetic slip in this kind of question: if your two changes do not cancel, a payoff is mis-stated. The examiner wants the label as well as the number, so write "the coinsurance effect". V2024 Problem 4 was exactly this computation.
How H2024 Exercise 1 was actually shaped, and how to stock the four cells Twelve points, and the question asked for four reasons in a grid: one reason the deal could be good for the acquirer, one bad for the acquirer, one good for the target, one bad for the target, at one to two sentences each, with synergies excluded. Prepare the grid rather than a list. Good for the acquirer: undervaluation; a tax shield from the target's losses or from the extra debt capacity; taking over badly run assets and running them better; buying a capability cheaper than building it. Bad for the acquirer: overpayment through the winner's curse or hubris; an empire-building deal at a negative NPV; coinsurance handing value to the creditors of both firms; a stock-financed offer that the market reads as a signal that the acquirer's own shares are overvalued. Good for the target: a 20% to 40% premium in cash, immediately and with certainty; relief of a financing constraint that was blocking its own projects; better management and better capital allocation; for an undiversified founder, an escape from having his whole fortune in one firm. Bad for the target: loss of independence, of jobs and of the private benefits of control; a weak auction with only one bidder, so the premium is small; being paid in acquirer stock that turns out to be overvalued; a coercive tender offer that pressures holders into tendering below the value they would get by holding out.
Treating diversification as a benefit because it reduces risk It does reduce risk, and that is not the same as creating value. Two mistakes hide inside the intuition. First, from k3, only systematic risk is priced: merging two firms diversifies away the part of the risk that shareholders were never compensated for in the first place, so the discount rate on the combined assets is the value-weighted average of the two, unchanged. Second, the risk reduction that does have a price consequence, the reduced probability of default, benefits creditors, and shareholders pay for it. Coinsurance is not a merger motive for shareholders unless the firm relevers afterwards to reclaim the transfer, or unless you count the larger tax shield the extra debt capacity buys. Say which of those you mean and the answer is worth full marks; say "diversification reduces risk, so it is good" and it is worth nothing.

26.3 The stock swap: exchange ratio, ownership, the two NPVs

Fix the notation, because every quantitative M&A question in this course is built from it. NA and NT are the two share counts, PA and PT the pre-announcement share prices, A = NAPA and T = NTPT the two stand-alone equity values, and S the synergies. The acquirer offers ER of its own shares for each target share:

x = ER × NT  ·  y = x/(NA + x)  ·  Pnew = (A + T + S)/(NA + x)
NPVA = (1 − y)(A + T + S) − A  ·  NPVT = y(A + T + S) − T
x is the number of new acquirer shares issued, y the fraction of the merged firm the target's former shareholders end up owning, Pnew the merged share price. The two NPVs and the exchange-ratio bound below are printed on the formula sheet; x, y and Pnew are not, so you supply them. A and T must be the stand-alone values: substituting a post-announcement market capitalisation double counts the synergy you are about to add.

Two identities follow immediately, and both are worth memorising because they are free consistency checks:

NPVA + NPVT = S   and   NPVA = NA(Pnew − PA)
The first says the deal divides the synergy and nothing else: every krone the acquirer gains beyond S is a krone the target loses. It is an accounting identity and cannot fail, so use it to locate a slip in x or y, never as evidence that the answer is right. The second says the acquirer's NPV is simply its old shares' price change, which gives you a second route to NPVA that does not pass through y.

Now the bargaining range. The acquirer will not pay so much that its own NPV turns negative, which caps ER; the target will not accept so little that its NPV turns negative, which floors it:

ER < [(T + S)/A] × (NA/NT)   equivalently   ERmax = (PT + S/NT)/PA
ERmin = [T/(A + S)] × (NA/NT)
The first line is the bound as it appears on the formula sheet, and the second form is the one to remember: the most the acquirer can pay per target share is the target's own price plus the entire synergy per target share, converted into acquirer shares at the acquirer's price. The floor is not on the sheet, but it is the same derivation with S moved from the numerator to the denominator, and exam questions do ask for the range rather than the cap.
Worked example 1: a share exchange from end to end

Nordvik Industrier ASA has 20 million shares trading at 150 NOK. It offers ER = 0.45 of its own shares for each share of Sørhavn Marine AS, which has 10 million shares trading at 60 NOK. Management expects synergies with a present value of S = 240 million NOK. All figures in millions of NOK.

InputValue
NA, PA20.00 · 150.00
NT, PT10.00 · 60.00
A = NAPA3000.00
T = NTPT600.00
S240.00
ER0.4500

Step 1 — new shares and total value. x = 0.4500 × 10.00 = 4.50 million new shares, so the merged firm has 20.00 + 4.50 = 24.50 million shares and is worth A + T + S = 3000.00 + 600.00 + 240.00 = 3840.00.

Step 2 — ownership and the merged price. y = 4.50/24.50 = 0.18367347, and Pnew = 3840.00/24.50 = 156.7347 NOK.

Step 3 — the two NPVs.
NPVA = (1 − 0.18367347)(3840.00) − 3000.00 = 3134.6939 − 3000.00 = +134.6939
NPVT = 0.18367347(3840.00) − 600.00 = 705.3061 − 600.00 = +105.3061

Step 4 — the split, in words. The acquirer keeps 134.6939/240.00 = 56.12% of the synergy and the target takes 43.88%. Both are positive, so both sets of shareholders should vote yes.

Step 5 — the bargaining range. ERmax = [(600.00 + 240.00)/3000.00] × (20.00/10.00) = 0.2800 × 2.0000 = 0.5600, and by the second form (60.00 + 240.00/10.00)/150.00 = 84.00/150.00 = 0.5600 ✓ The floor is ERmin = [600.00/(3000.00 + 240.00)] × 2.0000 = 0.185185 × 2.0000 = 0.370370. The offer of 0.4500 sits comfortably inside [0.3704, 0.5600].

Check A, the summation identity: 134.6939 + 105.3061 = 240.0000 = S ✓ It cannot fail if x and y are consistent, which is exactly what it is for: it verifies the arithmetic, not the reasoning.

Check B, an independent route to NPVA: the price-change identity gives NA(Pnew − PA) = 20.00(156.7347 − 150.0000) = 20.00 × 6.7347 = 134.6939 ✓ This one can fail, because it does not use y at all: get y wrong and the two routes disagree.

Check C, the bounds: at ER = 0.5600 you get x = 5.60, y = 5.60/25.60 = 0.21875000 and NPVA = (1 − 0.21875000)(3840.00) − 3000.00 = 0.0000, and Pnew = 3840.00/25.60 = 150.0000, exactly the pre-announcement price ✓ That is the price-change identity again: the acquirer breaks even precisely when its share price is unmoved.

26.4 Offered premium and actual premium

The offer is quoted before the market has repriced the acquirer, so the premium the press release advertises and the premium the target's shareholders actually receive are two different numbers. Both are examinable, and the exam has asked for each of them in the same question.

offered premium = (ER × PA)/PT − 1   ·   actual premium = (ER × Pnew)/PT − 1
1 + actual premium = (1 + offered premium) × (Pnew/PA)   ·   NPVT = T × actual premium
PA and PT are the pre-announcement prices. The offered premium values the offer at the acquirer's old price; the actual premium values the same ER of shares at the price those shares are worth once the market has absorbed the deal. Not on the formula sheet.
Which of the two premiums is larger, and why you can answer without computing The ratio between them is Pnew/PA, and the price-change identity from 26.3 says NPVA = NA(Pnew − PA). So the actual premium exceeds the offered premium exactly when the deal is positive-NPV for the acquirer, and falls short of it exactly when the acquirer overpays. The intuition is direct: the target's shareholders are being paid in acquirer shares, so they are paid in a currency whose value the deal itself changes. A bad deal debases the currency between the announcement and the moment the target's holders receive it. This is also why the effect vanishes in a cash deal, where the currency is fixed.
Worked example 2: what a 30% offer is really worth, and how to fix it

Fjellheim Kraft ASA has 50 million shares at 80 NOK and is bidding for Elvedal Energi AS, which has 12 million shares at 25 NOK. Synergies are S = 60 million. The board announces an offer "at a 30% premium", paid in Fjellheim shares. All figures in millions of NOK.

Step 1 — turn the premium into an exchange ratio. The offered price per Elvedal share is 25.00 × 1.30 = 32.50, so ER = 32.50/80.00 = 0.406250. Stand-alone values are A = 50.00 × 80.00 = 4000.00 and T = 12.00 × 25.00 = 300.00, so the merged firm is worth 4000.00 + 300.00 + 60.00 = 4360.00.

Step 2 — the merged share price. x = 0.406250 × 12.00 = 4.875 million new shares, total 54.875 million, so Pnew = 4360.00/54.875 = 79.4533 NOK, down from 80.00.

Step 3 — the actual premium. Each Elvedal share receives 0.406250 shares now worth 79.4533, that is 0.406250 × 79.4533 = 32.2779 NOK, not 32.50. The actual premium is 32.2779/25.00 − 1 = 0.291116 = 29.1116%, against an offered premium of 30.0000%.

Step 4 — the two NPVs. y = 4.875/54.875 = 0.08883827.
NPVA = (1 − 0.08883827)(4360.00) − 4000.00 = 3972.6651 − 4000.00 = −27.3349
NPVT = 0.08883827(4360.00) − 300.00 = 387.3349 − 300.00 = +87.3349
Fjellheim is paying more than the synergy is worth, which is why its share price fell and why the actual premium came in below the offered one. Both signs agree, as the mechanism box says they must.

Step 5 — the follow-up the exam likes: what ER delivers an actual premium of 30%? Set ER × Pnew = 32.50 with Pnew = 4360.00/(50.00 + 12.00 ER) and solve:

ER = PT(1 + prem*) NA / [(A + T + S) − PT(1 + prem*) NT]
prem* is the target actual premium. Here: 32.50 × 50.00 = 1625.00 on top, and 4360.00 − 32.50 × 12.00 = 4360.00 − 390.00 = 3970.00 underneath.

ER = 1625.00/3970.00 = 0.409320. Then x = 4.911839, total shares 54.911839, Pnew = 4360.00/54.911839 = 79.4000, and 0.409320 × 79.4000 = 32.5000 ✓ Delivering a 30% actual premium requires offering 0.409320 × 80.00 = 32.7456, that is an offered premium of 30.9824%.

Check (an independent route to NPVT): NPVT = T × actual premium = 300.00 × 0.300000 = 90.0000, and directly from y = 4.911839/54.911839 = 0.08944954, NPVT = 0.08944954(4360.00) − 300.00 = 90.0000 ✓ Then NPVA = 60.00 − 90.00 = −30.0000, which the price-change identity confirms: 50.00(79.4000 − 80.0000) = −30.0000 ✓

Step 6 — read the answer. To hand the target 30% the acquirer must give up 30.00 of value, which is half the synergy again. That is the number to quote: not "the premium is expensive", but "raising the actual premium from 29.1116% to 30.0000% costs Fjellheim's shareholders 2.6651 million, taking their NPV from −27.3349 to −30.0000".

Underline the noun before you compute Exam questions in this family run as a chain of near-identical sub-questions that differ by one word: the price per share of the combined firm, the acquirer's price immediately after the announcement, the target's price immediately after the announcement, the actual premium, and finally the exchange ratio that makes the actual premium equal some stated figure. The arithmetic is the same for all of them and the answers are different. This is the single most reliable way to lose points in an M&A exercise, so before computing, underline which price and which premium is being asked for, and write next to your answer which one you produced. The old spring 2017 paper devoted a 45-point exercise entirely to that distinction.

26.5 EPS after the merger, and why accretion is not value creation

A high price-earnings acquirer that buys a low price-earnings target will report higher earnings per share afterwards, always, and with no synergies whatsoever. The arithmetic is mechanical:

EPSnew = (EarningsA + EarningsT)/(NA + x)
the deal is accretive ⟺ P/E paid for the target < P/E of the acquirer
The condition falls out of the first line: EPS rises when the earnings bought per new share issued exceed the acquirer's own EPS, and dividing through by PA turns that into a comparison of multiples. Not on the formula sheet, and worth deriving once so you are not memorising it.
Worked example 3: EPS up, share price down

Høyfjell Teknologi ASA earns 400 million a year on 100 million shares and trades at 80 NOK. Dalen Verksted AS earns 90 million on 30 million shares and trades at 30 NOK. Høyfjell offers a 20% premium, paid in its own shares. There are no synergies at all: S = 0.

ItemHøyfjell (A)Dalen (T)
Earnings400.0090.00
Shares100.0030.00
EPS4.00003.0000
Share price80.0030.00
P/E20.000010.0000

Step 1 — the offer. Offered price per Dalen share = 30.00 × 1.20 = 36.00, so ER = 36.00/80.00 = 0.4500 and x = 0.4500 × 30.00 = 13.50 million new shares. Total shares 113.50 million.

Step 2 — EPS after the merger. Combined earnings = 400.00 + 90.00 = 490.00, so EPSnew = 490.00/113.50 = 4.3172, up from 4.0000. That is accretion of 4.3172/4.0000 − 1 = 0.079295 = 7.9295%, and the CFO's slide will say the deal is "7.9% accretive in year one".

Step 3 — what happened to the share price. With S = 0 the merged firm is worth A + T = 100.00 × 80.00 + 30.00 × 30.00 = 8000.00 + 900.00 = 8900.00, so Pnew = 8900.00/113.50 = 78.4141 NOK. The acquirer's shareholders have lost 80.0000 − 78.4141 = 1.5859 per share, a fall of 1.9824%.

Step 4 — put a number on it. NPVA = NA(Pnew − PA) = 100.00(78.4141 − 80.0000) = −158.5903, and with y = 13.50/113.50 = 0.11894273, NPVT = 0.11894273(8900.00) − 900.00 = +158.5903. EPS rose 7.9295% while 158.5903 million moved out of the acquirer's shareholders' pockets and into the target's.

Step 5 — the accretion test, checked. Høyfjell paid 36.00 for earnings of 3.00 per share, a P/E of 12.0000, well under its own 20.0000, so accretion was guaranteed before any analysis of the business. Equivalently the earnings yield bought is 90.00/(13.50 × 80.00) = 0.083333 against its own 4.0000/80.00 = 0.050000.

Check (the sharpest version of the point): redo the deal at a zero premium, ER = 30.00/80.00 = 0.3750. Then x = 11.25, total shares 111.25, EPSnew = 490.00/111.25 = 4.4045, higher still, while Pnew = 8900.00/111.25 = 80.0000, exactly unchanged ✓ EPS rose 10.11% in a transaction in which, by construction, not one krone of value was created or moved. Nothing else is needed to prove that EPS growth is not value creation.

Why the merged P/E falls, and what the market is doing The combined firm's implied multiple in step 4 is 78.4141/4.3172 = 18.1633, down from Høyfjell's 20.0000. A price-earnings ratio is a statement about the growth and risk of a stream of earnings, so buying slower-growing earnings must lower the average multiple. EPS accretion and multiple contraction are two descriptions of one event, and only the second is about value. The trick has a name from the conglomerate era, the bootstrap or chain letter effect: repeat the acquisition every year and reported EPS grows indefinitely, which looks like a growth company until the acquisitions stop. The exam version of this insight is one sentence: an EPS effect that follows mechanically from the two multiples cannot be evidence about the deal, because it would be there even at a zero premium.

26.6 Reading the announcement backwards

The two Kurbatov M&A exercises that carried numbers both worked in this direction: the prices are given, and you are asked what belief about the deal produces them. Two quantities can be recovered this way, and they need different assumptions.

The first is the market's estimate of the synergies. Whatever the payment method, the combined market capitalisation after the announcement is A + T + S, because the two sets of shareholders between them own the whole merged firm, in shares or in cash:

Simplied = (NAPApost + NTPTpost) − (NAPApre + NTPTpre)
The change in the two firms' combined market value is the market's synergy estimate. Assumes the deal is expected to close, that nothing else moved the prices that day, and that the announcement did not change what the market thinks the target is worth on its own. Not on the formula sheet.
Worked example 4: what the market thinks the synergies are

Bergen Bygg ASA has 40 million shares at 125 NOK. It announces an all-cash offer of 60 NOK per share for Målselv Materialer AS, which has 8 million shares at 45 NOK. On the announcement, Bergen Bygg rises to 126.50 and Målselv rises to 60.00. Figures in millions of NOK.

Step 1 — the two market capitalisations, before and after.
Before: A = 40.00 × 125.00 = 5000.00 and T = 8.00 × 45.00 = 360.00, combined 5360.00.
After: 40.00 × 126.50 = 5060.00 and 8.00 × 60.00 = 480.00, combined 5540.00.

Step 2 — the implied synergy. Simplied = 5540.00 − 5360.00 = +180.00. The market believes the combination creates 180 million of value.

Step 3 — who gets it. The premium paid in cash is 8.00 × (60.00 − 45.00) = 120.00, which is exactly the target shareholders' gain, and the acquirer's shareholders gain 40.00 × (126.50 − 125.00) = 60.00. The target takes 120.00/180.00 = 66.67% of the synergy, the acquirer 33.33%, which is the usual pattern from 26.1.

Check A (the cash-deal accounting, independent of step 2): the acquirer's shareholders end up owning A + T + S minus the cash paid out, so their gain must be S − premium = 180.00 − 120.00 = 60.00, and 40.00 × 1.50 = 60.00 ✓ The two routes use different prices, so a mis-keyed share count breaks the agreement.

Check B (the same identity under a stock offer): suppose instead the same deal, same S = 180.00, were paid at ER = 0.48. Then x = 0.48 × 8.00 = 3.84, total shares 43.84, and Pnew = 5540.00/43.84 = 126.3686, so the target's shares trade at 0.48 × 126.3686 = 60.6569. Combined market capitalisation: 40.00 × 126.3686 + 8.00 × 60.6569 = 5054.74 + 485.26 = 5540.00, which is also (40.00 + 3.84) × 126.3686 = 43.84 × 126.3686 = 5540.00, giving Simplied = 180.00 again ✓ One formula covers both payment methods.

What to say about the assumptions. If the deal might fail, the observed prices are probability-weighted and this calculation understates the synergy conditional on closing. If the announcement also told the market something about the target as a stand-alone business, part of the target's jump is a revaluation rather than a synergy, and Simplied overstates the gain from combining. Write both caveats down; the keys reward the second one explicitly.

The second quantity is the market's probability that the deal closes at all. The idea is the one from k22: build a position whose payoff you know in both outcomes, price it off the market, and solve for the probability implied. Because the announced ratio is a promise to convert, the natural position is long the target and short the acquirer in exactly the exchange ratio, which is what merger arbitrageurs hold and what the exam calls the long-short strategy.

cost of the strategy × (1 + rf) = p × payoff(deal completes) + (1 − p) × payoff(deal fails)
The certainty-equivalent equation. The left side is the cost grown at the risk-free rate, not the cost. It is legitimate to discount at rf because the remaining risk is whether a regulator or a shareholder vote blocks the deal, and that is diversifiable, so it carries no risk premium. p therefore plays the role that ρ plays in k22, with one difference worth a sentence in an exam: when the risk carries no premium, the risk-neutral probability and the market's actual belief coincide, so p here really is a forecast. Not on the formula sheet.
Worked example 5: the implied probability that the deal closes (H2025 Exercise 6, 12 of its 20 points)

Company A announces an offer for company B, with no anticipation in the prices beforehand. If the deal completes, in exactly one year, 5 shares of B are exchanged for 1 share of A, so ER = 0.2000 A-shares per B-share. If it fails, prices return to their original levels. Before the announcement PA = 110 and PB = 16; immediately after, PA = 105 and PB = 20. The one-year risk-free rate is rf = 3.0000%.

Step 1 — build a position that resolves cleanly. Buy 5 B shares at 20, costing 100. Short-sell 1 A share for 105 and put the 105 in the risk-free asset. Net outlay today: 100 out for the B shares, 105 in from the short sale, 105 back out into the deposit, so the strategy costs 100.

Step 2 — the payoff if the deal completes. The 5 B shares become exactly 1 A share, which is delivered to close the short. Whatever A is worth then, the two legs cancel, and you are left with the deposit: 105 × 1.03 = 108.15.

Step 3 — the payoff if the deal fails. No conversion, so you unwind at the reverted prices: sell 5 B shares at 16 for 80, buy back 1 A share at 110, and keep the deposit. Payoff = 108.15 + 80 − 110 = 78.15.

Outcome in one yearDepositB legA legTotal
Deal completes (p)108.155 B shares convert into the 1 A share owed108.15
Deal fails (1 − p)108.15+80.00−110.0078.15

Step 4 — the certainty equivalent. The strategy cost 100 and its only risk is deal risk, so its expected payoff must be 100 × 1.03 = 103.00. Solve:

103.00 = p(108.15) + (1 − p)(78.15) ⟹ 103.00 − 78.15 = p(108.15 − 78.15) ⟹ 24.85 = 30.00 p ⟹ p = 0.828333 = 82.8333%

Check A (an independent route, no strategy required): read the spread directly. One A share minus 5 B shares is worth 105 − 100 = 5 today. If the deal completes the spread is worth exactly 0, since 5 B shares become 1 A share; if it fails it is worth 110 − 80 = 30. So the spread is a pure bet on failure, and 5 = (1 − p)(30)/1.03 gives 1 − p = 5 × 1.03/30 = 0.171667, hence p = 0.828333 ✓ In general:

1 − p = (1 + rf) × [PApost − PBpost/ER] / [PApre − PBpre/ER]
The merger spread narrows towards zero as completion becomes certain, and reverts to its pre-announcement width if the deal dies. The ratio of the two widths, grown at rf, is the market's failure probability. Here: 1.03 × (105 − 100)/(110 − 80) = 5.15/30 = 0.171667.

Check B (price both shares under the same p): let As be A's price in one year if the deal completes. Then 1.03(105) = pAs + (1 − p)110 and 1.03(20) = p(0.2000)As + (1 − p)16. Multiplying the second by 5 and subtracting gives 108.15 − 103.00 = (1 − p)(110 − 80), the same equation, so p = 0.828333 ✓ and back-substituting, As = 107.7666 and B's completion price is 0.2000 × 107.7666 = 21.5533. Verify: [0.828333(107.7666) + 0.171667(110)]/1.03 = 105.0000 ✓ and [0.828333(21.5533) + 0.171667(16)]/1.03 = 20.0000 ✓

Step 5 — say what the numbers mean. The market puts 82.83% on completion. It also says that even if the deal does complete, A is worth 107.7666, which is 2.2334 below where it started: the market thinks the acquisition destroys value for A's shareholders and creates it for B's, since B's holders get 21.5533 against a stand-alone 16, a gain of 34.71%. That is the reasoning the follow-up questions want, and it points at empire building rather than at synergies.

Three errors that cost most of the twelve points One. Setting the expected payoff equal to the strategy's cost instead of the cost times (1 + rf). Here that gives 100 = 30p + 78.15 and p = 0.7283, and it is the error the H2025 key singles out. Two. Using the post-announcement prices in the failure branch. The question states that prices return to their original levels, so the failure branch uses 110 and 16, and it is the pre-announcement prices that do the work. Three. Getting the direction of the exchange ratio backwards. "5 shares of B for 1 share of A" means each B share receives 0.2000 A shares, so you hold 5 B against 1 A, not 5 A against 1 B. Write the ratio as a sentence in your own words before you use it.
What the rest of a question like this asks, and how to answer H2025 Exercise 6 gave 12 points to the probability and split the remaining 8 between two verbal parts. The first asked for a reason for the deal other than synergies, given the price reaction. The reaction is the answer: the acquirer fell and the target jumped, so the market thinks the acquirer is paying more than it gets, which is the signature of empire building, and could equally be hubris or a target whose management is expected to improve under new owners. The second asked how you would vote as an acquirer shareholder, and what would then happen. Vote against, since the deal is negative-NPV for you, and if enough holders do the same the prices revert towards 110 and 16. A good answer adds a qualification: they need not revert exactly, because the bid may have told the market something about the target that survives the failed deal, and because a determined management may try again.

26.7 Cash or shares, and what the choice signals

The method of payment is not a detail of settlement. It changes who bears which risk, what the market infers, and what the target's shareholders pay in tax.

DimensionCashAcquirer shares
Who bears synergy riskThe acquirer alone. The target is paid a fixed amount whatever happens nextShared. The target's holders own y of the combined firm, so they suffer if the synergies disappoint
Who bears the risk that the acquirer is mispricedNobody: cash is cashThe target, in a fixed-ratio offer. This is why targets negotiate collars and fixed-value structures
What the market infersLittle, or that the acquirer is confidentThat the acquirer thinks its own shares are overvalued, by the k12 and k13 logic: issuing equity is what a manager does when he knows more than the market and the news is bad
Tax to the target's shareholdersAn immediate taxable disposalUsually deferred until the new shares are sold
Effect on the acquirer's capital structureDebt-financed cash raises leverage, adding PV(TS) from k7 and discipline from k11, at the cost of distress risk from k8. Cash off the balance sheet removes free cash flowLowers leverage and dilutes existing holders, including any blockholder whose monitoring you were relying on (k27)
Probability of completionHigher and faster: no acquirer shareholder vote on an issue of new shares, no dependence on the acquirer's share priceLower, which feeds directly into the p of 26.6
Why a stock-financed bid gets a worse reception Run the Myers-Majluf argument from k12 with a merger attached. A manager who believes his shares are undervalued does not want to issue them at the current price, so he prefers cash; a manager who believes they are overvalued prefers to pay in the overvalued currency. A rational market knows this and marks the acquirer down on the announcement of a stock deal, which is what the evidence shows. Two consequences are examinable. First, the target should demand a higher nominal price in shares than in cash, which is a second reason the actual premium in 26.4 diverges from the offered one. Second, this is consistency check 5 in its M&A costume: if the market's inference is that stock bidders are overvalued, is it rational given who chooses to bid in stock? It is, and that is why the discount persists rather than being competed away.
Where the rest of this chapter lives The coinsurance computation is done state by state in k24 § 24.6, with consistency check 4 written out. The empire-building motive is the free cash flow problem of k11, and the disciplinary takeover is the market for corporate control, which k27 treats as a governance mechanism together with the defences that block it. The winner's curse behind hubris is the same mechanism as IPO underpricing in k14. k28 § 28.3 gives the six-step template for a stock swap and § 28.5 the templates for the two verbal exercise types.
↑ contents

27 · Corporate governance

Exercise 2 was corporate governance in H2024 and again in H2025, twelve points both times, with no arithmetic anywhere in it. In the new format that is the most predictable block of points on the whole paper: you know the topic before you open the exam, you know it will be verbal, and you know the shape. H2024 asked for two distinct ways of monitoring a CEO, naming who does the monitoring and what tools they have. H2025 put you in the position of a shareholder with 5 to 10% of a firm whose management was visibly shirking, and asked for three actions at four points each, two to three sentences per action. This chapter is not an overview. It is a stock of arguments, each of which you can write three sentences about, because that is exactly what the paper buys.

27.1 The agency problem, and why it does not solve itself

A listed firm separates the people who own it from the people who run it. The owners hold the residual claim and want the value of that claim maximised. The manager holds a job, and his objective includes his salary, his job security, the size of the empire he administers, the pleasantness of his working life and his reputation. Those objectives overlap but do not coincide, and every wedge between them has a name:

BehaviourWhat the manager getsWhat shareholders loseWhere else in this manual
ShirkingLeisure, an easier life, fewer difficult decisionsThe value the effort would have producedThe H2025 Exercise 2 setup
Private benefits and perksConsumption paid for by the firm, prestige, a comfortable headquartersThe cash spent, plus the culture it setsk11
Empire buildingA bigger firm, a bigger salary, more status, more securityThe negative NPV of the acquisitions and projects it fundsk11, k26 § 26.2
EntrenchmentBeing harder to replace: firm-specific investments, defences, a compliant boardThe value of replacing him when he should be replaced§ 27.6
Excessive cautionA quiet career, no visible failurePositive-NPV risks not taken, because the manager is undiversified and shareholders are notk3, k9
Earnings managementBonus targets met, a rising share price at vestingReal decisions distorted to hit an accounting number§ 27.4
agency cost of equity = V(first best) − V(under the manager's own objective)
The same definition k9 used for risk shifting, with the manager's objective substituted for the shareholders'. It is the value destroyed, never the value the manager captured: a perk that gives him 3.00 and costs the firm 20.00 has an agency cost of 20.00, of which 17.00 is pure deadweight loss that nobody receives. Not on the formula sheet.
Why the problem is not solved by the share price The market is not fooled. If investors know the manager will waste a predictable amount, they pay less for the shares at the outset, and the founder or the issuing shareholders bear the cost. That is the same ex ante logic as k9: creditors who expect risk shifting price the bond lower, so shareholders pay for the agency cost themselves. It has one important consequence and one important limit. The consequence is that current owners have a direct financial interest in credible governance, which is why firms adopt constraints voluntarily. The limit is that pricing the waste is not the same as preventing it. The value is still destroyed, and it is a deadweight loss, not a transfer: no one receives the projects that were never taken.

So why do the owners not simply monitor? Because monitoring costs money and the benefits are shared. A shareholder who spends to improve the firm receives only his own fraction of the improvement while every other holder receives theirs for nothing:

monitor if α × ΔV > C   ⟹   α* = C/ΔV
α is the fraction of the equity you hold, ΔV the increase in firm value the intervention would produce, C its full cost, including advisers, legal fees, proxy solicitation and your own time. α* is the smallest stake for which acting is privately worthwhile. This is the free-rider problem of dispersed ownership, and it is the reason governance is a problem of ownership structure and not only of rules. Not on the formula sheet.
Worked example 1: does a 7% owner bother?

Nordfjord Marine ASA has an equity market value of 4000 million NOK. An investor is convinced that a disciplined chief executive would raise the value of the firm by 8%, and that a campaign to force the change would cost 12 million NOK all in.

Step 1 — the size of the prize. ΔV = 0.0800 × 4000.00 = 320.00 million, against a cost of 12.00 million. From the point of view of the shareholders as a body the campaign is obviously worth running: 320.00 ≫ 12.00.

Step 2 — but each owner decides alone.

Stake αPrivate gain α × 320.00CostNet to that holderActs?
0.10%0.3212.00−11.68no
2.00%6.4012.00−5.60no
5.00%16.0012.00+4.00yes, barely
7.00%22.4012.00+10.40yes
10.00%32.0012.00+20.00yes

Step 3 — the break-even stake. α* = C/ΔV = 12.00/320.00 = 0.037500 = 3.7500%. Verify by substitution: 0.037500 × 320.00 = 12.00, exactly the cost ✓ Every holder below 3.75% rationally does nothing and waits for someone else.

Step 4 — the externality, quantified. If the 7% holder acts, he captures 22.40 and the other holders receive 0.9300 × 320.00 = 297.60 for contributing nothing. The two shares rebuild the whole improvement: 22.40 + 297.60 = 320.00 ✓ He is producing a public good and keeping 7% of it. That is why governance is chronically undersupplied and why the blockholder is the pivotal figure in this chapter.

Step 5 — add the realistic feature, uncertainty. Campaigns fail. Suppose the probability of success is 0.6000. The 7% holder's expected gain is 0.6000 × 22.40 = 13.44, still above the 12.00 cost but only by 1.44, and the break-even stake rises to 12.00/(0.6000 × 320.00) = 0.062500 = 6.2500%. This is the arithmetic behind the 5 to 10% band in H2025 Exercise 2: large enough that monitoring pays, small enough that you cannot simply impose the outcome and must persuade others.

Step 6 — compare the alternative, walking away. The stake is worth 0.0700 × 4000.00 = 280.00 million. Selling a block that size moves the price; at a 2% impact the exit costs 0.0200 × 280.00 = 5.60 million, and forgoes the 22.40 the campaign would have produced. Voice beats exit here, +10.40 against −5.60, and the comparison is the point: exit is not free for a blockholder, which is precisely why a blockholder engages when a small holder sells.

Do not confuse the cost of the agency problem with the transfer The manager's perks may cost the firm 20 while giving him 3. The agency cost is 20, the value destroyed, exactly as in k9 where the agency cost was first-best value minus chosen value and not the transfer between claimants. An exam answer that says "the CEO gains at shareholders' expense" has described a transfer; an answer that says "the firm gives up 20 of value to deliver 3 of private benefit, a deadweight loss of 17" has described the problem. The second one earns the marks.

27.2 The board of directors

The board is the only monitor with legal authority over the chief executive. It hires him, sets his pay, approves the strategy and the large transactions, and it is the body that can dismiss him. Everything else in this chapter works by influencing the board or by going around it. So the first question about any board is whether it is capable of using the power it has.

FeatureWhat good practice looks likeWhy it matters
IndependenceA majority of directors with no employment, family, supplier or advisory relationship with the firm or its managementAn inside director reports to the CEO in his day job and cannot credibly vote to dismiss him. A grey director, for example the firm's banker or a former executive, has business to lose. Only genuinely outside directors can act against management without personal cost
CommitteesAudit, remuneration and nomination committees composed of independent directorsCommittee work is where monitoring actually happens: the audit committee meets the auditor without management present, the remuneration committee designs the pay contract, the nomination committee controls who is proposed for election. A board that lets the CEO pick his own remuneration committee has already lost
Separating chair and chief executiveA chair who is not the CEO, and ideally independentThe chair sets the agenda, runs the meeting and leads the evaluation of the chief executive. If the two roles are held by one person, the CEO is monitoring himself
Size and workloadA board small enough to argue in, and directors who are not sitting on many other boardsLarge boards free-ride internally in exactly the way section 27.1 describes for shareholders, and a busy director has neither the time nor the incentive to fight
Skin in the gameDirectors paid partly in shares they must hold, with meaningful ownership relative to their own wealthMonitoring is costly and unpleasant; a director with no economic stake has no offsetting reason to do it
Election termsAll directors elected annuallyA staggered or classified board, where only a third stand each year, means two annual meetings are needed to replace a majority, which blunts both the shareholder vote and the takeover threat (§ 27.6)
Why a "captured" board is the normal failure, not the exceptional one Nothing here requires dishonest directors. The CEO usually controls the flow of information to the board, often influences who is nominated, and sits with those people repeatedly over years. A director who challenges him pays the full social cost of the confrontation and, holding a small stake, receives a sliver of the benefit. It is the free-rider calculus of section 27.1 transplanted into the boardroom, which is why the structural remedies above are all about changing who is in the room and who chooses them, rather than about exhorting directors to be diligent. When an exam answer says the board is the monitor, add the sentence that says when the board fails: the board is only a monitor to the extent it is independent of the person it is monitoring.

27.3 The other monitors, and the tool each one holds

H2024 Exercise 2 asked for two ways of monitoring the CEO, who performs each one and which tools they have. That is the shape to memorise: a monitor without a tool is not a monitor. Here is the full stock.

MonitorTools it actually holdsWhere it is weak
The boardDismissal, pay design, approval of budgets, acquisitions and major financing, access to the auditorCaptured boards, information supplied by management, weak incentives to fight (§ 27.2)
Blockholders (families, founders, the state, private equity, a 5 to 10% fund)Board seats, private access to the chair, credible threats to vote against, and the willingness to bear the cost of a campaignThey may extract private benefits of control at the expense of minority holders, turning one agency problem into another
Institutional investors (pension and index funds)Voting power aggregated across thousands of holdings, published voting policies, engagement teams, and the proxy advisers they followIndex funds cannot sell, so exit is unavailable, and their monitoring budget per holding is tiny. Voting is close to free, active engagement is not
The auditorAn independent examination of the accounts, a qualified opinion, and direct access to the audit committeePaid by the firm it audits, and historically sold consulting to the same client. The remedy is the independence rules in § 27.7
Analysts and the pressPublic scrutiny, questions on results calls, and the reputational cost of being written aboutNo formal power at all, and analysts covering a firm depend on management for access
LendersCovenants, collateral, the maturity structure, and the periodic need to refinance. A missed payment transfers controlLenders want to be repaid, not to maximise value, so they monitor risk rather than performance. Their interests diverge from shareholders' exactly as k9 and k10 describe
The product marketCompetition removes the slack that makes shirking survivableSlow, and absent in concentrated or protected industries. Useless as an immediate remedy
The managerial labour marketThe CEO's reputation determines his next job and his current market valueWeakest for a chief executive at the end of his career, which is when entrenchment is worst
The market for corporate controlA hostile takeover replaces the entire management team, and its threat disciplines even without an actual bid (k26 § 26.2)Blocked by defences (§ 27.6), expensive, and weakened by the free-rider problem of k26 § 26.1
Regulators and the exchangeDisclosure requirements, certification of accounts, listing standards on board independence, insider-trading enforcementRules set a floor, not a standard, and compliance can become a box-ticking exercise
Why debt is a governance mechanism and not only a financing choice Two channels, and naming both separates a good answer from an adequate one. First, cash: interest and principal are contractual, so leverage removes the free cash flow that funds empire building, and forces the manager back to the capital market where each new project is priced by outsiders. That is the agency benefit of debt from k11. Second, control: default hands the firm to creditors and usually ends the manager's career, so the penalty is severe and outside his control, which is what makes the commitment credible. Debt therefore substitutes for monitoring, which is why the leveraged buyout in § 27.6 uses it deliberately and why the same instrument produces the agency costs of k9 and k10. One friction, two signs.

27.4 Executive pay as a governance instrument

Monitoring detects bad behaviour after the fact. Pay design changes what the manager wants in the first place, which is why compensation is the governance tool boards spend most of their time on. The quantity that matters is not the level of pay but its sensitivity to value:

δ = Δ(manager's wealth)/Δ(firm value) = fraction of shares held + (options held as a fraction of shares) × N(d1)
δ is pay-performance sensitivity. Shares transmit value one for one; an option transmits it at the rate N(d1), the option delta from k23, so an out-of-the-money option on a struggling firm barely moves the manager's wealth at all. Not on the formula sheet.

Put a number on it, because the number is the argument. Suppose a chief executive holds 0.4000% of the equity outright, plus options over a further 1.5000% of the shares with a delta of 0.5500. Then δ = 0.004000 + 0.015000 × 0.5500 = 0.004000 + 0.008250 = 0.012250. He keeps 1.2250% of every krone of value he creates, and bears 1.2250% of every krone he destroys. A perk worth 3.00 million to him personally is therefore worth taking as long as it costs the firm less than 3.00/0.012250 = 244.90 million. Double his sensitivity and the threshold falls to 122.45 million. That single calculation is the case for equity-based pay, and it is also the case against thinking that a large salary is the problem: a large fixed salary has a sensitivity of zero.

Instrument or ruleWhat it fixesWhat it breaks
Salary and cash bonus on accounting targetsRewards measurable short-run performanceInvites earnings management and cutting investment to hit a target. Low sensitivity to actual value
Restricted stock with long vestingAligns wealth with the share price and lengthens the horizonRewards a rising market as much as a good manager, and can leave an executive dangerously undiversified
Share optionsHigh sensitivity for a given cost, and they encourage a cautious manager to take riskThe convexity of k9: options make risk shifting attractive, and once deeply out of the money they lose all sensitivity and stop working
Relative performance evaluation (benchmark against an index or peer group)Strips out the market and industry component, so the manager is paid for what he controls, which is exactly the k3 distinction between systematic and firm-specificRequires a defensible peer group, and executives resist a scheme that pays nothing in a rising market
Clawbacks and deferred vestingUndo bonuses paid on figures later restated, and stop the manager selling before the truth emergesEnforcement is slow and legally contested
Say-on-pay (a shareholder vote on the remuneration report)Gives every holder, including a 5 to 10% one, an annual, public, low-cost vote on the one thing directors care about defendingUsually advisory rather than binding, so its force is reputational: it works because directors dislike a large protest vote, not because it compels them
Golden parachuteRemoves the CEO's personal incentive to fight a value-increasing takeoverRewards failure, and can be set so generously that it becomes a takeover defence in its own right (§ 27.6)
Believing that more pay-performance sensitivity is always better It is not, and saying why is worth marks. A manager cannot diversify his human capital: his job, his reputation and now his wealth all ride on one firm, so loading him with stock makes him bear firm-specific risk that shareholders have already diversified away (k3). He must be compensated for it, which makes high-sensitivity pay expensive, and he will respond by avoiding risk that shareholders want taken. Options push in the opposite direction and overshoot, because a convex payoff makes the very risk shifting of k9 attractive. The optimum is interior, and the design question is not "how much sensitivity" but "sensitivity to what": relative performance, long vesting, and a mix of stock and options is the standard answer.

27.5 The shareholder's own toolkit: what a 5 to 10% holder can do

This is the section H2025 Exercise 2 examines directly. The band matters, so start by being able to say why it is the interesting one.

Why 5 to 10% is exactly the interesting stake From below: the free-rider arithmetic of worked example 1. A 0.1% holder who spends 12 million to create 320 million of value collects 0.32 million of it, so he does nothing and sells if he is unhappy. Somewhere around a few per cent the calculus flips, and once campaigns are allowed to fail the break-even stake rises further, into the mid single digits. A 5 to 10% holder is on the right side of that line: monitoring pays for him personally. From above: he still cannot simply impose the outcome. He cannot outvote the rest of the register, cannot appoint directors alone, and cannot force a sale, so every action he takes must persuade someone else, which is why coalition-building and public argument are on the list below.
