He is also large enough to be visible and constrained: in most European markets, Norway included, crossing 5% triggers a disclosure obligation, so his stake is public and management knows exactly who he is, while crossing the control thresholds much further up would trigger a mandatory offer for the whole company. And exit is expensive for him: as worked example 1 shows, a block that size cannot be sold without moving the price. The classical formulation is exit versus voice, and 5 to 10% is precisely the range in which exit is costly and voice is affordable.
value of voice = q × α × ΔV − C   against   cost of exit = (price impact) × α × V
q is the probability the campaign succeeds, α the stake, ΔV the improvement, C the cost of acting, V the firm's equity value. Below a few per cent the first expression is negative and exit is nearly free, so small holders sell. Above 5% the first turns positive while the second grows with the stake, so both terms become the same order of magnitude and the choice is a genuine decision. This is the exit versus voice trade-off, and it is why the exam put the question at 5 to 10%. Not on the formula sheet.

Ten actions follow. Learn them as a three-part skeleton, because that is the shape the marks are given for: the action, the mechanism by which it reaches the CEO, the limitation.

#ActionThe mechanism that reaches the CEOThe limitation
1Engage the chair and the board privatelyThe board is the only body that can set his pay and dismiss him, so a credible complaint from a large owner changes the incentives he faces daily. Most successful campaigns end here, before anything is publicDepends entirely on a board willing to listen, and gives management time to prepare
2Demand board representation, or nominate directors through the nomination committeeChanges who is in the room permanently, and gives the owner direct access to the information management controlsNeeds support from other holders to win the vote, and a staggered board can delay any majority for years
3Requisition an extraordinary general meetingForces the board to convene and face the questions on a date the owner chooses, rather than at its own convenience. In Norway holders of at least 5% of the share capital can require one, which is one reason the threshold in the exam question sits thereCostly, public, and pointless without a resolution that others will support
4Vote against directors' re-election and against the remuneration reportDirectors and executives care intensely about a visible protest vote, so even a non-binding say-on-pay vote against carries reputational force in the labour market for directorsAdvisory in most regimes, and 5 to 10% alone does not defeat a resolution
5Submit a shareholder proposal and put an item on the agendaCompels a discussion and a recorded vote on a specific change, for example the pay structure or the payout policy, which management must publicly argue againstNarrow subject matter, and boards frequently make a small concession to have it withdrawn
6Run a proxy fight to replace directors outrightSolicits other holders' votes to change the board, which is the only route to dismissal that does not need the incumbent board's cooperation. Directors who fear losing their seats usually act on the complaint before the ballotExpensive, slow, and the classic public good: the activist pays the whole cost and keeps only his own share of the gain
7Go public: an open letter, a presentation, media coverageImposes a reputational cost on the directors personally and lowers the coordination cost for other holders who agree but were free-ridingEscalates and can entrench the board's resistance, and a wrong public thesis damages the investor's own reputation
8Push a redesign of the pay contract: relative performance evaluation, longer vesting, clawbacks, more equity and less fixed salaryAttacks the cause rather than the symptom. Raising δ in § 27.4 makes shirking expensive for the manager himself, which is cheaper and more durable than watching himSensitivity is not free: it loads firm-specific risk on an undiversified manager, and options invite risk shifting
9Demand a payout or a leveraged recapitalisationThe free cash flow mechanism of k11: cash inside the firm is what funds the empire, and committing it to dividends, buybacks or debt service removes the discretion. It also forces management back to the capital market for each new projectLeverage brings distress and debt-agency costs (k8, k9, k10), and a firm with genuinely lumpy investment needs is harmed by losing the buffer
10Change control: invite a bidder, support a hostile offer, or lead a take-private with a financial sponsorThe market for corporate control replaces the whole team at once, and a 5 to 10% stake is a valuable toehold that both makes a bid more likely to succeed and lets the owner share in the premiumRequires a willing buyer and a board that cannot block it, which is exactly what the defences of § 27.6 are for

Three more are worth having in reserve, and H2025 gave credit for well-argued unusual answers:

Reserve actionMechanismLimitation
Litigate: a derivative suit or a claim for breach of the directors' duty of care or loyaltyThe only route with a court behind it, and the credible threat of personal liability concentrates directors' mindsRequires evidence of a breach, not merely poor performance. Shirking as such is rarely actionable, and litigation is slow and expensive
Demand an independent investigation of specific transactionsPuts an outsider inside the firm with a mandate to look, which produces the evidence the other actions need. Minority thresholds of about a tenth of the capital exist for this in several jurisdictions, Norway includedNeeds a concrete suspicion, and management controls the day-to-day cooperation the investigator depends on
Sell the stake, the "Wall Street walk"Not surrender. Concentrated selling depresses the price, which hurts a CEO whose pay is equity-linked and makes the firm cheaper to acquire, so the threat to leave disciplines even when it is not carried outCostly for a block this size because of the price impact, and it hands the problem to whoever buys the shares

27.6 Takeover defences, entrenchment, and the LBO

A hostile takeover is the monitor of last resort: it does not need the board's agreement, and it removes the management team entirely. Defences exist to blunt it. Every one of them raises the same question, which is the question an exam answer must answer: whose interest does it serve?

DefenceHow it worksThe shareholder-bargaining case for itThe entrenchment case against it
Poison pill (shareholder rights plan)If any buyer passes a trigger stake, every other shareholder may buy new shares at a deep discount, so the raider's stake is massively diluted. In practice it makes a hostile purchase impossible without the board's agreement, since only the board can redeem the pillIt forces the bidder to negotiate with the board, which can extract a higher premium for all shareholders and prevents a coercive low-ball tenderIt hands a veto over a decision about shareholders' own property to the very people the bid would displace. Firms with pills receive fewer bids
Staggered (classified) boardOnly a third of the directors are elected each year, so even a bidder owning a majority must win two annual meetings to control the boardContinuity, and directors insulated from short-term pressure can invest for the long runThe delay is the point, and combined with a pill it is the most effective entrenchment device known. Two annual cycles is longer than most bidders will wait
Golden parachuteA large payment to the executive if he loses his job in a change of controlRemoves his personal reason to resist a value-increasing bid, and lets him negotiate without fearing for his own incomePays for failure, and if the sums are big enough the cost itself deters bidders
Dual-class shares and voting capsFounders hold shares with several votes each, or no holder may vote more than a fixed percentage regardless of ownershipProtects a long-run vision from short-horizon pressurePermanently separates cash flow rights from control, which removes both the vote and the takeover threat at once
White knight, white squire, crown jewelFind a friendlier buyer, place a large block with an ally, or sell the asset the bidder wantedA genuine auction can raise the price, and a white knight is a real competing bidThe last of the three destroys value deliberately to defeat a bid, which is hard to defend as anything but entrenchment
GreenmailBuy the raider's block back at a premium not offered to anyone elseAlmost none. It ends a distractionUses the firm's money to buy off a threat to management, and pays one shareholder more than the others
How to answer "is this defence good for shareholders?" Never answer yes or no flatly. The examiner is testing whether you can hold both hypotheses at once, so name them: the shareholder-bargaining hypothesis, under which the defence is a negotiating instrument that raises the premium, and the managerial-entrenchment hypothesis, under which it protects a job. Then say what would separate them in the data, which is the part that earns the mark: if defences raise premiums, protected firms should receive higher-priced bids and defended firms that are eventually sold should sell for more; if they entrench, protected firms should receive fewer bids and show weaker operating performance. Close with the decisive point about the pill in particular: it puts the decision about whether to sell shares in the hands of directors rather than of the owners of those shares, and it can be adopted without a shareholder vote.

The leveraged buyout is the same market working in a different direction. A financial sponsor buys the whole company with a large slug of debt, takes it off the exchange, and replaces or re-incentivises management.

Why an LBO is a governance transaction and not just a financing one Four mechanisms operate at once, and listing them is the answer. Ownership is concentrated: the sponsor owns the whole firm, so the free-rider problem of section 27.1 disappears and monitoring becomes worth its cost by construction. Debt disciplines: heavy interest payments strip out the free cash flow that funded the empire, exactly the k11 mechanism, and default now genuinely ends the manager's career. Management equity is high-powered: the executive team is given a large percentage stake, so δ from section 27.4 rises by an order of magnitude. The listing goes away: no quarterly earnings to manage and no dispersed register to coordinate. The costs are the mirror image and belong in the same answer: the leverage brings the distress costs of k8 and the risk-shifting and overhang problems of k9 and k10, and the sponsor's own investors face an agency problem one level up.

27.7 Regulation, ownership structure, and the limits of governance

Rules set the floor everything else stands on. The two waves worth naming both followed scandals, and both are best remembered by what they changed rather than by their names.

InterventionWhat it requiresWhich failure it targets
Sarbanes-Oxley (2002)The chief executive and chief financial officer personally certify the accounts; management and the auditor must report on internal controls; the audit committee must be independent and hires the auditor; the auditor is restricted from selling consulting services to the same clientAccounting fraud and a captured auditor. It attacks the information the board and the market rely on
Dodd-Frank (2010)A periodic non-binding shareholder vote on executive pay (say-on-pay), disclosure of the relationship between pay and performance, and clawbacks of incentive pay based on restated figuresPay set by a board the executive influenced, and pay disconnected from value
Exchange listing standardsA majority of independent directors and fully independent audit and remuneration committeesBoard capture, directly (§ 27.2)
Comply-or-explain codes (Norway's code of practice for corporate governance is one)Not compliance but disclosure: state which recommendations you depart from and whyThe one-size problem. It lets governance quality be priced rather than mandated, and it works only if investors read and react
Ownership disclosure and mandatory bid rulesDisclosure once a stake crosses 5% in Norway and most of Europe, and an obligation to bid for all remaining shares once an acquirer passes the control thresholdStakes built in secret, and control changing hands without minority holders being offered the same price
Why the reader's own market has a different agency problem Almost everything above is written for the dispersed ownership of the United States and the United Kingdom, where the conflict is managers against shareholders. Continental Europe, the Nordic countries very much included, is dominated by concentrated ownership: founding families, foundations, industrial holding companies and the state. That solves the free-rider problem of section 27.1 outright, because someone with 30% of the shares always finds monitoring worth its cost, and management-driven empire building is correspondingly rarer. It replaces it with a different conflict, the controlling owner against the minority: related-party transactions on favourable terms, dual-class structures that keep control while selling cash flow rights, and a resistance to any takeover that would end the family's position. When an exam question hands you a firm with a dominant owner, that is the conflict to analyse, and the protective machinery is different too: mandatory bid rules, equal-treatment requirements, independent review of related-party deals, and minority thresholds for calling meetings and demanding investigations.
Governance is not free, and saying so is worth a mark Every mechanism in this chapter costs something. Monitoring consumes real resources. Independent directors know less about the business than insiders do. Say-on-pay and disclosure absorb management attention. Compliance with internal-control rules is expensive enough that it is a documented reason small firms delist or stay private, and takeover pressure can push managers toward decisions that look good this quarter. So the target is not maximum governance, it is the point where the marginal reduction in agency cost equals the marginal cost of the mechanism, which is the same trade-off shape as the optimal capital structure in k11. An answer that lists mechanisms and stops is an adequate answer; an answer that names the cost of one of them is a good one.

27.8 Writing the twelve points

Both papers gave twelve points and both stated a sentence budget. Write to it. One sentence naming the action or the monitor, one on the mechanism that reaches the manager, one on the limitation. Nothing else scores, and in particular a definition of corporate governance scores nothing at all.

Model answer 1: the H2024 Exercise 2 shape, two monitoring mechanisms, twelve points

The question, as it was set. Select and describe two distinct ways of monitoring the chief executive. Explain who performs the monitoring and which tools they have. At most three to four sentences per method.

Method 1 — the board of directors, and specifically its independent members. The monitor is the board, and within it the independent directors and the audit and remuneration committees, since an inside director reports to the chief executive in his day job and cannot credibly vote against him. Their tools are the strongest of any monitor: they hire and dismiss the chief executive, design and approve his pay contract, approve budgets, major acquisitions and large financings, and the audit committee can meet the external auditor with no member of management in the room. The mechanism reaching the chief executive is direct and continuous: the person who decides his pay and his tenure is not himself, so shirking has a personal cost. The weakness is capture, because management usually controls what information reaches the board and often influences who is nominated to it, which is why independence, separating the chair from the chief executive, and annual election of all directors are the standard remedies.

Method 2 — the market for corporate control. The monitor here is an outside acquirer, and behind it every shareholder who can decide to sell into a bid; no one needs the board's permission. The tool is the hostile takeover itself, most often a tender offer made directly to shareholders over the board's head, after which the entire management team is replaced. The mechanism works largely as a threat: a firm whose value is depressed by weak management trades below what a competent owner would pay, and that gap is itself the invitation, so a chief executive who wants to keep his job has a reason to close it before someone else does. Its limitations are the reason section 27.6 exists: poison pills and staggered boards can make a hostile purchase practically impossible without board consent, the free-rider problem forces bidders to pay most of the improvement away in the premium, and takeovers are slow and expensive, so this monitor works on years rather than on quarters.

Why this scores. Four things. The two methods are genuinely distinct, one internal and one external, rather than two versions of shareholder pressure, and the question said "distinct". Each names the monitor as a person or body, not as an abstraction. Each lists concrete tools, which is what the question literally asked for, and each closes with the condition under which the monitor fails, which is where the discrimination between a strong and a weak answer happens. Both stay inside the four-sentence budget.

Model answer 2: the H2025 Exercise 2 shape, three actions, four points each

The question, as it was set. You hold between 5% and 10% of the shares and votes in a listed company. Management is plainly exerting low effort and enjoying private benefits instead of maximising firm value. Give three actions you could take, two to three sentences per action.

Action 1 — run an activist campaign to change the pay contract. I would publish a specific proposal to cut fixed salary in favour of restricted stock with long vesting and a bonus measured relative to an industry benchmark, and vote against the remuneration report until it is adopted. This works because it raises the manager's pay-performance sensitivity: at present he keeps a small fraction of any value he creates and bears almost none of what he wastes, so effort is expensive for him and shirking is nearly free, and changing that changes his behaviour without anyone having to observe his effort. The limitation is that sensitivity is not free either, since an undiversified manager must be compensated for bearing firm-specific risk, and heavy use of options can push him from shirking into excessive risk taking.

Action 2 — build a coalition with the other large holders and requisition an extraordinary general meeting. With 5 to 10% I can force a meeting in most European regimes, including Norway, but I cannot win a vote alone, so the first move is to approach the other institutional holders, who share my interest and have been free-riding on it, and agree a joint slate of directors. The mechanism is that the board is the only body that can dismiss the chief executive or rewrite his contract, so changing its composition changes what he faces every day, and directors facing a contested election typically settle before the vote. The limitation is cost and time: I bear the full expense of organising while every other shareholder receives their share of the gain for nothing, and a staggered board can delay a change of majority by two annual cycles.

Action 3 — demand that the surplus cash be paid out, or that the firm be recapitalised with debt. I would press for a special dividend or a buyback funded partly by borrowing, so that the cash pile is committed rather than available. The mechanism is the free cash flow argument: cash inside the firm is what pays for the private benefits and the comfortable life, while contractual interest and principal must be paid on time, default would hand control to creditors and end the manager's career, and any new project must now be presented to outside investors who will price it. The limitation is that leverage is not free, since it brings expected distress costs and the shareholder-creditor conflicts of debt overhang and risk shifting, so this is a remedy for a mature cash-rich firm and not for one with lumpy investment needs.

A fourth if there is time. Support a change of control: use the stake as a toehold to invite a bidder, or lead a take-private with a financial sponsor, which replaces the whole team at once and concentrates ownership so that monitoring is worthwhile by construction. H2025 explicitly credits creative but well-argued answers, so a fourth action costs nothing.

Why this scores. Each item is three sentences in the same skeleton: the action, the mechanism that reaches the manager, the limitation. Each mechanism is named in the vocabulary the keys use, pay-performance sensitivity, free cash flow, the market for corporate control, and each identifies who is disciplined and how. The three are also different in kind, one changing incentives, one changing the board, one changing the balance sheet, which is what stops the second and third items reading as restatements of the first. Nothing is spent defining corporate governance.

Model answer 3: the shorter defence question, and the six-point version of the same answer

The question. The board of a listed firm adopts a poison pill without a shareholder vote. Is this in the shareholders' interest?

The six-point answer. "A poison pill lets existing shareholders buy new shares cheaply if a raider crosses a threshold, so it dilutes him and blocks the takeover. This protects management from being replaced, which is bad for shareholders because it removes the discipline of the takeover threat."

The twelve-point answer. "A poison pill allows every shareholder other than the bidder to buy new shares at a large discount once the bidder crosses a trigger stake, so the bidder's holding is diluted to the point where a hostile purchase is not worth attempting. Since only the board can redeem the pill, its real effect is to require the bidder to negotiate with the board rather than with the shareholders. Two readings of that are possible and the evidence does not settle it cleanly. Under the shareholder-bargaining hypothesis the board becomes a single negotiator for a dispersed and free-riding shareholder base, which is worth something precisely because the free-rider problem in takeovers otherwise forces bidders to offer nearly the whole improvement anyway, and a board can defeat a coercive low-ball tender. Under the managerial-entrenchment hypothesis, the board is composed of people the bid would displace, so a pill lets them refuse an offer that shareholders would have accepted, and firms with strong defences do receive fewer bids. The decisive objection to this pill is procedural rather than economic: it was adopted without a shareholder vote, so directors have taken to themselves a decision about whether the owners may sell their own property. My conclusion is that it is defensible as a negotiating instrument and indefensible as a permanent, unilaterally adopted one, and the natural remedy is to require it to be ratified at the next general meeting."

What the second answer has that the first does not.

ElementSix-point answerTwelve-point answer
The mechanismDescribed roughlyStated precisely, including who can redeem the pill, which is where its power lies
Both sides namedOne side onlyBoth hypotheses named in the terms the literature uses
EvidenceNoneWhat the two hypotheses predict differently, so the argument is testable
A conclusionAssertedConditional, with a concrete remedy attached

Check: the second answer is roughly three times as long, it fits the four to six sentence budget these questions state, and none of the extra length is description. If you are writing a fourth sentence that only explains what the defence is, you have left the budget without adding an element ✓

The five arguments that fit almost any governance question If the question is unfamiliar, run this list and pick the three that apply. One, incentives: what is the manager's pay-performance sensitivity and what would change it? Two, information: who can see what he is doing, and who controls the flow, the auditor, the board, analysts? Three, control rights: who can actually remove him, and what stands in the way, the board, the vote, the takeover market, the defences? Four, cash: does he have free cash flow, and would a payout or leverage take away the discretion? Five, ownership structure: is the register dispersed, in which case the conflict is management against shareholders, or concentrated, in which case it is the controlling owner against the minority? Every question in this chapter is one of those five wearing a costume.
Where governance connects to the rest of the manual The agency problem this chapter treats is the equity-side twin of the debt-side problems in k9 and k10, and the free cash flow mechanism it uses repeatedly is k11's agency benefit of debt. The market for corporate control is the disciplinary takeover motive from k26 § 26.2, and takeover defences are what lower the implied deal probability of k26 § 26.6. Option delta N(d1) in the pay-sensitivity formula is k23, and the reason option-heavy pay invites risk shifting is k9. k28 § 28.5 gives the answer template for verbal exercises and a further worked governance answer using a different set of three actions from the ones above, so read the two together: between them you have six actions already written out.

Part VII — Exam craft

↑ contents

28 · Answering the exam

You now know the material. This chapter is about the other half of the grade: recognising which of six routines a question is asking for, writing the answer in the order the examiner reads it, and finishing with checks rather than with the last line of arithmetic. Nothing new is taught here. Everything is a template, a check, a named error or a piece of timing, and all of it is drawn from what the two Kurbatov papers actually asked and what the solution keys actually rewarded.

28.1 The paper, and the first ten minutes

Six exercises, 100 points, three hours, no multiple choice. Points are printed next to every sub-question. The grade is set on the total, so no exercise has to be passed and no exercise is worth agonising over: a sub-question you cannot start is worth exactly as much as one you never read, and both are worth less than a rough answer with the method stated.

Spend the first ten minutes not writing. Read all six exercises, write the printed points next to each, and convert them into a clock at 1.6 minutes per point, the budget from k0 § 0.2. Then decide the order. Positions 1 and 2 have been the verbal exercises in both papers, and they are the cheapest points on the paper for a prepared candidate, so many people should start there. What you must not do is drift into the largest exercise first because it looks important, and arrive at minute 150 with two twelve-point discussions unwritten.

budget for an exercise = 1.6 × its printed points
closing minute = 10 + 1.6 × (points printed on this and all earlier exercises)
180 minutes less 10 for reading and 10 for checking leaves 160 for 100 points. The second line is the one to write in the margin of the paper: it converts the budget into a wall-clock time you can compare with the clock on the wall, which the first line does not. The table below assumes the 12 / 12 / 18 / 18 / 20 / 20 split of both Kurbatov papers; use the points your own paper prints.
ExercisePoints assumedBudgetClosed by minuteWhat it has been
Reading and planning—1010Points written down, order chosen, clock drawn
1121929Verbal: M&A motives (H2024), Black-Scholes intuition (H2025)
2121948Verbal: corporate governance, both papers
3182977MM without taxes, recapitalisation, dividends
41829106Risk shifting (H2024), WACC and APV over two periods (H2025)
52032138Twin firm to value by both methods (H2024), real options (H2025)
62032170Real option (H2024), M&A implied deal probability (H2025)
Consistency checks—10180§ 28.6, run over everything you wrote
The budget is a stop signal, not a target The column that matters is the last one. When the clock passes the closing minute for an exercise, stop and move on even if one sub-question is open, and come back at the end if there is time. The marginal point in an exercise you have half-finished is far cheaper than the marginal point in one you have not started, because the early sub-questions of every exercise are the easy ones. Candidates lose more marks to overrunning a single hard sub-question than to any conceptual error on this paper.

28.2 Which routine is this question asking for?

Kurbatov's exercises are built from a small number of standard set-ups, and each announces itself with a phrase. Train yourself to read for the phrase before you read for the numbers.

28.3 Five templates for the quantitative exercises

Each template is the sequence of lines to write. Write them in this order even when you can see the answer, because the marks are attached to the lines, not to the final number.

T1 · The twin-firm valuation. Present in all eleven mapped papers, and usually the largest exercise.

  1. βE of the comparable from its rE by the CAPM, and βD from its rD. If the question says the debt is risk-free, write βD = 0 and say why. If it quotes an rD above rf, βD is not zero.
  2. Unlever with the comparable's own weights: βU = [E/V]βE + [D/V]βD. No (1 − τc) anywhere.
  3. rU by the CAPM. Sanity line: the comparable's own weighted rates must return the same rU.
  4. Relever to your D/E, then rE by the CAPM. Cross-check against MM Proposition II.
  5. rwacc = [E/V]rE + [D/V]rD(1 − τc).
  6. Value by WACC. Then value by APV: VU at rU, plus the shield at the rate the debt policy dictates.
  7. State that the two agree, by name: consistency check 1.

T2 · The recapitalisation. H2024 Exercise 3 and H2025 Exercise 3, 18 points each.

  1. rU from the current firm, and the price and dividend per share before anything happens.
  2. Does the announcement move the price? No taxes means no, and say why in one sentence. With taxes, ΔP = PV(TS)/N0.
  3. Execute: shares repurchased n = Dnew/P, at the post-announcement price. Or, for a dividend, DPS = Dnew/N0 and the ex-dividend price.
  4. New D/E, new rE by MM II, new dividend per share.
  5. Discount the new dividend per share at the new rE and confirm it returns the price. This is the check that can fail.
  6. Set the seller against the holder against the shareholder who did nothing, in a table.

T3 · The real option. A fixed 20-point item since 2017, in both Kurbatov papers.

  1. Draw the tree. Chance nodes and decision nodes marked differently.
  2. Compute the NPV of each terminal branch, at the date it is decided.
  3. Fold backwards: maxima at decision nodes, expectations at chance nodes, never the reverse.
  4. Discount to today, at rf if you are using risk-neutral probabilities ρ, at the stated rate otherwise, and say which you are doing.
  5. Subtract the value of acting now: option value = the folded value − max(0, NPVnow).
  6. Answer the question in words: wait or invest, and what is learned by waiting.

T4 · The state-by-state agency problem. H2024 Exercise 4, and V2024 Problem 1 before it.

  1. Build the table: one row per state, columns for total cash flow, debt payoff min(CF, K), equity payoff max(CF − K, 0), under each alternative.
  2. Firm values first, so you know the first best.
  3. Split each firm value into D and E.
  4. Say what management does, and name it: risk shifting, debt overhang, the coinsurance effect.
  5. Quantify the agency cost as first-best value minus chosen value, not as the transfer.
  6. Close with consistency check 3 or 4 by number.

T5 · The M&A stock swap. H2025 Exercise 6, and 20 to 32 points in both Kurbatov papers.

  1. Convert the offer into an exchange ratio ER if it is quoted as a price per share.
  2. New shares x = ER × NT, and the target's ownership y = x/(NA + x).
  3. Pnew = (A + T + S)/(NA + x), using stand-alone A and T.
  4. NPVA = (1 − y)(A + T + S) − A and NPVT = y(A + T + S) − T.
  5. Check that NPVA + NPVT = S. Not one of the numbered five, but the examiner runs it.
  6. Answer exactly what was asked: the maximum exchange ratio, or the offered premium, or the actual premium. Say which one you computed.
x = ER × NT  ·  y = x/(NA + x)  ·  Pnew = (A + T + S)/(NA + x)
NPVA = (1 − y)(A + T + S) − A  ·  NPVT = y(A + T + S) − T  ·  NPVA + NPVT = S
The two NPVs and the exchange-ratio bound are on the formula sheet; the ownership fraction y and the summation identity are the parts you have to supply. A and T are stand-alone values, so never substitute a post-announcement market capitalisation, which already contains the synergy you are about to add. The identity is an accounting one and cannot fail, so use it to locate a slip in x or y rather than as evidence that the answer is right.
Why writing the template out is worth marks even when the arithmetic fails The keys award method marks. A candidate who writes "unlever the comparable, βU = [E/V]βE + [D/V]βD" and then mis-keys a division has demonstrated the thing being examined and loses one mark; a candidate who writes only a number has demonstrated nothing and loses them all if the number is wrong. H2017 states the rule in the other direction and just as bluntly: no calculation, no points.

28.4 A quantitative answer, written out as it should look

Model answer 1: twin firm to value by both methods, with the examiner's marginal notes

The question, in the shape of H2024 Exercise 5. Vestfjord Terminal AS is planning a logistics division. Nordvest Logistikk ASA is listed and in the same business, with equity worth 750 and debt worth 250, a cost of equity of 12.0000% and a cost of debt of 4.5000%. The risk-free rate is 3.0000% and the market risk premium 5.0000%. Vestfjord will run the division at a target debt-to-value ratio of 40%, borrowing at 4.5000%, with τc = 22%. The division's free cash flow next year is 50 and grows at 2.0000% in perpetuity. (a) Find rU. (b) Find Vestfjord's rE and rwacc. (c) Value the division by the WACC method and by APV. (d) What would you have got wrong by assuming the comparable's debt is risk-free?

(a) Method: the comparable shares the division's business risk but not its financing, so strip its leverage off to get βU, then turn that into rU. Its weights are E/V = 750/1000 = 0.7500 and D/V = 250/1000 = 0.2500.
βE = (0.120000 − 0.030000)/0.050000 = 1.8000
βD = (0.045000 − 0.030000)/0.050000 = 0.3000, not zero, because the comparable borrows above rf
βU = 0.7500(1.8000) + 0.2500(0.3000) = 1.3500 + 0.0750 = 1.4250
rU = 0.030000 + 1.4250(0.050000) = 0.030000 + 0.071250 = 0.101250 = 10.1250%
Sanity: 0.7500(0.120000) + 0.2500(0.045000) = 0.090000 + 0.011250 = 0.101250 ✓ the comparable's pre-tax WACC equals rU, as it must.

(b) Our target is D/V = 0.4000, so D/E = 0.4000/0.6000 = 0.6667.
βE = 1.4250 + 0.666667(1.4250 − 0.3000) = 1.4250 + 0.7500 = 2.1750
rE = 0.030000 + 2.1750(0.050000) = 0.030000 + 0.108750 = 0.138750 = 13.8750%
Cross-check with MM II: 0.101250 + 0.666667(0.101250 − 0.045000) = 0.101250 + 0.037500 = 0.138750 ✓
rwacc = 0.6000(0.138750) + 0.4000(0.045000)(1 − 0.22) = 0.083250 + 0.014040 = 0.097290 = 9.7290%

(c) Method: the question fixes a target D/V, so the firm rebalances, the tax shield carries asset risk and is discounted at rU.
WACC method: VL = 50/(0.097290 − 0.020000) = 50/0.077290 = 646.9142
APV method: VU = 50/(0.101250 − 0.020000) = 50/0.081250 = 615.3846
The debt is 0.4000 of the levered value, so the shield grows at g with the firm and PV(TS) = τcrD(0.4000 VL)/(rU − g). That is one equation in VL; collect the terms:
VL = FCF/[rU − g − τcrD(D/V)] = 50/(0.101250 − 0.020000 − 0.003960) = 50/0.077290 = 646.9142
So D0 = 0.4000 × 646.9142 = 258.7657, the first year's shield is 0.22 × 0.045000 × 258.7657 = 2.5618, and PV(TS) = 2.5618/0.081250 = 31.5296.
Consistency check 1: 615.3846 + 31.5296 = 646.9142, equal to the WACC value ✓ Equity is 646.9142 − 258.7657 = 388.1485.

(d) The comparable borrows at 4.5000%, above rf = 3.0000%, so its debt is not risk-free and βD = 0 would be inconsistent with the rate the question quotes. Suppose it is set to zero anyway and everything afterwards is done correctly with rD = 4.5000%.
βU = 0.7500(1.8000) = 1.3500, a positive term dropped from the weighting
rU = 0.030000 + 1.3500(0.050000) = 0.097500 = 9.7500%, against the true 10.1250%
rE = 0.097500 + 0.666667(0.097500 − 0.045000) = 0.097500 + 0.035000 = 0.132500
rwacc = 0.6000(0.132500) + 0.4000(0.045000)(0.78) = 0.079500 + 0.014040 = 0.093540
VL = 50/(0.093540 − 0.020000) = 679.9021
The error understates βU, and therefore rU and the WACC, and therefore overvalues the division, here by 679.9021 − 646.9142 = 32.9879, about 5.1%. Note also that the sanity line in (a) would have caught it: the beta route gives rU = 0.097500 while the comparable's weighted rates still give 0.101250. Get the direction right in words as well as in numbers.

Why this scores. Four things, and they are worth listing because they are transferable. First, each part opens with the method in one sentence, including the debt-policy assumption in (c), which is the single most-tested judgement in the course. Second, every substituted expression is on the page, so a mis-key costs one mark and not the sub-question. Third, the checks are named by number, and check 1 here is doing real work, though it is worth being precise about how much: the APV route never touches rE, so an error in relevering breaks the agreement, which is exactly the error most likely to be made. It cannot catch a wrong rU or a misread debt policy, because both routes rest on those. Consistency check 2 would also pass here, but it cannot fail, since βE was produced by relevering from that same βU; state it if you like, but do not rely on it. Fourth, part (d) answers with a direction, not only a number.

What the sanity line in part (a) catches, and what it cannot It catches exactly the error of part (d). Set βD = 0 and the beta route gives βU = 0.7500(1.8000) = 1.3500 and rU = 0.097500, while the rate route still gives 0.101250: the clash is unmissable and it costs ten seconds to find. It is blind to one thing, and knowing which is the difference between using a check and trusting it. Invert the weights consistently, taking E/V = 0.2500 in both lines, and the beta route gives 0.2500(1.8000) + 0.7500(0.3000) = 0.6750 while the rate route gives 0.2500(0.120000) + 0.7500(0.045000) = 0.063750, and both then produce rU = 0.063750 without complaint. Two routes that share an input cannot test that input. The guard against that error is not a check but a habit: write E/V and D/V down as numbers before you use them, and read them back against the balance sheet.

28.5 The two verbal exercises

Twenty-four of the hundred points have gone to prose in both papers, and they are the most reliably scoreable points on the paper, because there is no arithmetic to slip on. Two shapes recur.

V1 · The mechanism list. "Give three reasons, other than synergies, why a firm might acquire another" (H2024 Exercise 1). "Explain what happens to a call's value if true volatility turns out to be lower than implied volatility" (H2025 Exercise 1). Answer in a fixed shape, one paragraph per item: name the mechanism in the first three words, explain how it works in one or two sentences, then give one limitation or condition. Three of those is a full answer at four points each.

V2 · The action repertoire. "What can a shareholder holding 5 to 10% do about a CEO who is not acting in shareholders' interest" (H2025 Exercise 2). The same shape, but each item is an action rather than a mechanism, and the middle sentence must explain why the action disciplines the manager, not merely what it is.

Model answer 2: the governance exercise, three actions, twelve points

The question. An investor holds between 5 and 10% of a listed firm whose CEO appears to be pursuing size rather than value: a series of diversifying acquisitions, a large cash balance and flat returns. Describe three actions the investor can take. Four points each, two to three sentences per action.

Action 1 — engage the board and demand representation. A holder of this size can request meetings with the chair and press for one or more independent directors, or a seat, on the grounds that the board is failing at its core monitoring task. This works because the board is the body with the legal power to set the CEO's pay and to dismiss him, so changing its composition changes the incentives the CEO faces every day, rather than merely registering displeasure. Its limitation is speed and dependence on other shareholders: without allies, a 5 to 10% holder cannot elect a director against an entrenched board, and a staggered board can delay any change for years.

Action 2 — demand a payout or a leveraged recapitalisation. The investor can campaign publicly for a special dividend, a buyback programme or a debt-financed recapitalisation that disgorges the cash pile. The mechanism is the free cash flow argument: cash inside the firm is what funds empire building, and committing it to shareholders or to debt service removes the discretion that produced the diversifying acquisitions, while forcing the CEO to return to the capital market, where outsiders price each new project. The limitation is that leverage is not free, since it introduces distress and debt-agency costs, and a firm with genuinely lumpy investment needs may be damaged by losing the buffer.

Action 3 — vote against the board and, if necessary, run a proxy fight. At the annual meeting the investor can vote against re-election of directors and against the remuneration report, submit a shareholder proposal, and if that fails solicit proxies from other holders to replace directors outright. This disciplines the CEO because directors who fear losing their seats have a private reason to act on the complaint before the vote, which is why most campaigns settle before they reach a ballot. The limitation is cost: a proxy fight is expensive and its benefits accrue to every shareholder, so the activist bears the whole cost of a public good, which is the classic free-rider problem of dispersed ownership.

A fourth, if time allows. Sell the stake, the "Wall Street walk". This looks like giving up but is a governance mechanism: concentrated selling depresses the price, which hurts a CEO whose pay is equity-linked and makes the firm a target in the market for corporate control. H2025 gave credit for creative but well-argued answers, so an extra action costs nothing and may earn something.

Why this scores. Each item is exactly three sentences and follows the same skeleton: the action, the mechanism that reaches the CEO, the limitation. The mechanisms are named in the words the keys use, free cash flow and the market for corporate control, and each one identifies who is disciplined and how. Nothing is padded with definitions of corporate governance, which earn nothing.

Model answer 3: the same question answered for six points and for twelve

The question, in the shape of H2024 Exercise 1. Give three reasons, other than synergies, why one firm might acquire another.

The six-point answer. "A firm might acquire another because the target is undervalued. It might also acquire for diversification, to spread its risk across more businesses. A third reason is that managers want to build an empire and run a larger firm."

The twelve-point answer. "Undervaluation. If the acquirer believes the market price understates the target's true value, buying it is a positive-NPV investment even with no operational change. This requires the acquirer to have better information than the market, so it is plausible for a bidder in the same industry and implausible as a general claim.
Diversification and coinsurance. Combining two imperfectly correlated cash flow streams makes default less likely, which raises the value of the debt. Note the direction: this is a gain to creditors, and unless the firm relevers afterwards to capture it, shareholders can be worse off. Shareholders can also diversify on their own account far more cheaply, so diversification alone is a weak motive.
Agency: empire building. Managers derive private benefits from size, status and pay that scales with the firm, so they may acquire even at a negative NPV. The prediction is testable: this motive is most common in cash-rich firms with weak governance and few investment opportunities, and it typically shows up as a negative acquirer announcement return."

What the second answer has that the first does not. Four things, and they are the same four every time:

ElementSix-point answerTwelve-point answer
The mechanism namedReasons are labelled but not explainedEach label is followed by the channel through which value moves
A direction or a signAbsentWho gains, who loses, and by which route
A condition or limitationAbsentEach item says when it holds and when it fails
A link to course machineryAbsentCoinsurance, agency costs, announcement returns

Check: the twelve-point answer is only about three times as long, and none of the extra length is padding. The sentence budgets in the paper, two to three sentences per action or four to six in total, are large enough for exactly this and no larger. If you are writing a fourth sentence of description, you have already left the budget without adding an element.

28.6 The closing ritual: the five consistency checks

Reserve the last ten minutes for this and nothing else. Go through what you have written and, for each exercise, run whichever of the five applies and write one sentence saying it passed. The list is from k0 § 0.5 and the numbering is fixed.

#The checkRun it onThe sentence to write
1Value by WACC = value by APVT1, and any levered valuation"Both methods give 646.9142, so the valuation is internally consistent."
2[E/V]βE + [D/V]βD = βUT1, unlever and relever"Reweighting returns βU = 1.4250." Say if it cannot fail.
3Creditors' gain + shareholders' gain = the project's NPVT4, agency problems"−35 and +25 sum to −10, the NPV of switching."
4Creditors' gain = shareholders' loss when total value is fixedT4, hedging and risk shifting"Total value is unchanged, so this is a pure transfer of 35."
5Are investors' beliefs rational given the action taken?Asymmetric information, and any signalling answer"Given that the manager issues only when overvalued, the assumed beliefs are consistent."
Running a check that cannot fail, and believing it Checks 1 and 2 are redundant computations, but only if the second route uses different inputs. Recomputing βU from a βE you produced by relevering that same βU is algebra, not verification: it returns 1.4250 whatever else is wrong. The same trap catches P = E1/N1 after a repurchase where n was defined as D/P, and D + E = VL where E was computed as VL − D. Before you write "✓", ask what number would have to be wrong for this line to break. If the answer is "none", the check is decoration; find the route that does not share inputs, which is usually the one that discounts a new cash flow at a new rate.

28.7 Rounding, and the errors the keys name

H2025 asked for four decimal places, and the keys penalise drift beyond roughly 0.001. Two rules follow.

carry rates at 6 decimals · report rates at 4 · report money at 2
Round only at the point where you write the number down, never inside a chain. The danger is compounding: a rate rounded to 0.0973 instead of 0.097290 shifts a perpetuity value by several units, and in a nine-step exercise it shifts every step after it. When you print an intermediate breakdown, make the parts sum to the total you print: write 0.083250 + 0.014040 = 0.097290, not 0.0833 + 0.0140 = 0.0973.

The solution keys single out a short list of errors. They are worth reading as a checklist rather than as a warning, because each one is a specific line you can inspect on your own paper.

Named errorWhat it looks like on the pageFix
Using 100 rather than 100 × 1.03 as the certainty equivalentComparing an amount available today with a payoff a year awayMove every amount to a common date before comparing. Write the date next to each number
Rounding drift beyond about 0.001An answer that is nearly right and gets partial credit at bestSix decimals inside the chain, four when reported
Confusing the stand-alone NPV with the change in equity valueReporting a project's NPV when the question asked what shareholders gainWith risky debt these differ. Compute both claims and subtract
Believing shareholders gain from reducing debtA conclusion that ignores how much of the efficiency gain creditors captureRun consistency check 3, then compare the shareholders' column alone
No calculation, no points (H2017)A correct number with nothing above itWrite the substituted expression on every line
Answering a different questionThe offered premium when the actual premium was asked, or the reverseUnderline the noun in the question before you compute, and name what you computed
The five formulas that are not on the sheet and are asked for anyway VL = VU + PV(TS) and which rate discounts the shield · Dt = d·VtL and the debt adjustment between periods · put-call parity · βE = N(d1)(V/E)βU · the Myers-Majluf setup with α = I/(E[V | beliefs] + I + NPV), including the rationality check. Everything about real options is also absent from the sheet. Write these six lines on your rough paper in the first minute, before you read anything, while they are still intact.

28.8 When you are stuck in a chained sub-question

Kurbatov's exercises chain: (b) consumes (a), and H2025 Exercise 4 ran to nine linked steps. If (b) will not come, the worst response is to stop, and the second worst is to invent a number silently. The protocol is three lines.

  1. Name the missing quantity as a symbol and say so: "I could not obtain βU in (a); write it as βU and carry it forward."
  2. Carry it through the remaining algebra. rU = 0.030000 + 0.050000βU, then rE = rU + 0.666667(rU − 0.045000), then rwacc = 0.6000rE + 0.4000(0.045000)(0.78). Every one of those lines is a method mark and none of them needs the number.
  3. If a number is needed later, assume one and label it. "Assume βU = 1.4000, stated as an assumption because part (a) is unresolved."

Carried through with that assumption, the chain gives rU = 0.030000 + 1.4000(0.050000) = 0.100000, then rE = 0.100000 + 0.666667(0.100000 − 0.045000) = 0.100000 + 0.036667 = 0.136667, then rwacc = 0.6000(0.136667) + 0.4000(0.045000)(0.78) = 0.082000 + 0.014040 = 0.096040, and a value of 50/(0.096040 − 0.020000) = 657.5487. Every later sub-question is now answerable, the method is visible, and the only thing lost is the mark for part (a).

Why an explicitly stated assumption costs almost nothing The examiner is marking whether you can execute the routine, and the routine is unaffected by which number entered it. A labelled assumption converts an unanswerable chain into a fully answerable one at the price of a single mark, and it also removes the risk that the marker reads your later parts as wrong rather than as consistent with an earlier slip. The habit has a second use during the exam itself: writing the algebra symbolically often shows you what was wrong in the earlier step, because the structure of the expression becomes visible once the arithmetic is out of the way.
The last three minutes Three things, in this order. Check that every sub-question has something written under it, because an empty sub-question is the only guaranteed zero on the paper. Check that each verbal answer names a mechanism in its first line. And check that every rate you reported carries four decimals, which is what H2025 asked for and what the keys measure drift against.
Where to go from here This chapter is a set of pointers, not a substitute. T1 is k4 and k17 to k20, T2 is k6 and k15, T3 is k25, T4 is k9 and k10, T5 is k26, and the verbal repertoire is k11 § 11.4 and k27. k29 is the formula reference to drill against in the last week, and k0 § 0.5 is where the five checks were defined. Redo the worked examples with the solutions covered; that is the only study activity that resembles the paper.

Reference — Formula reference

↑ contents

29 · Formula reference

Every formula the exam can ask you to use, on one page, grouped the way the six exercises are grouped. Use it as a self-test rather than a lookup table: cover the two right-hand columns, read the concept, then write the formula and say out loud when it applies and which discount rate belongs to it. Notation is the same as in the rest of the manual: subscripts E, D and U mean equity, debt and unlevered, superscripts L and U mean levered and unlevered firm value, τc is the corporate tax rate and ρ is the risk-neutral probability. At 1.8 minutes per point you cannot afford to rediscover a formula during the exam, so the goal is recall, not recognition.

What the formula sheet gives you, and what you must memorise Handed out at the exam: the FCF definition, the perpetuity and growing-perpetuity formulas, the CAPM, rE = rU + (D/E)(rU − rD), the βU weighting, pre-tax and after-tax WACC, the binomial replication (Δ and the risk-neutral form with ρ), and the M&A stock-swap NPVs with the exchange-ratio bound. Those rows below are the ones you can read off the sheet.
Not on the sheet, so it has to be in your head: VL = VU + PV(TS) and which rate discounts the shield · the permanent-debt result τcD · Dt = d·VtL and the debt adjustment between periods · put-call parity · βE = N(d1)(V/E)βU · the risky-debt decompositions · the Myers-Majluf setup · and every single real-option formula, including the value of information. Sections 29.4, 29.7 and 29.8 below are almost entirely memory work, which is exactly why they separate a B from an A.

29.1 Time value and cash flow

ConceptFormulaKey point
Present value of one cash flowPV = Ct/(1 + r)tThe rate must match the risk and the length of the period; discounting two periods uses (1 + r)2, never 1 + 2r
PerpetuityPV = C/rOn the formula sheet. Values a stream whose first payment arrives one period from now, so a flow starting today is worth C more
Growing perpetuityPV = C/(r − g)On the formula sheet. Requires g < r; C is next period's cash flow, not today's
AnnuityPV = (C/r)[1 − (1 + r)−N]A perpetuity minus a perpetuity starting at N+1. This is the formula that prices an exit option, because exit truncates a stream
Growing annuityPV = [C/(r − g)][1 − ((1 + g)/(1 + r))N]The bracket is the share of the perpetuity you actually collect; it approaches 1 as N grows
Free cash flowFCF = EBIT(1 − τc) + Depreciation − CapEx − ΔNWC + OtherOn the formula sheet. Interest never appears here; financing enters through the discount rate, not the numerator
Change in net working capitalΔNWCt = NWCt − NWCt−1Subtract the change, not the level. Running down inventory releases cash, so a negative ΔNWC raises free cash flow
Terminal value with growthVN = FCFN+1/(r − g)The result sits at date N, so it still has to be discounted N periods back before you add it to anything

29.2 Risk and cost of capital

ConceptFormulaKey point
CAPM / security market lineri = rf + βi(E[Rmkt] − rf)On the formula sheet. Only systematic risk is compensated; a volatile but uncorrelated cash flow earns the risk-free rate
Beta as a covarianceβi = Cov(Ri, Rmkt)/Var(Rmkt)High standard deviation with low correlation still gives a low beta, which is why total risk is the wrong input
Unlevered (asset) betaβU = [E/(E + D)]βE + [D/(E + D)]βDOn the formula sheet. This course weights without (1 − τc), because it assumes rebalancing to a target ratio
Relevering the cost of equity (MM II)rE = rU + (D/E)(rU − rD)On the formula sheet. The multiplier is D/E, not D/V; using D/V is the single most common arithmetic slip in this course
Relevering the equity betaβE = βU + (D/E)(βU − βD)The same identity in risk units. Deriving it from the weighting formula is faster than memorising it separately
Pre-tax WACCrU = [E/V]rE + [D/V]rDOn the formula sheet. It equals the unlevered cost of capital, so it prices business risk alone and is unchanged by financing
After-tax WACCrwacc = [E/V]rE + [D/V]rD(1 − τc)On the formula sheet. Weights are market values at target leverage; equivalently rwacc = rU − [D/V]τcrD, which shows the whole effect of debt is the tax subsidy
Beta of risky debtβD = (rD − rf)/(E[Rmkt] − rf)Setting it to zero when the problem quotes a debt cost above rf drops a positive term from βU = [E/V]βE + [D/V]βD. You therefore understate βU, rU and the WACC, and overvalue the firm
The twin-firm routinecomparable rE → βE → βU → rU → your own D/E → rE → rwaccThe most examined procedure in the course. The comparable supplies business risk only; your own capital structure supplies the financing risk. Close by checking that [E/V]βE + [D/V]βD returns βU

29.3 Capital structure and the tax shield

ConceptFormulaKey point
MM Proposition IVL = VUHolds in perfect markets, and the argument is arbitrage through homemade leverage, not an assumption about what investors like
Recapitalisation: shares repurchasedn = D/PP is the pre-announcement price only when the recap is a genuine surprise. If value was created and announced, the price has already jumped and you must use the new one
Recapitalisation in a perfect marketP unchanged, V unchanged, E falls by exactly DValue moves from equity to debt one for one and nobody gains, which is the entire content of MM I. Compare a shareholder who tendered with one who held: identical wealth
Interest tax shield in one periodTSt = τc × rD × DtDriven by interest actually paid, so an amortising loan shrinks the shield year by year and the shield must be valued schedule in hand
Levered firm value with taxesVL = VU + PV(TS)Not on the formula sheet. State which rate you discounted the shield at every time you write it: rU under constant D/E, rD or rf under fixed permanent debt
Permanent debtVL = VU + τcDA perpetuity of τcrDD discounted at rD. Valid only for fixed, never-repaid debt; applying it to a target-leverage firm overstates value
Share price at the announcementΔP = PV(TS)/N0Existing shareholders capture the gain the moment the plan is credible, before any debt is issued or any share is bought back
Effective tax advantage with personal taxesτ* = 1 − (1 − τc)(1 − τe)/(1 − τi)Investors pay tax on interest income too. When τi is high enough the corporate advantage shrinks towards zero and can disappear
Trade-off theoryVL = VU + PV(TS) − PV(distress costs)Optimal leverage is where the marginal shield equals the marginal expected distress cost. Distress costs are priced in today, long before any default happens

29.4 Agency and information

ConceptFormulaKey point
State-by-state payoff splitEquity = E[max(CF − K, 0)] · Debt = E[min(CF, K)]The two must add back to total firm value in every state. Building the table and checking the sum catches almost every arithmetic error in these exercises
Risk shifting (asset substitution)Shareholders maximise equity value, not firm valueWith risky debt the downside belongs to creditors, so shareholders prefer the riskier project even when it destroys value. Write the words "risk shifting" or "asset substitution": the key awards the name
Agency cost of risk shiftingCost = V(first-best project) − V(chosen project)Measure it as forgone total value, not as the shareholders' gain. The two are different numbers and the examiner asks for this one
Indifference level of debtSolve Equity(safe project | K) = Equity(risky project | K) for KThe answer is a threshold, so report it as "for face values above K the manager switches", not as a bare number
Hedging with risky debt outstandingShareholders accept a zero-NPV hedge only if their own claim gainsA hedge cuts variance, which transfers value to creditors. The refusal is the same mechanism as risk shifting, running in reverse
Debt overhangInvest only if ΔEquity ≥ IA positive-NPV project is turned down whenever creditors capture more of it than its NPV. Name the mechanism; the computation alone is worth fewer points
Renegotiation consistency checkΔcreditors + Δshareholders = NPV of the projectIf your two gains do not sum to the project's NPV, one of the payoff splits is wrong. The examiner performs this check, so perform it first
Agency benefit of debtDebt commits free cash flow to interest paymentsThe counterweight to the two costs above: it disciplines empire building, and it matters most in mature, cash-rich firms with weak investment opportunities
Myers-Majluf issue fractionα = I/(E[V | investors' beliefs] + I + NPV)Not on the formula sheet. The denominator is the post-issue value under what investors believe: assets, plus the cash raised, plus the project's NPV. Forgetting the I is the standard slip, and it makes α too large
Myers-Majluf issue rule and equilibriumIssue if (1 − α)(Vtrue + NPV + I) ≥ VtrueComputing α is half an answer. Finish by checking that investors' beliefs are rational given who actually issues, and say whether the outcome is pooling or separating

29.5 Valuation with leverage

ConceptFormulaKey point
WACC methodVL = Σ FCFt/(1 + rwacc)tAssumes a constant debt-to-value ratio, maintained by rebalancing. The numerator is unlevered free cash flow, with no interest in it
WACC method with constant growthVL0 = FCF1/(rwacc − g)The exam usually runs this backwards: solve for g, or for the break-even price that makes value equal the investment. Rearrange algebraically rather than guessing
APV methodVL = VU + PV(TS), with VU discounted at rUThe unlevered value always uses rU. Only the shield's discount rate is ever in question, and that is the part the multiple-choice questions targeted
Discount rate for the tax shieldrU under constant D/E · rD or rf under fixed permanent debtNot on the formula sheet. Naming the assumption in one sentence earns credit even if the arithmetic afterwards goes wrong
Debt scaled to valueDt = d × VLtWith a target ratio the debt level is unknown until value is known, which is circular. Break the circle by working backwards from the final period
Rolling value back one periodVLt = (FCFt+1 + VLt+1)/(1 + rwacc)Do this one period at a time; it is what makes the whole debt schedule computable and it is how a nine-step valuation is actually organised
Debt adjustment between periodsΔDt = d(VLt − VLt−1)When levered value falls you must repay debt to hold the ratio. H2025 Exercise 4(f) turns on exactly this step and most candidates never take it
Flow to equityFCFE = FCF − (1 − τc)×Interest + Net borrowingDiscount at rE, and remember it values equity only. Add debt back before comparing with a WACC or APV answer
From enterprise value to equity valueE0 = VL0 + Excess cash − D0Excess cash is not operating value, so it is added after discounting and never inside FCF. This is the last line of a full valuation and the easiest point to drop

29.6 Options

ConceptFormulaKey point
Payoff at expiryCall: max(ST − K, 0) · Put: max(K − ST, 0)Payoff ignores the premium, profit subtracts it. Read the question wording carefully, because it tells you which of the two diagrams is wanted
Put-call parityC = P + S − K/(1 + rf)TNot on the formula sheet. European options, same strike, same maturity, no dividends. The exam uses it to hand you three prices and demand the fourth
Bounds on a callmax(S − PV(K), 0) ≤ C ≤ SA call is never worth more than the share itself, and an American call on a non-dividend-paying stock is never worth exercising early
Binomial replication: the hedge ratioΔ = (Cu − Cd)/(Su − Sd)On the formula sheet. It is the number of shares in the replicating portfolio; numerator and denominator must describe the same two states
Binomial replication: the borrowingB = (Cd − SdΔ)/(1 + rf) and C = SΔ + BOn the formula sheet. A negative B means you borrow. The option price follows from no arbitrage, with no probabilities used anywhere
Risk-neutral probabilityρ = [(1 + rf)S0 − Sd]/(Su − Sd)On the formula sheet. It is a pricing weight, not a forecast. Saying that the true probability is irrelevant is worth a mark on its own
Risk-neutral valuationC0 = [ρCu + (1 − ρ)Cd]/(1 + rf)Discount at the risk-free rate, never at a risk-adjusted one, because ρ has already absorbed the risk premium. Reverse it to back ρ out of an observed price
Black-Scholes callC = S·N(d1) − PV(K)·N(d2)N(d2) is the risk-neutral probability of finishing in the money; N(d1) is the hedge ratio Δ. The sheet no longer carries N(d) tables, so the exam asks for meaning, not arithmetic
Comparative statics and implied volatilityCall value rises in S, σ, T and rf, falls in KVolatility is the one that gets tested, and it raises calls and puts alike because the downside is truncated. If true σ is below implied, the option is overpriced: sell it and hedge with Δ shares

29.7 Credit risk

ConceptFormulaKey point
Yield to maturity of a zero-coupon bondP = K/(1 + y)Ty is a promised return, earned only if the borrower does not default. Never plug it into the CAPM or into a WACC as an expected return
Expected return on risky debtrD = y − p·Lp is the default probability and L the loss given default. The wedge y − rD = p·L is expected loss, which is compensation for nothing and never enters βD. βD comes from rD exceeding rf, that is from default risk being systematic
Credit spreadSpread = y − rfWider than expected loss alone, because default clusters in bad states and therefore carries systematic risk that has to be paid for
Equity as a call on the firmE = Call(V, K, T)Limited liability is the option. Shareholders buy the firm back from creditors by repaying the face value, and walk away otherwise
Risky debt, two decompositionsD = risk-free debt − Put(V, K, T) = V − Call(V, K, T)The put is the default option shareholders hold, and its value is the entire credit spread. Use whichever decomposition the question's data fits
Equity beta from the option deltaβE = N(d1)(V/E)βUNot on the formula sheet. It explains why equity beta explodes as a firm nears distress: V/E rises far faster than N(d1) falls
Risk-neutral default probability1 − N(d2)Larger than the true probability because it is measured under the pricing measure. Do not present it as a real-world forecast of default
Credit default swapPV(premiums) = PV(expected loss), both risk-neutralA CDS is insurance on default, so it is priced off market-implied probabilities, not off historical default frequencies

29.8 Real options

ConceptFormulaKey point
Value of the option to waitΣpi·max(0, NPVi)/(1 + r) − max(0, NPVnow)Not on the formula sheet. The max(0, ·) inside the sum is the whole mechanism: waiting lets you walk away from the bad states, so it truncates the downside
When to waitWait if that difference is positiveA positive NPV today does not mean invest today. Say explicitly what is learned during the wait, and what it costs in forgone cash flows
Value of perfect informationVOI = E[max(0, NPVi)] − max(0, E[NPV])Zero whenever you would take the same decision in every state. Information is only worth paying for if it can change your action
Information that arrives with a delayDiscount the informed payoff one extra periodA one-year wait costs a year of discounting and often a year of cash flows. The exam asks whether the information still pays for itself after that
Option to abandon or exitReplace C/r with (C/r)[1 − (1 + r)−N] + PV(salvage)Exit converts a perpetuity into a truncated annuity. Forgetting the salvage or resale value is the standard way to lose half the marks here
Option to expandA follow-on investment is a call on the second-stage projectIt justifies accepting a first stage with negative stand-alone NPV. Name it a growth option, or the reasoning reads as wishful thinking
Indifference pointSet the two branch values equal and solve for the unknownReport a threshold and a direction: "exercise only if the price exceeds …". Switching or exit costs move the threshold without changing the rule
Decision-tree disciplineFold backwards from the last decision nodeTake expectations at chance nodes and maxima at decision nodes. Mixing the two, or averaging before the decision, is the most common structural error

29.9 Mergers and acquisitions

ConceptFormulaKey point
New shares issued in a stock swapx = ER × NTER is acquirer shares per target share. The exam usually hides it inside an offer price per share, so convert first and only then compute anything
Post-merger share pricePnew = (A + T + S)/(NA + x)A and T must be stand-alone values. Using post-announcement market values double counts the synergy that the announcement already priced in
Target's ownership of the combined firmy = x/(NA + x)In a stock deal the target shares in the synergy, so the acquirer's outcome depends on y, not on the headline offer price
NPV to the acquirerNPVA = (1 − y)(A + T + S) − AOn the formula sheet. A positive synergy with a negative acquirer NPV is perfectly possible, and spotting that is usually the point of the question
NPV to the targetNPVT = y(A + T + S) − TOn the formula sheet. The two NPVs must sum to the synergy S. Run that check before you write your conclusion
Maximum exchange ratioERmax = (NA/NT)(T + S)/AOn the formula sheet. The ratio at which the acquirer's NPV is exactly zero, so it answers "how much can they afford to offer"
Offered versus actual premiumOffered uses the pre-announcement acquirer price; actual uses the post-announcement priceIn a stock deal the two differ whenever the acquirer's share price moves on the news. Read which one is asked for, and say which one you computed
EPS after the mergerEPSnew = (EarningsA + EarningsT)/(NA + x)Acquiring a lower-P/E target mechanically raises EPS with no value created. EPS accretion is arithmetic, not a motive
Implied probability that the deal closesp = (PT − Pfail)/(Pdeal − Pfail)Back it out of the target's traded price between announcement and completion, using the standalone price as the failure value. This was H2025 Exercise 6, 20 points
Motives other than synergiesundervaluation · market power · tax · coinsurance · diversification · hubris · empire building · disciplinary takeoverH2024 banned "synergies" as an answer, so have at least five of these ready with one sentence of mechanism each
The five consistency checks, in one place Every one of these is a check the examiner runs, and stating it in your answer is worth points even when the number it confirms was already right. (1) The WACC value and the APV value of the same project agree. (2) The weighted average of βE and βD returns βU. (3) Total gain to creditors plus total gain to shareholders equals the project's NPV. (4) When total value is fixed, the creditors' gain is exactly the shareholders' loss. (5) In an asymmetric-information problem, are investors' beliefs rational given the action the manager takes? Computing α is not the end of the answer. Two more that are worth stating where they apply, though they are not part of the numbered five: in a state-by-state split, debt value plus equity value equals firm value in every state; and in a stock swap, the acquirer's NPV plus the target's NPV equals the synergy. Write the check as a sentence, not as a number in the margin: "the two methods give 412.4 and 412.4, so the valuation is internally consistent."
